# Part I, The Burial of the Dead: the debate record

Ethan's instruction (20 September 2026): the commentary was written in the voice of an LLM; set up Eliot scholar agents and reader agents to debate every section so that it is clear, good in its wording, and real.

How this was run. Every piece of commentary hung on the title, epigraph, dedication and lines 1 to 76 was put to two reviewers working independently: a scholar (briefed on the 1971 facsimile, Ricks and McCue, Southam, Rainey, Grover Smith, Kenner, Gardner, Gordon, Crawford, and the source texts, with the web to check them) and a reader (an attentive non-specialist, briefed to ask whether a note is clear, whether it helps with the line, whether a person would say it, and whether it shows off). The editor read both reports, drafted the rewrite, and sent it back to both; each said whether it was satisfied or what still failed; the editor revised; a third round where needed. On substance the scholar had the last word, on wording the reader. Points not settled are listed at the end.

The items fall in two groups. Glosses, ways in, pathway stops and the Part I drafts record are in Part I's own files and were rewritten in place. Library notes, voice labels, tarot card notes and pathway intros live in files shared across the site, which the thread "Debate: front matter and notes" owns; that thread ran its own debate on them at the same time and applied its rewrite to the shared files while this one's fourth batch was out (between 18:44 and 18:51 UTC). The second round on those items was therefore run against its text, and what this debate still had to add is in `shared-proposals-part1.md`. Two other things about the fourth batch: the scholar's single packet of twenty-two library notes stalled twice before it was reviewed in three parts (its three reports are printed as one below), and the reader's round-one verdicts on the library notes, voices and drafts records were given on the text as it stood before the other thread's rewrite.

Below, each item is given as it stood, then each side's report in each round, then the editor's decision and draft. The wording that went into the files is the last editor entry under each item; for the shared items it is the other thread's text with this debate's proposal, if any.

A fourth pass, after the record was first written. The front-matter thread's own scholar and reader had read all the pathway stops before that thread learned the stops belonged to the part threads; their reports (`debates/site/r1/F-scholar.md` and `F-reader.md`) were on the stops as they stood before this debate's rewrite, and most of what they flagged was already met. Nine stops in Part I still carried something they had found: a reading given as fact, a source named without a word of what it is, a lake placed in a city, a knowing aside. The editor drafted revisions (printed below as the editor's entry after round three), the batch 5 scholar and reader each gave one verdict per stop (round four), and the text applied at 19:38 UTC is the editor's entry after round four. Two thin captions on the Eyes and Nothing pathways, which that thread's reader also queried, are a question about the pathways' design that the front-matter thread has put to the build thread, and were left alone.


# Batch 1: title, epigraph, dedication, lines 1–30


## title

**As it stood**

TITLE: Where the title comes from
BODY:
Eliot's first note says it plainly: the title, "the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism" came from Jessie L. Weston's *From Ritual to Romance* (1920). Weston's book argues that the Grail romances preserve, under a Christian skin, a much older fertility cult: a land laid waste because its king is wounded or impotent, healed only when a stranger arrives and asks the right question. The waste land is the country, the king, and the reader who has not yet asked anything.

In the typescript the poem had a different working title, written over the first two parts: *He Do the Police in Different Voices*. It is Betty Higden's praise of the boy Sloppy in Dickens's *Our Mutual Friend* (1865): "You mightn't think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices." A poem that reads the news of the world aloud in as many voices as it can do. Keep that title in your head; it explains more than the Grail does.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Eliot's headnote against work/poem-part1.md (the quoted phrase is exact). Dickens, Our Mutual Friend, Book I ch. 16, Gutenberg #883: "You mightn't think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices." Exact. Weston 1920 (Cambridge UP) from knowledge. The facsimile not reachable; Wikipedia confirms that only Parts I and II of the typescript carry the working title.
Substance:
1. "written over the first two parts": the phrase is typed as a heading on the typescripts of Parts I and II ("HE DO THE POLICE IN DIFFERENT VOICES: Part I. THE BURIAL OF THE DEAD", and likewise Part II). "Written over" suggests a hand-written overwriting. Say "typed at the head of".
2. "The waste land is the country, the king, and the reader who has not yet asked anything." A reading, unattributed, and the third term is not Weston's: her book has the king, the land and the questing knight, not the reader. Cut or mark as the site's own reading.
3. "Our Mutual Friend (1865)": serialised May 1864 to November 1865; "(1864–65)" is the usual form. Add "Book I, chapter 16" so a reader can find it.
4. "A poem that reads the news of the world aloud in as many voices as it can do. Keep that title in your head; it explains more than the Grail does." Reading passed as instruction, and it contradicts the paragraph above, which reports Eliot's own note crediting Weston. If a reading is wanted, give whose: Calvin Bedient's He Do the Police in Different Voices (1986) built a whole book on the working title. Otherwise cut.
5. "a much older fertility cult": Weston's own terms are a "Nature Cult" and the mystery rites of Adonis and Attis; "fertility cult" is a fair gloss. The summary of her thesis is otherwise accurate.
Draft:
Body: Eliot's first note credits the title, "the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism" of the poem to Jessie L. Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> (Cambridge, 1920). Weston argued that the Grail romances preserve, under a Christian surface, the rites of a much older fertility cult: a king wounded or impotent, his land laid waste with him, and a healing that depends on a stranger who arrives and asks the right question. Eliot's note recommends the book to "any who think such elucidation of the poem worth the trouble."

The typescript had another title. Parts I and II are headed <i>He Do the Police in Different Voices</i>, a phrase from Dickens's <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (1864–65), Book I, chapter 16, where Betty Higden praises her foundling Sloppy: "You mightn't think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices." The heading was dropped before publication and is known from the drafts, published in facsimile in 1971.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "The waste land is the country, the king, and the reader who has not yet asked anything." A triad that ends by making me the subject of the poem. It is a reading given as a fact, and it does not help me with the words "The Waste Land"; it wants to be quoted.
2. "written over the first two parts" I first read this as crossed out, written on top of. If the title stood at the head of the typescripts of Parts I and II, say that.
3. "A poem that reads the news of the world aloud in as many voices as it can do." A fragment, and the note admiring its own idea. It describes the poem instead of the title.
4. "Keep that title in your head; it explains more than the Grail does." An instruction to me, then a verdict on the whole poem as a closing line. Whose view is that? If it is a common one, name it; if it is the note's, it does not belong.
5. Small: "under a Christian skin" is a figure where "beneath their Christian story" would do. I would let it pass on its own; with the rest it is part of the manner.
Keep: "Eliot's first note says it plainly ... *From Ritual to Romance* (1920)." "Weston's book argues that ... asks the right question." The three sentences on *He Do the Police in Different Voices* and Dickens, with "written over" fixed.
Draft:
TITLE: Where the title comes from
BODY:
Eliot's first note credits the title, "the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism" to Jessie L. Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> (1920). Weston argued that the Grail romances preserve, beneath their Christian story, a much older fertility rite: a land lies waste because its king is wounded or impotent, and is healed only when a stranger arrives and asks the right question.

The typescript carried a different working title at the head of the first two parts [scholar: confirm this is what "written over" meant]: <i>He Do the Police in Different Voices</i>. The words are Betty Higden's praise of the boy Sloppy in Dickens's <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (1865): "You mightn't think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices." The working title was dropped before publication.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. The triad and the closing instruction go; "written over" becomes "typed at the head of"; Dickens dated and placed; the working title's fate stated.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Eliot's first note credits the title, "the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism" of the poem to Jessie L. Weston's..." The comma after "title" makes the quotation read as what the title is, and "of the poem" is left hanging outside the quotation marks; I read it twice. Put instead: Eliot's first note says that "not only the title, but the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism of the poem" were suggested by Jessie L. Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> (1920).
2. "the rites of a much older fertility cult: a king wounded or impotent, his land laid waste with him, and a healing that depends on a stranger who arrives and asks the right question." The colon promises rites and delivers a plot, in three phrases with no verb. It is the shape from Ethan's example, carrying real content this time, so it needs only to become a sentence. Put instead: Weston argued that the Grail romances preserve, beneath their Christian story, the rites of a much older fertility cult. In her account the king is wounded or impotent, his land lies waste with him, and it is healed only when a stranger arrives and asks the right question.
The second paragraph is right as it stands.

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar satisfied. Reader: the opening quotation restructured so that "of the poem" sits inside it; the colon-list of the Grail plot made a sentence. Both taken.

## epigraph

**As it stood**

TITLE: The Sibyl in the bottle
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): “Nam Sibyllam quidem Cumis ego ipse oculis meis vidi in ampulla pendere, et cum illi pueri dicerent: Σίβυλλα τί θέλεις; respondebat illa: ἀποθανεῖν θέλω.”
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): “For I myself, with my own eyes, saw the Sibyl at Cumae hanging in a jar, and when the boys said to her, ‘Sibyl, what do you want?’ she answered, ‘I want to die.’”
BODY:
The speaker is Trimalchio, the vulgar millionaire host of the dinner party in Petronius's *Satyricon* (first century AD), boasting between courses. The Sibyl of Cumae had asked Apollo for as many years of life as there were grains in a handful of sand, and forgot to ask for youth. She shrank until she could be kept in a bottle. Her wish is the poem's first word on the subject of living on after the point of living has gone. Latin, then Greek, then the poem's English: three languages before the first line, and a handful of sand before the "handful of dust" at line 30.

This was not the first epigraph. The typescript carried Kurtz's dying words from Conrad's *Heart of Darkness*: "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender during that supreme moment of complete knowledge? He cried in a whisper at some image, at some vision,—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath—'The horror! the horror!'" Pound wrote in January 1922 that he doubted "if Conrad is weighty enough to stand the citation." Eliot answered that it was "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and then replaced it anyway.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Petronius, Satyricon 48, Latin Library (thelatinlibrary.com/petronius1.html): wording matches the site's quote; speaker Trimalchio. Ovid, Metamorphoses XIV.130–153, Latin Library (ovid.met14.shtml): "ego pulveris hausti / ostendens cumulum, quot haberet corpora pulvis, / tot mihi natales contingere vana rogavi" (136–38); "excidit, ut peterem iuvenes quoque protinus annos" (139); the shrinking, 147–53. Conrad, Gutenberg #219: "at some vision—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath: / 'The horror! The horror!'" Pound's letter and Eliot's reply from Paige's Selected Letters of Ezra Pound (1950) and The Letters of T. S. Eliot vol. 1, from knowledge; not reachable online today. The 1930 Liveright printing on archive.org carries the Petronius epigraph on its title page.
Substance:
1. "as many years of life as there were grains in a handful of sand": Ovid's word is dust, "pulvis" (XIV.136–37: a heap of dust she had scooped up, "pulveris hausti... cumulum"). "Sand" is the translators' habit. Since the note goes on to connect this to "a handful of dust" at line 30, the correction strengthens it. The same slip is in red-rock.
2. "She shrank until she could be kept in a bottle": the shrinking is Ovid's (she will dwindle until only her voice is left, XIV.147–53); the jar is Petronius's. The note should say the story is Ovid's, since the epigraph itself tells none of it.
3. "three languages before the first line": with the dedication in Italian on the same page, four. Drop the count.
4. The Conrad quotation. Conrad's published text reads "at some vision—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath: 'The horror! The horror!'" The site's comma-dash, the dash for the colon and the lower-case second "the horror" reproduce the epigraph as Eliot typed it (facsimile p. 3; I could not reach the facsimile today and give this from memory of it). Either is defensible, but say which is being quoted: "as Eliot typed it" or Conrad's text.
5. "Pound wrote in January 1922": right, but this is the same letter that the dedication note dates "either 24 December 1921 or 24 January 1922" ("Complimenti, you bitch" and "I doubt if Conrad is weighty enough" are in one letter, dated "24 Saturnus, An 1"). The site should date it once. Paige printed it as 24 December 1921; Valerie Eliot's facsimile introduction and The Letters of T. S. Eliot vol. 1 give 24 January 1922, which fits the sequence (Eliot left Paris for London about 16 January and the letter answers a revised typescript sent from London).
6. "the vulgar millionaire host": Trimalchio is a freedman, a freed slave grown rich. The word tells a reader more than "vulgar".
7. Missing and worth one sentence: the Petronius epigraph first appeared in the Boni & Liveright edition (December 1922); the Criterion printing of October 1922 had neither epigraph nor notes (King's College Cambridge centenary page, kcctreasures.com, says the Boni & Liveright edition "included the epigraph and the 'Notes' to the poem for the first time"). This is what "and then replaced it anyway" leaves out: when.
8. "Her wish is the poem's first word on the subject of living on after the point of living has gone": a reading. Mark it or drop it.
Draft:
Body: The speaker is Trimalchio, a freed slave grown enormously rich, host of the dinner party that fills the longest surviving stretch of Petronius's <i>Satyricon</i> (first century AD). In chapter 48 he is boasting to his guests of what he has read and seen. The Sibyl's story is in Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV, 130–153: Apollo offered her whatever she wished; she picked up a heap of dust and asked for as many years as it had grains, and forgot to ask for youth. Ovid has her foretell that she will shrink until only her voice is left. The jar is Petronius's detail. Ovid's word is dust, "pulvis," and line 30 of the poem ends in "a handful of dust."

This epigraph was not the first. The typescript of Part I carried Kurtz's death from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, from "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender" to "'The horror! The horror!'" Pound wrote on 24 January 1922: "I doubt if Conrad is weighty enough to stand the citation." Eliot answered that it was "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and gave it up. The Petronius epigraph first appeared in the Boni & Liveright edition of December 1922; the <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 had none.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "Her wish is the poem's first word on the subject of living on after the point of living has gone." "The poem's first word on the subject" treats the poem as a speaker with opinions, and "living on after the point of living has gone" is a phrase built to be admired. It also states the poem's theme as fact. What I wanted was plainer: she has long life without youth and wants only to die.
2. "Latin, then Greek, then the poem's English: three languages before the first line, and a handful of sand before the "handful of dust" at line 30." This is the sentence-shape Ethan quoted: a colon, a count, a tidy echo. The sand-and-dust link is worth a plain sentence of its own. The count of languages tells me nothing; if the Greek matters, tell me why it is there (is it Petronius's own Greek?).
3. "hanging in a jar" in the translation, "bottle" in the title and body. Same object, two words; I stopped to wonder whether they were the same. Pick one (the scholar's call which).
4. "the vulgar millionaire host ... boasting between courses" Colour I can live with, but with "vulgar millionaire" it is a small performance. "The rich freedman who hosts the dinner, and the story is one of his boasts" gives me the same.
5. What I wanted and did not get: where the grains-of-sand story comes from (Ovid, I think; scholar to supply), and which chapter of the *Satyricon* this is.
6. "and then replaced it anyway" is how a person would say it. Keep.
Keep: "The Sibyl of Cumae had asked Apollo ... forgot to ask for youth. She shrank until she could be kept in a bottle." The whole second paragraph.
Draft:
TITLE: The Sibyl in the bottle [or "jar", to match the translation]
BODY:
The speaker is Trimalchio, the rich freedman who hosts the dinner in Petronius's <i>Satyricon</i> (first century AD), and the story is one of his boasts. The Sibyl of Cumae had asked Apollo for as many years of life as there were grains in a handful of sand, and forgot to ask for youth [scholar: add the source of the story]. She shrank until she could be kept in a bottle. Her handful of sand is echoed by the "handful of dust" at line 30. [If true, one sentence: The boys' question and her answer are in Greek in Petronius's Latin text.]

This was not the first epigraph. [second paragraph unchanged]

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. Trimalchio a freed slave; the Sibyl's story given to Ovid, with dust for sand (the scholar's correction, which makes the link to line 30); the jar Petronius's; the count of languages gone; the Conrad passage kept in full (an 1899 text) and marked as Eliot's typing; the Pound letter dated once, 24 January 1922, to agree with the dedication note; when the epigraph first appeared added.
TITLE: The Sibyl in her jar
BODY:
The speaker is Trimalchio, a freed slave grown enormously rich, whose dinner party fills the longest surviving stretch of Petronius's <i>Satyricon</i> (first century AD). In chapter 48 he is boasting to his guests of what he has seen. The Sibyl's story is in Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV, 130–153: Apollo offered her whatever she wished; she picked up a heap of dust and asked for as many years as it had grains, and forgot to ask for youth. Ovid has her foretell that she will shrink until only her voice is left; the jar is Petronius's detail, and the boys' question and her answer are in Greek in his Latin. Ovid's word for what she held is dust, "pulvis," and line 30 of the poem ends in "a handful of dust."

This epigraph was not the first. The typescript carried Kurtz's death from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, as Eliot typed it: "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender during that supreme moment of complete knowledge? He cried in a whisper at some image, at some vision,—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath—'The horror! the horror!'" Pound wrote on 24 January 1922 that he doubted "if Conrad is weighty enough to stand the citation." Eliot answered that it was "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and gave it up. The Petronius epigraph first appeared in the Boni & Liveright edition of December 1922; the <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 had none. [scholar: did the Dial printing of November 1922 have it?]

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "The Petronius epigraph first appeared in the Boni & Liveright edition of December 1922; the <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 had none." The first half is false, and it answers the bracketed question: the Dial printing of November 1922 carries the Petronius epigraph. I checked the issue itself (archive.org, sim_dial_1922-11_73): the epigraph stands under "THE WASTE LAND / BY T. S. ELIOT," before "THE BURIAL OF THE DEAD," and a footnote on the first page reads "Copyright 1922 by T. S. Eliot. An edition of The Waste Land with annotations by Mr Eliot will presently be issued by Boni & Liveright—The Editors." So the first appearance of the epigraph in print is the Dial (its November issue was on sale before the Boni & Liveright book of December). The second half, that the Criterion had no epigraph, I could not verify: my round-one source for the whole sentence (the King's College Cambridge centenary page, which says the Boni & Liveright edition carried the epigraph "for the first time") is disproved on this very point by the Dial scan, and the Criterion pages themselves are out of reach today. The claim should not stand on that source. Replace the sentence and the bracketed question with wording that claims only what is checked:
"Neither the <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 nor the <i>Dial</i>'s of November carried Eliot's notes, but the <i>Dial</i> printed the epigraph, with a note from the editors that an edition “with annotations by Mr Eliot” was coming from Boni & Liveright. The book followed in December 1922."
(The no-notes claim for both periodical printings is uncontested — the Dial's own footnote presumes it, and every account of the publication history states it. If the editor wants the stronger claim that the Criterion also lacked the epigraph, it needs someone with the Criterion's page 50 or Gallup's bibliography in hand; I could reach neither.)

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "The typescript carried Kurtz's death from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, as Eliot typed it:" A typescript cannot carry a death; I stopped to work out that a passage was meant. Put instead: The typescript carried the passage on Kurtz's death from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, as Eliot typed it:
The first paragraph reads once from start to end, and the Ovid sentence gives me the dust-to-dust link without a count.

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: the open question answered from the <i>Dial</i> itself (November 1922): the epigraph is there, with the editors' footnote promising an annotated edition; so "first appeared in the Boni & Liveright edition" was false, and the claim that the <i>Criterion</i> had no epigraph rested on the same disproved source and could not be checked. The scholar's replacement, claiming only what was seen, is taken. Reader: "the passage on Kurtz's death". Taken.
TITLE: The Sibyl in her jar
BODY:
The speaker is Trimalchio, a freed slave grown enormously rich, whose dinner party fills the longest surviving stretch of Petronius's <i>Satyricon</i> (first century AD). In chapter 48 he is boasting to his guests of what he has seen. The Sibyl's story is in Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV, 130–153: Apollo offered her whatever she wished; she picked up a heap of dust and asked for as many years as it had grains, and forgot to ask for youth. Ovid has her foretell that she will shrink until only her voice is left; the jar is Petronius's detail, and the boys' question and her answer are in Greek in his Latin. Ovid's word for what she held is dust, "pulvis," and line 30 of the poem ends in "a handful of dust."

This epigraph was not the first. The typescript carried the passage on Kurtz's death from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, as Eliot typed it: "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender during that supreme moment of complete knowledge? He cried in a whisper at some image, at some vision,—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath—'The horror! the horror!'" Pound wrote on 24 January 1922 that he doubted "if Conrad is weighty enough to stand the citation." Eliot answered that it was "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and gave it up. Neither the <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 nor the <i>Dial</i>'s of November carried Eliot's notes, but the <i>Dial</i> printed the epigraph, with a note from the editors that an edition "with annotations by Mr Eliot" was coming from Boni & Liveright. The book followed in December 1922.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Neither the <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 nor the <i>Dial</i>'s of November carried Eliot's notes, but the <i>Dial</i> printed the epigraph, with a note from the editors that an edition "with annotations by Mr Eliot" was coming from Boni & Liveright. The book followed in December 1922." The "neither ... nor ... but the <i>Dial</i>" construction leads a reader to conclude that the <i>Criterion</i> lacked the epigraph, which is exactly what the scholar says cannot be checked; and "notes" and "a note" sit in one sentence in two senses. Leading with the <i>Dial</i> states only what was seen. Put instead: The <i>Dial</i> printing of November 1922 carried the epigraph but not Eliot's notes; its editors added a footnote that an edition "with annotations by Mr Eliot" was coming from Boni & Liveright. The <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 had no notes either. The book followed in December 1922. ("Footnote" is the editor's own word in the decision line; "note" will do if the scholar prefers it.)
The rest of the note stands as it was in round two.

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The Sibyl in her jar
BODY:
The speaker is Trimalchio, a freed slave grown enormously rich, whose dinner party fills the longest surviving stretch of Petronius's <i>Satyricon</i> (first century AD). In chapter 48 he is boasting to his guests of what he has seen. The Sibyl's story is in Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV, 130–153: Apollo offered her whatever she wished; she picked up a heap of dust and asked for as many years as it had grains, and forgot to ask for youth. Ovid has her foretell that she will shrink until only her voice is left; the jar is Petronius's detail, and the boys' question and her answer are in Greek in his Latin. Ovid's word for what she held is dust, "pulvis," and line 30 of the poem ends in "a handful of dust."

This epigraph was not the first. The typescript carried the passage on Kurtz's death from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, as Eliot typed it: "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender during that supreme moment of complete knowledge? He cried in a whisper at some image, at some vision,—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath—'The horror! the horror!'" Pound wrote on 24 January 1922 that he doubted "if Conrad is weighty enough to stand the citation." Eliot answered that it was "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and gave it up. The <i>Dial</i> printing of November 1922 carried the epigraph but not Eliot's notes; its editors added a footnote that an edition "with annotations by Mr Eliot" was coming from Boni &amp; Liveright. The <i>Criterion</i> printing of October 1922 had no notes either. The book followed in December 1922.


## dedication

**As it stood**

TITLE: The better craftsman
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): “O frate,” disse, “questi ch’io ti cerno / col dito,” e additò un spirto innanzi, / “fu miglior fabbro del parlar materno.”
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): “O brother,” he said, “this one I point out to you with my finger,” and he pointed to a spirit ahead, “was a better craftsman of the mother tongue.”
BODY:
*Purgatorio* XXVI, line 117. The poet Guido Guinizelli, doing penance among the lustful in the refining fire, points Dante toward Arnaut Daniel, the Provençal troubadour, and calls him the better smith of his native speech. The same canto supplies the poem's last page: "Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina" at line 428 is Arnaut stepping back into the flames.

The dedication is not in the 1922 printings. Eliot wrote it by hand in Pound's copy of the Boni & Liveright edition in January 1923 and first printed it in *Poems 1909–1925*. It credits the cutting, not the writing: Pound worked over the typescript in Paris in January 1922 and by post afterwards, and roughly halved it. His verdict, in a letter dated in his own invented calendar and read as either 24 December 1921 or 24 January 1922: "Complimenti, you bitch. I am wracked by the seven jealousies."

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Purgatorio XXVI.115–17 and 148 at it.wikisource: the quoted tercet is exact; line 148 reads "Poi s'ascose nel foco che li affina" (Eliot's "gli"). Guinizzelli speaks; Arnaut is named at 142. Eliot's 1922 note "427. V. Purgatorio, XXVI, 148" (archive.org scan); every-line count 428. The inscription in Pound's copy: Wikipedia and the King's College Cambridge page confirm the autograph inscription in Pound's copy of the first edition and the first printing in Poems 1909–1925; the date January 1923 is Ricks and McCue's (their textual headnote prints the inscription with "Jan. 1923"), which I could not reach today. Pound's Spirit of Romance chapter title confirmed by web search (Wikipedia, Arnaut Daniel).
Substance:
1. "Pound worked over the typescript in Paris in January 1922 and by post afterwards": Pound saw the typescript twice in Paris. Eliot showed him what existed (Parts I–III) in November 1921 on the way to Lausanne, and the two went through the whole poem in January 1922 on Eliot's way back; the letters followed (Valerie Eliot's introduction to the facsimile; Rainey, Revisiting The Waste Land). Add November.
2. "roughly halved it": Eliot's own figure. In "Ezra Pound" (Poetry, September 1946) he said the manuscript "left his hands, reduced to about half its size." Give the source; the phrase is within the rights rule.
3. The letter date: see epigraph 5. "read as either 24 December 1921 or 24 January 1922" is honest, but the site's two notes should agree. Recommend: "24 January 1922 ('24 Saturnus, An 1' in Pound's private calendar; D. D. Paige's 1950 edition of the letters printed it as 24 December 1921)".
4. "It credits the cutting, not the writing": a reading, though Eliot's 1946 account supports it. Fine if kept plain.
5. Missing, one sentence: "Il Miglior Fabbro" was already Pound's phrase, the title of his chapter on Arnaut Daniel in <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910). Ricks and McCue note it; a reader of a dedication to Pound would want it.
6. "not in the 1922 printings": right; nor in the Hogarth Press edition of September 1923. Worth adding so no one goes looking there.
7. "Complimenti, you bitch. I am wracked by the seven jealousies": exact.
Draft:
Body (second paragraph only): The dedication is not in the printings of 1922, nor in the Hogarth Press edition of 1923. Eliot wrote it by hand in Pound's copy of the Boni & Liveright edition (Ricks and McCue date the inscription January 1923) and first printed it in <i>Poems 1909–1925</i>. Pound had seen the typescript in Paris in November 1921, on Eliot's way to Lausanne, and gone through the whole poem with him there in January 1922 on his way back; further comments came by post. Eliot said in 1946 that the poem left Pound's hands "reduced to about half its size." Pound's letter of 24 January 1922, dated "24 Saturnus, An 1" in a calendar of his own devising, contains the verdict: "Complimenti, you bitch. I am wracked by the seven jealousies." (D. D. Paige's 1950 edition of Pound's letters printed it as 24 December 1921.) "Il miglior fabbro" was already Pound's phrase: it is the title of his chapter on Arnaut Daniel in <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910).

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "*Purgatorio* XXVI, line 117." A bare fragment as an opener. Fine in a footnote, but the rest of the note is in sentences, so make it one.
2. "The same canto supplies the poem's last page" "Supplies" and "last page" are a little pleased with themselves. "The same canto is quoted near the end of the poem" says it.
3. "It credits the cutting, not the writing:" The antithesis with a colon, and it decides for me what the dedication means. Say what Pound did and I will draw the line myself.
4. "in a letter dated in his own invented calendar and read as either 24 December 1921 or 24 January 1922" I stumbled twice: "invented calendar" with no explanation, and "read as" by whom? Give the calendar one plain clause and say that editors differ on the date.
5. "and roughly halved it" Plain and useful. Keep.
Keep: "The poet Guido Guinizelli ... the better smith of his native speech." "The dedication is not in the 1922 printings. Eliot wrote it by hand ... *Poems 1909–1925*." "Pound worked over the typescript in Paris in January 1922 and by post afterwards." The "Complimenti" quotation.
Draft:
TITLE: The better craftsman
BODY:
The line is <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, 117. The poet Guido Guinizelli, doing penance among the lustful in the refining fire, points Dante toward Arnaut Daniel, the Provençal troubadour, and calls him the better smith of his native speech. The same canto is quoted near the end of the poem: "Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina" at line 428 is Arnaut stepping back into the flames.

The dedication is not in the 1922 printings. Eliot wrote it by hand in Pound's copy of the Boni & Liveright edition in January 1923 and first printed it in <i>Poems 1909–1925</i>. Pound worked over the typescript in Paris in January 1922 and by post afterwards, and cut it by about half. His letter of congratulation is dated in a calendar of his own devising, and editors have read the date as 24 December 1921 or as 24 January 1922: "Complimenti, you bitch. I am wracked by the seven jealousies."

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim and correct. Opener made a sentence; "credits the cutting, not the writing" gone; November 1921 added; "halved" given to Eliot's 1946 account; the letter dated once, with Paige's date in brackets; Pound's own earlier use of the phrase added; the Hogarth edition added.
TITLE: The better craftsman
BODY:
The line is <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, 117. The poet Guido Guinizelli, doing penance among the lustful in the refining fire, points Dante toward Arnaut Daniel, the Provençal troubadour, and calls him the better smith of his native speech. The same canto is quoted near the end of the poem: "Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina" at line 428 is Arnaut stepping back into the flames. "Il miglior fabbro" was already Pound's phrase: it is the title of his chapter on Arnaut Daniel in <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910).

The dedication is not in the printings of 1922, nor in the Hogarth Press edition of 1923. Eliot wrote it by hand in Pound's copy of the Boni & Liveright edition, in January 1923 by Ricks and McCue's dating, and first printed it in <i>Poems 1909–1925</i>. Pound had seen the typescript in Paris in November 1921, on Eliot's way to Lausanne, and went through the whole poem with him there in January 1922 on his way back; further comments came by post. Eliot said in 1946 that the poem left Pound's hands "reduced to about half its size." Pound's letter of 24 January 1922, dated "24 Saturnus, An 1" in a calendar of his own devising, gives his verdict: "Complimenti, you bitch. I am wracked by the seven jealousies." (D. D. Paige's 1950 edition of Pound's letters printed it as 24 December 1921.)

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Every sentence reads once, and the Paige date in brackets is exactly how I wanted the disagreement handled.

**Editor, after round 2**

Both sides satisfied. Unchanged from round two.

BODY:

## april

**As it stood**

TITLE: Chaucer's April, turned inside out
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): Whan that Aprill with his shoures soote / The droghte of March hath perced to the roote, / And bathed every veyne in swich licour / Of which vertu engendred is the flour . . .
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): When April with its sweet showers has pierced the drought of March to the root, and bathed every vein in such liquid as has the power to engender the flower . . .
BODY:
The most famous opening in English poetry says that April is kind: rain ends the drought, sap rises, people want to go on pilgrimages. Eliot's first line answers it. Rain is still there ("stirring / Dull roots with spring rain"), but it is unwelcome, because it stirs "memory and desire" in something that would rather stay numb. Winter was kinder: "forgetful snow," "a little life with dried tubers." Read the first seven lines aloud and notice how five of them end on a present participle left hanging, breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding, so that the verbs never quite arrive at their objects. Growth that will not finish.

Behind Chaucer stand two other Aprils. Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (1865) mourns Lincoln with lilacs, spring, and a hermit thrush, and that thrush turns up at line 357 of this poem. And Rupert Brooke, dead in April 1915, whose "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester" opens with the lilac in bloom. The line's "us" is a group that never gets named. It might be the leisured Europeans of the next paragraph, or the dead, or you.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Chaucer, General Prologue 1–4 and 12 (Skeat, Gutenberg #22120; the site's spelling is Riverside's, also fine). Lines 1–18 of the poem are one verse paragraph in Gutenberg #1321 and in the 1922 setting on archive.org; the first break is after line 18. Hermit-thrush line: 357 in the every-line count. Brooke, "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester," Gutenberg #262: headed "(Cafe des Westens, Berlin, May 1912)", opens "Just now the lilac is in bloom, / All before my little room". Brooke died 23 April 1915. Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (Sequel to Drum-Taps, 1865) from knowledge: the poem names spring, not April.
Substance:
1. "so that the verbs never quite arrive at their objects": false. Each participle's object is the first thing on the next line: lilacs, memory and desire, dull roots, earth, a little life. The effect is a delay of one line, not a missing object. The count of five participles (breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding) is right.
2. "the leisured Europeans of the next paragraph": same paragraph. Lines 1–18 run without a break in 1922; the Munich lines begin at 8 with no break. Say "of lines 8–18".
3. "Behind Chaucer stand two other Aprils": neither poem names April. Whitman's says "ever-returning spring" (Lincoln was shot on 14 April 1865, which is the April); Brooke's is dated Berlin, May 1912, and the only April is his death. Rephrase as two other lilacs, which is what the poems share.
4. The Brooke connection is a suggestion and is not in Eliot's notes; I cannot attribute it to a standard commentary. Mark it as a suggestion. The Whitman connection is generally accepted (Ricks and McCue), and Eliot's own note to line 357 names the hermit-thrush.
5. "or you": second person; a reading. The plain fact is that "us" is not identified.
6. Title "Chaucer's April, turned inside out" and "Eliot's first line answers it": the Chaucer echo is accepted by every commentary; fine.
Draft:
Body (from "Read the first seven lines" to the end): Five of the first seven lines end on a participle: breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding. Each object is held over to the next line (lilacs, memory and desire, dull roots, earth, a little life), so each verb waits a line for what it governs.

Two other lilac poems are usually mentioned. Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (1865) mourns Lincoln, shot in April, with lilacs, "ever-returning spring" and a hermit thrush; Eliot's own note to line 357 names the hermit-thrush. Rupert Brooke's "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester," dated "Café des Westens, Berlin, May 1912," opens "Just now the lilac is in bloom"; Brooke died in April 1915. The Whitman connection is generally accepted; the Brooke one is a suggestion. "Us" in line 5 is never identified. The voice that goes on to Munich at line 8 belongs to the same verse paragraph, which runs to line 18.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: yes
Problems:
1. Title, "Chaucer's April, turned inside out". A figure for its own sake. "Chaucer's April, reversed" or plain "Chaucer's April" is enough.
2. "The most famous opening in English poetry says that April is kind: rain ends the drought, sap rises, people want to go on pilgrimages." The colon and the triad; and "most famous" is the writer's flourish, not a fact I can check.
3. "in something that would rather stay numb" Tells me what the speaker feels. It is a reading and is not marked as one.
4. "Winter was kinder: "forgetful snow," "a little life with dried tubers."" A colon and two quoted scraps standing in for a sentence.
5. "Read the first seven lines aloud and notice how five of them end on a present participle left hanging ... so that the verbs never quite arrive at their objects." An instruction to me, a count for effect, and, when I did read them, not true: each participle gets its object on the next line ("breeding / Lilacs", "stirring / Dull roots", "feeding / A little life"). The real point is that the object is held over the line break. That I can see for myself once it is stated.
6. "Growth that will not finish." A fragment tied as a bow.
7. "Behind Chaucer stand two other Aprils." Staging. Poems do not stand behind one another; commentators cite them.
8. "And Rupert Brooke, dead in April 1915, whose "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester" opens with the lilac in bloom." A fragment, and I am not told what the link is beyond the word lilac. If the opening line is the link, quote it.
9. "The line's "us" is a group that never gets named. It might be the leisured Europeans of the next paragraph, or the dead, or you." "Or you" is the bow, aimed at me; the list of three is for rhythm. And the "us" is in line 5, not line 1.
10. "that thrush turns up at line 357 of this poem" Useful. Keep.
Keep: The Whitman sentence. The dry-roots quotation and the "memory and desire" point, once they are one plain sentence.
Draft:
TITLE: Chaucer's April, reversed
BODY:
Chaucer's opening welcomes April. Its showers end the drought of March, the sap rises, and people set out on pilgrimages. Eliot's first line is usually read as an answer to it [the note says "answers it"; scholar may restore]. The rain is still there ("stirring / Dull roots with spring rain"), but it is unwelcome, since it stirs "memory and desire" where the winter snow was "forgetful". Five of the first seven lines end on a participle (breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding) whose object is held over to the next line.

Commentators also cite two other poems with lilacs. Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (1865) mourns Lincoln with lilacs, spring and a hermit thrush; the thrush returns at line 357 of this poem. Rupert Brooke's "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester" opens "Just now the lilac is in bloom" [scholar: confirm the quotation]; Brooke died in April 1915. The "us" of line 5 is never identified.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Most famous opening" and the triad go; the participle claim corrected (the objects arrive, a line late); "next paragraph" corrected (same verse paragraph); the two lilac poems no longer called Aprils, Whitman marked as accepted and Brooke as a suggestion; "or you" gone; "us" placed at line 5.
TITLE: Chaucer's April, reversed
BODY:
Chaucer's opening welcomes April. Its showers end the drought of March, the sap rises, and people set out on pilgrimages. Eliot's first line answers it; every commentary notes the echo. The rain is still there ("stirring / Dull roots with spring rain"), but the month is called cruel, because what the rain stirs is "memory and desire", and the snow of winter was "forgetful". Five of the first seven lines end on a participle (breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding), and each object is held over to the next line, so each verb waits a line for what it governs.

Two other lilac poems are usually mentioned. Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (1865) mourns Lincoln, shot in April, with lilacs, "ever-returning spring" and a hermit thrush; Eliot's own note to line 357 names the hermit-thrush. Rupert Brooke's "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester", written in Berlin in May 1912, opens "Just now the lilac is in bloom"; Brooke died in April 1915. The Whitman connection is generally accepted; the Brooke one is a suggestion. "Us" in line 5 is never identified, and the voice that goes on to Munich at line 8 belongs to the same verse paragraph, which runs to line 18.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "but the month is called cruel, because what the rain stirs is "memory and desire", and the snow of winter was "forgetful". Five of the first seven lines end on a participle (breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding), and each object is held over to the next line, so each verb waits a line for what it governs." The "and" after "desire" makes the forgetful snow a second reason for April's cruelty, which it is not; and the participle sentence makes its point twice, the second time as a flourish. Put instead: The rain is still there ("stirring / Dull roots with spring rain"), but the month is called cruel because what the rain stirs is "memory and desire". Winter's snow, by contrast, was "forgetful". Five of the first seven lines end on a participle (breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding) whose object is held over to the next line.
2. "mourns Lincoln, shot in April, with lilacs, "ever-returning spring" and a hermit thrush" On first reading Lincoln is shot with lilacs. Put instead: Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (1865) mourns Lincoln, who was shot in April; it has lilacs, "ever-returning spring" and a hermit thrush, and Eliot's own note to line 357 names the hermit-thrush.
3. ""Us" in line 5 is never identified, and the voice that goes on to Munich at line 8 belongs to the same verse paragraph, which runs to line 18." A voice belonging to a paragraph is a roundabout way of saying the lines are continuous. Put instead: "Us" in line 5 is never identified. The same verse paragraph runs on without a break through the Munich lines, which begin at line 8, to line 18.

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar satisfied. Reader: the "because ... and the snow" muddle and the doubled participle clause; Lincoln "shot in April, with lilacs"; the roundabout sentence on "us" and the verse paragraph. All three taken.

BODY:

The rain is still there ("stirring / Dull roots with spring rain"), but the month is called cruel because what the rain stirs is "memory and desire". Winter's snow, by contrast, was "forgetful". Five of the first seven lines end on a participle (breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding) whose object is held over to the next line.

Two other lilac poems are usually mentioned. Whitman's "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" (1865) mourns Lincoln, who was shot in April; it has lilacs, "ever-returning spring" and a hermit thrush, and Eliot's own note to line 357 names the hermit-thrush. Rupert Brooke's "The Old Vicarage, Grantchester", written in Berlin in May 1912, opens "Just now the lilac is in bloom"; Brooke died in April 1915. The Whitman connection is generally accepted; the Brooke one is a suggestion. "Us" in line 5 is never identified. The same verse paragraph runs on without a break through the Munich lines, which begin at line 8, to line 18.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied
All three round-two changes are in. The note now gives Chaucer, the rain, the participles, the two lilac poems with their standing, and the "us", each in a sentence I can use.

**As it now stands**

The same verse paragraph runs on without a break through the Munich lines, which begin at line 8, to line 18.


## boston-cut

**As it stood**

TITLE: What came before "April"
BODY:
In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" was not the first line. It was preceded by fifty-four lines of a night on the town in Boston, in the voice of a young man out with his friends: a bar, a burlesque show, a drunken run from a policeman, a sunrise. The whole passage was struck through with a single pencil line, apparently by Eliot himself before Pound saw it. What survived is the second beginning, a poem that starts already exhausted. The manuscript is at the New York Public Library's Berg Collection and is reproduced in the 1971 facsimile edited by Valerie Eliot.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: fifty-four lines confirmed by several secondary accounts (Yale Review; the facsimile itself not reachable). Attribution of the cancellation: Edmund Wilson, "The First Waste Land—II," NYRB 18 November 1971 ("made by Eliot himself"); the Yale Review: "it was Eliot's pencil, according to Mrs. Eliot, that scored through the opening scene in Boston." Two popular web pages say Pound struck it through; they are not authorities. Contents of the passage: Wilson's summary. Berg Collection and 1971 facsimile from knowledge.
Substance:
1. "apparently by Eliot himself before Pound saw it": give the ground. Valerie Eliot's facsimile attributes the cancellation to Eliot, and the page carries no annotation by Pound, whose marks begin on the second page. "Before Pound saw it" is an inference from the absence of his marks; say so or drop it.
2. "struck through with a single pencil line": pencil is right (Valerie Eliot, via the Yale Review). I could not verify "single" against the facsimile; "struck through in pencil" is safe.
3. "a bar, a burlesque show, a drunken run from a policeman, a sunrise": Wilson's summary of the passage has drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning where the narrator is not welcome, a policeman, and a cab home to see the sunrise. The note omits Myrtle's, and I could not verify "burlesque" or "a run from" the policeman against the facsimile; Wilson has the narrator picked up by the policeman. Wilson also notes that the narrator is evidently Irish (Irish names, a snatch of an Irish song).
4. "a poem that starts already exhausted": a reading, and metaphor. Cut.
5. "The manuscript is at the New York Public Library's Berg Collection": right; it came with John Quinn's papers, bought by the Berg in 1958 and made public in 1968. Optional.
Draft:
Body: In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" is not the first line; it begins the second page. The first page holds fifty-four lines of a night out in Boston, told by one of a party of young men: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning where he is not welcome, a policeman, and a cab home at sunrise. The page is struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin on the next page, which is why the cut is credited to Eliot rather than Pound. The typescript is in the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library, among John Quinn's papers, and is reproduced in the facsimile edition of 1971.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "in the voice of a young man out with his friends: a bar, a burlesque show, a drunken run from a policeman, a sunrise." The colon and the list of four. This is content I want, so write it as a sentence with verbs in it.
2. "What survived is the second beginning, a poem that starts already exhausted." The common brief's own example. The note stops describing the draft and starts describing the poem. Cut.
3. "apparently by Eliot himself before Pound saw it" A good hedge, but whose judgement is "apparently"? If it is Valerie Eliot's, say so; the source sentence at the end is doing that work already and could sit next to it.
4. "fifty-four lines" A count, but one I wanted: it tells me how much was cut. Keep.
Keep: "In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" was not the first line." "The whole passage was struck through with a single pencil line, apparently by Eliot himself before Pound saw it." "The manuscript is at the New York Public Library's Berg Collection and is reproduced in the 1971 facsimile edited by Valerie Eliot."
Draft:
TITLE: What came before "April"
BODY:
In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" was not the first line. Fifty-four lines came before it, describing a night on the town in Boston in the voice of a young man out with his friends, who drink in a bar, see a burlesque show, run drunk from a policeman and watch the sun rise. The whole passage was struck through with a single pencil line, apparently by Eliot himself before Pound saw it [scholar: whose judgement]. The manuscript is in the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library and is reproduced in the 1971 facsimile edited by Valerie Eliot.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim and correct. The list becomes a sentence with the contents the scholar could verify (Wilson's summary), dropping "burlesque" and "run from"; the cancellation credited to Eliot on the facsimile's authority and the absence of Pound's marks; "starts already exhausted" gone.
TITLE: What came before "April"
BODY:
In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" is not the first line. Fifty-four lines come before it, a night out in Boston told by one of a party of young men: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning where he is not welcome, a policeman, and a cab home at sunrise. The passage is struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after it, which is why the cut is credited to Eliot rather than Pound. The typescript is in the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library, among John Quinn's papers, and is reproduced in the facsimile edition of 1971.

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Fifty-four lines come before it, a night out in Boston told by one of a party of young men: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning where he is not welcome, a policeman, and a cab home at sunrise." An inventory with no verb, and "a policeman" on its own tells me nothing I can use. Put instead: Fifty-four lines come before it. They tell of a night out in Boston, in the voice of one of a party of young men: they drink at "Tom's place," see a show, and call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning, where the speaker is not welcome; a policeman appears, and the night ends with a cab home at sunrise. For the scholar: if the passage says in a word or two what the policeman does, that word belongs here.
The rest of the note, on the pencil stroke and why it is credited to Eliot, is exactly what I wanted to know.

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar satisfied. Reader: the verbless inventory made sentences, and a word wanted on what the policeman does. Taken; the policeman is left as "a policeman appears" for the scholar to complete in round three, in a word or two, if the facsimile allows it. TITLE: What came before "April"
BODY:
In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" is not the first line. Fifty-four lines come before it. They tell of a night out in Boston, in the voice of one of a party of young men: they drink at "Tom's place," see a show, and call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning, where the speaker is not welcome; a policeman appears, and the night ends with a cab home at sunrise. The passage is struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after it, which is why the cut is credited to Eliot rather than Pound. The typescript is in the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library, among John Quinn's papers, and is reproduced in the facsimile edition of 1971.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: not yet
Answer to the bracketed question: the policeman stops the speaker in the street — the draft's words for the charge are "committing a nuisance" (Wilson glosses it: "apparently for urinating in the street") — and a man the speaker knows gets him off; they go off in a cab, whose other occupants get out to run a hundred yards on a bet, and the speaker gets out to see the sunrise and walks home. Sources: Wilson, NYRB, 18 November 1971 ("Leaving the brothel, he is picked up by 'a fly cop,' ... but he is rescued by a man he knows, with whom he goes off in a cab"), and the draft lines as quoted there and in the 2025 post ("a fly cop came along, / Looking for trouble; committing a nuisance, he said"; "So I got out to see the sunrise, and walked home"). "Committing a nuisance" is two words of the facsimile, within the rights rule.
1. The same sentence has two small slips the answer can fix at a stroke: the call at Myrtle's is the speaker's alone, not the party's (the draft is first person singular there: he turns up at two in the morning and is told he is too drunk; Wilson's summary agrees), and the night does not end "with a cab home" — he leaves the cab and walks. Replace the sentence "They tell of a night out in Boston, in the voice of one of a party of young men: they drink at "Tom's place," see a show, and call at "Myrtle's place" after two in the morning, where the speaker is not welcome; a policeman appears, and the night ends with a cab home at sunrise." with:
"They tell of a night out in Boston, in the voice of one of a party of young men: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, then a call alone at "Myrtle's place" at two in the morning, where he is told he is too drunk; a policeman picks him up for "committing a nuisance," a man he knows gets him off, and he leaves their cab at sunrise to walk home."

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied
The inventory is now sentences. "A policeman appears" reads well enough as it stands; if the scholar supplies the word for what he does, it slots in without disturbing the sentence.

**As it now stands**

TITLE: What came before "April"
BODY:
In the typescript, "April is the cruellest month" is not the first line. Fifty-four lines come before it. They tell of a night out in Boston, in the voice of one of a party of young men: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, then a call alone at "Myrtle's place" at two in the morning, where he is told he is too drunk; a policeman picks him up for "committing a nuisance," a man he knows gets him off, and he leaves their cab at sunrise to walk home. The passage is struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after it, which is why the cut is credited to Eliot rather than Pound. The typescript is in the Berg Collection of the New York Public Library, among John Quinn's papers, and is reproduced in the facsimile edition of 1971.


## starnbergersee

**As it stood**

TITLE: Munich, August 1911
BODY:
The Starnberger See is the long lake south of Munich; the Hofgarten is the court garden in the city centre, with an arcaded walk on two sides, which is the "colonnade" the speakers shelter in. Eliot spent August 1911 in Munich, at the end of his year in Paris, and finished "The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock" there. The lake is also where King Ludwig II of Bavaria, Wagner's patron and the builder of Neuschwanstein, was found drowned in June 1886, a death by water for the king who paid for *Tristan und Isolde*. The poem never says so. It only stops for coffee.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Ludwig II (Wikipedia): found dead in Lake Starnberg, then called the Würmsee, on 13 June 1886; official verdict suicide by drowning; the autopsy found no water in his lungs; he attended the Munich premiere of Tristan (1865) as Wagner's patron; he built Neuschwanstein. The typescript's "Königsee" for line 8: reported from the facsimile by several sources (web search); the facsimile itself not reachable. Eliot's Munich dates: Jean Verdenal wrote to him in Bavaria in mid-July 1911 (Letters vol. 1), so he was there by July; Crawford's Young Eliot has July and August. Hofgarten arcades on two sides (north and west): knowledge.
Substance:
1. "Eliot spent August 1911 in Munich": July and August. He was in Munich by mid-July (Verdenal's letter), and left for northern Italy in late August before returning to Paris and America.
2. "was found drowned": found dead in the shallows of the lake. The official verdict was suicide by drowning; the autopsy found no water in the lungs, and the cause has been disputed ever since. "Found dead in the lake" is what is known.
3. "the king who paid for Tristan und Isolde": Ludwig paid for its first performance (Munich, 10 June 1865); Wagner had composed it in 1857–59, before Ludwig came to the throne. Say "paid for the first performance of".
4. The Ludwig connection is a reading, a common one (Southam's Guide notes the drowning at the lake). Mark it. It has a second support the note does not give: Countess Marie Larisch, whose memoir lies behind lines 8–18 (see marie), was Elisabeth of Austria's niece, and the Wittelsbach house at Possenhofen on this lake was Elisabeth's family home; Larisch's book mentions the lake (G. L. K. Morris, 1954).
5. Missing: in the typescript the lake was the Königssee, in the far south-east of Bavaria; Eliot altered it to the Starnbergersee (facsimile). A reader of a "place" note would want this.
6. "which is the 'colonnade' the speakers shelter in": a plausible identification, not a fact. "Probably".
7. "The poem never says so. It only stops for coffee.": the second sentence is a flourish; cut. The title "Munich, August 1911" states the biographical reading as fact and gets the month wrong.
Draft:
Title: The lake and the Hofgarten
Body: The Starnberger See is the long lake about twenty-five kilometres south-west of Munich; the Hofgarten is the court garden beside the Residenz in the city centre, with arcades along two sides, which are probably the "colonnade." In the typescript the lake was the Königssee, in the far south-east of Bavaria; Eliot changed it (facsimile, 1971). Eliot was in Munich in July and August 1911, at the end of his year in Paris, and finished "The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock" there. King Ludwig II of Bavaria, Wagner's patron and the builder of Neuschwanstein, was found dead in the lake on 13 June 1886; the verdict was suicide by drowning, though the autopsy found no water in his lungs. Commentators since Southam have set that death beside the poem's "death by water"; the poem itself says nothing of it. The lake also belongs to Countess Marie Larisch, whose memoir lies behind lines 8–18: her aunt the Empress Elisabeth spent her childhood summers at Possenhofen on its shore.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "a death by water for the king who paid for *Tristan und Isolde*" The connection made for me with a wink. Lay out the facts (Ludwig drowned in this lake; he was Wagner's patron; the poem quotes *Tristan* twenty lines later) and I will see it. "Paid for" also sounds loose to me: what did he pay for, the opera or its staging? Scholar's call.
2. "The poem never says so. It only stops for coffee." The bow, and the poem behaving like a person. Cut both sentences.
3. Minor: the poem prints "Starnbergersee" as one word and the note has "Starnberger See". One clause saying so would spare a reader the flicker of doubt about whether they are the same place.
4. "which is the "colonnade" the speakers shelter in" An identification I am glad of. "Probably" would be honest unless it is certain.
Keep: The first two sentences. "The lake is also where King Ludwig II of Bavaria, Wagner's patron and the builder of Neuschwanstein, was found drowned in June 1886."
Draft:
TITLE: Munich, August 1911
BODY:
The Starnberger See (one word in the poem) is the long lake south of Munich; the Hofgarten is the court garden in the city centre, with an arcaded walk on two sides, which is probably the "colonnade" the speakers shelter in. Eliot spent August 1911 in Munich, at the end of his year in Paris, and finished "The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock" there. King Ludwig II of Bavaria, Wagner's patron and the builder of Neuschwanstein, was found drowned in the lake in June 1886. The poem does not mention him. It quotes Wagner's <i>Tristan und Isolde</i> at lines 31 to 34 and 42, and Madame Sosostris says "Fear death by water" at line 55.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. Title changed, since "August 1911" was wrong and read the biography into the lines; July and August; "found dead", with the disputed verdict; "paid for the first performance"; the Königssee of the typescript added; the Ludwig connection marked as the commentators'; "It only stops for coffee" gone; the Larisch link to the lake added.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "her aunt the Empress Elisabeth grew up at Possenhofen on its shore": overstated. Elisabeth was born in Munich, and the family wintered at the Herzog-Max-Palais there; Possenhofen was the summer house ("The family's homes were the Herzog-Max-Palais in Munich during winter and Possenhofen Castle in the summer months" — Wikipedia, Empress Elisabeth of Austria, and the standard biographies). Replace with:
"her aunt the Empress Elisabeth spent her childhood summers at Possenhofen on its shore."
2. "In the typescript the lake was the Königssee, in the far south-east of Bavaria; Eliot changed it." A draft fact with no source, where the parallel notes (boston-cut, marie, red-rock) all carry the facsimile. Replace with:
"In the typescript the lake was the Königssee, in the far south-east of Bavaria; Eliot changed it (the typescript is reproduced in the 1971 facsimile)."

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Plain throughout; the Ludwig connection is given as the commentators' and the poem's silence as a fact, which is the right way round.

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader satisfied. Scholar: Elisabeth's childhood summers, not her upbringing, at Possenhofen; the Königssee fact to carry the facsimile. Both taken.

## marie

**As it stood**

TITLE: Countess Marie Larisch
BODY:
Valerie Eliot, in the facsimile edition, reported that her husband had met Countess Marie Larisch (1858–1940), a niece of the Empress Elisabeth of Austria, and that the sledding story and the tone of these lines came from her conversation, not from any book. Larisch had been the go-between in the affair of her cousin Crown Prince Rudolf and Mary Vetsera, which ended with both dead at Mayerling in 1889; her memoir *My Past* (1913) made her notorious. "The archduke's, my cousin's" fits Rudolf. What the poem takes from her is a voice: a displaced aristocrat of a vanished empire, at once frightened and free, reading much of the night. "In the mountains, there you feel free" is the poem's first quotation of someone who is not sure they mean it.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Larisch's dates (24 February 1858 to 4 July 1940), parentage (Duke Ludwig Wilhelm in Bavaria and the actress Henriette Mendel), niece of Elisabeth, go-between at Mayerling (30 January 1889), banished from court, memoir My Past (1913): Wikipedia. Valerie Eliot's facsimile note as quoted on the Parker page: Eliot "had met the author (when and where is not known), and his description of the sledding... was taken verbatim from a conversation he had with this niece and confidante of the Austrian Empress Elizabeth." G. L. K. Morris, "Marie, Marie, Hold On Tight," Partisan Review, March–April 1954, and the parallels he found in My Past (the archduke who was her cousin, the Starnbergersee, the mountains, reading at night, winters in the south): Parker page, from knowledge of Morris.
Substance:
1. "the sledding story and the tone of these lines came from her conversation, not from any book": overstated and half wrong. Morris found most of lines 8–18 in My Past in 1954: the archduke cousin, the lake, the mountains where one feels free, reading much of the night, going south in the winter. Valerie Eliot's note adds only that Eliot had met Larisch and that the sledding, which is not in the book, came verbatim from her conversation. "The tone of these lines" is not in the note. Say both.
2. "reported that her husband had met Countess Marie Larisch": add her caveat, "when and where is not known." The guess that it was Munich in 1911 is a guess.
3. "her memoir My Past (1913) made her notorious": she had been notorious since 1889, when she was exposed as the go-between and banished from court. The memoir revived the scandal.
4. "'The archduke's, my cousin's' fits Rudolf": right (Rudolf was an archduke and her first cousin) and it is Morris's point; attribute.
5. "at once frightened and free" and "the poem's first quotation of someone who is not sure they mean it": readings, and flourishes. Cut or mark.
6. Line range: the Larisch material begins at line 8 (the lake) or 13 (the archduke), not 15. The anchor on "Marie" can stay; the body should say lines 8–18.
Draft:
Body: Lines 8–18 draw on Countess Marie Larisch (1858–1940), a niece of the Empress Elisabeth of Austria. G. L. K. Morris pointed out in 1954 ("Marie, Marie, Hold On Tight," <i>Partisan Review</i>) that her memoir <i>My Past</i> (1913) has the archduke who was her cousin, the Starnbergersee, the mountains where one feels free, the reading late at night and the winters in the south. Valerie Eliot's note in the 1971 facsimile adds that Eliot had met her, though when and where is not known, and that the sledding, which is not in the book, came word for word from her conversation. Larisch had carried messages between her cousin Crown Prince Rudolf and Mary Vetsera before the two were found dead at Mayerling in January 1889; she was banished from court, and the memoir revived the scandal. "The archduke's, my cousin's" fits Rudolf.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "What the poem takes from her is a voice: a displaced aristocrat of a vanished empire, at once frightened and free, reading much of the night." The colon, the triad, and "at once frightened and free" is the common brief's own example of the portentous pairing. "Reading much of the night" hands the poem's own line back to me as if it were an insight.
2. ""In the mountains, there you feel free" is the poem's first quotation of someone who is not sure they mean it." A bow. It claims to know what the speaker means and tells me how to hear the line. "The poem's first ..." is a formula this batch uses three times.
3. Everything before those two sentences is what I came for: sourced, clear, and it explains "the archduke's, my cousin's".
Keep: The first three sentences, word for word.
Draft:
TITLE: Countess Marie Larisch
BODY:
[first three sentences unchanged, ending ""The archduke's, my cousin's" fits Rudolf."] Lines 13 to 18 are usually read in a voice like hers, an aristocrat of the old Austrian empire remembering. [Or stop at "fits Rudolf." Either is better than what is there.]

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Not from any book" reversed the evidence, as the scholar showed: Morris found most of lines 8–18 in <i>My Past</i>; Valerie Eliot's note covers the sledding and the meeting, with her caveat. The two closing readings go.

## litauen

**As it stood**

TITLE: A café sentence
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): I am not Russian at all; I come from Lithuania, real German.
BODY:
Overheard, unattributed, in a language the reader may not have. After 1918 the old multinational empires had become new nation states, and a woman from the Baltic gentry could be Russian by passport, Lithuanian by birthplace and German by blood, and need to say so quickly. The poem's first foreign sentence is about identity, and it is defensive.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Checked: the German against my own reading: "Bin gar keine Russin, stamm' aus Litauen, echt deutsch" is "[I] am not Russian at all, [I] come from Lithuania, real German." "Russin" is the feminine form. Lithuania's status (Russian Empire until 1918, republic from 1918): knowledge.
Substance:
1. "Overheard, unattributed": a reading. The line may be the same speaker's (Marie's) or a companion's or something overheard in the Hofgarten; the poem does not say. Commentaries divide. Say so.
2. The translation loses the one hard fact in the line: "Russin" is feminine, so the speaker is a woman. Add it.
3. "After 1918 the old multinational empires had become new nation states, and a woman from the Baltic gentry could be Russian by passport, Lithuanian by birthplace and German by blood": the poem does not date the remark, and the Munich visit the note leans on was 1911, when Lithuania was a province of the Russian Empire. The disclaimer makes sense either way: a German speaker from Lithuania would be taken for a Russian subject. "Baltic gentry" is a guess (the Baltic German nobility was mostly in Courland, Livonia and Estonia, not Lithuania). Drop it.
4. "The poem's first foreign sentence": the epigraph is Latin and Greek and the dedication Italian. "The first line of the poem proper not in English."
5. "and it is defensive": a reading.
Draft:
Title: The German line
Body: "I am not Russian at all; I come from Lithuania, real German." "Russin" is feminine, so the speaker is a woman. Whether she is the Marie of the lines that follow, a companion, or someone overheard, the poem does not say. Lithuania was part of the Russian Empire until 1918 and a republic after it; either way a German speaker from there might expect to be taken for Russian and want to correct it. It is the first line of the poem proper not in English.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: yes
Problems:
1. Title, "A café sentence". Cute, and the poem does not say where the sentence is spoken; the café is the note's inference from line 11. "German, not Russian" would do.
2. "Overheard, unattributed, in a language the reader may not have." A fragment in three beats, and it talks about me ("the reader may not have") instead of about the line.
3. "Russian by passport, Lithuanian by birthplace and German by blood, and need to say so quickly" The three-part list is doing real explaining and can stay as a sentence; "and need to say so quickly" is the flourish on the end.
4. "The poem's first foreign sentence is about identity, and it is defensive." "The poem's first" again, and "defensive" tells me how to hear it.
5. What I wanted and did not get: "Russin" is the feminine form, so the speaker is a woman. The English translation loses that, and it matters for who is talking. [scholar: confirm it is worth stating]
6. Translation: "real German" is odd English for "echt deutsch"; "a real German" or "genuinely German" read better. Accuracy is the scholar's call.
Keep: The historical sentence, reworked.
Draft:
TITLE: German, not Russian
BODY:
The line is in German and no speaker is named for it. "Russin" is the feminine form, so the speaker is a woman. After 1918 the old multinational empires had become new nation states, and a woman from the Baltic gentry could be Russian by passport, Lithuanian by birthplace and German by blood. This one insists on the last.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The one hard fact ("Russin" is feminine) added; the café, the Baltic gentry, the passport triad and "defensive" gone; the attribution left open, with the site's own choice stated.
