## son-of-man

**As it stood**

TITLE: The prophet's address
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): And he said unto me, Son of man, stand upon thy feet, and I will speak unto thee.
BODY:
Eliot's note points to Ezekiel 2:1, where God addresses the prophet as "son of man" before sending him to a rebellious people. The next chapter has the prophet eat a scroll; chapter 37 has him prophesy over a valley of dry bones until they stand up as an army ("Dry bones can harm no one," says line 391, with the same book in mind). "A heap of broken images" is Ezekiel too: the Lord promises to break the idols of Israel (6:4–6). The voice that speaks here is not the café voice of Marie. It is the one Eliot would use again in "Ash-Wednesday" and the choruses of *The Rock*, the desert voice, and it has been given a stony landscape, a "red rock," and a question with no answer: what grows here?

Eliot's second note, on line 23, sends you to Ecclesiastes 12:5, the chapter on old age where "the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail: because man goeth to his long home, and the mourners go about the streets." Cricket, grasshopper: the same small dry noise.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: KJV Ezekiel 2:1, 2:3, 3:1–3, 6:4, 6:6 and Ecclesiastes 12:5 at en.wikisource (all quotations exact). Eliot's notes 20 and 23 in the archive.org scan. "Dry bones can harm no one" is line 391 in the every-line count. Ash-Wednesday 1930, The Rock 1934: knowledge.
Substance:
1. "Eliot's second note, on line 23": his first note is the headnote on Weston, so this is his third; say "Eliot's note on line 23".
2. "'A heap of broken images' is Ezekiel too": the usual identification (Southam; Ricks and McCue), not a certainty. "Is usually traced to Ezekiel 6:4–6, where the images of Israel are to be broken."
3. "Cricket, grasshopper: the same small dry noise": the link in Ecclesiastes is weight, not sound. "The grasshopper shall be a burden" is part of the chapter's picture of old age, in which even so light a thing becomes heavy. Eliot's cricket gives "no relief". The note's sentence asserts a connection by noise that the source does not make.
4. "It is the one Eliot would use again in 'Ash-Wednesday' and the choruses of The Rock, the desert voice": a reading. Mark it ("readers have compared").
5. "with the same book in mind": widely shared, but a reading; "usually referred to Ezekiel 37".
6. "before sending him to a rebellious people": right (2:3, "a rebellious nation"). "The next chapter has the prophet eat a scroll": right (3:1–3; the roll is shown to him at 2:9–10).
Draft:
Body: Eliot's note sends the reader to Ezekiel 2:1, where God addresses the prophet as "son of man" and sends him to "a rebellious nation" (2:3). The address runs through the book; in chapter 37 the prophet is set down in a valley of dry bones and told to prophesy until they stand up. "Dry bones can harm no one" (line 391) is usually referred to the same chapter. "A heap of broken images" is usually traced to Ezekiel 6:4–6, where the idols and images of Israel are to be broken. The voice here is unlike anything in the first eighteen lines; readers have compared it to "Ash-Wednesday" (1930) and the choruses of <i>The Rock</i> (1934).

Eliot's note on line 23 points to Ecclesiastes 12:5, in the chapter on old age: "the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail: because man goeth to his long home, and the mourners go about the streets." The grasshopper there is a weight, not a sound: in age even so light a thing is a burden. Eliot's cricket gives "no relief."

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. Title, "The prophet's address". I read "address" as where he lives before I read it as a speech, and then it is God's speech, not the prophet's. ""Son of man" in Ezekiel" or "God's address to the prophet".
2. "The next chapter has the prophet eat a scroll" Nothing to do with this line. A detail for its own sake.
3. "says line 391, with the same book in mind" A line does not have a mind. Say that line 391 is usually referred to the same chapter.
4. "The voice that speaks here is not the café voice of Marie." Staging; the note steps back to narrate the poem. The point, that the speaker changes, takes four words.
5. "It is the one Eliot would use again in "Ash-Wednesday" and the choruses of *The Rock*, the desert voice, and it has been given a stony landscape, a "red rock," and a question with no answer: what grows here?" "The desert voice" is a label the note invents; then a triad, a colon and a rhetorical question for a finish. The one fact in it, that Eliot used a similar manner later, is worth one plain sentence.
6. "sends you to" Second person; the first paragraph's "points to" is right.
7. "Cricket, grasshopper: the same small dry noise." The fragment from the common brief's list. The point is that Eliot changed the insect, which is a sentence.
Keep: "Eliot's note points to Ezekiel 2:1, where God addresses the prophet as "son of man" before sending him to a rebellious people." ""A heap of broken images" is Ezekiel too: the Lord promises to break the idols of Israel (6:4–6)." The Ecclesiastes quotation.
Draft:

BODY:
Eliot's note points to Ezekiel 2:1, where God addresses the prophet as "son of man" before sending him to a rebellious people. Chapter 37 of the same book has the prophet prophesy over a valley of dry bones until they stand up as an army; line 391, "Dry bones can harm no one", is usually referred to it. "A heap of broken images" also recalls Ezekiel, where the Lord promises to break the idols of Israel (6:4–6). The speaker changes here, from Marie to a prophetic voice that Eliot used again in "Ash-Wednesday" and the choruses of <i>The Rock</i>.

Eliot's second note, on line 23, points to Ecclesiastes 12:5, the chapter on old age, where "the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail: because man goeth to his long home, and the mourners go about the streets." Eliot has a cricket where Ecclesiastes has a grasshopper.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Eliot's note sends the reader to Ezekiel 2:1, where God addresses the prophet as "son of man" and sends him to "a rebellious nation" (2:3)." Two "sends" in one sentence, the note sending me and God sending the prophet; I read it twice. ## red-rock

**As it stood**

TITLE: Eliot borrowing from himself
BODY:
"Come in under the shadow of this red rock" is lifted, almost word for word, from a poem Eliot wrote in 1915 and withdrew from publication, "The Death of Saint Narcissus," where the invitation is to come in under the shadow of a gray rock and be shown something different from two shadows, one sprawling over the sand at daybreak, the other leaping behind a fire against a red rock. The saint's grey rock has turned red. The two shadows, morning and evening, are a sundial reading of a whole life, and the line that follows, "I will show you fear in a handful of dust," carries Isaiah's "shadow of a great rock in a weary land" (32:2), the Sibyl's handful of sand, and Tennyson's *Maud*: "Dead, long dead, / Long dead! / And my heart is a handful of dust."

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Isaiah 32:2, en.wikisource raw text: "as the shadow of a great rock in a weary land." Tennyson, Maud, Part II section V, stanza 1, Gutenberg #56913: "Dead, long dead, / Long dead! / And my heart is a handful of dust," exact. Ovid XIV.136–37 (see epigraph). "The Death of Saint Narcissus": first line "Come under the shadow of this gray rock" (Wikipedia); Valerie Eliot dates it early 1915; set in type for Poetry for October 1915 and withdrawn; first printed in Poems Written in Early Youth (privately, 1950; Faber, 1967); in the facsimile (secondary sources; the poem itself I checked from memory). The paraphrase of its opening in the note is accurate and keeps within the rights rule.
Substance:
1. "the line that follows, 'I will show you fear in a handful of dust,' carries Isaiah's 'shadow of a great rock in a weary land' (32:2)": misattached. Isaiah 32:2 is cited (Southam and others) for the shelter under the rock, lines 25–26; it has nothing to do with the handful of dust. Move it.
2. "the Sibyl's handful of sand": dust in Ovid ("pulvis," XIV.136–37); see epigraph 1. The correction makes the note's point for it.
3. "The two shadows, morning and evening, are a sundial reading of a whole life": a reading, unattributed. Mark it as one.
4. Tennyson: a comparison editors make (Ricks and McCue), not a source Eliot acknowledged. "Editors cite".
5. "a poem Eliot wrote in 1915": Valerie Eliot's dating is early 1915; "about 1915" is safer. Add that it was set in type for Poetry and withdrawn, and first printed in 1950, so a reader knows why it is little known.
6. "lifted, almost word for word": right. Line 27, "And I will show you something different from either," is carried over unchanged; line 26 changes only "gray" to "red".
Draft:
Body: "Come in under the shadow of this red rock" comes almost unchanged from "The Death of Saint Narcissus," a poem Eliot wrote about 1915, had set in type for <i>Poetry</i> (Chicago) that autumn and then withdrew; it was first printed in 1950 and is reproduced in the 1971 facsimile. There the rock of shelter is grey, and the speaker promises to show something different from two shadows, one sprawling over the sand at daybreak, the other leaping behind a fire against a red rock. Line 27 is carried over word for word. For the shelter of lines 25–26 commentators cite Isaiah 32:2, "the shadow of a great rock in a weary land." The two shadows of lines 28–29, behind you in the morning and rising to meet you at evening, have been read as a life from birth to death; that is a reading. For "a handful of dust" editors cite Tennyson's <i>Maud</i> (Part II, section V): "Dead, long dead, / Long dead! / And my heart is a handful of dust." The Sibyl of the epigraph, in Ovid, asked for as many years as there were grains in a heap of dust.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. The first sentence is eighty words long. I lost it at "be shown something different from two shadows", and by "against a red rock" I had to go back to find which rock was which. Break it up.
2. "gray rock" and then "grey rock", two lines apart.
3. "The saint's grey rock has turned red." A dramatic little sentence, and by the note's own account the earlier poem already has a red rock (the one behind the fire), so "turned red" is not quite what happened. Say which rock the new poem kept.
4. "The two shadows, morning and evening, are a sundial reading of a whole life" A metaphor about a metaphor, asserted as fact. If it is a reading, mark it as one.
5. "the line that follows, "I will show you fear in a handful of dust," carries Isaiah's "shadow of a great rock in a weary land" (32:2), the Sibyl's handful of sand, and Tennyson's *Maud*" Isaiah's shadow of a rock belongs with lines 25 and 26 (shadow, rock), not with the handful of dust. Bundling all three into one triad on line 30 muddles which line echoes what, and I could not have told you afterwards.
6. Title is fine.
Keep: ""Come in under the shadow of this red rock" is lifted, almost word for word, from a poem Eliot wrote in 1915 and withdrew from publication, "The Death of Saint Narcissus"". The *Maud* quotation.
Draft:
TITLE: Eliot borrowing from himself
BODY:
"Come in under the shadow of this red rock" is lifted, almost word for word, from "The Death of Saint Narcissus", a poem Eliot wrote in 1915 and withdrew from publication. There the invitation is to come in under the shadow of a grey rock and be shown something different from two shadows: one sprawling over the sand at daybreak, the other leaping behind a fire against a red rock. In <i>The Waste Land</i> the rock of the invitation is the red one. "Shadow under this red rock" also recalls Isaiah 32:2, "the shadow of a great rock in a weary land". One reading takes the morning and evening shadows as the two ends of a life. "A handful of dust" at line 30 recalls the Sibyl's handful of sand in the epigraph, and Tennyson's <i>Maud</i>: "Dead, long dead, / Long dead! / And my heart is a handful of dust."

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The eighty-word sentence broken up; Isaiah moved to lines 25–26 where it belongs; the sundial marked as a reading; sand corrected to dust; the earlier poem's history added so a reader knows why it is little known.

There the rock of shelter is grey, and the speaker promises to show something different from two shadows, one sprawling over the sand at daybreak, the other leaping behind a fire against a red rock. Everything after that sentence reads once, the Isaiah echo now sits on the right lines, and the note ends on a fact.

## The scholar's summary, round 1

1. april: two statements are false. The participles' objects do arrive, at the head of the next line; and the Munich lines are in the same verse paragraph as lines 1–7 (the first break is after line 18). Neither Whitman's nor Brooke's poem names April; the Brooke connection is an unattributed suggestion.
2. red-rock: Isaiah 32:2 is attached to line 30; it belongs to the shelter of lines 25–26.
3. epigraph and red-rock: Ovid's Sibyl holds a heap of dust, "pulvis," not sand (Met. XIV.136–37); the shrinking is Ovid's, the jar Petronius's. The Conrad quotation follows Eliot's typescript, not Conrad's punctuation; say which.
4. epigraph and dedication date the same Pound letter two ways. Use 24 January 1922 ("24 Saturnus, An 1"), noting Paige's 24 December 1921.
5. dedication: Pound saw the typescript in Paris in November 1921 as well as January 1922; "halved" is Eliot's own 1946 figure; "Il Miglior Fabbro" was Pound's chapter title in The Spirit of Romance (1910); the dedication is also absent from the Hogarth 1923 edition.
6. marie: "not from any book" reverses the evidence. Morris (1954) found most of lines 8–18 in My Past; Valerie Eliot says only that the sledding came from conversation and that when and where Eliot met Larisch is unknown.
7. starnbergersee: Munich was July and August 1911; Ludwig was found dead, and drowning is disputed; he paid for Tristan's first performance, not the opera; the typescript read Königssee.
8. boston-cut: attribute the cancellation to Valerie Eliot's facsimile and the absence of Pound's marks; the summary of the passage omits Myrtle's, and "burlesque" and "run from a policeman" are unverified.
9. son-of-man: "second note" should be "note on line 23"; the Ecclesiastes grasshopper is a burden, not a noise.
10. litauen: mostly reading; the one hard fact, that "Russin" makes the speaker a woman, is missing.
11. Line numbers in this batch (30, 357, 391, 428) are right in the every-line count. Aside for the README: the 1922 marginal numbers slip between 341 and 351 (the Liveright setting prints "350" beside "A spring", every-line 351), but Eliot's own notes 357 (hermit-thrush) and 360 match the every-line count, so "one behind from 346" does not describe the note numbers between 346 and 360. Worth a check against the 1922 book before Part V is edited.


## The reader's summary, round 1

What I threw out most often was the last sentence. Six of the ten notes end on a line written to be quoted ("It only stops for coffee", "or you", "Keep that title in your head", "it is defensive", "the same small dry noise", "someone who is not sure they mean it"), and two more put one at the end of a paragraph ("Growth that will not finish", "a poem that starts already exhausted"); in every case the note was better without it: the facts before it were doing the work, and the bow told me what to feel about them. Next most common was the colon followed by a list of three or four, used as the default way to end a thought (epigraph, april, marie, son-of-man, boston-cut, dedication), and the formula "the poem's first X" (epigraph, litauen, marie), which makes the poem a person with opinions. Two clarity problems were also real ones: the april note's claim that the participles "never quite arrive at their objects" is contradicted by the lines themselves, and the red-rock note pins Isaiah's shadow of a rock on the wrong line. The good news is that the notes are well stocked with facts and sources; in most of them the first two-thirds can stand, and the fix is to cut the finish and let the fact be the last thing said.



# Batch 2: lines 31–59


## frisch-weht

**As it stood**

TITLE: The sailor's song from the masthead
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): Frisch weht der Wind / der Heimat zu: / mein irisch Kind, / wo weilest du?
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): Fresh blows the wind toward home: my Irish child, where do you linger?
BODY:
*Tristan und Isolde* opens with this song, sung by a young sailor high in the rigging, unseen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall to marry a king she does not love. Isolde hears it as mockery. The wind blows toward the sailor's home and away from hers. Eliot's note gives the reference and nothing else; the four short lines sit in the poem like a snatch of song heard through a wall, and their subject, love that has not happened yet and will end in death, opens the hyacinth garden that follows. The reply comes at line 42.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: stands

Checked: Wagner's libretto, Act I sc. 1 and Isolde's reply (murashev.com German libretto, Act 1; projekt-gutenberg.org returned only an index page). Eliot's note against the 1922 text in `poem-part1.md`.

Substance:
1. Everything checkable is right. The stage direction is "Stimme eines jungen Seemanns (aus der Höhe, wie vom Mast her, vernehmbar)", so "from the masthead" and "unseen" are exact. The quote matches the libretto word for word. Isolde's first words after the song are "Wer wagt mich zu höhnen?" (Who dares to mock me?), so "hears it as mockery" is exact. Eliot's "I, verses 5–8" is exact: the song's lines 1–4 are "Westwärts / schweift der Blick; / ostwärts / streicht das Schiff" (westward roams the gaze; eastward runs the ship), which is also what settles the direction of the wind: toward the sailor's Cornwall, away from Isolde's Ireland. The note could say this in a clause instead of asserting it.
2. "The reply comes at line 42" is the site's reading, not a fact about the opera. Line 42 is from Act III, sung by a different character; the juxtaposition is Eliot's. Say "Eliot's second quotation from the opera, at line 42, is from Act III".
3. "its subject, love that has not happened yet and will end in death" is an anticipation of the whole opera laid on four lines. Not wrong, but a reading; harmless if cut.
4. Small addition a reader would want: the king is Marke of Cornwall, Tristan's uncle.

Draft:
TITLE: The sailor's song
BODY: <i>Tristan und Isolde</i> opens with this song, sung from the masthead by a young sailor who is heard but not seen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall, where she is to marry King Marke, Tristan's uncle. The four lines before the ones Eliot quotes give the ship's course ("westward roams the gaze; eastward runs the ship"), so the wind that blows "toward home" blows toward the sailor's Cornwall and away from Isolde's Ireland. She takes the song as mockery; her first words in the opera are "Who dares to mock me?" Eliot's note gives the act and line numbers and nothing else. His second quotation from the opera, at line 42, comes from Act III.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "the four short lines sit in the poem like a snatch of song heard through a wall" — a metaphor about how the lines feel, not an explanation of them. It tells me how to hear the poem, which I can do for myself, and gives me no fact.
2. "their subject, love that has not happened yet and will end in death, opens the hyacinth garden that follows" — I read this twice. The appositive is long, and a subject "opening" a garden is a stretch. The information underneath (where the song falls in the opera's story; that the garden comes next in the poem) is worth keeping in plainer words.
3. "The reply comes at line 42." — a short quotable closer. Line 42 is not a reply in the opera; it is a second quotation from a different act. "Reply" is the writer's pairing dressed as a fact. A plain cross-reference does the job.
4. "*Tristan und Isolde* opens with this song" — for the scholar: the opera opens with an orchestral prelude; the song opens Act I, and these are verses 5 to 8 of it, which is what Eliot's note says. "Act I opens with this song" would be exact. I have written it that way in the draft; the scholar should confirm.
5. "a king she does not love" — the king has a name, Mark of Cornwall, and a reader would rather have it. Scholar to confirm before it goes in.

Keep: "sung by a young sailor high in the rigging, unseen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall to marry a king she does not love. Isolde hears it as mockery. The wind blows toward the sailor's home and away from hers. Eliot's note gives the reference and nothing else". The title, and the quote and translation, are fine as they are.

Draft:
TITLE: The sailor's song from the masthead
BODY: Act I of Wagner's <i>Tristan und Isolde</i> opens with this song. A young sailor sings it from high in the rigging, unseen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall to marry King Mark, whom she does not love. Isolde hears it as mockery. The wind blows toward the sailor's home and away from hers. Eliot's note gives the reference, verses 5 to 8 of Act I, and nothing else. At this point in the opera Tristan and Isolde are not yet lovers; by its end both are dead. Eliot quotes the opera once more at line 42, from its last act, after the hyacinth garden.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. "Act I opens" replaces "the opera opens" (the reader's point: the Prelude comes first); the king named, with Wagner's spelling; the ship's course from the four lines before Eliot's quotation, which is what settles "toward home" (scholar); Isolde's first words added; "a snatch of song heard through a wall", the anticipation of the whole opera, and "the reply" go. The second quotation is placed as Eliot's, not as an answer.
TITLE: The sailor's song from the masthead
BODY:
Act I of Wagner's <i>Tristan und Isolde</i> opens with this song. A young sailor sings it from high in the rigging, heard but not seen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall, where she is to marry King Marke, Tristan's uncle. The four lines before the ones Eliot quotes give the ship's course ("westward roams the gaze; eastward runs the ship"), so the wind that blows "toward home" blows toward the sailor's Cornwall and away from Isolde's Ireland. She takes the song as mockery; her first words in the opera are "Who dares to mock me?" Eliot's note gives the act and the verse numbers, 5 to 8, and nothing else. His second quotation from the opera, at line 42, comes from its last act.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

Everything in the rewrite checks against the libretto (murashev.com, Act I): the stage direction "aus der Höhe, wie vom Mast her", the ship's course in the four lines before Eliot's, Marke as Tristan's uncle, Isolde's "Wer wagt mich zu höhnen?", and "verses 5–8". One precision the editor may take or leave, since it is not an error a reader would be misled by: the orchestral Prelude comes first and the libretto prints it under Act I ("Erster Aufzug. Einleitung"), so "Act I opens with this song" has the same small looseness as "the opera opens". Exact would be: "The first words sung in Wagner's <i>Tristan und Isolde</i> are this song."

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Editor, after round 2**

Both sides satisfied. The scholar's optional precision taken (the Prelude is printed under Act I, so "Act I opens with this song" was loose): the first words sung.

A young sailor sings them from high in the rigging, heard but not seen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall, where she is to marry King Marke, Tristan's uncle." TITLE: The sailor's song from the masthead
BODY:
These are the first words sung in Wagner's <i>Tristan und Isolde</i>. A young sailor sings them from high in the rigging, heard but not seen, as Tristan's ship carries Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall, where she is to marry King Marke, Tristan's uncle. The four lines before the ones Eliot quotes give the ship's course ("westward roams the gaze; eastward runs the ship"), so the wind that blows "toward home" blows toward the sailor's Cornwall and away from Isolde's Ireland. She takes the song as mockery; her first words in the opera are "Who dares to mock me?" Eliot's note gives the act and the verse numbers, 5 to 8, and nothing else. His second quotation from the opera, at line 42, comes from its last act.


## hyacinth-girl

**As it stood**

TITLE: The hyacinth garden
BODY:
Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus; the flower grew from his blood, and in Ovid its petals carry the letters of a cry of grief (*Metamorphoses* X). In the Greek world the hyacinth had its own festival, the Hyacinthia at Sparta, and Hyacinth is, in Frazer's reading, another dying and reviving god. All of that is available, and none of it explains the passage, which is one of the few places in the poem where a speaker admits to having felt something. "I could not / Speak, and my eyes failed, I was neither / Living nor dead, and I knew nothing, / Looking into the heart of light, the silence." The heart of light is Conrad's heart of darkness turned inside out, and it is also the mystical blank that Eliot's later poems circle.

Two lives have been read into the garden. Lyndall Gordon's biographies attach it to Emily Hale, the Bostonian Eliot loved before he sailed for Europe in 1914; his letters to her, unsealed at Princeton in 2020, show how long that attachment lasted. Others hear Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in 1915, to whom *Prufrock and Other Observations* is dedicated. In 1934 Eliot remembered "a friend coming across the Luxembourg Gardens in the late afternoon, waving a branch of lilac, a friend who was later (so far as I could find out) to be mixed with the mud of Gallipoli." Lilacs, line 2. The poem does not choose.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> X 162–219 (thelatinlibrary.com, ovid.met10). Frazer, <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, 3rd ed. 1914, vol. I, Book II ch. VII "Hyacinth", pp. 313–17 (archive.org, in.ernet.dli.2015.174186). Hyacinthia (Wikipedia; Frazer p. 314). Emily Hale, Jean Verdenal (Wikipedia); Princeton's announcement of the letters (research.princeton.edu). The 1917 dedication of <i>Prufrock</i> (Gutenberg #1459). John Peter 1952 and James E. Miller 1977 (Essays in Criticism archive; Wikipedia). The Criterion sentence (LRB, Mark Ford, "Hyacinth Boy"; other secondary copies agree on the wording). <i>Burnt Norton</i> I (poets.org). <i>Inferno</i> XXXIV 25 (it.wikisource). Eliot's 1929 <i>Dante</i>, the paragraph on Canto XXXIV, in a scan of <i>Selected Essays</i> 1932 (archive.org, dli.ernet.544072).

Substance:
1. "Hyacinth is, in Frazer's reading, another dying and reviving god." Frazer is more tentative. His chapter opens: Hyacinth "has been supposed to belong to the class of gods here discussed" and "has been interpreted as the vegetation which blooms in spring and withers under the scorching heat of the summer sun"; Frazer's own suggestion is that he was an aboriginal deity, "perhaps a dead king". The chapter is the last of the Attis book in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot's headnote names, which is worth saying. (Frazer also notes the flower is "usually supposed" not to be our hyacinth but "a little purple iris" with AI on its petals.)
2. "the Hyacinthia at Sparta": a Spartan festival, held at Amyclae; three days, the first of mourning, then rejoicing (Frazer p. 314; Wikipedia). "at Amyclae, near Sparta" is exact.
3. Ovid checks out: discus rebounds from the ground (X 182–85), the flower springs from the blood (209–12), "AI AI / flos habet inscriptum" (215–16).
4. "one of the few places in the poem where a speaker admits to having felt something": a reading, and a shaky one; Marie says "I was frightened" at line 15. Cut.
5. "The heart of light is Conrad's heart of darkness turned inside out": a reading, unattributed. What is documented: the typescript's epigraph was a passage from <i>Heart of Darkness</i> ending "The horror! the horror!"; Pound wrote on 24 January 1922 that he doubted Conrad was "weighty enough to stand the citation", and Eliot gave it up (Valerie Eliot's notes in the 1971 facsimile; <i>Letters</i> vol. 1). Give the fact and mark the inversion as a reading.
6. "the mystical blank that Eliot's later poems circle": vague where a fact exists. <i>Burnt Norton</i> I (1935) reuses the phrase: "The surface glittered out of heart of light". A three-word quotation is within the rights rule.
7. "Emily Hale, the Bostonian": she was born in East Orange, New Jersey, and grew up in Chestnut Hill outside Boston (Wikipedia). "of Boston" is safe. Gordon's attachment of the garden to Hale runs from <i>Eliot's Early Years</i> (1977) to <i>The Hyacinth Girl</i> (2022). The letters: 1,131 of them, opened at Princeton on 2 January 2020 (Princeton's announcement).
8. "Others hear Jean Verdenal": name them. John Peter, "A New Interpretation of The Waste Land", <i>Essays in Criticism</i> 1952, withdrawn at the demand of Eliot's solicitors and reprinted in 1969; James E. Miller, <i>T. S. Eliot's Personal Waste Land</i> (1977). Verdenal, a medical officer, was killed at the Dardanelles on 2 May 1915. The 1917 dedication reads "To Jean Verdenal 1889–1915" (Gutenberg text); "mort aux Dardanelles" and the Purgatorio epigraph were added in 1925.
9. Rights: the Criterion sentence (April 1934, "A Commentary") is quoted in full, forty-odd words. The common brief allows the 1934 commentary "a phrase or two". Cut to "waving a branch of lilac" and "the mud of Gallipoli" and give the source. The wording as quoted is otherwise accurate.
10. Missing that matters: "I was neither / Living nor dead" is regularly set beside Dante's words at the sight of Satan, <i>Inferno</i> XXXIV 25, "Io non mori' e non rimasi vivo" (I did not die and did not stay alive), text verified at Wikisource. I could not check which commentary first drew the parallel (Southam and Ricks–McCue, from memory). One caution: Eliot's 1929 <i>Dante</i> discusses Canto XXXIV but does not quote this line (checked in the scan), so the note must not say he did.
11. "Lilacs, line 2. The poem does not choose." The lilac link is a reading; the last sentence is the brief's own example of the bow. Both go.

Draft:
TITLE: The hyacinth garden
BODY: Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus. The flower sprang from his blood, and in Ovid its petals carry the letters of a cry of grief, AI AI (<i>Metamorphoses</i> X, 162–219). Sparta kept a three-day festival for him at Amyclae, the Hyacinthia, a day of mourning followed by rejoicing. Frazer gives him a short chapter at the end of the Attis book of <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot names in his headnote, as a figure who "has been interpreted as the vegetation which blooms in spring and withers". None of this is needed to read the lines, which describe a moment of speechlessness and blankness. "Neither / Living nor dead" is often set beside Dante's words at the sight of Satan, <i>Inferno</i> XXXIV 25: "Io non mori' e non rimasi vivo" (I did not die and did not stay alive). "The heart of light" has been read against Conrad's title; the typescript's epigraph was a passage from <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, which Pound talked Eliot out of in January 1922. Eliot used the phrase again in <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1935): "The surface glittered out of heart of light".

Two lives have been read into the garden. Lyndall Gordon, from <i>Eliot's Early Years</i> (1977) to <i>The Hyacinth Girl</i> (2022), attaches it to Emily Hale of Boston, to whom Eliot declared his love before sailing for Europe in 1914; his 1,131 letters to her, opened at Princeton on 2 January 2020, show how long the attachment lasted. John Peter (1952) and James E. Miller (1977) hear Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> is dedicated. In the <i>Criterion</i> for April 1934 Eliot recalled that friend crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac", and his death in "the mud of Gallipoli".

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "the letters of a cry of grief" — coy. Ovid says which letters (AI AI). Tell me.
2. "All of that is available, and none of it explains the passage" — the writer lays out the learning and then waves it away, which is a performance. Either the myth bears on the lines or it does not; say which, plainly.
3. "which is one of the few places in the poem where a speaker admits to having felt something" — a confident claim about the whole poem, and it tells me what to make of the passage before I have read it. Whose observation is this? If it stays, mark it as a reading.
4. The four lines of the poem quoted in full ("I could not / Speak … the silence.") — the lines are beside the note already. Quoting them back to me is padding.
5. "The heart of light is Conrad's heart of darkness turned inside out" — "turned inside out" is a flourish. The fact underneath (the phrase inverts Conrad's title) is useful and can be said plainly.
6. "the mystical blank that Eliot's later poems circle" — the sentence that lost me. What is a mystical blank, and which poems circle it? If the point is that Eliot reused the phrase, name the poem: "Burnt Norton" has "the heart of light" in its opening section. Scholar to confirm.
7. "Two lives have been read into the garden" — arch. "Two people in Eliot's life have been proposed" says it.
8. "Others hear Jean Verdenal" — "hear" is precious, and "Others" is nobody. Gordon gets a name; whoever first proposed Verdenal should get one too. Scholar to supply.
9. The 1934 quotation ("a friend coming across the Luxembourg Gardens … the mud of Gallipoli") runs to about thirty-five words. The common brief says the 1934 Criterion commentary may be quoted only in a phrase or two. Paraphrase and keep one phrase.
10. "Lilacs, line 2." — a fragment I had to decode (the lilac in the memory, the lilacs in line 2). A plain cross-reference does the same work.
11. "The poem does not choose." — the bow at the end, and the exact sentence the common brief gives as an example of what has to go. The point (the text names no one) belongs at the start of the paragraph as a fact, not at the end as a line.
12. For the scholar: the original says "the hyacinth had its own festival"; the Hyacinthia honoured Hyacinthus, so I have written "a festival in his name". Confirm.

Keep: "Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus; the flower grew from his blood"; "Hyacinth is, in Frazer's reading, another dying and reviving god"; the whole Emily Hale sentence ("Lyndall Gordon's biographies attach it to Emily Hale … show how long that attachment lasted"); "the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in 1915, to whom *Prufrock and Other Observations* is dedicated". The title.

Draft:
TITLE: The hyacinth garden
BODY: Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus. The flower grew from his blood, and in Ovid its petals carry the letters AI AI, a cry of grief (<i>Metamorphoses</i> X). Sparta held a festival in his name, the Hyacinthia, and in Frazer's reading he is another of the dying and reviving gods. The passage itself takes nothing from the myth but the name. Its last phrase, "the heart of light", inverts the title of Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>; Eliot used it again in "Burnt Norton".

Two people in Eliot's life have been proposed for the figure in the garden, and the text names neither. Lyndall Gordon's biographies attach it to Emily Hale, the Bostonian Eliot loved before he sailed for Europe in 1914; his letters to her, unsealed at Princeton in 2020, show how long that attachment lasted. Other readers have proposed Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> is dedicated. In a note in the <i>Criterion</i> in 1934 Eliot recalled a friend crossing the Luxembourg Gardens in the late afternoon "waving a branch of lilac", a friend who was afterwards killed at Gallipoli. Compare the lilacs of line 2.

(If the editor wants to keep the point that the passage records a felt experience, it can go in as a marked reading after the Ovid and Frazer sentences. I would leave it out. "Other readers have proposed" should become a name once the scholar supplies one.)

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. AI AI spelled out with Ovid's lines; Amyclae and the shape of the festival; Frazer's own hedged words in place of "in Frazer's reading"; the quoted lines, "admits to having felt something", "turned inside out" and "the mystical blank" go; Dante XXXIV 25 added as the commentators' parallel; Conrad given as a reading, with the fact of the discarded epigraph; <i>Burnt Norton</i> named without quotation beyond the phrase; Peter and Miller named; the 1934 sentence cut to two phrases with its source; the two closing fragments go and the point that the text names no one moves to the head of the paragraph. "Compare the lilacs of line 2" stays as a bare cross-reference, which asserts nothing; the scholar may strike it.
TITLE: The hyacinth garden
BODY:
Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus. The flower sprang from his blood, and in Ovid its petals carry the letters AI AI, a cry of grief (<i>Metamorphoses</i> X, 162–219). Sparta kept a three-day festival for him at Amyclae, the Hyacinthia, a day of mourning followed by rejoicing. Frazer gives him a short chapter at the end of the Attis book of <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot's headnote names, as a figure who "has been interpreted as the vegetation which blooms in spring and withers". The lines themselves take nothing from the myth but the name. Commentators set "neither / Living nor dead" beside Dante's words at the sight of Satan, <i>Inferno</i> XXXIV 25: "Io non mori' e non rimasi vivo" (I did not die and did not stay alive). "The heart of light" has been read against the title of Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, the book the typescript's epigraph came from (see the note on the epigraph). Eliot used the phrase again in the first section of <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1935).

Two people in Eliot's life have been proposed for the figure in the garden; the text names neither. Lyndall Gordon, from <i>Eliot's Early Years</i> (1977) to <i>The Hyacinth Girl</i> (2022), attaches it to Emily Hale of Boston, to whom Eliot declared his love before sailing for Europe in 1914; his 1,131 letters to her, opened at Princeton in January 2020, show how long the attachment lasted. John Peter (1952) and James E. Miller (1977) proposed Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> (1917) is dedicated. In the <i>Criterion</i> for April 1934 Eliot recalled that friend crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac", and his death in "the mud of Gallipoli". Compare the lilacs of line 2.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "The lines themselves take nothing from the myth but the name." A reading given as fact, and one that cuts against Eliot's own headnote, which tells readers of Frazer that they "will immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies"; the hyacinth is usually counted among them, which is why the commentaries give the myth at all. Put: "None of this is needed to read the lines. The commentaries give it because Eliot's headnote promises readers of Frazer 'certain references to vegetation ceremonies', and the hyacinth is usually counted as one."

2. "the first section of <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1935)". The poem was written in 1935 but first published in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (Faber, 1936); the note's other dates are publication dates (<i>Crome Yellow</i> November 1921, <i>Prufrock</i> 1917, <i>Eliot's Early Years</i> 1977). Put: "the first section of <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1936)". Source: Wikipedia, "Burnt Norton" ("first published in his <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> (1936)").

3. "John Peter (1952) and James E. Miller (1977) proposed Jean Verdenal". Peter's 1952 essay ("A New Interpretation of <i>The Waste Land</i>", <i>Essays in Criticism</i> II.3, July 1952, pp. 242–66) read the poem as an elegy for a dead friend and did not name him; Eliot's solicitors had the issue withdrawn. The name came only in the "Postscript" Peter added when the essay was reprinted in <i>Essays in Criticism</i> XIX (1969), after Eliot's death. Sources: Wikipedia, "Jean Jules Verdenal" ("In this second essay Peter identified the friend as Verdenal"); OUP, <i>Essays in Criticism</i> II.3 (1952) and XIX.2 (1969). Put: "John Peter, whose 1952 essay read the poem as an elegy for a dead friend and whose 1969 postscript named him, and James E. Miller (1977) proposed Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> (1917) is dedicated."

Otherwise sound. Gordon's 1977 book did already make the Hale link (her own account, <i>Modernist Review</i> interview, December 2019), so that sentence stands. "(see the note on the epigraph)" lands: the batch-1 epigraph rewrite gives the Conrad passage and Pound's letter. "Compare the lilacs of line 2" may stand; it asserts nothing.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Frazer gives him a short chapter at the end of the Attis book of <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot's headnote names, as a figure who "has been interpreted as ..."" — the "as a figure who" clause hangs off "gives him a short chapter" across an appositive, and I read it twice to find what it belonged to. Put: "In <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot's headnote names, Frazer gives him a short chapter at the end of the Attis book. He writes there that Hyacinth "has been interpreted as the vegetation which blooms in spring and withers"."
2. "Sparta kept a three-day festival for him at Amyclae, the Hyacinthia, a day of mourning followed by rejoicing." — "three-day" and "a day" trip over each other; I stopped to count. Put: "Sparta kept a three-day festival for him at Amyclae, the Hyacinthia, which began with a day of mourning and ended in rejoicing."
3. ""The heart of light" has been read against the title of Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>" — "read against" is critic's shorthand; a reader can take it as "read in opposition to". Put: ""The heart of light" has been read as an inversion of the title of Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, the book the typescript's epigraph came from (see the note on the epigraph)."
For the scholar: "<i>Burnt Norton</i> (1935)" — a bracketed date reads as the date of publication; 1935 is the year of writing, and the poem was first printed in <i>Collected Poems 1909–1935</i> in April 1936.

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: "take nothing from the myth but the name" was a reading as fact, against Eliot's headnote (replacement taken); <i>Burnt Norton</i> published 1936; Peter named Verdenal only in his 1969 postscript. Reader: the Frazer sentence split; the festival sentence; "read against" made plain. All six taken.
TITLE: The hyacinth garden
BODY:
Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus. The flower sprang from his blood, and in Ovid its petals carry the letters AI AI, a cry of grief (<i>Metamorphoses</i> X, 162–219). Sparta kept a three-day festival for him at Amyclae, the Hyacinthia, which began with a day of mourning and ended in rejoicing. In <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot's headnote names, Frazer gives him a short chapter at the end of the Attis book. He writes there that Hyacinth "has been interpreted as the vegetation which blooms in spring and withers". None of this is needed to read the lines. The commentaries give it because Eliot's headnote promises readers of Frazer "certain references to vegetation ceremonies", and the hyacinth is usually counted as one. Annotators set "neither / Living nor dead" beside Dante's words at the sight of Satan, <i>Inferno</i> XXXIV 25: "Io non mori' e non rimasi vivo" (I did not die and did not stay alive). "The heart of light" has been read as an inversion of the title of Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, the book the typescript's epigraph came from (see the note on the epigraph). Eliot used the phrase again in the first section of <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1936).

Two people in Eliot's life have been proposed for the figure in the garden; the text names neither. Lyndall Gordon, from <i>Eliot's Early Years</i> (1977) to <i>The Hyacinth Girl</i> (2022), attaches it to Emily Hale of Boston, to whom Eliot declared his love before sailing for Europe in 1914; his 1,131 letters to her, opened at Princeton in January 2020, show how long the attachment lasted. John Peter, whose 1952 essay read the poem as an elegy for a dead friend and whose 1969 postscript named him, and James E. Miller (1977) proposed Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> (1917) is dedicated. In the <i>Criterion</i> for April 1934 Eliot recalled that friend crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac", and his death in "the mud of Gallipoli". Compare the lilacs of line 2.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

The reader's reshaping introduced nothing wrong. "Began with a day of mourning and ended in rejoicing" is Frazer's account (<i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i> I, 314–15: on the first day the people mourned, "wearing no wreaths, singing no paeans, eating no bread"; on the second "All was joy and bustle"). The Frazer quotation, the <i>Inferno</i> line, <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1936) and the Peter sentence all stand as corrected.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: not yet
1. "John Peter, whose 1952 essay read the poem as an elegy for a dead friend and whose 1969 postscript named him, and James E. Miller (1977) proposed Jean Verdenal, the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> (1917) is dedicated." — the subject runs for twenty-five words before its verb, and "named him" comes before the reader has been told who "him" is. Put: "John Peter's 1952 essay read the poem as an elegy for a dead friend, and his 1969 postscript named the friend as Jean Verdenal; James E. Miller (1977) made the same identification. Verdenal was the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> (1917) is dedicated."

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The hyacinth garden
BODY:
Hyacinthus was the boy Apollo loved and killed by accident with a discus. The flower sprang from his blood, and in Ovid its petals carry the letters AI AI, a cry of grief (<i>Metamorphoses</i> X, 162–219). Sparta kept a three-day festival for him at Amyclae, the Hyacinthia, which began with a day of mourning and ended in rejoicing. In <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the volumes Eliot's headnote names, Frazer gives him a short chapter at the end of the Attis book. He writes there that Hyacinth "has been interpreted as the vegetation which blooms in spring and withers". None of this is needed to read the lines. The commentaries give it because Eliot's headnote promises readers of Frazer "certain references to vegetation ceremonies", and the hyacinth is usually counted as one. Annotators set "neither / Living nor dead" beside Dante's words at the sight of Satan, <i>Inferno</i> XXXIV 25: "Io non mori' e non rimasi vivo" (I did not die and did not stay alive). "The heart of light" has been read as an inversion of the title of Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, the book the typescript's epigraph came from (see the note on the epigraph). Eliot used the phrase again in the first section of <i>Burnt Norton</i> (1936).

Two people in Eliot's life have been proposed for the figure in the garden; the text names neither. Lyndall Gordon, from <i>Eliot's Early Years</i> (1977) to <i>The Hyacinth Girl</i> (2022), attaches it to Emily Hale of Boston, to whom Eliot declared his love before sailing for Europe in 1914; his 1,131 letters to her, opened at Princeton in January 2020, show how long the attachment lasted. John Peter's 1952 essay read the poem as an elegy for a dead friend, and his 1969 postscript named the friend as Jean Verdenal; James E. Miller (1977) made the same identification. Verdenal was the friend of Eliot's Paris year, killed at the Dardanelles in May 1915, to whom <i>Prufrock and Other Observations</i> (1917) is dedicated. In the <i>Criterion</i> for April 1934 Eliot recalled that friend crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac", and his death in "the mud of Gallipoli". Compare the lilacs of line 2.


## oed-und-leer

**As it stood**

TITLE: Empty and desolate the sea
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): Öd und leer das Meer!
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): Desolate and empty the sea!
BODY:
Act III of *Tristan*. Tristan lies dying in Brittany, waiting for Isolde's ship. A shepherd keeps watch and is to play a merry tune the moment a sail appears. Asked whether he sees anything, he answers with these four words. Between line 31 (the ship setting out, love ahead) and line 42 (the ship not coming, love behind), the hyacinth garden happens. Eliot's note, "Id. III, verse 24," is as brief as the shepherd.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: stands

Checked: Wagner's libretto, Act III sc. 1 (murashev.com German libretto, Act 3), counting the sung lines from the shepherd's "Kurwenal! He!". Eliot's note in `poem-part1.md`.

Substance:
1. Eliot's "Id. III, verse 24" is exact: "Öd und leer das Meer!" is the twenty-fourth sung line of the act (stage directions and speakers' names not counted). The note calls the reference "brief"; it could say it is right.
2. The sequence is slightly compressed. Kurwenal asks "Sahst du noch nichts? Kein Schiff noch auf der See?"; the shepherd says Kurwenal would hear a merry tune if he had; Kurwenal tells him to keep watch and play "lustig und hell" if a ship appears; the shepherd turns, shades his eyes, looks out to sea and says the line. "Asked whether he sees anything, he answers with these four words" is close enough.
3. The scene: Tristan lies wounded at Kareol, his castle in Brittany; Kurwenal has sent to Cornwall for Isolde, "die Ärztin ... die einz'ge, die uns hilft" (the physician, the only one who can help us). "dying" and "waiting for Isolde's ship" are right.
4. Title: "Empty and desolate the sea" reverses the German. <i>Öd</i> is desolate or waste (as in <i>Ödland</i>, waste land), <i>leer</i> is empty; the translation line under the quote has them in the right order. Match the title to it. The word <i>öd</i> being the German for "waste" is a dictionary fact a reader might want; I know of no critic to attribute it to.
5. Quote: editions differ between "Öd' und leer" (with the elision mark, as in Eliot's "Oed'") and "Öd und leer". No change needed; the apostrophe form would sit closer to Eliot's text.
6. "(the ship setting out, love ahead) ... (the ship not coming, love behind)" is a reading. Isolde's ship does come in Act III; Tristan dies as she lands. Fine if marked, better cut.

Draft:
TITLE: Desolate and empty the sea
BODY: Act III of <i>Tristan</i>. Tristan lies wounded at Kareol, his castle in Brittany, and Kurwenal has sent to Cornwall for Isolde, the only one who can heal him. A shepherd keeps watch from the wall and is to play a merry tune the moment a sail appears; told to look, he looks out to sea and answers with these four words. Eliot's note, "Id. III, verse 24", is exact: the line is the twenty-fourth sung in the act. <i>Öd</i> is the German for desolate or waste (<i>Ödland</i>, waste land). Between the sailor's song of Act I at line 31 and this line from Act III, the poem sets the hyacinth garden.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Act III of *Tristan*." — a fragment standing in for a sentence.
2. "Between line 31 (the ship setting out, love ahead) and line 42 (the ship not coming, love behind), the hyacinth garden happens." — the two bracketed pairs are a matched antithesis built for rhythm, and "the hyacinth garden happens" is arch. The point underneath is good and worth keeping: the two quotations stand either side of the garden, one from the start of the story and one from its end.
3. "Eliot's note, "Id. III, verse 24," is as brief as the shepherd." — a joke to close on. What I wanted from that sentence was to be told what "Id." means.
4. Title: "Empty and desolate the sea" reverses the order of the translation under the quote ("Desolate and empty the sea!"). One or the other. A title that says what the note is about ("The shepherd's answer") would match the other echo titles in this batch ("The sailor's song from the masthead", "Ariel's song").
5. For the scholar, not me: the quote reads "Öd und leer das Meer!"; some printings of the libretto have "Öd' und leer". Whichever matches the source the library cites.

Keep: "Tristan lies dying in Brittany, waiting for Isolde's ship. A shepherd keeps watch and is to play a merry tune the moment a sail appears. Asked whether he sees anything, he answers with these four words."

Draft:
TITLE: The shepherd's answer
BODY: The words are from Act III of <i>Tristan und Isolde</i>. Tristan lies dying in Brittany, waiting for Isolde's ship. A shepherd keeps watch and is to play a merry tune the moment a sail appears. Asked whether he sees anything, he answers with these four words. This quotation and the one at line 31 stand on either side of the hyacinth garden. That one comes from the opening of the opera, before Tristan and Isolde have fallen in love; this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying. Eliot's note, "Id. III, verse 24," points back to the note on line 31: "Id." is short for <i>idem</i>, the same work.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. Title changed to the reader's, which matches the other echo titles and sidesteps the word-order problem; the fragment opener made a sentence; Kareol, the wound and Kurwenal's errand added (scholar); "Id." explained and Eliot's count confirmed; <i>öd</i> glossed; the "love ahead / love behind" pair replaced by a plain statement of where the two quotations fall; the joke on the shepherd goes. The quote field stands.
TITLE: The shepherd's answer
BODY:
The words are from Act III of <i>Tristan und Isolde</i>. Tristan lies wounded at Kareol, his castle in Brittany, and Kurwenal has sent to Cornwall for Isolde, the only one who can heal him. A shepherd keeps watch and is to play a merry tune the moment a sail appears; told to look, he looks out to sea and answers with these four words. Eliot's note, "Id. III, verse 24," points back to the note on line 31 ("Id." is <i>idem</i>, the same work), and the count is exact: the line is the twenty-fourth sung in the act. <i>Öd</i> is the German for desolate or waste, as in <i>Ödland</i>, waste land. This quotation and the one at line 31 stand on either side of the hyacinth garden, that one from the opening of the opera, before Tristan and Isolde have fallen in love, this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "that one from the opening of the opera, before Tristan and Isolde have fallen in love". In Wagner's Act I the love is already there: Isolde's narrative in scene 3 tells how she spared the wounded Tristan, calling himself "Tantris", when he looked into her eyes, and her rage is at his betrayal of that; what the act ends with is the potion (scene 5, "Der Liebestrank"), which releases it. Source: the libretto, Act I scenes 3 and 5 (murashev.com). Put: "that one from the opening of the opera, before the love potion, this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying."

Everything else checks: Kareol is named in the libretto (Kurwenal: "Kareol, Herr: kennst du die Burg der Väter nicht?"); the count to "verse 24" is exact; "Id." for <i>idem</i> is right.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Kurwenal has sent to Cornwall for Isolde" — a name with nobody attached to it; a reader who does not know the opera cannot use it. Put: "his servant Kurwenal has sent to Cornwall for Isolde" (the scholar to choose the word: servant, squire or retainer).
2. "This quotation and the one at line 31 stand on either side of the hyacinth garden, that one from the opening of the opera, before Tristan and Isolde have fallen in love, this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying." — one sentence with two tails hung on commas; I lost where "that one" ended and "this one" began. Put: "This quotation and the one at line 31 stand on either side of the hyacinth garden. That one comes from the opening of the opera, before Tristan and Isolde have fallen in love; this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying."

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: "before Tristan and Isolde have fallen in love" is wrong for Wagner's Act I; "before the love potion". Reader: Kurwenal identified; the last sentence split. All taken.
TITLE: The shepherd's answer
BODY:
The words are from Act III of <i>Tristan und Isolde</i>. Tristan lies wounded at Kareol, his castle in Brittany, and his servant Kurwenal has sent to Cornwall for Isolde, the only one who can heal him. A shepherd keeps watch and is to play a merry tune the moment a sail appears; told to look, he looks out to sea and answers with these four words. Eliot's note, "Id. III, verse 24," points back to the note on line 31 ("Id." is <i>idem</i>, the same work), and the count is exact: the line is the twenty-fourth sung in the act. <i>Öd</i> is the German for desolate or waste, as in <i>Ödland</i>, waste land. This quotation and the one at line 31 stand on either side of the hyacinth garden. That one comes from the opening of the opera, before the love potion; this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

"His servant Kurwenal" is the standard description of the role (the cast list in English gives "Kurwenal, Tristan's servant"; the libretto itself gives him no label). "Has sent to Cornwall for Isolde" is Kurwenal's own account in Act III ("Nach Kornwall hab ich ausgesandt"). "Before the love potion" as agreed.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: not yet
1. "That one comes from the opening of the opera, before the love potion; this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying." — "the love potion" arrives with nothing to attach it to, and "they" has no antecedent in the sentence. Put: "That one comes from the opening of the opera, before Tristan and Isolde drink the love potion; this one from its last act, when they are apart and Tristan is dying."

**As it now stands**

III, verse 24," points back to the note on line 31 ("Id." is <i>idem</i>, the same work), and the count is exact: the line is the twenty-fourth sung in the act. <i>Öd</i> is the German for desolate or waste, as in <i>Ödland</i>, waste land. This quotation and the one at line 31 stand on either side of the hyacinth garden. ## sosostris

**As it stood**

TITLE: The clairvoyante with a cold
BODY:
The name is borrowed and bent. In Aldous Huxley's novel *Crome Yellow*, published in November 1921 while Eliot was drafting, the cynic Mr. Scogan dresses up at a village fair as "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana" to tell fortunes. Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian pharaoh. Eliot feminises the fake and gives her a head cold. "Clairvoyante," with the French feminine ending, is the trade term; "the wisest woman in Europe" is the trade puff. She is the poem's most fully drawn comic character, and she is also its first prophet, since everything she deals turns out to be true.

"One must be so careful these days": fortune-telling for money was an offence under the Vagrancy Act of 1824, and prosecutions of palmists and mediums were routine in the London of the 1920s. She is not being philosophical. She is afraid of the police.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Crome Yellow</i> ch. XXV and XXVII (Gutenberg #1999). Grover Smith, "The Fortuneteller in Eliot's Waste Land", <i>American Literature</i> 25 (Jan. 1954), 490–92, and Eliot's letter to him of 10 March 1952, as reported in a 2019 article on Sosostris and Scogan (Garmian University journal; the PDF would not parse, so this rests on the search summary) and in Rainey. Rainey, <i>Revisiting The Waste Land</i> (2005), ch. 1, on the dating of the Parts I–II typescript (ebin.pub copy). Vagrancy Act 1824 s. 4 wording (Hansard, Lords, 3 May 1951); the 1921 ruling (Encyclopedia of Occultism and Parapsychology via encyclopedia.com). Sesostris in Herodotus II 102–10, from knowledge.

Substance:
1. "The name is borrowed" from <i>Crome Yellow</i>: the novel checks out (ch. XXVII: Mr Scogan "in a little canvas hut", "dressed in a black skirt and a red bodice" and a black wig, a placard announcing "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana", reading palms), but the borrowing is disputed on chronology. Eliot told Grover Smith in 1952 that he was "almost certain" he had taken the name from the novel without being aware of it. Lawrence Rainey, from the paper and typewriter of the typescript, dates Parts I and II to 9–22 May 1921, and Lyndall Gordon had already pointed out that Huxley finished the novel only in August 1921 (published November). If Rainey is right the name cannot come from the book unless the line was added later. The brief's own "usually traced to" is the right verb; add the caveat in a sentence.
2. "at a village fair": it is "Crome's yearly Charity Fair", a Bank Holiday fair in the park of the house, in aid of the local hospital (ch. XXV).
3. "Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian pharaoh": fine (Herodotus II 102–10).
4. "she is also its first prophet": the Sibyl of the epigraph comes first. And "everything she deals turns out to be true" is a reading. What Eliot's note says is the fact to give: the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later", so do the crowds, and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV". Not everything is dealt true: she does not find the Hanged Man, and the blank card is never read.
5. "prosecutions of palmists and mediums were routine in the London of the 1920s": unsourced overstatement. What can be shown: s. 4 of the Vagrancy Act 1824 made "every person pretending or professing to tell fortunes, or using any subtle craft, means, or device, by palmistry or otherwise, to deceive and impose on any of his Majesty's subjects" a rogue and vagabond; prosecutions of palmists and mediums under it continued through the 1920s (a 1921 appeal held that "the act of fortune-telling is an offence in itself"), and in 1951 the Lords were told such prosecutions "have been confined to palmists, fortune-tellers, and the like". "still prosecuted" is safe; "routine" is not.
6. "She is not being philosophical. She is afraid of the police." The Vagrancy Act gloss is the usual one in the commentaries, but it is a reading of the line, and should be marked as one.
7. "'Clairvoyante,' with the French feminine ending, is the trade term": plausible, unsourced; harmless.

Draft:
TITLE: Madame Sosostris
BODY: The name is usually traced to Aldous Huxley's <i>Crome Yellow</i> (November 1921), where Mr Scogan, dressed as a gipsy woman, reads palms at the Bank Holiday charity fair in the grounds of Crome under a placard reading "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana". Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian king. Eliot, asked by Grover Smith in 1952, said he was "almost certain" he had taken the name from the novel without knowing it; Lawrence Rainey has since dated the typescript of Parts I and II to May 1921, before Huxley wrote the book, so the debt is in doubt. The spelling and the head cold are Eliot's own. His note ties her cards to what follows: the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later", so do the crowds, and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV".

"One must be so careful these days" is usually read as a fortune-teller's caution. Telling fortunes for money was an offence under section 4 of the Vagrancy Act 1824 ("every person pretending or professing to tell fortunes, or using any subtle craft, means, or device, by palmistry or otherwise, to deceive"), and palmists and mediums were still being prosecuted under it in the 1920s.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "The name is borrowed and bent." — a one-line opener that sounds good and tells me nothing until the next sentence does the work. The common brief quotes it as an example of what has to go.
2. "Eliot feminises the fake and gives her a head cold." — the brief's second example. Alliteration and a joke in place of the fact (the spelling and the cold are Eliot's own).
3. "the cynic Mr. Scogan" — harmless, but a label I do not need to follow the note.
4. "She is the poem's most fully drawn comic character, and she is also its first prophet, since everything she deals turns out to be true." — this is criticism, not a gloss: a ranking ("most fully drawn"), a title ("first prophet"), and a claim I am asked to take on trust ("everything … turns out to be true"). The useful fact behind it is that the cards reappear, and Eliot's note says so.
5. "She is not being philosophical. She is afraid of the police." — the two-beat punchline. The Vagrancy Act sentence before it already makes the point; these two lines exist to be quoted.
6. The second paragraph glosses line 59 under a note anchored at 43–46. That is fine if the site has no separate note on lines 57–59, but the body should say which line it is glossing.

Keep: the Huxley sentence, minus "the cynic"; "Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian pharaoh."; ""Clairvoyante," with the French feminine ending, is the trade term; "the wisest woman in Europe" is the trade puff." (this one explains something and I would keep it as it is); "fortune-telling for money was an offence under the Vagrancy Act of 1824, and prosecutions of palmists and mediums were routine in the London of the 1920s". The title.

Draft:
TITLE: The clairvoyante with a cold
BODY: The name is usually traced to Aldous Huxley's novel <i>Crome Yellow</i>, published in November 1921 while Eliot was drafting the poem. In it Mr. Scogan dresses up at a village fair as "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana" to tell fortunes. Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian pharaoh; Eliot's spelling and the head cold are his own. "Clairvoyante," with the French feminine ending, is the trade term; "the wisest woman in Europe" is the trade puff. The cards she deals reappear later in the poem; Eliot's note on line 46 says which.

Her parting line, "One must be so careful these days" (line 59), can be read as a practical worry. Fortune-telling for money was an offence under the Vagrancy Act of 1824, and prosecutions of palmists and mediums were routine in London in the 1920s.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Borrowed and bent", "feminises the fake", "the cynic", "most fully drawn", "first prophet", "everything she deals turns out to be true" and the two-beat ending go. The Huxley debt is given with the evidence for it (Eliot to Grover Smith, 1952) and the chronology against it (Rainey's dating of the typescript), as the scholar found; the fair placed; "pharaoh" becomes "king"; what Eliot's note actually says about the cards replaces the prophecy claim. The Vagrancy Act sentence keeps the section number, quotes the Act in a phrase, and says "still being prosecuted" rather than "routine"; the reading is marked as the usual one. The reader's title stands.
TITLE: The clairvoyante with a cold
BODY:
The name is usually traced to Aldous Huxley's <i>Crome Yellow</i>, published in November 1921, where Mr Scogan, dressed as a gipsy woman, reads palms at the charity fair in the grounds of Crome under a placard announcing "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana". Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian king. Eliot, asked by Grover Smith in 1952, said he was "almost certain" he had taken the name from the novel without knowing it. Lawrence Rainey has since dated the typescript of Parts I and II to May 1921, before Huxley wrote the book, so the debt is in doubt. The spelling and the head cold are Eliot's own. "Clairvoyante," with the French feminine ending, is the trade term; "the wisest woman in Europe" is the trade puff. Eliot's note ties her cards to what follows: the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later", so do the crowds, and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV".

Her parting line, "One must be so careful these days" (line 59), is usually read as a fortune-teller's caution. Section 4 of the Vagrancy Act 1824 made "every person pretending or professing to tell fortunes" a rogue and vagabond, and palmists and mediums were still being prosecuted under it in the 1920s.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

What the rewrite adds was checked: the fair, the disguise and the placard are in <i>Crome Yellow</i> chs. XXV and XXVII (Gutenberg #1999); Eliot's "almost certain" and his being unconscious of the borrowing are from his letter to Grover Smith of 10 March 1952, reported in Smith's "The Fortuneteller in Eliot's <i>Waste Land</i>", <i>American Literature</i> 25 (January 1954), 490–92 (I could reach only secondary reports of the letter, which agree on the words); Rainey's dating of the typescript of Parts I and II to 9–22 May 1921, and Gordon's point that Huxley finished the novel only in August 1921, are in Rainey, <i>Revisiting The Waste Land</i> (2005), ch. 1; the wording of section 4 and the continuing prosecutions of palmists are in Hansard, Lords, 3 May 1951 (Fraudulent Mediums Bill). Nothing to object to.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later", so do the crowds, and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV"" — a comma splice in the middle ("appear later, so do the crowds") that I had to reread. Put: "Eliot's note ties her cards to what follows: the Sailor, the Merchant and the crowds "appear later", and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV"."
2. "is usually read as a fortune-teller's caution" — caution about what? The reader has to wait for the next sentence to find out. Put: "is usually read as a fortune-teller's caution about the law."
3. "Mr Scogan" — the tarot note has "Mr. Eugenides" and the poem prints "Mrs. Equitone"; one form on the site. Put: "Mr. Scogan".

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar satisfied. Reader: the comma splice in the sentence on the note; "caution" given its object; "Mr." for consistency. All taken.
TITLE: The clairvoyante with a cold
BODY:
The name is usually traced to Aldous Huxley's <i>Crome Yellow</i>, published in November 1921, where Mr. Scogan, dressed as a gipsy woman, reads palms at the charity fair in the grounds of Crome under a placard announcing "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana". Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian king. Eliot, asked by Grover Smith in 1952, said he was "almost certain" he had taken the name from the novel without knowing it. Lawrence Rainey has since dated the typescript of Parts I and II to May 1921, before Huxley wrote the book, so the debt is in doubt. The spelling and the head cold are Eliot's own. "Clairvoyante," with the French feminine ending, is the trade term; "the wisest woman in Europe" is the trade puff. Eliot's note ties her cards to what follows: the Sailor, the Merchant and the crowds "appear later", and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV".

Her parting line, "One must be so careful these days" (line 59), is usually read as a fortune-teller's caution about the law.
