Verdict: satisfied

"The Sailor, the Merchant and the crowds 'appear later'" is a fair rendering of the note ("The Phoenician Sailor and the Merchant appear later; also the 'crowds of people,'").

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The clairvoyante with a cold
BODY:
The name is usually traced to Aldous Huxley's <i>Crome Yellow</i>, published in November 1921, where Mr. Scogan, dressed as a gipsy woman, reads palms at the charity fair in the grounds of Crome under a placard announcing "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana". Sesostris was a legendary Egyptian king. Eliot, asked by Grover Smith in 1952, said he was "almost certain" he had taken the name from the novel without knowing it. Lawrence Rainey has since dated the typescript of Parts I and II to May 1921, before Huxley wrote the book, so the debt is in doubt. The spelling and the head cold are Eliot's own. "Clairvoyante," with the French feminine ending, is the trade term; "the wisest woman in Europe" is the trade puff. Eliot's note ties her cards to what follows: the Sailor, the Merchant and the crowds "appear later", and "Death by Water is executed in Part IV".

Her parting line, "One must be so careful these days" (line 59), is usually read as a fortune-teller's caution about the law. Section 4 of the Vagrancy Act 1824 made "every person pretending or professing to tell fortunes" a rogue and vagabond, and palmists and mediums were still being prosecuted under it in the 1920s.


## tarot

**As it stood**

TITLE: The cards, one by one
BODY:
Eliot's own note confesses that he did not know the Tarot well and "departed from it to suit my own convenience." What she deals:

**The drowned Phoenician Sailor.** Not a Tarot card. He is Phlebas, who gets Part IV to himself, and the merchant of Part III, and Ferdinand in *The Tempest*, whose father is the one lying "full fathom five" with pearls for eyes.

**Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks.** "Beautiful lady" in Italian, also the poisonous nightshade, also the eye-drops women used to dilate their pupils. Leonardo's *Virgin of the Rocks* stands behind the title; the society woman of "A Game of Chess" stands in front of it. "The lady of situations" sounds like a phrase from the gossip columns.

**The man with three staves.** The Three of Wands, a real card: a figure on a headland with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot says he associated it, "quite arbitrarily," with the Fisher King.

**The Wheel.** The Wheel of Fortune, card X. "O you who turn the wheel," line 320.

**The one-eyed merchant.** Not a card; Mr. Eugenides of Smyrna, seen in profile at line 209, with his blank card of something carried on his back.

**The Hanged Man.** Card XII: a young man hanging serenely by one foot from a living tree, a halo round his head. Eliot links him to Frazer's hanged god, the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain, and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris cannot find him. No sacrifice, no resurrection, is in this reading. "Fear death by water" is the reading's one instruction, and it is the one thing the Grail legend says you should not fear: water is what the waste land needs.

The cards shown here are Pamela Colman Smith's designs for the pack published in London by William Rider & Son in 1909, the Tarot that an English buyer of the 1910s would have found in a shop.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Eliot's note 46 in `poem-part1.md`; his notes 218, 126 and 192 in the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321). Line numbers 209, 320 and 369 by count against the Gutenberg text. Waite, <i>The Pictorial Key to the Tarot</i> (1911), Three of Wands and Hanged Man (archive.org scan). Rider–Waite publication (Wikipedia). Frazer, <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i> vol. I, Book II ch. V "The Hanged God", pp. 288–97 (archive.org, contents summary). Weston, <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, ch. VI "The Symbols" (Gutenberg #4090).

Substance:
1. Misquotation. Eliot's note reads "from which I have obviously departed to suit my own convenience", not "departed from it to suit my own convenience". Use his words.
2. "He is Phlebas ... and the merchant of Part III, and Ferdinand": Eliot's note on 218 is hedged, and the hedges are the point: the merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor, and the latter is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples". Keep them.
3. "Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i> stands behind the title": a reading, though the usual one; "usually taken to echo". "'The lady of situations' sounds like a phrase from the gossip columns" is a guess with no source; cut or mark.
4. Three of Wands: right. Waite's own description, quotable: "A calm, stately personage, with his back turned, looking from a cliff's edge at ships passing over the sea. Three staves are planted in the ground." Eliot's "an authentic member of the Tarot pack" and "quite arbitrarily" are quoted correctly.
5. The Wheel of Fortune is X; line 320 is right in the site's count.
6. Mr. Eugenides at 209 is right. "seen in profile" is one reading of "one-eyed" (as court cards are shown), not a fact; mark it.
7. Hanged Man: the card is right. Waite: "The gallows from which he is suspended forms a Tau cross ... There is a nimbus about the head ... the tree of sacrifice is living wood, with leaves thereon ... the face expresses deep entrancement, not suffering." But "Frazer's hanged god, the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain" misdescribes Frazer. His chapter "The Hanged God" (Book II ch. V of <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>) is about Attis and his doubles: Marsyas hanged on a pine, Odin on the gallows-tree, the hanged Artemis, human and animal victims hanged, skins used "to effect the resurrection". Rain is not in it; the rain is the poem's, in Part V. Fix.
8. "'Fear death by water' ... is the one thing the Grail legend says you should not fear: water is what the waste land needs." The Grail legend says no such thing. Two facts can replace it: Eliot's note, "Death by Water is executed in Part IV"; and Weston's claim, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, that the Tarot's original use "would seem to have been, not to foretell the Future in general, but to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land".
9. "No sacrifice, no resurrection, is in this reading": a reading (Cleanth Brooks's, in outline, 1939), fine if marked.
10. Rider & Son, London, 1909 is right (December 1909; Pamela Colman Smith drawing to A. E. Waite's instructions). "the Tarot that an English buyer of the 1910s would have found in a shop" is unsourced; "the English pack of Eliot's time" is safe. Whether Eliot ever handled one is not known; his note says his knowledge of the pack was slight.
11. Bold card names: the batch shows Markdown asterisks; the site's rule is plain text with a little HTML. The editor will know the convention; the draft below uses <b>.

Draft:
TITLE: The cards
BODY: Eliot's note says he was "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack" and had "obviously departed" from it "to suit my own convenience". The cards she deals:

<b>The drowned Phoenician Sailor.</b> Not a Tarot card. Eliot's note on line 218 says the one-eyed merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor", who "is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples"; Part IV, "Death by Water", is Phlebas the Phoenician's.

<b>Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks.</b> "Beautiful lady" in Italian; also deadly nightshade, and the drops made from it that women used to widen the pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" is usually taken to echo Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>.

<b>The man with three staves.</b> The Three of Wands, which Eliot calls "an authentic member of the Tarot pack". In the Rider pack a man stands at a cliff's edge with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot "quite arbitrarily" associated him with the Fisher King.

<b>The Wheel.</b> The Wheel of Fortune, card X. Compare "O you who turn the wheel", line 320.

<b>The one-eyed merchant.</b> Not a card. He returns as Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant, at line 209. One reading of "one-eyed" is that he is seen in profile, as court cards are.

<b>The Hanged Man.</b> Card XII. In the Rider pack a young man hangs by one foot from a T-shaped tree of living wood, a halo round his head, his face calm. Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer" (the chapter of that name in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, on Attis, Marsyas and Odin, gods hanged on trees) and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris does not find him. Her one instruction, "Fear death by water", is answered by Eliot's note: "Death by Water is executed in Part IV." Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, says the Tarot was first used "to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land".

The cards shown here are Pamela Colman Smith's designs for the pack published in London by William Rider & Son in December 1909, the English pack of Eliot's time. By his own note, his knowledge of the pack was slight.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Eliot's own note confesses that he did not know the Tarot well and "departed from it to suit my own convenience."" — "confesses" colours it, and the words inside the quotation marks are not Eliot's. His note says he is "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack of cards, from which I have obviously departed to suit my own convenience." A quotation should be exact.
2. "What she deals:" — a fragment as a lead-in. A sentence costs nothing.
3. The bold **headings** are Markdown. The common brief says no Markdown in the final wording; whatever the site's convention for run-in headings is, the editor should apply it here.
4. "Not a Tarot card." / "Not a card;" — fragments.
5. "He is Phlebas, who gets Part IV to himself" — "gets Part IV to himself" is chatty. And "He is Phlebas … and the merchant … and Ferdinand" is stated as bare fact when the linkage is Eliot's own, in his notes on lines 46 and 218. Say so; it is a better sentence with the source in it.
6. "whose father is the one lying "full fathom five" with pearls for eyes" — clumsy ("is the one lying"), and the father is not in fact lying anywhere. There is a separate note on line 48 for this; a pointer will do.
7. "Leonardo's *Virgin of the Rocks* stands behind the title; the society woman of "A Game of Chess" stands in front of it." — the matched pair (behind / in front) made me stop and decode a metaphor when I wanted two facts: the title recalls the painting; the card has been identified with the woman of Part II. The second is a reading and should be marked as one, with a name if the scholar has one.
8. ""The lady of situations" sounds like a phrase from the gossip columns." — an impression with no source. If a commentator says this, cite them; if not, cut.
9. "seen in profile at line 209" — a neat explanation of "one-eyed" (a figure on a card shows one eye) slipped in as if it were in the poem. If it is somebody's reading, say whose; if it is the writer's, cut.
10. "with his blank card of something carried on his back" — I had to reread this. The poem says the blank card is something he carries on his back; the note's phrase garbles it.
11. "the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain" — a compressed summary of Frazer and Weston that sounds very sure of itself. Scholar to check that it describes Frazer's "Hanged God"; as a reader I would settle for "a god killed so that the land may revive".
12. "No sacrifice, no resurrection, is in this reading." — an inverted, quotable line. The plain fact: the card that would stand for the dying and reviving god is missing.
13. ""Fear death by water" is the reading's one instruction, and it is the one thing the Grail legend says you should not fear: water is what the waste land needs." — the paradox is the writer's, not the legend's, and it closes the list with a flourish. Eliot's note says "Death by Water is executed in Part IV"; that is the cross-reference I wanted.
14. Title "The cards, one by one" — "one by one" is a small tour-guide touch. "The cards she deals" says the same.

Keep: the Rider pack sentence at the end, entire; "The Three of Wands, a real card: a figure on a headland with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot says he associated it, "quite arbitrarily," with the Fisher King."; "The Wheel of Fortune, card X."; "Madame Sosostris cannot find him."; the three meanings of belladonna (beautiful lady; nightshade; eye-drops).

Draft:
TITLE: The cards she deals
BODY: Eliot's note says he was "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack" and had departed from it "to suit my own convenience." The cards, in the order she names them, are these.

The drowned Phoenician Sailor. There is no such card in the Tarot. Eliot's note on line 46 says the Sailor "appears later": he is Phlebas the Phoenician, who drowns in Part IV. The note on line 218 adds that the one-eyed merchant "melts into" the Sailor, who "is not wholly distinct from" Ferdinand in <i>The Tempest</i>. The words in brackets at line 48 are from a song telling Ferdinand that his father has drowned; see the note there.

Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks. Belladonna is Italian for "beautiful lady". It is also the name of the poisonous nightshade, and of the eye-drops made from it, which women used to dilate their pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" recalls Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>. The card has been identified with the woman of "A Game of Chess" in Part II.

The man with three staves. This is the Three of Wands, which is a card in the pack. In the design shown here a figure stands on a headland with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot's note says he associated it, "quite arbitrarily," with the Fisher King.

The Wheel. This is the Wheel of Fortune, card X. Compare "O you who turn the wheel" at line 320.

The one-eyed merchant. There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note on line 46 says the Merchant appears later; he is Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant, at line 209. Neither the poem nor Eliot's note says what the blank card, or the thing on his back, is.

The Hanged Man. Card XII. In the design shown here a young man hangs by one foot from a living tree, his face calm and a halo round his head. Eliot's note links him to the Hanged God of Frazer's <i>Golden Bough</i>, a god killed so that the land may revive, and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris cannot find him, so the card that on Eliot's account stands for the god's death and return is the one missing from her reading. "Fear death by water" is the only advice she gives. Eliot's note says that "Death by Water is executed in Part IV", where the Phoenician sailor drowns.

The cards shown here are Pamela Colman Smith's designs for the pack published in London by William Rider & Son in 1909, the Tarot that an English buyer of the 1910s would have found in a shop.

(The run-in headings above are plain text; the editor should mark them in whatever way the site marks them. "The card has been identified with the woman of Part II" wants a name from the scholar or should be cut. The "in profile" and "gossip columns" points can come back if the scholar can source them.)

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. Eliot's note quoted in his words; the lead-in made a sentence; the fragments made sentences; Eliot's hedges on 218 kept; the Ferdinand line handed to the note on 48; Leonardo marked "usually taken to echo"; the Part II identification and the gossip columns cut for want of a name; "in profile" cut for the same reason; the merchant's blank card left to the poem; the Hanged Man given Frazer's actual chapter, with no rain and no sacrificed king; the Grail paradox replaced by Eliot's note and Weston's sentence; the missing card given to Brooks as a reading; the last paragraph kept with the month and "the English pack of Eliot's time". Title follows the reader. [scholar: confirm that Brooks (1939) makes the missing Hanged Man the point of the reading; if not, the sentence goes.]
TITLE: The cards she deals
BODY:
Eliot's note says he was "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack" and had "obviously departed" from it "to suit my own convenience". The cards, in the order she names them, are these.

<b>The drowned Phoenician Sailor.</b> There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note says the Sailor "appears later": Part IV, "Death by Water", is the drowning of Phlebas the Phoenician. His note on line 218 adds that the one-eyed merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor", who "is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples" in <i>The Tempest</i>. The bracketed line 48 is from a song telling Ferdinand that his father has drowned; see the note there.

<b>Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks.</b> Belladonna is Italian for "beautiful lady". It is also the name of the deadly nightshade, and of the drops made from it that women used to widen their pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" is usually taken to echo Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>.

<b>The man with three staves.</b> The Three of Wands, which Eliot calls "an authentic member of the Tarot pack". In the Rider pack a man stands at a cliff's edge with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot "quite arbitrarily" associated him with the Fisher King.

<b>The Wheel.</b> The Wheel of Fortune, card X. Compare "O you who turn the wheel" at line 320.

<b>The one-eyed merchant.</b> There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note says the Merchant appears later; he is Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant, at line 209.

<b>The Hanged Man.</b> Card XII. In the Rider pack a young man hangs by one foot from a T-shaped gallows of living wood, a halo round his head, his face calm. Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer", the chapter of that name in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, on Attis, Marsyas and Odin, gods hanged on trees, and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris does not find him. Since Cleanth Brooks (1939) the missing card has been read as the point of her reading: the god who dies and returns is absent from it. Her one instruction, "Fear death by water", is answered by Eliot's note: "Death by Water is executed in Part IV." Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, says the Tarot was first used "to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land".

The cards shown here are Pamela Colman Smith's designs for the pack published in London by William Rider &amp; Son in December 1909, the English pack of Eliot's time.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. Misquotation and a loose fact in the Sailor entry. "Eliot's note says the Sailor 'appears later'": the note reads "The Phoenician Sailor and the Merchant appear later" (Eliot's note on 46, in `poem-part1.md`); the singular does not occur. And "Part IV, 'Death by Water', is the drowning of Phlebas": Part IV opens with Phlebas "a fortnight dead"; the drowning is over before it starts. Put: "Eliot's note says the Sailor and the Merchant 'appear later': Part IV, 'Death by Water', is Phlebas the Phoenician, 'a fortnight dead'."

2. "Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, says the Tarot was first used 'to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land'." Weston does not say it; she conditions it: "if the connection with the Egyptian and Chinese monuments, referred to above, is genuine, the original use of the 'Tarot' would seem to have been, not to foretell the Future in general, but to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land" (<i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, ch. VI, "The Symbols"; Gutenberg #4090). Put: "Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, suggests that the Tarot's original use 'would seem to have been, not to foretell the Future in general, but to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land'."

3. The bracketed question: no, Brooks (1939) does not make the missing card the point of her reading, and the sentence as drafted misstates him. I read the essay in its 1947 reprint ("The Waste Land: An Analysis", in B. Rajan, ed., <i>T. S. Eliot: A Study of His Writings by Several Hands</i>, archive.org in.ernet.dli.2015.53128; the text is that of <i>Modern Poetry and the Tradition</i>, 1939, as also reprinted in the Cox and Hinchliffe <i>Casebook</i>, 1968, pp. 128–61). Brooks does address her not finding the card, but explains it by the hood: "The Hanged Man, who represents the hanged god of Frazer (including the Christ), Eliot states in a note, is associated with the hooded figure who appears in 'What the Thunder Said'. That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him; or rather, here again the palaver of the modern fortune-teller is turned to new and important account by the poet's shifting the matter into a new and serious context." His general claim is that her fortune "becomes true as the poem develops, true in a sense in which Madame Sosostris herself does not think it true". The words "Madame Sosostris does not find" occur in no reprint of the essay (archive.org full-text search finds them only in six later critics). The critic who makes something of the card she cannot find is Hugh Kenner, <i>The Invisible Poet</i> (1959), of the protagonist in the hyacinth garden: "It is not clear whether he is comforted to learn that the clairvoyante does not find the Hanged Man." Put, in place of "Since Cleanth Brooks (1939) the missing card has been read as the point of her reading: the god who dies and returns is absent from it.": "Cleanth Brooks (1939), who read her whole fortune as coming true in the poem, ties the card she cannot find to the hooded figure of Part V: 'That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him.'" Or cut the sentence; the note loses nothing it can support.

Not an objection: the same page of Brooks supplies the name the editor wanted for "in profile", cut in round one for want of one: "The fact that the merchant is one-eyed apparently means in Madame Sosostris's speech no more than that the merchant's face on the card is shown in profile." If the editor wants it back in the Merchant entry: "Brooks (1939) took 'one-eyed' to mean no more than that the face on the card is shown in profile."

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer", the chapter of that name in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, on Attis, Marsyas and Odin, gods hanged on trees, and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V." — the second "with" arrives after a nested appositive and I had to go back to find what it paired with. Put: "Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer" and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. "The Hanged God" is a chapter in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, on Attis, Marsyas and Odin, gods hanged on trees."
2. "Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, says the Tarot was first used "to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land"." — standing last in the Hanged Man entry, after "Death by Water is executed in Part IV", it has nothing to attach to; the reader cannot tell why it is there. It belongs with Eliot's own remark about the pack, before the list. Put the opening paragraph as: "Eliot's note says he was "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack" and had "obviously departed" from it "to suit my own convenience". Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, says the Tarot was first used "to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land". The cards, in the order Madame Sosostris names them, are these." and strike the Weston sentence from the Hanged Man entry. ("Madame Sosostris" for "she" so that the pronoun does not fall on Weston.)
3. "The bracketed line 48 is from a song telling Ferdinand that his father has drowned; see the note there." — "the bracketed line 48" made me pause to work out what was bracketed. Put: "Line 48, the line in brackets, is from a song telling Ferdinand that his father has drowned; see the note there."

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: Eliot's note has "appear later", plural, and Part IV opens with Phlebas already dead; Weston's sentence is conditional; the Brooks sentence misstated him (his explanation is the hood), and the same page of Brooks supplies the owner for "in profile". Reader: the Hanged God sentence split; Weston moved to the opening paragraph, with "Madame Sosostris" for "she"; "Line 48, the line in brackets". All taken; the Brooks sentence replaced by the scholar's, and Brooks restored to the Merchant entry.
TITLE: The cards she deals
BODY:
Eliot's note says he was "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack" and had "obviously departed" from it "to suit my own convenience". Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, suggests that the Tarot's original use "would seem to have been, not to foretell the Future in general, but to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land". The cards, in the order Madame Sosostris names them, are these.

<b>The drowned Phoenician Sailor.</b> There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note says the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later": Part IV, "Death by Water", is Phlebas the Phoenician, "a fortnight dead". His note on line 218 adds that the one-eyed merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor", who "is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples" in <i>The Tempest</i>. Line 48, the line in brackets, is from a song telling Ferdinand that his father has drowned; see the note there.

<b>Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks.</b> Belladonna is Italian for "beautiful lady". It is also the name of the deadly nightshade, and of the drops made from it that women used to widen their pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" is usually taken to echo Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>.

<b>The man with three staves.</b> The Three of Wands, which Eliot calls "an authentic member of the Tarot pack". In the Rider pack a man stands at a cliff's edge with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot "quite arbitrarily" associated him with the Fisher King.

<b>The Wheel.</b> The Wheel of Fortune, card X. Compare "O you who turn the wheel" at line 320.

<b>The one-eyed merchant.</b> There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note says the Merchant appears later; he is Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant, at line 209. Cleanth Brooks (1939) took "one-eyed" to mean no more than that the face on the card is shown in profile.

<b>The Hanged Man.</b> Card XII. In the Rider pack a young man hangs by one foot from a T-shaped gallows of living wood, a halo round his head, his face calm. Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer" and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. "The Hanged God" is a chapter in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, on Attis, Marsyas and Odin, gods hanged on trees. Madame Sosostris does not find him. Brooks, who read her whole fortune as coming true in the poem, ties the card she cannot find to the hooded figure of Part V: "That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him." Her one piece of advice, "Fear death by water", is answered by Eliot's note: "Death by Water is executed in Part IV."

The cards shown here are Pamela Colman Smith's designs for the pack published in London by William Rider &amp; Son in December 1909, the English pack of Eliot's time.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: not yet

The two Brooks sentences, checked against the 1947 reprint of the essay (Rajan, ed., <i>T. S. Eliot: A Study of His Writings by Several Hands</i>, archive.org in.ernet.dli.2015.53128), the text of <i>Modern Poetry and the Tradition</i> (1939):

The hood is fairly given. Brooks writes: "The Hanged Man, who represents the hanged god of Frazer (including the Christ), Eliot states in a note, is associated with the hooded figure who appears in 'What the Thunder Said'. That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him; or rather, here again the palaver of the modern fortune-teller is turned to new and important account by the poet's shifting the matter into a new and serious context." The note's "ties the card she cannot find to the hooded figure of Part V", with the quoted sentence, is what he says; "who read her whole fortune as coming true in the poem" is his general claim ("true in a sense in which Madame Sosostris herself does not think it true").

The profile is half of what he says, and the half left out reverses the point for the reader of this entry. Brooks writes: "The fact that the merchant is one-eyed apparently means in Madame Sosostris's speech no more than that the merchant's face on the card is shown in profile. But Eliot applies the term to Mr Eugenides for a totally different effect. The defect corresponds somewhat to Madame Sosostris's bad cold." The note's sentence, coming straight after "he is Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant, at line 209", has Brooks deflating "one-eyed" for Mr. Eugenides too, which is the opposite of what he says about him.

1. Put, in place of "Cleanth Brooks (1939) took 'one-eyed' to mean no more than that the face on the card is shown in profile.": "Cleanth Brooks (1939) took 'one-eyed', on the card, to mean no more than that the face is shown in profile, and the same word, once Eliot applies it to Mr. Eugenides, to mean a defect, like Madame Sosostris's cold." If the editor would rather keep one clause, the minimum that is fair to Brooks is "on the card" in place of "the face on the card": "Cleanth Brooks (1939) took 'one-eyed', on the card, to mean no more than that the face is shown in profile."

Everything else in the note stands: Eliot's note quoted in the plural, Phlebas "a fortnight dead", Weston's conditional, the Hanged God chapter, Waite's card.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Eliot's note says the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later": Part IV, "Death by Water", is Phlebas the Phoenician, "a fortnight dead"." — a section of the poem "is" a man; the sentence has to be reread to see that it means the Sailor turns up as Phlebas. Put: "Eliot's note says the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later". The Sailor is Phlebas the Phoenician of Part IV, "Death by Water", already "a fortnight dead" when the section opens."

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The cards she deals
BODY:
Eliot's note says he was "not familiar with the exact constitution of the Tarot pack" and had "obviously departed" from it "to suit my own convenience". Weston, in her chapter on the Grail symbols, suggests that the Tarot's original use "would seem to have been, not to foretell the Future in general, but to predict the rise and fall of the waters which brought fertility to the land". The cards, in the order Madame Sosostris names them, are these.

<b>The drowned Phoenician Sailor.</b> There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note says the Sailor and the Merchant "appear later". The Sailor is Phlebas the Phoenician of Part IV, "Death by Water", already "a fortnight dead" when the section opens. His note on line 218 adds that the one-eyed merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor", who "is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples" in <i>The Tempest</i>. Line 48, the line in brackets, is from a song telling Ferdinand that his father has drowned; see the note there.

<b>Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks.</b> Belladonna is Italian for "beautiful lady". It is also the name of the deadly nightshade, and of the drops made from it that women used to widen their pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" is usually taken to echo Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>.

<b>The man with three staves.</b> The Three of Wands, which Eliot calls "an authentic member of the Tarot pack". In the Rider pack a man stands at a cliff's edge with his back to us, watching ships. Eliot "quite arbitrarily" associated him with the Fisher King.

<b>The Wheel.</b> The Wheel of Fortune, card X. Compare "O you who turn the wheel" at line 320.

<b>The one-eyed merchant.</b> There is no such card in the pack. Eliot's note says the Merchant appears later; he is Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant, at line 209. Cleanth Brooks (1939) took "one-eyed", on the card, to mean no more than that the face is shown in profile, and the same word, once Eliot applies it to Mr. Eugenides, to mean a defect, like Madame Sosostris's cold.

<b>The Hanged Man.</b> Card XII. In the Rider pack a young man hangs by one foot from a T-shaped gallows of living wood, a halo round his head, his face calm. Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer" and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. "The Hanged God" is a chapter in <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, on Attis, Marsyas and Odin, gods hanged on trees. Madame Sosostris does not find him. Brooks, who read her whole fortune as coming true in the poem, ties the card she cannot find to the hooded figure of Part V: "That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him." Her one piece of advice, "Fear death by water", is answered by Eliot's note: "Death by Water is executed in Part IV."

The cards shown here are Pamela Colman Smith's designs for the pack published in London by William Rider &amp; Son in December 1909, the English pack of Eliot's time.


## pearls

**As it stood**

TITLE: Ariel's song
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): Full fathom five thy father lies; / Of his bones are coral made; / Those are pearls that were his eyes: / Nothing of him that doth fade / But doth suffer a sea-change / Into something rich and strange.
BODY:
*The Tempest*, Act I scene ii. Ariel sings this to Ferdinand, who believes his father the king drowned in the wreck. The father is not dead; the song is a lie that turns out to be a description of the play, in which everyone is transformed by water. The poem quotes it twice (here and at line 125), works its "sea-change" into Phlebas in Part IV, and never lets the transformation happen. Eliot's note on line 218 says the Phoenician Sailor "is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples."

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>The Tempest</i> I. ii, Ariel's song (Gutenberg #23042). Line 125 by count against the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321), and Eliot's notes numbered 126, 192, 218 and 257 there.

Substance:
1. Quote and scene are right; the song's wording matches the text.
2. "The father is not dead; the song is a lie that turns out to be a description of the play, in which everyone is transformed by water": the first clause is fact, the rest is a reading, and "everyone is transformed by water" is not so (no one in the play drowns). Mark or cut.
3. "The poem quotes it twice (here and at line 125)": right, and the note should add what Eliot's own note there says. The 1922 note is misnumbered 126 for line 125 ("Those are pearls that were his eyes") and reads "Cf. Part I, l. 37, 48": Eliot himself ties the pearls to the hyacinth garden. That is the most useful fact missing from this note and the hyacinth one.
4. Missing: two more passages come from the same scene. Ferdinand's "the king my father's wreck" is behind lines 191–2 (Eliot's note 192: "V. The Tempest, I, ii"), and "This music crept by me upon the waters" is quoted at 257 (note: "V. The Tempest, as above").
5. "works its 'sea-change' into Phlebas in Part IV, and never lets the transformation happen": a reading; Part IV does not use the phrase. Mark it as one.
6. Eliot's note on 218 is quoted correctly.

BODY: <i>The Tempest</i>, I. ii. Ariel sings this to Ferdinand, who believes his father, the King of Naples, drowned in the wreck; the father is alive. ## ring

**As it stood**

TITLE: Crowds in a ring
BODY:
The clairvoyante's last card is not a card but a vision, and it comes true four lines later on London Bridge. Dante's neutrals in *Inferno* III run forever after a whirling banner; Frazer's villagers dance in rings around the maypole; the commuters go round and round between the station and the office. Eliot's note on the Tarot says "the 'crowds of people'" appear later. They do, at line 62, and again as the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at line 369.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: line count against the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321): the crowd on London Bridge is line 62, "Unreal City" is 60; "Who are those hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" is 369–70 in the site's count (Eliot's 368–69). <i>Inferno</i> III 52–57 (it.wikisource). Frazer, <i>The Golden Bough</i> (1922 abridgement), ch. X, the Swedish midsummer May-pole (Gutenberg #3623). Eliot's note 46 in `poem-part1.md`.

Substance:
1. "it comes true four lines later on London Bridge": wrong count. The crowd flows over London Bridge at line 62, six lines on; four lines on is "Unreal City" (60). Say "at line 62".
2. "Dante's neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III run forever after a whirling banner": right ("una 'nsegna / che girando correva tanto ratta", III 52–53), and Eliot's note on 63 cites III 55–57. The link to line 56 is a reading, though a natural one.
3. "Frazer's villagers dance in rings around the maypole": the passage exists (ch. X, the Swedish midsummer May-pole: "the people flock to it from all quarters, and dance round it in a great ring"), but its bearing on this line is the note's own association, not Frazer's or Eliot's. Mark it as a reading or cut.
4. "the commuters go round and round between the station and the office": a reading with nothing behind it. Cut.
5. Eliot's note is quoted correctly ("also the 'crowds of people,'"); 62 and 369 are right in the site's count. "at line 369" for a two-line quotation should be "lines 369–70".

Draft:
TITLE: Crowds in a ring
BODY: Not a card. Eliot's note lists "the 'crowds of people'" with the Sailor and the Merchant among the figures that "appear later". They do, at line 62, the crowd flowing over London Bridge, and again as the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at lines 369–70. The ring has been read against Dante's neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III, who run forever after a whirling banner; Eliot's note on line 63 cites the same canto.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "The clairvoyante's last card is not a card but a vision" — a neat antithesis where a plain sentence would do: she does not deal this, she says she sees it.
2. "it comes true four lines later on London Bridge" — a count for effect, and a shaky one: line 60 is four lines on, but the crowd on London Bridge is at line 62.
3. "Dante's neutrals in *Inferno* III run forever after a whirling banner; Frazer's villagers dance in rings around the maypole; the commuters go round and round between the station and the office." — three parallel clauses on semicolons, a rhythm first and information second. I cannot tell which of the three the note is asking me to take seriously, or whose readings they are. Dante is Eliot's own source (his note on line 63). The maypole is unsourced. The commuters are the writer's gloss and add nothing the line 62 cross-reference does not.
4. "They do, at line 62" — a knowing aside, the tone of someone finishing my sentence for me.

Keep: the facts of the last two sentences (Eliot's note on the crowds; lines 62 and 369). The title.

Draft:
TITLE: Crowds in a ring
BODY: Madame Sosostris does not deal this; she says she sees it. Eliot's note on line 46 says the "crowds of people" appear later. They are the crowd on London Bridge at line 62 and the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at line 369. Eliot's note on line 63 cites <i>Inferno</i> III, where the souls who were neither good nor evil run for ever behind a whirling banner.

(The Frazer ring-dance can come back as a marked reading if the scholar can name who made it.)

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Not a card but a vision" made plain; "four lines later" corrected to line 62; the Dante, maypole and commuters triad cut, Dante alone kept as a reading with Eliot's own note; "They do" gone; "at line 369" corrected to lines 369–70.
TITLE: Crowds in a ring
BODY:
Madame Sosostris does not deal this; she says she sees it. Eliot's note on line 46 lists "the 'crowds of people'" with the Sailor and the Merchant among the figures that "appear later". They come at line 62, the crowd flowing over London Bridge, and again as the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at lines 369–70. The ring has been read against Dante's neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III, who run for ever behind a whirling banner; Eliot's note on line 63 cites the same canto.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

Line 62, lines 369–70, Eliot's note on 46 and his note on 63 all check in the site's count against the 1922 text; the banner is <i>Inferno</i> III 52–54.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Eliot's note on line 46 lists "the 'crowds of people'" with the Sailor and the Merchant among the figures that "appear later"." — quotation marks inside quotation marks, and "figures" for a crowd; I read it twice. Put: "Eliot's note on line 46 says that the "crowds of people", like the Sailor and the Merchant, "appear later"."
2. "The ring has been read against Dante's neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III, who run for ever behind a whirling banner" — "neutrals" is a label a reader may not know, and "read against" is the same shorthand as in the hyacinth note. Put: "The ring has been compared with Dante's neutrals, the souls in <i>Inferno</i> III who were neither good nor evil and run for ever behind a whirling banner; Eliot's note on line 63 cites the same canto." (The gloss on "neutrals" is mine, from the canto; the scholar to confirm the phrase.)

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar satisfied. Reader: the nested quotation marks; "neutrals" glossed and "read against" made plain. Both taken, with Dante's own words for the gloss (the same words the note on line 63 uses) rather than a paraphrase.
TITLE: Crowds in a ring
BODY:
Madame Sosostris does not deal this; she says she sees it. Eliot's note on line 46 says that the "crowds of people", like the Sailor and the Merchant, "appear later". They come at line 62, the crowd flowing over London Bridge, and again as the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at lines 369–70. The ring has been compared with the souls in <i>Inferno</i> III who lived "without infamy and without praise" and run for ever behind a whirling banner; Eliot's note on line 63 cites the same canto.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

The gloss is right. In <i>Inferno</i> III Virgil explains the souls in the vestibule as "l'anime triste di coloro / che visser sanza 'nfamia e sanza lodo" (III 35–36), mixed with the angels who were neither rebels nor faithful; the banner and the long train behind it (III 52–57, the lines Eliot's note on 63 cites) are those same souls: Dante recognises among them "colui che fece per viltade il gran rifiuto" (III 59–60), and Virgil calls them "Questi sciaurati, che mai non fur vivi" (III 64). "Without infamy and without praise" is the literal sense of "sanza 'nfamia e sanza lodo", and it matches the note on line 63. Text checked at it.wikisource.org, Canto III.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: Crowds in a ring
BODY:
Madame Sosostris does not deal this; she says she sees it. Eliot's note on line 46 says that the "crowds of people", like the Sailor and the Merchant, "appear later". They come at line 62, the crowd flowing over London Bridge, and again as the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at lines 369–70. The ring has been compared with the souls in <i>Inferno</i> III who lived "without infamy and without praise" and run for ever behind a whirling banner; Eliot's note on line 63 cites the same canto.


## The scholar's summary, round 1

Nothing in the batch is badly wrong; the facts to fix are these. (1) tarot: Eliot's note is misquoted ("obviously departed to suit my own convenience" is his wording); Frazer's "Hanged God" chapter is about Attis, Marsyas and Odin hanged on trees, not "the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain"; and "the one thing the Grail legend says you should not fear" is not a thing the Grail legend says (Weston's actual claim, that the Tarot was first used "to predict the rise and fall of the waters", and Eliot's "Death by Water is executed in Part IV" can replace it). (2) hyacinth-girl: the 1934 Criterion sentence is quoted in full against the rights rule (cut to "waving a branch of lilac", "the mud of Gallipoli"); Frazer only reports the dying-god reading of Hyacinth, in the Attis book of the volumes Eliot cites; the Conrad inversion and "mystical blank" are readings, and the facts behind them (the discarded Conrad epigraph; "heart of light" in Burnt Norton) are better; name Peter (1952) and Miller (1977); add Inferno XXXIV 25 for "neither / Living nor dead", without claiming Eliot quoted it in 1929 (he did not). (3) sosostris: the Crome Yellow debt is disputed on chronology (Rainey dates the Parts I–II typescript to May 1921; Huxley wrote the novel June–August); Eliot's own 1952 "almost certain" is the evidence for it; "first prophet" overlooks the Sibyl; "routine" prosecutions is unsourced, "still prosecuted" is not. (4) ring: "four lines later" is six. (5) pearls: add Eliot's note 126 (for line 125), "Cf. Part I, l. 37, 48", which ties the pearls to the hyacinth garden, and the other Tempest passages at 191–2 and 257. (6) frisch-weht and oed-und-leer stand on substance; Eliot's line references to the libretto are exact in both, "reply" at line 42 is the site's reading, and the oed title reverses the German word order.


## The reader's summary, round 1

What I threw out most often was the last sentence. Five of the seven notes end on a line built to be quoted ("The poem does not choose.", "as brief as the shepherd", "She is afraid of the police.", "never lets the transformation happen", "water is what the waste land needs"), and in every case the fact the note needed was already in the sentence before. Next came matched pairs and triads that exist for their rhythm ("love ahead / love behind", "stands behind … stands in front", "not a card but a vision", Dante / Frazer / commuters); metaphors that describe the poem instead of explaining it ("a snatch of song heard through a wall", "turned inside out", "the mystical blank that Eliot's later poems circle"); and fragments ("Lilacs, line 2.", "Not a Tarot card.", "Act III of Tristan."). Readings offered as facts with nobody's name on them ("Others hear", the gossip columns, the maypole, the merchant "in profile", the woman of Part II as Belladonna) I have asked the scholar to source or cut. Two things outside wording for the editor: the tarot note misquotes Eliot's own note and uses Markdown bold; the hyacinth note quotes the 1934 Criterion at more than the phrase or two the rights rule allows. What works, and I kept: the plain story-telling about the opera and the play (the sailor, the shepherd, Ariel and Ferdinand), the Vagrancy Act, the Rider pack, and the Emily Hale and Verdenal facts with their sources.



# Batch 3: lines 60–76


## unreal-city

**As it stood**

TITLE: Baudelaire's swarming city
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): Fourmillante cité, cité pleine de rêves, / Où le spectre en plein jour raccroche le passant!
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): Swarming city, city full of dreams, where the ghost in broad daylight accosts the passer-by!
BODY:
The opening of "Les Sept Vieillards" ("The Seven Old Men") from the *Tableaux parisiens* section of *Les Fleurs du mal*, in which the poet, walking in a yellow fog, meets the same hideous old man seven times over. Eliot said in 1950 that Baudelaire had taught him "the poetical possibilities . . . of the more sordid aspects of the modern metropolis, of the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric." That is the recipe for the next sixteen lines: a real bridge, a real street, a real church clock, and the dead.

The fog is coal smoke. Eliot went into the City through it every working morning from 1917, to Lloyds Bank in Lombard Street. "Unreal City" returns at line 207 (a winter noon) and, stripped to one word, at line 377.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Baudelaire, "Les Sept Vieillards", 1861 text (fr.wikisource, Les_Fleurs_du_mal/1861/Les_Sept_Vieillards): section <i>Tableaux parisiens</i>; opening couplet as quoted, ending "!"; "Un brouillard sale et jaune inondait tout l'espace"; "je comptai sept fois ... ce sinistre vieillard qui se multipliait". Eliot's 1950 talk "What Dante Means to Me" (Italian Institute, London, 4 July 1950; collected in <i>To Criticize the Critic</i>, 1965), wording confirmed in secondary quotation (tseliot.com; wordandsilence.com). Site HTML fetched directly: "Unreal City" is anchored L207, "Unreal" L377. Lloyds dates from the Eliot Estate's site (tseliot.com/foundation/t-s-eliot-at-lloyds-bank-2): joined 19 March 1917, Colonial & Foreign Department, left November 1925; head office 71 Lombard Street (Wikipedia, Lombard Street, London).

Substance:
1. "Eliot said in 1950 that Baudelaire had taught him 'the poetical possibilities . . . of the more sordid aspects of the modern metropolis, of the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric.'" The words are Eliot's, but the note does not say where they come from, and at some 25 words the quotation is longer than the rights rule allows for a post-1929 text. Name the source (the talk "What Dante Means to Me", 1950) and cut the quotation to a phrase: "the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric".
2. "That is the recipe for the next sixteen lines: a real bridge, a real street, a real church clock, and the dead." The count is right (61–76), but the sentence turns a remark about Baudelaire's method into a claim about how Eliot built the passage, which the talk does not say. It is a reading; either mark it as one or cut it. The next note (london-bridge-dead) already makes the point about the named London route.
3. "The fog is coal smoke." True in substance (the London winter fog of the period was a smoke fog) but stated as if the poem said so. Harmless; "The brown fog is London's coal-smoke fog" would do.
4. "Eliot went into the City through it every working morning from 1917, to Lloyds Bank in Lombard Street." He joined Lloyds on 19 March 1917; the head office was 71 Lombard Street, on the block running back to Cornhill (his 1917 bank letterhead gives 17 Cornhill, the other side of the same block, but I saw that only in secondary reproduction, so do not add it). "Every working morning" overstates: he was away from the bank for three months in late 1921, the leave on which the poem was finished, and on other sick leave. "From 1917" without "every" is enough.
5. "'Unreal City' returns at line 207 (a winter noon) and, stripped to one word, at line 377." Right in the site's count (checked against the page anchors). Eliot's marginal number for the last is 376; the site runs one ahead there, as the brief says.
6. Quote and translation: the quote matches Baudelaire (the "!" is his; Eliot's note prints a full stop). "Accosts" for "raccroche" is right; the verb is the one used of street-walkers soliciting.

Draft:
TITLE: Baudelaire's swarming city
BODY: The opening of "Les Sept Vieillards" ("The Seven Old Men"), in the <i>Tableaux parisiens</i> section of <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i>: the poet, walking through a dirty yellow fog, meets the same hideous old man seven times over. In a talk on Dante in 1950 ("What Dante Means to Me") Eliot said that Baudelaire had shown him what poetry could make of the modern city, the fusion of "the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric".

The brown fog is London's coal-smoke fog. Eliot went in to the City through it from March 1917, when he joined Lloyds Bank at 71 Lombard Street. "Unreal City" returns at line 207, under the same fog at noon, and as the single word "Unreal" at line 377.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "That is the recipe for the next sixteen lines: a real bridge, a real street, a real church clock, and the dead." — the colon-and-list aphorism from the brief; "recipe" is a metaphor for the poem, "sixteen" is a count that exists to be quoted, and "and the dead" is a drumroll. The point underneath (real London, Dante's dead) is worth one plain sentence.
2. "Eliot said in 1950 that Baudelaire had taught him..." — said where? I want the talk named. Not mine to rule on, but the site's own rule for Eliot's later texts is a phrase or two, and this is a full sentence; the scholar should say whether it stays at this length.
3. "stripped to one word" — a small flourish that made me stop and work out which word. Say the word.
4. The first sentence is a long fragment ("The opening of ... in which the poet ... meets ...") of the kind editors do use, but it reads more easily as two sentences.

Keep: "The fog is coal smoke. Eliot went into the City through it every working morning from 1917, to Lloyds Bank in Lombard Street." Also the title.

Draft:
TITLE: Baudelaire's swarming city
BODY:
The opening of “Les Sept Vieillards” (“The Seven Old Men”), in the <i>Tableaux parisiens</i> section of <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i>. The poet, walking in a yellow fog, meets the same hideous old man seven times over. In a talk in 1950 [scholar to name it] Eliot said that Baudelaire had taught him “the poetical possibilities . . . of the more sordid aspects of the modern metropolis, of the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric.” The lines that follow do this. London Bridge, King William Street and St Mary Woolnoth are real places, and the crowd on them is taken from Dante’s dead.

The fog is coal smoke. Eliot walked through it into the City every working morning from 1917, to Lloyds Bank in Lombard Street. “Unreal City” returns at line 207, at a winter noon, and at line 377 as the single word “Unreal.”

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim, as both sides said. The 1950 quotation is cut to a phrase and given its source; "the recipe for the next sixteen lines" goes; "every working morning" becomes "from March 1917"; the Lloyds fact is left to the St Mary Woolnoth note, which carries it in full.
TITLE: Baudelaire's swarming city
BODY:
The opening of "Les Sept Vieillards" ("The Seven Old Men"), in the <i>Tableaux parisiens</i> section of <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i>. The poet, walking through a dirty yellow fog, meets the same hideous old man seven times over. In a talk on Dante in 1950 ("What Dante Means to Me") Eliot said that Baudelaire had shown him what poetry could make of the modern city, the fusion of "the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric".

The brown fog is London's coal-smoke fog. Eliot went in to the City through it from March 1917, when he joined Lloyds Bank in Lombard Street. "Unreal City" returns at line 207, under the same fog at noon, and as the single word "Unreal" at line 377.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

Clear, and every sentence is a fact with its source. I paused once, at "what poetry could make of the modern city, the fusion of ...", but it reads on the second look and I would leave it alone.

**Editor, after round 2**

Both sides satisfied. The round-two draft stands unchanged.

## london-bridge-dead

**As it stood**

TITLE: The crowd at the gate of Hell
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): . . . sì lunga tratta / di gente, ch’i’ non averei creduto / che morte tanta n’avesse disfatta.
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): . . . so long a train of people, that I would never have believed death had undone so many.
BODY:
*Inferno* III, lines 55–57. Just inside the gate, before the river, Dante sees the neutrals, the souls who lived "without infamy and without praise," and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, "Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled," is from the next canto, the Limbo of the virtuous unbaptised, where "there was no weeping but only sighs, which made the eternal air tremble" (IV, 25–27). So the commuters crossing from London Bridge station into the City between half past eight and nine are damned twice over, once as people who never chose anything, once as people who were merely born too early. Eliot worked among them. "Each man fixed his eyes before his feet" is what a crowd on a bridge in a fog actually looks like.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Inferno</i> III, 31–42 and 55–57, and IV, 25–42, Petrocchi text (it.wikisource, Divina_Commedia/Inferno/Canto_III and Canto_IV). Eliot's 1950 talk (as above) for "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices", the "deliberately modified" line, and his reason for giving references in the notes. The older reading "ch'io non avrei mai creduto" is in nineteenth-century editions (Lana and Tommaseo page scans on it.wikisource), so Eliot's note is not a misquotation.

Substance:
1. The quote gives the modern critical text ("ch'i' non averei creduto"); Eliot's note beside it has the older reading "ch'io non avrei mai creduto", the text of the Temple Classics edition he used. Both are legitimate. A reader who compares them may take one for a misprint; the editor may prefer to print the text Eliot printed, since the note sits under it. Not an error.
2. "the neutrals, the souls who lived 'without infamy and without praise'": right (III, 36, "che visser sanza 'nfamia e sanza lodo"). Add that they are in the vestibule, refused by Heaven and by Hell alike (III, 40–41); it bears on item 4.
3. "Line 64 ... is from the next canto, the Limbo of the virtuous unbaptised, where 'there was no weeping but only sighs, which made the eternal air tremble' (IV, 25–27)." Right (IV, 26–27). The English is the annotator's own rendering, not a named translator's; the quotation marks make it look like one. Eliot said in 1950 that he had "deliberately modified" this line of Dante; worth a clause.
4. "So the commuters ... are damned twice over, once as people who never chose anything, once as people who were merely born too early." Two misstatements. Neither crowd is damned in Dante's scheme: Hell itself will not receive the neutrals (III, 41), and the souls of Limbo suffer no torment, only hopeless longing ("sanza speme vivemo in disio", IV, 42). And Limbo is not for those "born too early": Virgil says they did not sin but lacked baptism, and that those who lived before Christianity "did not worship God rightly" (IV, 34–38); Dante puts Saladin, Avicenna and Averroes there as well. Give the facts and let the reader draw the parallel, or mark any parallel as a reading.
5. "commuters crossing from London Bridge station into the City between half past eight and nine": the half-hour is an inference from line 68 with no source; drop it. The railway station is Eliot's own gloss: "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices" (1950). Cite it; a phrase of that length is within the rights rule.
6. "'Each man fixed his eyes before his feet' is what a crowd on a bridge in a fog actually looks like." Assertion without source. Cut, or mark as the annotator's observation.
7. Missing: Eliot's stated reason for the notes, in the same talk: he gave the references so that a reader who recognised the allusion would know he was meant to. One clause; it also serves the hypocrite-lecteur note.

Draft:
TITLE: The crowd at the gate of Hell
BODY: <i>Inferno</i> III, 55–57. Just inside the gate of Hell, before the river Acheron, Dante sees the souls of those who lived "without infamy and without praise" (III, 36), refused by Heaven and by Hell alike, and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, "Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled", comes from the next canto, Limbo, where the unbaptised and the virtuous pagans live "without hope, in desire", and there is no weeping, only sighs that make the eternal air tremble (IV, 25–42). Eliot said in 1950 that he had altered Dante's line on purpose, and that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised them would know he was meant to. In the same talk he called this crowd "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices". He was one of them: he worked at Lloyds Bank, at the top of King William Street, from 1917 to 1925.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "So the commuters ... are damned twice over, once as people who never chose anything, once as people who were merely born too early." — this is the brief's own example of the portentous pairing, and it is a reading given as a fact. "Born too early" stopped me: it means born before Christ, which fits Dante's Limbo and not a clerk on London Bridge, so the clever parallel does not survive a second look.
2. "“Each man fixed his eyes before his feet” is what a crowd on a bridge in a fog actually looks like." — the writer vouching for the poem; "actually" stands in for an argument that is not there, and the sentence is a bow to end on.
3. "between half past eight and nine" — a precise time with no source. The poem gives nine, which is enough.
4. "Eliot worked among them." — true and to the point, but staged as a beat of its own; it can go inside the sentence about the crowd.

Keep: "<i>Inferno</i> III, lines 55–57. Just inside the gate, before the river, Dante sees the neutrals, the souls who lived “without infamy and without praise,” and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, “Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled,” is from the next canto, the Limbo of the virtuous unbaptised, where “there was no weeping but only sighs, which made the eternal air tremble” (IV, 25–27)." Also the title.

Draft:
TITLE: The crowd at the gate of Hell
BODY:
<i>Inferno</i> III, lines 55–57. Just inside the gate, before the river, Dante sees the neutrals, the souls who lived “without infamy and without praise,” and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, “Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled,” comes from the next canto, the Limbo of the virtuous unbaptised, where “there was no weeping but only sighs, which made the eternal air tremble” (IV, 25–27). Eliot’s crowd is the morning rush from London Bridge station into the City, which he walked in himself on his way to Lloyds Bank. Its size comes from the first passage and its sighs from the second. One reading is that the commuters are like Dante’s neutrals, people who never chose anything.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Damned twice over" and "born too early" go, as the scholar showed they misstate Dante; the half-hour goes; the crowd is described in Eliot's own 1950 words, which the scholar supplied; the parallel with the neutrals is left for the reader to draw from the facts, as the scholar preferred, rather than stated as a reading.
TITLE: The crowd at the gate of Hell
BODY:
<i>Inferno</i> III, 55–57. Just inside the gate of Hell, before the river Acheron, Dante sees the souls of those who lived "without infamy and without praise" (III, 36), refused by Heaven and by Hell alike, and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, "Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled," comes from the next canto, Limbo, where the unbaptised and the virtuous pagans live without hope, in desire, and there is no weeping, only sighs that make the eternal air tremble (IV, 25–42). Eliot said in 1950 that he had altered Dante's line on purpose, and that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised them would know he was meant to. In the same talk he called this crowd "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices". He was one of them, at Lloyds Bank at the top of King William Street, from 1917 to 1925.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

The note is now good, and the three things below are small.

1. "live without hope, in desire, and there is no weeping" — "without hope, in desire" is Dante's phrase, but unmarked it reads as the annotator's own pairing, and "in desire" on its own stopped me. Put: "where the unbaptised and the virtuous pagans live, in Dante's words, "without hope, in desire", and there is no weeping, only sighs that make the eternal air tremble (IV, 25–42)."
2. "Eliot said in 1950 that he had altered Dante's line on purpose, and that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised them would know he was meant to." — two things in one sentence. "Dante's line": which one? Coming after the sentence on line 64 I took it to be the sighs line, but the note hangs on line 63 and a reader may take it the other way; the scholar should say which line Eliot meant. And "would know he was meant to" leaves the reader to finish the sentence. If it is the sighs line, put: "Eliot said in 1950 that he had altered the line about the sighs on purpose, and that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised a borrowing would know he was meant to recognise it."
3. "He was one of them, at Lloyds Bank at the top of King William Street, from 1917 to 1925." — the two "at"s make it stumble. Put: "He was one of them. Lloyds Bank, where he worked from 1917 to 1925, was at the top of King William Street."

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar satisfied. Reader: mark "without hope, in desire" as Dante's; say which line Eliot altered on purpose (the scholar's round-one report gives the 1950 talk: the altered line is the one about the sighs, and Eliot's sentence about the notes ends "know that I meant him to recognize it"); split the Lloyds sentence. All three taken.
TITLE: The crowd at the gate of Hell
BODY:
<i>Inferno</i> III, 55–57. Just inside the gate of Hell, before the river Acheron, Dante sees the souls of those who lived "without infamy and without praise" (III, 36), refused by Heaven and by Hell alike, and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, "Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled," comes from the next canto, Limbo, where the unbaptised and the virtuous pagans live, in Dante's words, "without hope, in desire", and there is no weeping, only sighs that make the eternal air tremble (IV, 25–42). Eliot said in 1950 that he had altered the line about the sighs on purpose, and that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised a borrowing would know he was meant to recognise it. In the same talk he called this crowd "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices". He was one of them. Lloyds Bank, where he worked from 1917 to 1925, was at the top of King William Street.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

Checked, as asked: Eliot's 1950 sentence about the notes is fairly given. His words ("What Dante Means to Me", 1950) are that he "gave the references in my notes, in order to make the reader who recognized the allusion, know that I meant him to recognize it", and the sentence goes on that such a reader would have missed the point had he not recognised it. The rewrite's "he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised a borrowing would know he was meant to recognise it" keeps the subject (the references, not the notes as a whole) and the purpose; the dropped second clause is not needed here. "Altered the line about the sighs on purpose" is a fair rendering of "deliberately modified a line of Dante by altering it", with that line quoted.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

All three points taken. It now reads as a person explaining the two Dante passages and what Eliot said about them, and it stops.

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The crowd at the gate of Hell
BODY:
<i>Inferno</i> III, 55–57. Just inside the gate of Hell, before the river Acheron, Dante sees the souls of those who lived "without infamy and without praise" (III, 36), refused by Heaven and by Hell alike, and is astonished at how many there are. Line 64, "Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled," comes from the next canto, Limbo, where the unbaptised and the virtuous pagans live, in Dante's words, "without hope, in desire", and there is no weeping, only sighs that make the eternal air tremble (IV, 25–42). Eliot said in 1950 that he had altered the line about the sighs on purpose, and that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised a borrowing would know he was meant to recognise it. In the same talk he called this crowd "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices". He was one of them. Lloyds Bank, where he worked from 1917 to 1925, was in Lombard Street, a few doors from the top of King William Street.


## woolnoth

**As it stood**

TITLE: The dead sound at nine
BODY:
King William Street runs from London Bridge up to the Bank, and at its top, on the corner of Lombard Street, stands Nicholas Hawksmoor's St Mary Woolnoth (1716–27), a squat, heavily rusticated church with two little turrets. Lloyds Bank, where Eliot worked in the Colonial and Foreign Department from 1917 to 1925, was across the road. The church's clock struck the start of his working day.

"A phenomenon which I have often noticed," says Eliot's note on the dead sound of the final stroke, the poem's driest joke about its own apparatus. There is a literal death under this church too: when the Bank station of the City and South London Railway was built beneath it in 1897–1900, the bodies in the crypt were cleared out and reburied at Ilford, and the crypt became the station's booking hall. The commuters come up out of it every morning.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Wikipedia, St Mary Woolnoth (Hawksmoor; work began 1716, reopened Easter Day 1727; corner of Lombard Street and King William Street; two flat-topped turrets; C&SLR works 1897–1900; bones reburied at the City of London Cemetery, Manor Park; lift and stair shafts beneath the church floor). Wikipedia, Bank and Monument stations (C&SLR station opened 25 February 1900; ticket hall and lift entrance in the crypt; church underpinned). Wikipedia, City of London Cemetery (St Mary Woolnoth crypt transferred 1897–1900 for Bank station). Eliot Estate site: Lloyds 19 March 1917 to November 1925, Colonial & Foreign Department; Henry Eliot's 1921 photograph of the corner, "Lloyds on the left, St Mary Woolnoth on the right" (Houghton Library, MS Am 2560 (263)). Hours: letter to his mother, March 1917, "sitting in an office from 9.15 to 5" (<i>Letters</i> vol. 1; quoted in Lisa Levy, The Rumpus, 31 Jan. 2012). 71 Lombard Street as Lloyds' headquarters: Wikipedia, Lombard Street, London.

Substance:
1. "Nicholas Hawksmoor's St Mary Woolnoth (1716–27)": right.
2. "Lloyds Bank, where Eliot worked in the Colonial and Foreign Department from 1917 to 1925, was across the road." "Across the road" is right: the head office at 71 Lombard Street faced the church across Lombard Street, as Henry Eliot's 1921 photograph of the corner shows. Eliot was at Lloyds from 19 March 1917 to November 1925 and began in the Colonial and Foreign Department; whether he stayed in that department throughout I could not confirm (there is a recent article on his work for the bank's Intelligence Department). Safer: "where Eliot worked from 1917 to 1925".
3. "The church's clock struck the start of his working day." Not quite. His hours, he told his mother in March 1917, were "from 9.15 to 5". Nine o'clock would have found him on his way in. Either drop the sentence or make it exact.
4. "'A phenomenon which I have often noticed,' says Eliot's note ... the poem's driest joke about its own apparatus." The quotation is right. "Driest joke" is a reading (others take the note as plain testimony); mark it or cut it.
5. "the Bank station of the City and South London Railway was built beneath it in 1897–1900": right; the station opened on 25 February 1900.
6. "the bodies in the crypt were cleared out and reburied at Ilford": the reburial was in the City of London Cemetery at Manor Park. The cemetery lay in the old parish of Little Ilford and was commonly given as "Ilford" at the time, so this is not wrong, but Manor Park is what a reader will find. Suggest "in the City of London Cemetery at Manor Park".
7. "the crypt became the station's booking hall": right (a sub-surface ticket hall and lift entrance in the crypt, the church above underpinned on steel girders).
8. "The commuters come up out of it every morning." Misleading beside the poem: the crowd of lines 62–68 comes over the bridge on foot from the railway station, as Eliot said in 1950; nobody in the poem comes up out of the Tube. The station is worth a sentence as a fact about the building, not attached to the poem's crowd.

Draft:
TITLE: The dead sound at nine
BODY: King William Street runs up from London Bridge to the Bank, and at its top, on the corner of Lombard Street, stands Nicholas Hawksmoor's St Mary Woolnoth (1716–27), a squat, heavily rusticated church with two flat-topped turrets. Lloyds Bank, whose head office at 71 Lombard Street faced the church across the street, employed Eliot from March 1917 to November 1925; his hours, he told his mother in 1917, were "from 9.15 to 5".

Eliot's note on the dead sound of the final stroke, "A phenomenon which I have often noticed", vouches for the sound from his own hearing. The crypt has its own history: when the City and South London Railway built Bank station beneath the church in 1897–1900, the bodies in the crypt were reburied in the City of London Cemetery at Manor Park, and the crypt became the station's booking hall, the church above it underpinned on steel girders.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "the poem's driest joke about its own apparatus" — an opinion in jargon ("apparatus"), and it tells me to find the note funny. Quote the note and let me.
2. "There is a literal death under this church too:" — a tour-guide set-up with a colon. The fact that follows is good and needs no drumroll.
3. "The commuters come up out of it every morning." — the bow. If the point is that the station entrance under the church is still in use, say that plainly, or stop at the booking hall.
4. "heavily rusticated" — I had to guess at it. A few words of plain English would help, unless the image beside the note does the job.
5. Title "The dead sound at nine" — I clicked on the church's name and expected the note to be about the church. Title it by the church.

Keep: the whole first paragraph, from "King William Street" to "his working day". In the second paragraph, the sentence from "when the Bank station" to "booking hall".

Draft:
TITLE: St Mary Woolnoth
BODY:
King William Street runs from London Bridge up to the Bank, and at its top, on the corner of Lombard Street, stands Nicholas Hawksmoor’s St Mary Woolnoth (1716–27), a squat church with two little turrets and heavily rusticated stone, cut in deep bands. Lloyds Bank, where Eliot worked in the Colonial and Foreign Department from 1917 to 1925, was across the road, and the church’s clock struck the start of his working day. Eliot’s note on line 68 says only: “A phenomenon which I have often noticed.”

When the Bank station of the City and South London Railway was built beneath the church in 1897–1900, the crypt was cleared, the bodies were reburied at Ilford, and the crypt became the station’s booking hall.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. Title by the church, as the reader asked. "Struck the start of his working day" corrected by the scholar's 9.15; "driest joke" and the Tube sentence go; the reburial place corrected to Manor Park; the department dropped from the dates.
