**As it now stands**

TITLE: St Mary Woolnoth
BODY:
King William Street runs up from London Bridge to the Bank of England, and at its top, on the corner of Lombard Street, stands Nicholas Hawksmoor's St Mary Woolnoth (1716–27), a squat church with two flat-topped turrets and its stone cut in deep bands. Lloyds Bank's head office, at 71 Lombard Street, stood a few doors along from the church. Eliot worked there from March 1917 to November 1925; his hours, he told his mother in 1917, were "from 9.15 to 5". His note on line 68 says only: "A phenomenon which I have often noticed."

When the City and South London Railway built Bank station beneath the church in 1897–1900, the bodies in the crypt were reburied in the City of London Cemetery at Manor Park, and the crypt became the station's booking hall.


## stetson

**As it stood**

TITLE: Stetson, and the ships at Mylae
BODY:
Mylae is the sea battle of 260 BC in which Rome first beat Carthage at sea, early in the First Punic War, a trade war between two commercial empires. The friend met in the City of London, the financial centre of another commercial empire four years after another war, was "in the ships" there. The war dead and the office crowd are the same people, and all wars are the one war. Stetson has often been taken for Pound, who wore wide-brimmed hats; nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular. The name is a hat, an American hat, on a bridge in London.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Checked: Wikipedia, Battle of Mylae (260 BC; Gaius Duilius against Hannibal Gisco; Rome's first naval victory; the war was for Sicily). Cleanth Brooks, "The Waste Land: Critique of the Myth" (<i>Modern Poetry and the Tradition</i>, 1939), wording confirmed by archive.org full-text search of the reprints (Jay Martin's collection, 1968; Norton Critical Edition): "Mylae, as Mr. Matthiessen has pointed out, is the name of a battle between the Romans and the Carthaginians in the Punic War. The Punic War was a trade war—might be considered a rather close parallel to our late war. ... all the wars are one war; all experience, one experience." B. C. Southam, <i>A Guide to the Selected Poems of T. S. Eliot</i> (6th ed., 1994), note on l. 69, seen only in archive.org search snippets: Pound's "sombrero-stetson", a denial, John Hayward's note, the Australian felt slouch hat. TLS, 11 May 1973 (a letter in the correspondence on "The Waste Land as a Buddhist Poem"; the scan is restricted, so I have a snippet only and could not see the signature): "'Stetson' is not Ezra Pound. Eliot said he was not referring to anyone in particular, but simply meant any superior bank clerk: a person in a bowler hat, black jacket and striped trousers." Quoted by Donald J. Childs, "Stetson in The Waste Land", <i>Essays in Criticism</i> 38 (1988), 131–48, who links Stetson with Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears ("The Finest Story in the World"), as does Robert Crawford, <i>The Savage and the City in the Work of T. S. Eliot</i> (1987). Line 69 in the 1922 text: "There I saw one I knew".

Substance:
1. "Mylae is the sea battle of 260 BC in which Rome first beat Carthage at sea, early in the First Punic War": right.
2. "a trade war between two commercial empires": this is Cleanth Brooks's reading of 1939, following Matthiessen (1935), who first identified Mylae: "The Punic War was a trade war—might be considered a rather close parallel to our late war." The note gives it as history. Historians describe the First Punic War as a war for Sicily, and Rome in 264 BC was no commercial empire. Attribute it: "Cleanth Brooks read the Punic War as a trade war and so a parallel to 1914–18".
3. "The war dead and the office crowd are the same people, and all wars are the one war." Brooks again, near verbatim ("all the wars are one war; all experience, one experience"). Attribute.
4. "Stetson has often been taken for Pound, who wore wide-brimmed hats": fair; Southam's <i>Guide</i> records the identification and Pound's "sombrero-stetson". There is also a report of Eliot's denying it: the TLS correspondent of 1973 (above) has Eliot saying he meant no one in particular, "simply ... any superior bank clerk" in a bowler hat, black jacket and striped trousers. If the note uses this it should say "a correspondent in the TLS in 1973 reported Eliot as saying ..." and no more; I could not read the whole letter.
5. "nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular": fine, and the 1973 report supports it.
6. "The name is a hat, an American hat, on a bridge in London." The name is a hat (John B. Stetson Company, Philadelphia), but the meeting is not on the bridge: "There I saw one I knew" (line 69) is at the top of King William Street, by St Mary Woolnoth, where the crowd has just arrived. "In King William Street" or "in the City".
7. Worth a clause, since a reader will meet them: the felt slouch hat of the Australian troops at Gallipoli (recorded in Southam), and Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, who remembers rowing in an ancient galley (Crawford 1987; Childs 1988). Neither need be endorsed.

Draft:
TITLE: Stetson, and the ships at Mylae
BODY: Mylae was the sea battle of 260 BC in which Rome first beat Carthage at sea, early in the First Punic War. F. O. Matthiessen identified the battle in 1935, and Cleanth Brooks in 1939 read the Punic War as a trade war and so a parallel to the war of 1914–18: Eliot's point, on that reading, is that "all the wars are one war". The friend met in the City of London a few years after the Armistice was "in the ships" twenty-two centuries before.

Stetson has often been taken for Pound, who wore a wide-brimmed Stetson hat; Southam's <i>Guide</i> records the identification. A correspondent in the <i>Times Literary Supplement</i> in 1973 reported Eliot as saying that he meant no one in particular, only a superior bank clerk in a bowler hat. Other suggestions are the slouch hat of the Australian troops at Gallipoli, and Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, who remembers rowing in an ancient galley (Robert Crawford, 1987; Donald Childs, 1988). Nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular. The meeting is in King William Street, not on the bridge.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "the financial centre of another commercial empire four years after another war" — the doubled "another" made me read it twice, and the whole analogy is squeezed into one subordinate clause.
2. "The war dead and the office crowd are the same people, and all wars are the one war." — a pronouncement given as fact. It is a reading, a well-known one, and should be marked as one.
3. "The name is a hat, an American hat, on a bridge in London." — a bow to end on. The useful part, that Stetson is an American name and the name of a hat, should be said plainly.
4. "Stetson has often been taken for Pound" — by whom? One name would do (for the scholar).
5. "a trade war between two commercial empires" — a characterisation added to set up the parallel; it reads as loading the dice. Fine if the scholar stands behind it, but keep it short.

Keep: "Mylae is the sea battle of 260 BC in which Rome first beat Carthage at sea, early in the First Punic War"; "nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular." Also the title.

Draft:
TITLE: Stetson, and the ships at Mylae
BODY:
Mylae was a sea battle of 260 BC, early in the First Punic War, in which Rome beat Carthage at sea for the first time. Rome and Carthage were trading empires, and the war was a war over trade. Stetson is met in the City of London, the financial centre of the British Empire, four years after the end of the war of 1914–18. The usual reading is that the ancient war stands for the recent one, and that the man who was “in the ships” at Mylae had been in that war too. Stetson is an American name and the name of an American hat. He has often been taken for Pound, who wore wide-brimmed hats, but nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The trade-war and one-war claims are given to Brooks and Matthiessen, as the scholar established; the meeting moved off the bridge; the 1973 report of Eliot's denial added as a report; the other identifications given in one sentence, unendorsed; the hat sentence made plain.
TITLE: Stetson, and the ships at Mylae
BODY:
Mylae was the sea battle of 260 BC in which Rome first beat Carthage at sea, early in the First Punic War. F. O. Matthiessen identified the battle in 1935, and Cleanth Brooks in 1939 read the Punic War as a trade war and so a parallel to the war of 1914–18; on that reading, in Brooks's words, "all the wars are one war". The friend met in the City of London a few years after the Armistice was "in the ships" twenty-two centuries before. The meeting is in King William Street, where the crowd has just arrived, not on the bridge.

Stetson is an American name and the name of an American hat. He has often been taken for Pound, who wore a wide-brimmed hat; Southam's <i>Guide</i> records the identification. A correspondent in the <i>Times Literary Supplement</i> in 1973 reported Eliot as saying that he meant no one in particular, only a superior bank clerk in a bowler hat. Other suggestions are the slouch hat of the Australian troops at Gallipoli, and Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, who remembers rowing in an ancient galley. Nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "A correspondent in the <i>Times Literary Supplement</i> in 1973 reported Eliot as saying ..." The correspondent was Valerie Eliot. Donald Childs, "Stetson in The Waste Land", <i>Essays in Criticism</i> 38 (1988), quotes the letter as hers: "Valerie Eliot, for instance, responding to speculation that Stetson might be Ezra Pound, writes: '"Stetson" is not Ezra Pound. Eliot said he was not referring to anyone in particular, but simply meant any superior bank clerk: a person in a bowler hat, black jacket and striped trousers'" (the same words are in the TLS of 11 May 1973, in the correspondence on "The Waste Land as a Buddhist Poem"). A report by his widow carries different weight from one by a stranger, and the note should say whose it is. Put: "Valerie Eliot, answering that identification in a letter to the <i>Times Literary Supplement</i> (11 May 1973), wrote that Eliot was not referring to anyone in particular but "simply meant any superior bank clerk", a man in a bowler hat, black jacket and striped trousers."
2. "Other suggestions are the slouch hat of the Australian troops at Gallipoli, and Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, who remembers rowing in an ancient galley." Neither suggestion carries its source, and Southam's sentence names no battle: the felt slouch hats "(a version of the stetson) were the characteristic and identifying head-gear of Australian and New Zealand soldiers in the First World War" (<i>Guide</i>, 6th ed., note on l. 69). The Kipling connection is Robert Crawford's (<i>The Savage and the City in the Work of T. S. Eliot</i>, 1987) and Childs's (1988). Put: "Southam's <i>Guide</i> also records the felt slouch hat, a kind of stetson, worn by the Australian and New Zealand troops of 1914–18; Robert Crawford (1987) and Donald Childs (1988) connect the name with Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, in "The Finest Story in the World", who remembers rowing in an ancient galley."

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "Mylae was the sea battle of 260 BC in which Rome first beat Carthage at sea, early in the First Punic War. F. O. Matthiessen identified the battle in 1935, and Cleanth Brooks in 1939 read the Punic War as a trade war and so a parallel to the war of 1914–18; on that reading, in Brooks's words, "all the wars are one war"." — "sea battle ... at sea" says it twice; "identified the battle" left me asking "as what?"; "and so a parallel" is missing a word; "on that reading, in Brooks's words" qualifies the same thing twice. Put: "Mylae was the battle of 260 BC, early in the First Punic War, in which Rome beat Carthage at sea for the first time; the identification is F. O. Matthiessen's (1935). Cleanth Brooks (1939) read the Punic War as a trade war, and so as a parallel to the war of 1914–18: in his words, "all the wars are one war"."
2. "The friend met in the City of London a few years after the Armistice was "in the ships" twenty-two centuries before." — the "few years" against "twenty-two centuries" is the count for effect that the brief warns against, and the sentence states the poem's conceit as if it were the note's own fact. Put: "The speaker greets a friend in the City of London, a few years after the Armistice, as someone who was "in the ships" with him at Mylae."
3. "Other suggestions are the slouch hat of the Australian troops at Gallipoli, and Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears" — a hat and a man are not the same kind of suggestion, and I had to work out that the hat is a suggestion about the name. Put: "The name has also been linked to the slouch hat of the Australian troops at Gallipoli, and Stetson himself to Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, who remembers rowing in an ancient galley."

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: the 1973 correspondent was Valerie Eliot (Childs 1988; TLS 11 May 1973), and the two other suggestions need their sources (Southam for the slouch hat, with no battle named; Crawford 1987 and Childs 1988 for Charlie Mears). Reader: the doubled "sea battle ... at sea"; the "twenty-two centuries" sentence; the hat and the man are different kinds of suggestion. All five taken; the scholar's facts in the reader's shape.
TITLE: Stetson, and the ships at Mylae
BODY:
Mylae was the battle of 260 BC, early in the First Punic War, in which Rome beat Carthage at sea for the first time; the identification is F. O. Matthiessen's (1935). Cleanth Brooks (1939) read the Punic War as a trade war, and so as a parallel to the war of 1914–18: in his words, "all the wars are one war". The speaker greets a friend in the City of London, a few years after the Armistice, as someone who was "in the ships" with him at Mylae. The meeting is in King William Street, where the crowd has just arrived, not on the bridge.

Stetson is an American name and the name of an American hat. He has often been taken for Pound, who wore a wide-brimmed hat; Southam's <i>Guide</i> records the identification. Valerie Eliot, answering it in a letter to the <i>Times Literary Supplement</i> (11 May 1973), wrote that Eliot was not referring to anyone in particular but "simply meant any superior bank clerk", a man in a bowler hat, black jacket and striped trousers. The name has also been linked, by Southam, to the felt slouch hat, a kind of stetson, worn by the Australian and New Zealand troops of 1914–18; Stetson himself has been linked by Robert Crawford (1987) and Donald Childs (1988) to Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, in "The Finest Story in the World", who remembers rowing in an ancient galley. Nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: not yet

1. "The name has also been linked, by Southam, to the felt slouch hat ..." Southam records the suggestion; he does not make it. His note on l. 69 introduces someone else and continues "He points out that felt slouch hats (a version of the stetson) were the characteristic and identifying head-gear of Australian and New Zealand soldiers in the First World War" (<i>Guide</i>, 6th ed.). I could not recover the name from the archive.org search window, so the note should credit the <i>Guide</i> as the place where the suggestion is recorded, not as its author. Put: "Southam's <i>Guide</i> also records a suggestion that links the name to the felt slouch hat, a kind of stetson, worn by the Australian and New Zealand troops of 1914–18; Stetson himself has been linked by Robert Crawford (1987) and Donald Childs (1988) to Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, in "The Finest Story in the World", who remembers rowing in an ancient galley."

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: not yet

One thing, which is new in this draft. Valerie Eliot's "not referring to anyone in particular" now sits three sentences above the note's own last sentence, "Nothing in the poem needs him to be anyone in particular", so the note ends by repeating her words as if they were its own. Put: "Nothing in the poem requires him to be any of these." The rest of the note, including the long sentence on the slouch hat and Charlie Mears, I would leave as it is.

**As it now stands**

TITLE: Stetson, and the ships at Mylae
BODY:
Mylae was the battle of 260 BC, early in the First Punic War, in which Rome beat Carthage at sea for the first time; the identification is F. O. Matthiessen's (1935). Cleanth Brooks (1939) read the Punic War as a trade war, and so as a parallel to the war of 1914–18: in his words, "all the wars are one war". The speaker greets a friend in the City of London, a few years after the Armistice, as someone who was "in the ships" with him at Mylae. The meeting is in King William Street, where the crowd has just arrived, not on the bridge.

Stetson is an American name and the name of an American hat. He has often been taken for Pound, who wore a wide-brimmed hat; Southam's <i>Guide</i> records the identification. Valerie Eliot, answering it in a letter to the <i>Times Literary Supplement</i> (11 May 1973), wrote that Eliot was not referring to anyone in particular but "simply meant any superior bank clerk", a man in a bowler hat, black jacket and striped trousers. Southam's <i>Guide</i> also records a suggestion that links the name to the felt slouch hat, a kind of stetson, worn by the Australian and New Zealand troops of 1914–18; Stetson himself has been linked by Robert Crawford (1987) and Donald Childs (1988) to Kipling's bank clerk Charlie Mears, in "The Finest Story in the World", who remembers rowing in an ancient galley. Nothing in the poem requires him to be any of these.


## corpse

**As it stood**

TITLE: The buried god
BODY:
Frazer's *The Golden Bough*, in the volumes Eliot's headnote singles out (*Adonis, Attis, Osiris*), describes the "gardens of Adonis": pots of quick-sprouting seed carried in the god's funeral procession and thrown into the sea, and the "Osiris beds," effigies of the god filled with earth and corn and buried so that the sprouting grain showed his resurrection. The corpse in Stetson's suburban garden is that ritual gone domestic and gone wrong. Will it bloom? Has the frost got at it? The question is asked in the voice of a man talking about bulbs, and it is the question of the whole poem: can anything come back?

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Frazer, <i>The Golden Bough</i>, 1922 abridgement (Gutenberg #3623), ch. XXXIII "The Gardens of Adonis" and ch. XXXIX "The Ritual of Osiris"; and <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, 3rd ed. (1914), vol. II, text at archive.org (in.ernet.dli.2015.167743): "effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn", "corn-stuffed effigies of Osiris"; the phrase "Osiris beds" occurs in neither. Eliot's headnote in poem-part1.md ("the two volumes <i>Atthis Adonis Osiris</i>").

Substance:
1. "the volumes Eliot's headnote singles out (<i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>)": Frazer's title is <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris: Studies in the History of Oriental Religion</i> (Part IV of the third edition, two volumes, 1914). Eliot's note misspells and reorders it. The gloss is right to give Frazer's title; it could say in passing that Eliot's note garbles it, since the reader sees both.
2. "the 'gardens of Adonis': pots of quick-sprouting seed carried in the god's funeral procession and thrown into the sea": matches Frazer ("baskets or pots filled with earth, in which wheat, barley, lettuces, fennel, and various kinds of flowers were sown and tended for eight days ... carried out with the images of the dead Adonis, and flung with them into the sea or into springs"). Right.
3. "the 'Osiris beds,' effigies of the god filled with earth and corn and buried so that the sprouting grain showed his resurrection": the description is Frazer's, the phrase in quotation marks is not. Frazer writes of "effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn" buried at the festival of sowing in the month of Khoiak and taken up when the corn had sprouted, and of "corn-stuffed effigies" found in tombs, whose sprouting (he quotes Erman) "is intended to signify the resurrection of the god". "Osiris bed" is the Egyptologists' term from the tomb reports of 1907–15 (Davies, Quibell, Gardiner). Use Frazer's words or drop the quotation marks.
4. "The corpse in Stetson's suburban garden": "suburban" is not in the poem; the garden is not placed. Cut or mark as a guess.
5. "Will it bloom? Has the frost got at it? The question is asked in the voice of a man talking about bulbs, and it is the question of the whole poem: can anything come back?" The last clause is a reading (the death-and-rebirth reading that runs from Matthiessen and Brooks onward); mark it or cut it. The "voice of a man talking about bulbs" is a fair observation and needs no source.
6. Missing: Eliot's headnote says readers of Frazer "will immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies". This is the plainest of them; a clause saying so ties the gloss to the note the reader has just seen.

Draft:
TITLE: The buried god
BODY: Frazer's <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the two volumes Eliot's headnote names (with the title garbled), describes the "gardens of Adonis": pots of earth sown with quick-sprouting wheat and barley, tended for eight days, then carried out with the images of the dead god and thrown into the sea or into springs. It also describes the effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn that were buried at the Egyptian festival of sowing and taken up when the grain had sprouted, and the corn-stuffed figures of the god found in tombs, whose sprouting, Frazer says, signified his resurrection. Eliot's headnote promises that readers of Frazer will "immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies". This is the plainest of them, asked in the voice of a man talking to a neighbour about his bulbs.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. The first sentence carries two definitions on colons and appositives; I lost the thread at "and the “Osiris beds,”". Three sentences would carry the same facts.
2. "that ritual gone domestic and gone wrong" — a pairing for effect, and a reading given as fact.
3. "Will it bloom? Has the frost got at it?" — the poem's own questions staged back at me.
4. "it is the question of the whole poem: can anything come back?" — the bow. A claim about what the poem is, and it tells me what to feel.
5. "Stetson's suburban garden" — nothing in the lines says suburban; it is an assumption dressed as a detail.

Keep: the facts of the first sentence (the volumes, the gardens of Adonis, the Osiris beds). Also the title.

Draft:
TITLE: The buried god
BODY:
In <i>The Golden Bough</i>, in the volumes Eliot’s headnote names (<i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>), Frazer describes two rites of a god who dies and returns. The “gardens of Adonis” were pots of quick-sprouting seed, carried in the god’s funeral procession and then thrown into the sea. The “Osiris beds” were effigies of the god filled with earth and corn and buried, so that the sprouting grain showed his resurrection. The corpse planted in Stetson’s garden is usually read as a version of these rites, a body buried so that something will grow from it. The questions about blooming and frost are the ones a man asks about his bulbs.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. Frazer's own words replace "Osiris beds"; "suburban" and "the question of the whole poem" go; the headnote's promise about vegetation ceremonies added, as the scholar suggested; the bulbs sentence kept, both sides having allowed it.
TITLE: The buried god
BODY:
Frazer's <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the two volumes Eliot's headnote names (with the title garbled), describes two rites of a god who dies and returns. The "gardens of Adonis" were pots of earth sown with quick-sprouting wheat and barley, tended for eight days, then carried out with the images of the dead god and thrown into the sea or into springs. Effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn were buried at the Egyptian festival of sowing and taken up when the grain had sprouted, and corn-stuffed figures of the god have been found in tombs; their sprouting, Frazer says, signified his resurrection. Eliot's headnote promises that readers of Frazer will "immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies". This is the plainest of them, asked in the voice of a man talking to a neighbour about his bulbs.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "describes two rites of a god who dies and returns." Adonis and Osiris are two gods, treated by Frazer as parallel figures, not one; as written the sentence makes the gardens and the effigies rites of the same god. Put: "describes two rites of gods who die and return."

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "Frazer's <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the two volumes Eliot's headnote names (with the title garbled), describes" — "two volumes ... describes" trips the ear, and "garbled" sends me off to find out how. Eliot's version is on the site in the headnote, so show it. Put: "In <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the two volumes Eliot's headnote names as "Atthis Adonis Osiris", Frazer describes two rites of a god who dies and returns."
2. "Effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn were buried at the Egyptian festival of sowing and taken up when the grain had sprouted, and corn-stuffed figures of the god have been found in tombs; their sprouting, Frazer says, signified his resurrection." — three facts in one sentence, and by the time "their sprouting" arrives I have lost what "their" is. Put: "Effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn were buried at the Egyptian festival of sowing and taken up when the grain had sprouted. Corn-stuffed figures of the god have also been found in tombs. The sprouting of the grain, Frazer says, signified his resurrection."
3. "This is the plainest of them, asked in the voice of a man talking to a neighbour about his bulbs." — "this" is a reference, and a reference is not "asked". Put: "This is the plainest of them. The questions are the ones a man asks a neighbour about his bulbs."

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: "gods who die and return", since Adonis and Osiris are two. Reader: show Eliot's "Atthis Adonis Osiris" instead of "garbled"; split the Osiris sentence; "this ... asked". All taken.
TITLE: The buried god
BODY:
In <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the two volumes of <i>The Golden Bough</i> that Eliot's headnote names as "Atthis Adonis Osiris", Frazer describes two rites of gods who die and return. The "gardens of Adonis" were pots of earth sown with quick-sprouting wheat and barley, tended for eight days, then carried out with the images of the dead god and thrown into the sea or into springs. Effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn were buried at the Egyptian festival of sowing and taken up when the grain had sprouted. Corn-stuffed figures of the god have also been found in tombs. The sprouting of the grain, Frazer says, signified his resurrection. Eliot's headnote promises that readers of Frazer will "immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies". This is the plainest of them. The questions are the ones a man asks a neighbour about his bulbs.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The buried god
BODY:
In <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i>, the two volumes of <i>The Golden Bough</i> that Eliot's headnote names as "Atthis Adonis Osiris", Frazer describes two rites of gods who die and return. The "gardens of Adonis" were pots of earth sown with quick-sprouting wheat and barley, tended for eight days, then carried out with the images of the dead god and thrown into the sea or into springs. Effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn were buried at the Egyptian festival of sowing and taken up when the grain had sprouted. Corn-stuffed figures of the god have also been found in tombs. The sprouting of the grain, Frazer says, signified his resurrection. Eliot's headnote promises that readers of Frazer will "immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies". This is the plainest of them. The questions are the ones a man asks a neighbour about his bulbs.


## dog

**As it stood**

TITLE: Webster's dirge, with one word changed
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): Call for the robin-redbreast and the wren, / Since o’er shady groves they hover, / And with leaves and flowers do cover / The friendless bodies of unburied men. . . . / But keep the wolf far thence, that’s foe to men, / For with his nails he’ll dig them up again.
BODY:
Cornelia's mad song over her murdered son in *The White Devil* (1612), Act V scene iv. Eliot swaps the wolf for a dog and "foe" for "friend," and capitalises the Dog. The domestic pet, man's friend, becomes the thing that will not let the buried god stay buried long enough to sprout. Readers who want more have found the Dog Star, Sirius, whose rising announced the flooding of the Nile and the return of Osiris. The change of one word is the kind of thing Eliot meant when he wrote, in "Philip Massinger" (1920), that "immature poets imitate; mature poets steal."

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Webster, <i>The White Devil</i> (1612), Gutenberg #12915: the dirge is in Act V, scene iv, sung by Cornelia over Marcello, whom his brother Flamineo kills in V.ii ("Flamineo runs Marcello through"); stage direction, "Cornelia doth this in several forms of distraction"; the ten lines as quoted. Eliot, "Philip Massinger" (1920), <i>The Sacred Wood</i> (en.wikisource): "Immature poets imitate; mature poets steal; bad poets deface what they take, and good poets make it into something better, or at least something different." Brooks 1939 on the Dog, in archive.org full-text snippets of the Merrill Studies reprint and of <i>Notes and Queries</i>, 19 Aug. 1950: "the Dog (the capital letter is Eliot's) as Humanitarianism and the related philosophies ...". Frazer, <i>Golden Bough</i> abr. ch. XXXIX and <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i> II: the rising of Sirius, "the star of Isis", marked the rise of the Nile and the start of the sacred year. Representative Poetry Online (Toronto), note on l. 74: Sirius given as one of four readings.

Substance:
1. TITLE "Webster's dirge, with one word changed", and the last sentence, "The change of one word": wrong. Set the couplets side by side. Webster: "But keep the wolf far thence, that's foe to men, / For with his nails he'll dig them up again." Eliot: "Oh keep the Dog far hence, that's friend to men, / Or with his nails he'll dig it up again!" Five words change (wolf/Dog, thence/hence, foe/friend, For/Or, them/it) and "Oh" is added. The body itself names two changes. The title must go, and the Massinger sentence be reworded ("changes of a word here and there").
2. "Cornelia's mad song over her murdered son in <i>The White Devil</i> (1612), Act V scene iv": right. Marcello, killed by his brother Flamineo (V.ii); the stage direction has her sing "in several forms of distraction".
3. "Eliot swaps the wolf for a dog and 'foe' for 'friend,' and capitalises the Dog": right as far as it goes (see 1).
4. "The domestic pet, man's friend, becomes the thing that will not let the buried god stay buried long enough to sprout." A reading; mark it. The reading on record is Cleanth Brooks's (1939): the capitalised Dog as "Humanitarianism and the related philosophies", the well-meant modern thing that digs up what should be left to lie and grow.
5. "Readers who want more have found the Dog Star, Sirius, whose rising announced the flooding of the Nile and the return of Osiris." The Sirius reading is in several annotated editions and student guides (the Toronto RPO notes, for one). Its basis is in Frazer's own pages, where the rising of Sirius, "the star of Isis", marks the rising of the Nile and the goddess coming to wake her dead lover. "The return of Osiris" stretches that; "the flooding of the Nile" is enough. Say "some annotators" or name one.
6. Massinger: wording, essay and date are right; a 1920 text, free to quote.
7. Quote: matches Webster, the four omitted lines marked by the ellipsis. Some editions print "robin redbreast" unhyphenated; not an issue.

"the well-meant modern thing that digs up what should be left to lie" — "left to lie" sounds like letting the dead rest, which is the opposite of the point (the god has to stay buried to come back), and the phrase has a knowing tone. TITLE: Webster's dirge, altered
BODY:
Cornelia's song over her murdered son Marcello in Webster's <i>The White Devil</i> (1612), Act V scene iv. The stage direction says she sings it "in several forms of distraction". Eliot alters the couplet in six places. Webster's "But keep the wolf far thence" becomes "Oh keep the Dog far hence", the Dog with a capital; "foe to men" becomes "friend to men"; "For" becomes "Or", and "them" becomes "it". In Webster the wolf digs up the unburied dead; in Eliot the danger to the corpse in the garden is the dog, man's friend. Cleanth Brooks (1939) read the capitalised Dog as humanitarianism, the modern concern for man that digs up what was buried and so prevents its return; some annotators see the Dog Star, Sirius, whose rising marked the flooding of the Nile in the Egyptian calendar Frazer describes. Eliot's own account of such borrowing is in "Philip Massinger" (1920): "Immature poets imitate; mature poets steal; bad poets deface what they take, and good poets make it into something better, or at least something different."

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

Checked, as asked: "the modern concern for man that digs up what was buried and so prevents its return" fairly states Brooks. His words (<i>Modern Poetry and the Tradition</i>, 1939; confirmed in the archive.org text of a reprint) are that he takes the Dog, "the capital letter is Eliot's", as "Humanitarianism and the related philosophies which, in their concern for man, extirpate the supernatural—dig up the corpse of the buried god and thus prevent the rebirth of life." The rewrite keeps the concern for man, the digging up, and the rebirth prevented, and the direction is now right (the round-one "left to lie" had reversed it). What it drops is the means, "extirpate the supernatural". If the editor wants that too: "the modern concern for man that, rooting out the supernatural, digs up what was buried and so prevents its return". Not an objection.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

The six changes are clear as listed, and "digs up what was buried and so prevents its return" says what Brooks meant without the knowing tone; the scholar's check against Brooks is the only thing left, and it is not a wording matter.

**As it now stands**

## hypocrite-lecteur

**As it stood**

TITLE: The reader accused
QUOTE (source passage shown above the note): C’est l’Ennui! — l’œil chargé d’un pleur involontaire, / Il rêve d’échafauds en fumant son houka. / Tu le connais, lecteur, ce monstre délicat, / — Hypocrite lecteur, — mon semblable, — mon frère!
TRANSLATION (shown under the quote): It is Boredom! Eye charged with an involuntary tear, he dreams of scaffolds while smoking his hookah. You know him, reader, this delicate monster, hypocrite reader, my double, my brother!
BODY:
The last line of "Au Lecteur," the poem that opens *Les Fleurs du mal* (1857). Baudelaire's catalogue of vices ends with the worst, boredom, and then turns on the reader and makes him an accomplice. Eliot ends the first section by doing the same thing to you. The "you" that has been Stetson is suddenly whoever is holding the book, and the Frenchness of the line is part of the accusation: you were meant to recognise it.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: Baudelaire, "Au Lecteur", 1861 text (fr.wikisource, Les_Fleurs_du_mal/1861/Au_lecteur): stands at the head of the collection before "Bénédiction"; the "ménagerie infâme de nos vices" stanza, then "Il en est un plus laid, plus méchant, plus immonde !", then the final stanza exactly as the site quotes it, dashes included. Eliot's note 76 ("V. Baudelaire, Preface to <i>Fleurs du Mal</i>") and the 1922 punctuation of line 76 in poem-part1.md.

Substance:
1. Quote: matches the 1861 text.
2. Translation: "my double" for "mon semblable" is loose; "semblable" is one's like or fellow-creature, not a doppelgänger. "My likeness" (or "my fellow") is closer. "Boredom" for "Ennui" is the usual choice and fine.
3. "The last line of 'Au Lecteur,' the poem that opens <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i> (1857)": right; it is the prefatory poem in both the 1857 and 1861 editions, which is why Eliot's note calls it the preface.
4. "Baudelaire's catalogue of vices ends with the worst, boredom, and then turns on the reader and makes him an accomplice": right.
5. "Eliot ends the first section by doing the same thing to you. The 'you' that has been Stetson is suddenly whoever is holding the book, and the Frenchness of the line is part of the accusation: you were meant to recognise it." A standard reading, told in the second person. One fact to keep straight: in the 1922 text the line is still inside Stetson's speech, opening with a quotation mark like the lines before it and beginning "You!"; the turn to the reader comes through Baudelaire's line, not through a change of addressee in the text. "You were meant to recognise it" can be grounded in Eliot's 1950 remark about giving references in the notes so that a reader who recognised an allusion would know he was meant to (see london-bridge-dead).
6. Nothing missing.

Draft:
TITLE: The reader accused
BODY: The last line of "Au Lecteur", the prefatory poem of <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i> (1857), which Eliot's note calls its preface. Baudelaire's catalogue of vices ends with the ugliest of them, Ennui, and then turns on the reader as its accomplice. In the 1922 text the line is still Stetson's, inside the quotation marks and opening with "You!"; but Baudelaire's line is addressed to the reader, and a reader who knows it is addressed too. Eliot said in 1950 that he gave his references in the notes so that a reader who recognised an allusion would know he was meant to.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Eliot ends the first section by doing the same thing to you." — the "you" is the poem's, so it is arguable, but from here the note keeps addressing me.
2. "the Frenchness of the line is part of the accusation: you were meant to recognise it." — the colon flourish and the bow; a reading given as fact, and it tells me what I was meant to do.
3. "whoever is holding the book" — dramatised. "The reader" says it.

Keep: "The last line of “Au Lecteur,” the poem that opens <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i> (1857). Baudelaire's catalogue of vices ends with the worst, boredom, and then turns on the reader and makes him an accomplice." Also the title.

Draft:
TITLE: The reader accused
BODY:
The last line of “Au Lecteur,” the poem that opens <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i> (1857). Baudelaire’s catalogue of vices ends with the worst of them, boredom, and then turns on the reader and makes him an accomplice. Eliot ends Part I with the same turn. The “you” who has been Stetson becomes the reader of the poem. One reading is that the line is left in French on purpose, since only a reader who knows Baudelaire will recognise the charge.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. The second-person ending goes; the scholar's point that the line is still Stetson's in the 1922 text is added, with Eliot's 1950 remark about the notes in place of "you were meant to recognise it". The translation's "my double" becomes "my likeness" (an accuracy change to the trans field).

Verdict: not yet

1. "Eliot said in 1950 that his notes were there so that a reader who recognised an allusion would know it was meant." That widens the 1950 sentence into an account of what the notes as a whole were for. Eliot's sentence is about the references he gave in them ("I gave the references in my notes, in order to ..."), and in 1956, in "The Frontiers of Criticism", he gave a different account of the notes as a whole (added to fill out the volume; "bogus scholarship"). The line-63 note has the subject right; this one should too, in its own words. Put: "Eliot said in 1950 that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised an allusion would know it was meant."

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: The reader accused
TRANS: It is Boredom! Eye charged with an involuntary tear, he dreams of scaffolds while smoking his hookah. You know him, reader, this delicate monster, hypocrite reader, my likeness, my brother!
BODY:
The last line of "Au Lecteur", the poem that opens <i>Les Fleurs du mal</i> (1857) and that Eliot's note calls its preface. Baudelaire's catalogue of vices ends with the ugliest of them, Ennui, and then turns on the reader as an accomplice. In the 1922 text the line is spoken to Stetson: it is the last line of the speech that begins at line 69, inside its quotation marks, and it opens with "You!". But Baudelaire's line is spoken to the reader, and a reader who knows it is addressed as well. Eliot said in 1950 that he gave the references in his notes so that a reader who recognised an allusion would know it was meant.


## The scholar's summary, round 1

1. dog: the title and last sentence say "one word changed"; Eliot changes five (wolf/Dog, thence/hence, foe/friend, For/Or, them/it) and adds "Oh". Act V scene iv, Cornelia, Marcello: all right.
2. stetson: "a trade war between two commercial empires" and "all wars are the one war" are Cleanth Brooks's reading (1939, after Matthiessen 1935) given as history; the meeting is in King William Street, not "on a bridge". Eliot's reported denial ("any superior bank clerk ... in a bowler hat") comes from a TLS letter of 11 May 1973, cited by Childs (1988); usable only as a report.
3. london-bridge-dead: "damned twice over ... merely born too early" misstates Dante (the neutrals are refused by Hell itself; Limbo has no torment; its souls lacked baptism, and include Saladin and Averroes). The "half past eight" is invented. Eliot's own "my city clerks trooping over London Bridge from the railway station to their offices" (1950) is the source to cite.
4. woolnoth: Eliot's day at the bank began at 9.15 (letter to his mother, March 1917), so the clock did not strike the start of it; the reburial was in the City of London Cemetery at Manor Park; the poem's crowd does not come out of the Tube. "Across the road" and the dates are right; drop the department from "1917 to 1925".
5. corpse: "Osiris beds" is not Frazer's phrase (he writes "effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn", "corn-stuffed effigies"); "suburban" is not in the poem; "the question of the whole poem" is a reading.
6. unreal-city: the 1950 quotation is longer than the rights rule allows and lacks its source ("What Dante Means to Me"); "every working morning" overstates. Lines 207 and 377 are right in the site's count.
7. hypocrite-lecteur: "my double" should be "my likeness" for "mon semblable"; the body otherwise stands, with the note that in 1922 the line is still Stetson's.


## The reader's summary, round 1

What I threw out most often was the last sentence. Six of the seven notes end on a quotable line ("and the dead"; "actually looks like"; "come up out of it every morning"; "an American hat, on a bridge in London"; "can anything come back?"; "you were meant to recognise it"), and in every case the sentence before it was the useful one. Second, readings presented as facts, usually in pairs: "damned twice over", "gone domestic and gone wrong", "all wars are the one war". I asked for these to be marked as readings or dropped. The facts in the batch are good and I kept all of them; the notes on Dante, Woolnoth and Webster are at their best where they say what the source is and what happens in it, and the drafts mostly cut back to that. Two small things for the editor: the title of "dog" says one word changed when the body counts two; and three notes in a row (unreal-city, london-bridge-dead, woolnoth) each tell me that Eliot worked at Lloyds Bank, so woolnoth should carry the full fact and the other two a clause.



# Batch 4: library notes, voices, drafts records


## source chaucer

**As it stood**

TITLE: The Canterbury Tales, General Prologue
AUTHOR: Geoffrey Chaucer   DATE: c. 1387   LANGUAGE: Middle English   KIND: poem
WHAT: The kind April that Eliot's first line contradicts.
PASSAGE: Whan that Aprill with his shoures soote / The droghte of March hath perced to the roote, / And bathed every veyne in swich licour / Of which vertu engendred is the flour; / Whan Zephirus eek with his sweete breeth / Inspired hath in every holt and heeth / The tendre croppes, and the yonge sonne / Hath in the Ram his half cours yronne, / And smale foweles maken melodye, / That slepen al the nyght with open ye / (So priketh hem Nature in hir corages), / Thanne longen folk to goon on pilgrimages.
TRANSLATION: When April with its sweet showers has pierced the drought of March to the root and bathed every vein in the liquid whose power engenders the flower; when Zephyrus with his sweet breath has quickened the tender shoots in every wood and heath, and the young sun has run half his course in the Ram, and small birds make melody that sleep all night with open eye (so Nature pricks them in their hearts), then people long to go on pilgrimages.
NOTE:
English poetry's first great spring, in which rain ends drought, sap rises and the whole country wants to travel. Eliot's poem also ends in a kind of pilgrimage, to the Chapel Perilous, but its April breeds lilacs out of dead land and its people would rather have stayed under the snow.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: General Prologue 1–12 against Skeat's text (Gutenberg #22120); the site prints the Riverside spelling ("Aprill", "soote"), which is fine, and the twelve lines and the translation are accurate. Eliot's Part V headnote ("the approach to the Chapel Perilous") in the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321). The date c. 1387 is the usual one.
Substance:
1. "English poetry's first great spring": not so. The spring opening is a convention Chaucer inherited. "Sumer is icumen in" (c. 1250) and the Harley lyric "Lenten ys come with love to toune" (c. 1300) are earlier English springs, and the Prologue's own opening follows Guido delle Colonne and Boccaccio. "The most famous spring opening in English poetry" is defensible; "first" is a flourish that misstates.
2. "Eliot's poem also ends in a kind of pilgrimage, to the Chapel Perilous": the poem does not end there. The chapel is in Part V (Eliot's headnote names "the approach to the Chapel Perilous"; the empty chapel is at site lines 388–391), and the thunder, the Fisher King and the fragments follow it. Say "Part V has a journey of its own, to the Chapel Perilous."
3. The comparison with Chaucer is not in Eliot's notes. It is the commentators' (Southam; Ricks and McCue), and it should be introduced as such so a reader knows what kind of claim it is.
4. "its people would rather have stayed under the snow": a reading of lines 5–7, reasonable, but a reading. Fine if kept plain ("the 'we' of lines 5–7 were kept warm by winter").
5. "the whole country wants to travel": a paraphrase of "Thanne longen folk to goon on pilgrimages"; Chaucer's folk go to a shrine, not travelling for its own sake. Minor.
Draft:
Body: The opening of the General Prologue is the spring that Eliot's first line is usually set against: April's showers end the drought of March, the sap rises, the birds sing, and people long to go on pilgrimage. Eliot's notes do not mention Chaucer; the comparison is the commentators' (Southam; Ricks and McCue), and it is hard to avoid. In the poem April breeds lilacs out of dead land, and the "we" of lines 5–7 were kept warm by the snow. Part V has a journey of its own, to the Chapel Perilous of the Grail romances (Eliot's headnote to Part V).

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "English poetry's first great spring, in which rain ends drought, sap rises and the whole country wants to travel." A fragment for an opener, a superlative I cannot check, and a triad for rhythm. I want to be told what Chaucer's lines say, not sold them.
2. "its people would rather have stayed under the snow" — a witty last clause that puts a wish into the poem's people. The poem says winter kept them warm; say that and give the lines.
3. WHAT: "The kind April that Eliot's first line contradicts." "contradicts" tells me Eliot meant it. Nothing in the note says whether that is known or a reading. "is usually read against" is safer unless the scholar knows more.
4. "Eliot's poem also ends in a kind of pilgrimage" — "a kind of" hedges what could be said plainly: there is a journey to a chapel in Part V.
Keep: nothing as it stands; every fact survives in the draft.
Draft:
WHAT: The April of sweet showers that Eliot's first line answers.
NOTE: Chaucer's Prologue opens with April rain ending the drought of March, sap rising, birds singing and people setting out on pilgrimage. Eliot's first line is usually read against it. In his opening it is winter that kept people warm (lines 5–7) and April that is cruel. The poem too has a journey to a chapel, in Part V.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "English poetry's first great spring" and "ends in a kind of pilgrimage" go (both wrong, as the scholar showed); the comparison marked as the commentators'; the reader's sentence form kept.
WHAT: The April of sweet showers that Eliot's first line answers.
NOTE: The opening of the General Prologue is the spring that Eliot's first line is usually set against: April's showers end the drought of March, the sap rises, the birds sing, and people long to go on pilgrimage. Eliot's notes do not mention Chaucer; the comparison is the commentators' (Southam; Ricks and McCue). In the poem April breeds lilacs out of dead land, and the "we" of lines 5–7 were kept warm by winter's snow. Part V has a journey of its own, to the Chapel Perilous of the Grail romances, which Eliot's headnote to that part names.

**Scholar, round 2**

satisfied. Round one asked for four things: drop "first great spring," drop the pilgrimage that "ends" at the Chapel Perilous, say the comparison is not Eliot's, and mark the reading as one. The new text does all four, and "lines 5–6" is right. Minor and optional: "birds singing through the night" is an inference; Chaucer's birds "maken melodye" and "slepen al the nyght with open ye" (GP 9–10). "Birds that sleep all night with open eye," or simply "birds singing," would be closer.

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: The Canterbury Tales, General Prologue
AUTHOR: Geoffrey Chaucer
DATE: c. 1387
WHAT: Chaucer’s April of sweet showers and pilgrimages, behind Eliot’s “cruellest month.”
LINES: 1
NOTE: The Prologue opens with April rain ending the drought of March, sap rising, birds singing through the night and people longing to go on pilgrimage. Eliot’s notes say nothing of Chaucer; readers have long set “April is the cruellest month” against these lines, in which the same month and the same rain are welcome. In Eliot’s poem it is winter that “kept us warm” (lines 5–6).

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “birds singing through the night”.
Reason: an inference. Chaucer’s birds “maken melodye” and “slepen al the nyght with open ye” (General Prologue 9–10); “birds singing” would be closer.

**As it now stands**

The front-matter thread's text, as it now stands. With a proposal from Part I in shared-proposals-part1.md.

## source ecclesiastes

**As it stood**

TITLE: Ecclesiastes 12
AUTHOR: The Bible (King James Version)   DATE: 3rd century BC; translation 1611   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: scripture
WHAT: "the cricket no relief": the chapter on old age and death.
PASSAGE: Also when they shall be afraid of that which is high, and fears shall be in the way, and the almond tree shall flourish, and the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail: because man goeth to his long home, and the mourners go about the streets: or ever the silver cord be loosed, or the golden bowl be broken, or the pitcher be broken at the fountain, or the wheel broken at the cistern. Then shall the dust return to the earth as it was: and the spirit shall return unto God who gave it.
NOTE:
Eliot's note sends the reader to verse 5, where "the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail." The rest of the chapter, with its broken pitcher, its dry cistern and its dust, could be a plan for Part V.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Ecclesiastes 12:5–7, KJV, en.wikisource: the passage as displayed is word for word. Eliot's note 23 ("Cf. Ecclesiastes XII, v.") in work/poem-part1.md. Site line 385, "And voices singing out of empty cisterns and exhausted wells," counted in the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321).
Substance:
1. "its dry cistern": there is no dry cistern in Ecclesiastes 12. Verse 6 has "the wheel broken at the cistern"; the drought is the poem's (line 385, "empty cisterns and exhausted wells"). The note has read Part V back into the chapter.
2. "could be a plan for Part V": a reading, unattributed, and loose: the chapter's dust that returns to the earth (12:7) has no counterpart in Part V, whose dust is Part I's (line 30). What can be said plainly is that the broken pitcher and the wheel at the cistern (12:6) can be set beside line 385.
3. Missing, one clause: the King James text (like the Vulgate's "locusta" and the Revised Version) has a grasshopper; the cricket is Eliot's word. The WHAT line quotes "the cricket no relief" against a passage that has no cricket, so a reader will look for one. The grasshopper is a burden because the old can carry nothing (12:1–7 is an allegory of the body's decay); the note might say so.
4. Date "3rd century BC": the usual scholarly dating (c. 250–225 BC). Fine.
Draft:
Body: Eliot's note sends the reader to verse 5, in the chapter's allegory of old age: the grasshopper is a burden because the old can carry nothing, and desire fails. The cricket is Eliot's word; the King James Bible, like the Vulgate, has a grasshopper. The verses that follow, with the golden bowl broken and "the wheel broken at the cistern," can be set beside Part V's "empty cisterns and exhausted wells" (line 385).

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "could be a plan for Part V" — a quotable close that asks me to admire the connection rather than see it. "with its broken pitcher, its dry cistern and its dust" is a triad built for the sentence; the passage says the wheel is broken at the cistern, not that the cistern is dry.
2. The note never says the plain thing: Eliot's cricket is this verse's grasshopper. The WHAT sets the two side by side; the body should say it.
3. Nothing tells me why a grasshopper should be a burden. If the scholar can say in a clause what the verse is taken to mean, this is the place for it.
Keep: "Eliot's note sends the reader to verse 5, where "the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail.""
Draft:
NOTE: Eliot's note sends the reader to verse 5, where "the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail." The chapter is a picture of old age and death. Eliot's cricket that gives no relief is the Bible's grasshopper that has become a burden. The chapter's later verses, with the pitcher broken at the fountain and the dust returning to the earth, are close to the imagery of Part V.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Its dry cistern" is not in the chapter and "a plan for Part V" goes; the cricket given as Eliot's word; the wheel at the cistern set beside line 385, which the scholar counted.
NOTE: Eliot's note points to verse 5, in the chapter's picture of old age, where the grasshopper is a burden because the old can carry nothing, and desire fails. The cricket is Eliot's word; the King James Bible has a grasshopper. The verses that follow, with the golden bowl broken and "the wheel broken at the cistern," can be set beside Part V's "empty cisterns and exhausted wells" (line 385).

**Scholar, round 2**

satisfied. Round one: no "dry cistern" in the chapter; the cricket is Eliot's word; the Part V link as a reading. All three made. "Pitcher broken at the fountain" and "wheel broken at the cistern" are 12:6 word for word, and line 385 is right in the every-line count.

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: Ecclesiastes 12
AUTHOR: The Bible (King James Version)
DATE: 3rd century BC; translation 1611
WHAT: “the cricket no relief”: the chapter on old age and death.
LINES: 23
NOTE: Eliot’s note on line 23 cites verse 5, where “the grasshopper shall be a burden, and desire shall fail”; the poem has a cricket in the grasshopper’s place. The chapter is an allegory of old age and death, and its pitcher broken at the fountain and wheel broken at the cistern have been heard again in the “empty cisterns and exhausted wells” of line 385.

Nothing to add: both sides satisfied with the text as it stands.

## source tristan

**As it stood**

TITLE: Tristan und Isolde
AUTHOR: Richard Wagner   DATE: 1865 (first performed Munich)   LANGUAGE: German   KIND: opera
WHAT: The sailor's song (lines 31–34) and the shepherd's "Öd und leer das Meer" (line 42).
PASSAGE: Westwärts / schweift der Blick; / ostwärts / streicht das Schiff. / Frisch weht der Wind / der Heimat zu: / mein irisch Kind, / wo weilest du? / Sind’s deiner Seufzer Wehen, / die mir die Segel blähen? / Wehe, wehe, du Wind! / Weh, ach wehe, mein Kind! / Irische Maid, / du wilde, minnige Maid! /  / . . . /  / HIRT: Öd und leer das Meer!
TRANSLATION: Westward the gaze wanders; eastward the ship runs. Fresh blows the wind toward home: my Irish child, where do you linger? Is it the breath of your sighs that fills my sails? Woe, woe, you wind! Woe, ah woe, my child! Irish maid, you wild, lovely maid! . . . SHEPHERD: Desolate and empty the sea!
NOTE:
Eliot's note cites the opera by act and verse as if it were a book, and it was: the libretto was sold and read. The young sailor's song is the first thing sung in Act I, from the masthead, unseen, above the deck where Isolde lies; the shepherd's four words in Act III answer Kurwenal's question whether any ship is in sight while Tristan lies dying. Between the two quotations the poem puts its hyacinth garden, which is to say that the garden happens between a ship setting out and a ship not arriving. Eliot heard *Tristan* in Boston in 1909, as a Harvard senior, and wrote a poem called "Opera" about it; Stravinsky, who met him in 1956, came away thinking that *Tristan* "must have been one of the most passionate experiences of his life." Ludwig II, who paid for the first performance, drowned in the Starnberger See of line 8.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: The libretto at Projekt Gutenberg-DE (projekt-gutenberg.org, Wagner, Tristan und Isolde, chapters 2 and 4): the sailor's song as displayed is word for word, under the direction "Stimme eines jungen Seemannes, aus der Höhe, wie vom Mast her, vernehmbar"; "Frisch weht der Wind" to "wo weilest du?" are lines 5–8 of the song; in Act III "Öd und leer das Meer!" is the 24th verse (the shepherd's 4 lines, Kurwenal's 8, the shepherd's 6, Kurwenal's 5, then it), spoken after Kurwenal's "Sahst du noch nichts? Kein Schiff noch auf der See?" and "siehst du ein Schiff, so spiele lustig und hell!" So Eliot's "I, verses 5–8" and "III, verse 24" both count from a printed libretto. Premiere 10 June 1865, Munich Hof- und Nationaltheater, Hans von Bülow conducting, mounted under Ludwig II's patronage; composed 1857–59 (Wikipedia, Tristan und Isolde). Ludwig's death (Wikipedia, Ludwig II): 13 June 1886, Lake Starnberg, "officially ruled a suicide by drowning"; no water found in the lungs at autopsy. Stravinsky: Jewel Spears Brooker in Time Present 101 (Summer 2020), quoting "Memories of T. S. Eliot," Esquire, 1 August 1965, p. 92, and citing "Opera," dated Nov. 1909, at Ricks and McCue, Poems I.236, commentary I.1078. Eliot's degrees: Britannica ("received a B.A. in 1909, after three years"; 1909–10 "an assistant in philosophy at Harvard"), Wikipedia (M.A. 1910). Metropolitan Opera archives: Tristan at the Boston Theatre 11 April 1908, no Boston Tristan in 1909 or early 1910; the Boston Opera Company opened 8 November 1909 with La Gioconda (Wikipedia).
Substance:
1. "the shepherd's four words": five. "Öd und leer das Meer" is five words, and so is Eliot's "Oed' und leer das Meer." A count that is wrong; drop it.
2. "Eliot heard Tristan in Boston in 1909, as a Harvard senior": neither half is secure. The evidence is the poem "Opera," dated November 1909 in his notebook, which opens with the words "Tristan and Isolde"; no performance is documented. The Metropolitan's Boston Tristan was in April 1908, with none in 1909, and the Boston Opera Company had only just opened (8 November 1909, with La Gioconda). Brooker, writing from the Hale letters, says Eliot "attended a performance (or heard an orchestral arrangement) of Tristan und Isolde in Boston" in October 1909. Say what is known: the poem and its date. "Senior": Eliot took his AB in June 1909 after three years and spent 1909–10 in the Graduate School as an assistant in philosophy, taking the AM in 1910 (Britannica; Wikipedia). He remained a member of the class of 1910, which is presumably why Brooker calls him a senior; "as a Harvard student" is safe.
3. "wrote a poem called 'Opera' about it": fair. Add where it is: first printed in Inventions of the March Hare (1996), not in the Collected Poems, so the reader knows why it is unfamiliar.
4. "Stravinsky, who met him in 1956, came away thinking that Tristan 'must have been one of the most passionate experiences of his life'": exact, and the meeting was December 1956 in London. Give the source ("Memories of T. S. Eliot," Esquire, August 1965). The words are Stravinsky's, so the rights rule does not bite.
5. "Ludwig II, who paid for the first performance, drowned in the Starnberger See of line 8": he was found dead in the lake on 13 June 1886, with his doctor; the official verdict was suicide by drowning, the autopsy found no water in the lungs, and the death is disputed. Batch 1's starnbergersee note is being corrected to "found dead"; this note should say the same. "Paid for" is fair. The poem prints "Starnbergersee" as one word; quote it as the site does.
6. "the garden happens between a ship setting out and a ship not arriving": a reading, and the first half misdescribes Act I. The ship is in mid-crossing, carrying Isolde from Ireland to Cornwall ("ostwärts streicht das Schiff"; Brangäne answers that they will make land by evening). In Act III Isolde's ship does arrive, too late. If the point is kept: a ship under way and a ship not yet in sight.
7. "Eliot's note cites the opera by act and verse as if it were a book, and it was: the libretto was sold and read": right, and the numbers fit a printed libretto exactly (see Checked). Worth stating as fact rather than as a quip, since it shows Eliot counting lines on a page.
8. "the first thing sung in Act I, from the masthead, unseen, above the deck where Isolde lies": right. The direction has the voice "from above, as if from the mast"; Isolde lies on a couch in a curtained cabin on the foredeck. The sailor sings the "Frisch weht der Wind" lines a second time in scene 2.
9. "answer Kurwenal's question whether any ship is in sight while Tristan lies dying": right; Kurwenal asks "Kein Schiff noch auf der See?" and tells him to pipe a merry tune if he sights one, and the shepherd looks out to sea before answering.
10. DATE "1865 (first performed Munich)": right; 10 June 1865, Hof- und Nationaltheater, Bülow conducting; composed 1857–59.
Draft:
Body: Eliot's note cites the opera by act and line, as from a printed libretto, and the numbers fit one: "Frisch weht der Wind" begins the fifth line of the sailor's song, and the shepherd's "Öd und leer das Meer" is the twenty-fourth line of Act III. The song is the first thing sung after the prelude, by a young sailor heard "from above, as if from the mast," over the curtained cabin on the foredeck where Isolde lies; the ship is carrying her from Ireland to Cornwall to marry King Marke. In Act III Tristan lies wounded in Brittany, waiting for Isolde's ship. Kurwenal tells the shepherd to pipe a merry tune if he sights a sail; the shepherd looks out to sea and answers that it is empty. The poem puts the hyacinth garden between the two quotations. Eliot's poem "Opera," dated November 1909 in his notebook and first printed in <i>Inventions of the March Hare</i> (1996), records a <i>Tristan</i> heard in Boston that autumn. Stravinsky, who met Eliot in December 1956, wrote in 1965 that "Tristan must have been one of the most passionate experiences of his life" ("Memories of T. S. Eliot," <i>Esquire</i>, August 1965). The first performance, at the Munich Court Theatre on 10 June 1865, was paid for by Ludwig II of Bavaria, who was found dead in the Starnbergersee of line 8 in June 1886.

## source baudelaire-sept-vieillards

**As it stood**

TITLE: Les Sept Vieillards (from Les Fleurs du mal)
AUTHOR: Charles Baudelaire   DATE: 1859; in the 1861 edition of Les Fleurs du mal   LANGUAGE: French   KIND: poem
WHAT: "Unreal City."
PASSAGE: Fourmillante cité, cité pleine de rêves, / Où le spectre en plein jour raccroche le passant! / Les mystères partout coulent comme des sèves / Dans les canaux étroits du colosse puissant.
TRANSLATION: Swarming city, city full of dreams, where the ghost in broad daylight accosts the passer-by! Mysteries everywhere flow like sap in the narrow channels of the mighty giant.
NOTE:
The poet, walking through a yellow fog in a Paris morning, meets a hideous old man, then another exactly like him, and another, seven in all, and flees home half mad. Eliot's note quotes only the first two lines, and the whole "Unreal City" passage is built on their method: a real street, and a ghost on it at nine in the morning.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: stands
Checked: The poem in Gutenberg #6099 (Les Fleurs du mal, French text): the stanza as displayed is exact. The fog: "Un brouillard sale et jaune inondait tout l'espace"; the morning: "Un matin"; the count: "je comptai sept fois"; the retreat: "Je rentrai, je fermai ma porte, épouvanté, / Malade et morfondu, l'esprit fiévreux et trouble." fr.wikisource confirms the 1861 placement in "Tableaux parisiens" and the dedication "À Victor Hugo"; the Revue contemporaine printing of 15 September 1859 is from knowledge. Eliot's note 60 in work/poem-part1.md quotes the first two lines only.
Substance:
1. The summary of the poem is accurate: a dirty yellow fog, a morning in a faubourg, an old man, then his double, seven in all, and the poet home with the door shut and his reason adrift.
2. "the whole 'Unreal City' passage is built on their method: a real street, and a ghost on it at nine in the morning": a reading, though one Eliot's own account supports. In "What Dante Means to Me" (1950) he quoted these two lines as what he had learned from Baudelaire, "the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric," and said he knew what they meant because he had lived it (the passage is well known; I could not reach the printed text today, only the phrase). If the sentence is kept, attach it to that; otherwise mark it as the site's.
3. Missing and cheap to add: the poem's first printing (Revue contemporaine, 15 September 1859) and its dedication to Victor Hugo; the DATE line half gives the first.
4. TRANSLATION: "accosts" for "raccroche" is right, the verb of a streetwalker soliciting; "mighty giant" for "colosse puissant" is fine.
Draft: none. If the "method" sentence is kept: "Eliot quoted these two lines in 1950 as the model for what he had learned from Baudelaire, 'the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric'; the 'Unreal City' passage is a street at nine in the morning with a ghost on it."

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

NOTE: In the poem the speaker, walking through a dirty yellow fog on a Paris morning, meets a hideous old man, then a second exactly like him, then more, seven in all, and goes home and shuts his door, sick and half out of his mind. The poem was first printed in the <i>Revue contemporaine</i> on 15 September 1859 and is dedicated to Victor Hugo. Eliot's note quotes only its first two lines, and in 1950 he quoted the same two lines as what he had learned from Baudelaire, "the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric". The fog and the daylight ghost both reappear in the London passage, as the brown fog of line 61 and the crowd of the dead at lines 62–63.

**Scholar, round 2**

satisfied. Round one had nothing against the substance; the "method" sentence is now a reading marked as one. "Dirty yellow fog" is the poem's "brouillard sale et jaune"; lines 69–70 are right in the every-line count; Mylae (260 BC, First Punic War) is rightly called a battle.

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

AUTHOR: Charles Baudelaire

WHAT: “Unreal City”: the swarming Paris of “The Seven Old Men.”
LINES: 60
NOTE: The poet, walking through a dirty yellow fog on a Paris morning, meets a hideous old man, then another exactly like him, and another, seven in all, and flees home half mad. Eliot’s note on line 60 quotes the first two lines. Stetson, hailed on King William Street at nine in the morning as a shipmate from the battle of Mylae (lines 69–70), has been read as Eliot’s version of Baudelaire’s daylight ghost.

Nothing to add: both sides satisfied with the text as it stands.

LINES: 60

Eliot’s note on line 60 quotes the first two lines. Stetson, hailed on King William Street at nine in the morning as a shipmate from the battle of Mylae (lines 69–70), has been read as Eliot’s version of Baudelaire’s daylight ghost.


## source baudelaire-au-lecteur

**As it stood**

TITLE: Au Lecteur (the preface poem of Les Fleurs du mal)
AUTHOR: Charles Baudelaire   DATE: 1857   LANGUAGE: French   KIND: poem
WHAT: "hypocrite lecteur!—mon semblable,—mon frère!"
PASSAGE: C’est l’Ennui! — l’œil chargé d’un pleur involontaire, / Il rêve d’échafauds en fumant son houka. / Tu le connais, lecteur, ce monstre délicat, / — Hypocrite lecteur, — mon semblable, — mon frère!
TRANSLATION: It is Boredom! Its eye charged with an involuntary tear, it dreams of scaffolds while smoking its hookah. You know it, reader, this delicate monster, hypocrite reader, my double, my brother!
NOTE:
Baudelaire opens his book by listing the sins of mankind, saving the worst for last, boredom, and then turning on whoever is reading. Eliot closes his first section the same way. He said in 1930 that Baudelaire was "the greatest exemplar in modern poetry in any language," and in 1950 that he had learned from him "the possibility of fusion between the sordidly realistic and the phantasmagoric."

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Gutenberg #6099: the stanza as displayed is exact ("C'est l'Ennui!--L'oeil chargé d'un pleur involontaire ... --Hypocrite lecteur,--mon semblable,--mon frère!"), and the stanza before it makes Ennui the worst vice ("Il en est un plus laid, plus méchant, plus immonde!"). First printing 1 June 1855 in the Revue des deux mondes, at the head of the eighteen poems that first carried the title Les Fleurs du mal (fr.wikisource, "Les Fleurs du mal (Revue des Deux Mondes)"). The 1930 sentence, "Baudelaire is indeed the greatest exemplar in modern poetry in any language," is from "Baudelaire," Eliot's introduction to Christopher Isherwood's translation of the Intimate Journals (1930), reprinted in Selected Essays; the 1950 phrase is from "What Dante Means to Me" (Italian Institute, London, 4 July 1950; in To Criticize the Critic). 3. "saving the worst for last, boredom": right. "Boredom" is the usual English for Ennui but weaker than the French; the translation's capitalised "Boredom" might simply be "Ennui."
4. TRANSLATION: "my double" for "mon semblable" is a shade strong; "my like" or "my fellow" is closer. Minor.
5. "Eliot closes his first section the same way": fair.

## source death-of-st-narcissus

**As it stood**

TITLE: The Death of Saint Narcissus
AUTHOR: T. S. Eliot   DATE: 1915 (withdrawn from publication)   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: poem
WHAT: "Come in under the shadow of this red rock."
PASSAGE: Come under the shadow of this gray rock— / Come in under the shadow of this gray rock,
NOTE:
Set in type for *Poetry* (Chicago) in 1915 and withdrawn at proof stage, the poem about a saint who wanted to be a tree, a fish and a girl, and died pierced with arrows, survived among the manuscripts Eliot sent to John Quinn in 1922 along with the *Waste Land* drafts. Its opening goes on, in the cadence the later poem keeps, to promise the listener something other than his own shadow on the sand or against the fire: the saint's bloody cloth and limbs. The gray rock's shadow, made red, became the poem's first prophecy. The poem is still in copyright, so only its first two lines are given here.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: The poem's text (faculty.wiu.edu PDF, the Poems Written in Early Youth text as far as I can tell). Lines 1–7: "Come under the shadow of this gray rock— / Come in under the shadow of this gray rock, / And I will show you something different from either / Your shadow sprawling over the sand at daybreak, or / Your shadow leaping behind the fire against the red rock: / I will show you his bloody cloth and limbs / And the gray shadow on his lips." Later: "First he was sure that he had been a tree," "Then he knew that he had been a fish," "Then he had been a young girl," "So he became a dancer to God, / Because his flesh was in love with the burning arrows." Publication history from Wikipedia (John Davy Hayward) and the abstracts of Gottlieb's and the Illinois articles: set up for Poetry in 1915; galley proof in the Harriet Monroe papers at Chicago; first printed in Poems Written in Early Youth (twelve copies, 1950; Faber trade edition 1967); Valerie Eliot, "may have been early in 1915," Eliot could not remember. The facsimile itself not reachable.
Substance:
1. "a saint who wanted to be a tree, a fish and a girl": the poem says he had been them, or knew he had: "he was sure that he had been a tree," "he knew that he had been a fish," "he had been a young girl." Past lives, not wishes.
2. "The gray rock's shadow, made red, became the poem's first prophecy": the red rock is already in the 1915 poem, in its fifth line, behind the fire ("against the red rock"). What The Waste Land changes is the rock of shelter, grey to red, in the line it borrows. "The poem's first prophecy" is a reading (the "I will show you" is a prophet's formula); say so or drop it.
3. "Set in type for Poetry (Chicago) in 1915 and withdrawn at proof stage": right; the galley survives in the Poetry papers at Chicago, and Hayward printed from it. Missing: that first printing (Poems Written in Early Youth, twelve copies, 1950; trade edition 1967), which tells the reader where the text comes from and why it is little known. Batch 1's red-rock draft says "first printed in 1950"; this note should agree with it.
4. "survived among the manuscripts Eliot sent to John Quinn in 1922 along with the Waste Land drafts": right in substance. The two manuscripts are in the Berg Collection with the Waste Land papers and are reproduced in the 1971 facsimile.
5. DATE "1915": Valerie Eliot's "may have been early in 1915," with Eliot unable to remember; "c. 1915" is the honest form.
6. "Its opening goes on ... to promise the listener something other than his own shadow on the sand or against the fire: the saint's bloody cloth and limbs": accurate.
7. "The poem is still in copyright, so only its first two lines are given here": right (first published 1950 and 1967), and the rights rule allows a phrase or two more, no passage.
Draft:
Body: Eliot could not remember when he wrote the poem; Valerie Eliot puts it early in 1915. It was set in type for <i>Poetry</i> (Chicago) that year and not published. The galley proof survives in the magazine's papers, and John Hayward printed the poem from it in <i>Poems Written in Early Youth</i> (twelve copies, 1950; trade edition 1967). Two manuscripts went to John Quinn with the <i>Waste Land</i> drafts in 1922 and are reproduced in the 1971 facsimile. The saint of the title has been, or believes he has been, a tree, a fish and a girl; he becomes a dancer before God and dies pierced by arrows. Its first seven lines are the source of lines 26–30 of Part I: the rock of shelter is grey there, and the speaker promises something different from two shadows, one sprawling over the sand at daybreak, one leaping behind a fire against a red rock, and then the saint's bloody cloth and limbs. The poem is in copyright, so only its first two lines are given here.

2. "in the cadence the later poem keeps" — an aside for the admirer, and it stands between "goes on" and what the opening goes on to do.

Its opening gave the poem lines 25–26, and what follows in the earlier poem runs parallel to lines 27–30: the speaker promises to show the listener something other than his own shadow on the sand or against the fire, and shows him the saint's bloody cloth and limbs. The poem is still in copyright, so only its first two lines are given here.
