## source huxley-crome-yellow

**As it stood**

TITLE: Crome Yellow, chapter 27
AUTHOR: Aldous Huxley   DATE: 1921   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: novel
WHAT: "Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana": the name behind Madame Sosostris.
PASSAGE: Mr. Scogan had been accommodated in a little canvas hut. Dressed in a black skirt and a red bodice, with a yellow-and-red bandana handkerchief tied round his black wig, he looked—sharp-nosed, brown, and wrinkled—like the Bohemian Hag of Frith’s Derby Day. A placard pinned to the curtain of the doorway announced the presence within the tent of “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana.”
NOTE:
Huxley's country-house satire came out in November 1921, while Eliot was at Margate and Lausanne; the cynical Mr. Scogan telling fortunes in drag at a village fête is the immediate source of the "famous clairvoyante." Huxley had been at Garsington with Eliot, and the two knew each other's work.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Gutenberg #1999: the passage is exact and opens chapter XXVII; Huxley's event is "the Fair," the annual charity fair in the park at Crome, for the hospital; Scogan reads palms with a magnifying glass, not cards. Wikipedia (Crome Yellow): written in the summer of 1921 at Forte dei Marmi, published by Chatto & Windus in November 1921. Grover Smith, "The Fortuneteller in Eliot's Waste Land," American Literature 25 (January 1954), 490–92, reporting Eliot's letter of 10 March 1952: I could not read the article; the date and the words "almost certain" come from summaries of it. Rainey, Revisiting The Waste Land (2005), chapter 1 (ebin.pub copy): Crome Yellow "completed only in August 1921"; the typescripts of Parts I and II assigned to 9–22 May 1921 on the evidence of the paper and typewriter of Eliot's letters of those weeks, the "London Letter" of May 1921, and his telling Quinn on 9 May that the poem was "partly on paper." Eliot's movements (Margate about 22 October to 12 November 1921, then London and Paris, Lausanne from about 22 November to early January) from the facsimile introduction and the Letters, from knowledge.
Substance:
1. "is the immediate source of the 'famous clairvoyante'": passed off as settled. It is Eliot's own recollection, and it is disputed, and the reader should have both. Eliot told Grover Smith in a letter of 10 March 1952 that he was "almost certain" he had borrowed the name from Crome Yellow, though unconsciously (Smith, American Literature, January 1954). Rainey dates the typescript of Parts I and II to May 1921 from its paper and typewriter, and Huxley finished the novel only in August 1921; on that dating Eliot could not have read the fair scene before he wrote the passage, and Rainey suggests the name may be Eliot's own coinage. Ricks and McCue print the letter; I could not check what they make of Rainey's dating.
2. "while Eliot was at Margate and Lausanne": right for the month (Margate to 12 November, Lausanne from about 22 November), but the sentence implies the Sosostris passage was written after the book came out, which is the point in dispute. Give the chronology and let it stand as a difficulty.
3. What was borrowed, if anything, is the name. Scogan reads palms, in a skirt, bodice and black wig; the "wicked pack of cards," the head cold and the spelling are Eliot's. "The immediate source of the 'famous clairvoyante'" claims more than the name.
4. "at a village fête": Huxley's word is "the Fair," the annual charity fair in the park at Crome. "Fair."
5. "Huxley had been at Garsington with Eliot, and the two knew each other's work": true in outline (Huxley worked on the Garsington farm in 1916–17 while the Eliots were guests of Ottoline Morrell there) but unanchored and not load-bearing. Keep only if grounded; otherwise cut.
6. TITLE "chapter 27" and the WHAT line: right. The common brief's own example for this name ("usually traced to") is the right register.
Draft:

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: Crome Yellow, chapter 27
AUTHOR: Aldous Huxley
DATE: 1921
WHAT: “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana”: the name behind Madame Sosostris.
LINES: 43
NOTE: Huxley’s country-house satire was published in November 1921. Its Mr. Scogan, a cynical guest who dresses up as an old gypsy woman to tell fortunes at the village fête under the name “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana,” is the accepted source of Madame Sosostris. Asked about it by the critic Grover Smith, Eliot wrote to him on 10 March 1952 that he was “almost certain” he had taken the name from <i>Crome Yellow</i> without being conscious of doing so. Huxley and Eliot had known each other since the war years through Lady Ottoline Morrell’s circle at Garsington, her house near Oxford.

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “who dresses up as an old gypsy woman to tell fortunes at the village fête under the name “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana,” is the accepted source of Madame Sosostris.”
Proposed: “who dresses up as an old gypsy woman to tell fortunes at the charity fair in the park under the name “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana,” is the source usually given for Madame Sosostris.” And, after the sentence on the 1952 letter: “Lawrence Rainey (<i>Revisiting The Waste Land</i>, 2005) has doubted it: he dates the typescript of Parts I and II to May 1921 from its paper and typewriter, before Huxley had finished the novel, and suggests the name was Eliot’s own.”
Reason: Huxley’s event is “the Fair,” the annual charity fair in the park at Crome (chapters XXV and XXVII), not a village fête. “The accepted source” hides a real difficulty: Rainey’s dating of the typescript puts it before the novel was finished (August 1921). Eliot’s letter of 1952 says he was “almost certain” of the debt; the note should carry both, as the Part I gloss on line 43 now does.

**As it now stands**

DATE: 1921
WHAT: “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana”: the name behind Madame Sosostris.
LINES: 43
NOTE: Huxley’s country-house satire was published in November 1921. Its Mr. Scogan, a cynical guest who dresses up as an old gypsy woman to tell fortunes at the village fête under the name “Sesostris, the Sorceress of Ecbatana,” is the accepted source of Madame Sosostris. Asked about it by the critic Grover Smith, Eliot wrote to him on 10 March 1952 that he was “almost certain” he had taken the name from <i>Crome Yellow</i> without being conscious of doing so. Huxley and Eliot had known each other since the war years through Lady Ottoline Morrell’s circle at Garsington, her house near Oxford.


## source dante-inf3

**As it stood**

TITLE: Inferno, canto III
AUTHOR: Dante Alighieri   DATE: c. 1308   LANGUAGE: Italian   KIND: poem
WHAT: The crowd on London Bridge: "I had not thought death had undone so many."
PASSAGE: E io, che riguardai, vidi una ’nsegna / che girando correva tanto ratta, / che d’ogne posa mi parea indegna; / e dietro le venìa sì lunga tratta / di gente, ch’i’ non averei creduto / che morte tanta n’avesse disfatta.
TRANSLATION: And I, who looked, saw a banner which, whirling, ran so fast that it seemed to scorn all pause; and behind it came so long a train of people that I would never have believed death had undone so many.
NOTE:
Inside the gate ("Abandon all hope") but before the river Acheron, Dante finds the souls who were neither good nor bad, "who lived without infamy and without praise," rejected by Heaven and refused by Hell, running forever after a banner and stung by wasps. Eliot's commuters, going round in a ring, are these. He said in 1950 that Dante's poetry was still, "after forty years," "the most persistent and deepest influence" on his own verse.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Inferno III.1–9, 34–42, 52–57 and 64–69 at it.wikisource (Petrocchi text): the passage as displayed is exact; "Lasciate ogne speranza, voi ch'intrate" (9); "che visser sanza 'nfamia e sanza lodo" (36); "Caccianli i ciel per non esser men belli, / né lo profondo inferno li riceve" (40–41); "da mosconi e da vespe" (66). Eliot's notes 63 and 64 in work/poem-part1.md; his text of the tercet is the Temple Classics reading ("ch'io non avrei mai creduto"), the edition he used (Ricks and McCue, from knowledge). The 1950 sentence ("I still, after forty years, regard his poetry as the most persistent and deepest influence upon my own verse") from "What Dante Means to Me," Italian Institute, London, 4 July 1950, checked against a web copy. Site lines 56, 62–64 counted in the 1922 text.
Substance:
1. "Eliot's commuters, going round in a ring, are these": stated as fact; it is a reading, and it fuses two passages. Line 56 is Madame Sosostris's "crowds of people, walking round in a ring"; lines 62–63 are the crowd on London Bridge, which Eliot's note ties to Inferno III. And the very next note, to line 64, sends the reader to Inferno IV, the Limbo of the virtuous unbaptised, which is not the vestibule of the neutrals. Eliot's own notes put the London crowd in two places at once; the note should say that, and give the line-56 link as a reading (it is a common one).
2. Passage: the site prints the Petrocchi text ("ch'i' non averei creduto") where Eliot's note has the Temple Classics text ("ch'io non avrei mai creduto"). A reader comparing the two will wonder. One clause could say that Eliot quoted from the Temple Classics parallel text he carried.
3. "stung by wasps": wasps and horseflies ("mosconi e vespe," 66). Minor.
4. The description of the neutrals (inside the gate, before Acheron; "without infamy and without praise"; cast out by heaven, refused by hell; running after a banner) is accurate.
5. The 1950 quotation is exact and within the rights rule; give the title ("What Dante Means to Me") so it can be found.
6. DATE "c. 1308": conventional (Inferno c. 1304–08); fine.
Draft:
Body: The gate's inscription ends "Abandon all hope, you who enter" (III.9). Inside it, before the river Acheron, Dante finds the souls "who lived without infamy and without praise" (III.36), cast out by heaven and refused by hell; they run naked after a whirling banner, stung by wasps and horseflies, in a train so long that Dante had not believed death had undone so many. Eliot's note attaches the tercet to line 63, the crowd on London Bridge; his note to the next line sends the reader to <i>Inferno</i> IV and the Limbo of the virtuous pagans, so the crowd is given two places at once. The "crowds of people, walking round in a ring" that Madame Sosostris sees at line 56 are often read as the same crowd. Eliot quoted from the Temple Classics text, which is why his tercet differs in small ways from the one printed above. He said in "What Dante Means to Me" (1950) that Dante's poetry was still, "after forty years," "the most persistent and deepest influence" on his own verse.

## source dante-inf4

**As it stood**

TITLE: Inferno, canto IV
AUTHOR: Dante Alighieri   DATE: c. 1308   LANGUAGE: Italian   KIND: poem
WHAT: "Sighs, short and infrequent, were exhaled."
PASSAGE: Quivi, secondo che per ascoltare, / non avea pianto mai che di sospiri / che l’aura etterna facevan tremare.
TRANSLATION: Here, as far as I could tell by listening, there was no weeping but only sighs, which made the eternal air tremble.
NOTE:
Limbo, the first circle, home of the virtuous who died unbaptised: Homer, Virgil, Aristotle, the "great-hearted" souls who are not tormented but live in desire without hope. One line of the poem, one canto of Dante: the office crowd sigh like the noble pagans, not like the damned.

**Sources cited in Part I and elsewhere, or in the front matter (review the same way)**

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Inferno IV.25–27 at it.wikisource (Petrocchi): the passage as displayed is exact ("non avea pianto mai che di sospiri"; Eliot's note has the Temple Classics "ma' che di sospiri"). "sanza speme vivemo in disio" (42); "li spiriti magni" (119). Eliot's note 64 in work/poem-part1.md. Site line 64 counted in the 1922 text.
Substance:
1. "the 'great-hearted' souls": Dante's phrase is "li spiriti magni" (IV.119), the great spirits. "Great-hearted" renders "magnanimo," which Dante uses of Farinata (X.73), not here. Say "the great spirits," or drop the quotation marks.
2. "the office crowd sigh like the noble pagans, not like the damned": a reading, and on substance shaky. Limbo is the first circle of hell and its souls are lost ("semo perduti," IV.41); and Eliot's sighs are "short and infrequent" where Dante's make the eternal air tremble. The likeness is the sigh; the difference is the scale. "Office crowd" is an inference from the hour and the street, a fair one.
3. "home of the virtuous who died unbaptised: Homer, Virgil, Aristotle": right (IV.88, 131; Virgil's own place, IV.39–42), with the unbaptised infants (IV.30, 35–36). "who are not tormented but live in desire without hope": right (IV.28, 41–42).
4. "One line of the poem, one canto of Dante": true as far as the notes go (note 64 cites IV), and the note to line 63 cites III; the two notes together are the fact worth giving (see dante-inf3).
Draft:
Body: Limbo, the first circle, holds the unbaptised and the virtuous pagans, Homer and Aristotle among them, and Virgil himself. They are not tormented, but, as Virgil says, "without hope we live in desire" (IV.42); their sighs, not weeping, make the eternal air tremble. Eliot's note attaches the tercet to line 64. The line before it he had sent to the vestibule of <i>Inferno</i> III, so the crowd on London Bridge is given, in successive notes, the neutrals' number and the noble pagans' sighs.

2. "One line of the poem, one canto of Dante:" — a count made to be quoted. It tells me nothing I cannot see from the line numbers.
3. "the office crowd sigh like the noble pagans, not like the damned" — the antithesis is the point of the note, and a good point, but it is dressed. Say it in plain words.
Keep: the description of Limbo, in substance.
Draft:
NOTE: Limbo is the first circle of Hell, where the virtuous who died unbaptised live: Homer, Virgil and Aristotle among them, the "great-hearted" souls who are not tormented but live in desire without hope. Eliot's note for line 64 sends the reader here, one canto on from the crowd of line 63. The sighs of the London crowd are the sighs of these souls, not of the damned.

## source ezekiel

**As it stood**

TITLE: Ezekiel 2 and 37
AUTHOR: The Bible (King James Version)   DATE: 6th century BC; translation 1611   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: scripture
WHAT: "Son of man"; "a heap of broken images"; the valley of dry bones.
PASSAGE: And he said unto me, Son of man, stand upon thy feet, and I will speak unto thee. (2:1) /  / The hand of the LORD was upon me, and carried me out in the spirit of the LORD, and set me down in the midst of the valley which was full of bones, and caused me to pass by them round about: and, behold, there were very many in the open valley; and, lo, they were very dry. And he said unto me, Son of man, can these bones live? And I answered, O Lord GOD, thou knowest. (37:1–3)
NOTE:
Ezekiel, prophet of the Babylonian exile, is addressed by God ninety-odd times as "son of man," which is to say mortal. Eliot's note points to the moment of his commission; the poem's dry stone, broken images and dry bones come from the same book (chapters 6 and 37). In the valley the bones stand up. In the poem they do not.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Ezekiel 2:1, 6:4–6 and 37:1–4, KJV, en.wikisource: the passage as displayed is word for word. "Son of man" occurs 93 times in the book (counted in the Wikisource text). Eliot's note 20 in work/poem-part1.md. Site line 391 "Dry bones can harm no one" counted in the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321); it matches the batch's 391.
Substance:
1. "the poem's dry stone, broken images and dry bones come from the same book (chapters 6 and 37)": two of the three, not three. "Broken images" answers 6:4 and 6:6 ("your images shall be broken"; "your images may be cut down"), and "dry bones" 37:2–4 ("O ye dry bones"). There is no dry stone in Ezekiel; the phrase is the poem's (line 24). Cut it from the list.
2. The chapter 6 link is a commentator's comparison (Southam; Ricks and McCue), not Eliot's, whose note cites only 2:1. Mark it as such.
3. "ninety-odd times": 93 in the King James text; the exact figure is as short to say.
4. "which is to say mortal": a fair gloss of "ben adam," a human being, and the reason Eliot's note is useful: it keeps the phrase from being read as Christ's title in the Gospels.
5. "In the valley the bones stand up. In the poem they do not.": accurate as a contrast (37:7–10, the bones come together, are given breath and stand up "an exceeding great army"; the poem's bones lie by the empty chapel). Fine on substance; the two fragments are a register matter for the reader.
6. "prophet of the Babylonian exile," "6th century BC," "the moment of his commission": right (1:1–3; 2:1–5).
Draft:
Body: Ezekiel prophesied among the exiles in Babylon in the sixth century BC, and God addresses him as "son of man," a mortal, ninety-three times in the King James text. Eliot's note points to the first of them, at the prophet's commission (2:1). Commentators add 6:4–6, where the idolaters' "images shall be broken," for line 22, and the valley of dry bones (37:1–14) for line 391, "Dry bones can harm no one." In Ezekiel the bones are given breath and stand up, "an exceeding great army"; in the poem they lie still.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "In the valley the bones stand up. In the poem they do not." — two short sentences for effect, the bow. The contrast is worth having; make it one plain sentence with the line number.
2. "which is to say mortal" — "which is to say" is a tic. The gloss itself, that "son of man" means a human being, is the most useful thing here; give it room.
3. "the moment of his commission" — I had to think about "commission". "the moment God first speaks to him" is what the passage shows.
Keep: "Ezekiel, prophet of the Babylonian exile, is addressed by God ninety-odd times as "son of man""; "the poem's dry stone, broken images and dry bones come from the same book (chapters 6 and 37)".

NOTE: Ezekiel was a prophet of the Babylonian exile. God addresses him ninety-odd times as "son of man," which means simply a human being, a mortal. Eliot's note points to the moment God first speaks to him. The poem's dry stone, broken images and dry bones all come from the same book: chapter 6 has the broken images and chapter 37 the valley of bones. In Ezekiel the bones are made to live again; the poem's "Dry bones" at line 391 are not.

Decision: rewrite. "Dry stone" cut from the list (not in Ezekiel); the chapter 6 link marked as the commentators'; the count made exact; the contrast with the poem's bones kept as a sentence.
NOTE: Ezekiel prophesied among the exiles in Babylon in the sixth century BC, and God addresses him as "son of man," a mortal, ninety-three times in the King James text. Eliot's note points to the first of them, at the prophet's commission (2:1). Commentators add 6:4–6, where the idolaters' "images shall be broken," for line 22, and the valley of dry bones (37:1–14) for line 391, "Dry bones can harm no one." In Ezekiel the bones are given breath and stand up, "an exceeding great army"; in the poem they lie still.

satisfied. Round one: "dry stone" is gone; chapter 6 is given as a tracing, not as Eliot's note; "some ninety times" is fine (93 in the King James text). **Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: Ezekiel 2 and 37
AUTHOR: The Bible (King James Version)
DATE: 6th century BC; translation 1611
WHAT: “Son of man”; “a heap of broken images”; the valley of dry bones.
LINES: 20, 22, 391
NOTE: Ezekiel, prophet of the Babylonian exile, is addressed by God some ninety times as “son of man,” that is, mortal man. Eliot’s note on line 20 cites 2:1, where God first speaks to him. The “heap of broken images” of line 22 has been traced to chapter 6, where the idols of Israel are to be destroyed (“your images shall be broken”), and the dry bones of line 391 to chapter 37, where the bones are joined, clothed with flesh and made to live.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Isaiah 32:1–2 and 38:1–5, KJV, en.wikisource: the passage as displayed is word for word; 38:2–5 for the wall, the weeping and the fifteen years. Substance:
1. Neither passage is Eliot's; both are commentators' comparisons (Southam; Ricks and McCue). The note should say so; as it stands it reads as if Eliot had pointed to Isaiah.
2. "The red rock of Part I offers the prophet's shelter without the prophet's promise of water": a reading built on a slip. In 32:2 the shelter is not the prophet's: in the reign of a righteous king (32:1) "a man shall be as an hiding place from the wind," and "rivers of water in a dry place" is one of four likenesses for that man. The contrast with line 24 ("the dry stone no sound of water") is fair; phrase it as the site's own observation.
3. "The Fisher King of line 426 asks the question and gets no answer": that the speaker of lines 424–426 is the Fisher King rests on Eliot's note to line 425 pointing to Weston's chapter on him; it is still a reading, and "gets no answer" is a reading of the fragments that follow. Say "the speaker, whom Eliot's note to line 425 links to Weston's Fisher King."
4. Missing, one word: Isaiah's "house" becomes Eliot's "lands." The change is the point, since the Fisher King's land lies waste with him.
5. DATE "8th century BC": right for the prophet; chapters 36–39 are a narrative parallel to 2 Kings 18–20 and usually dated later. "8th century BC and later" would be safer. Minor.

Body: Neither passage is in Eliot's notes; both are commentators' comparisons (Southam; Ricks and McCue). For the shadow of the red rock (line 25) they cite 32:2, where in the reign of a righteous king a man is "as rivers of water in a dry place, as the shadow of a great rock in a weary land"; the poem's rock gives shadow and no water (line 24). For "Shall I at least set my lands in order?" (line 426) they cite the sentence on King Hezekiah, "Set thine house in order: for thou shalt die." Hezekiah turned his face to the wall and wept, and was given fifteen more years (38:2–5). Eliot's note to line 425 sends the reader to Weston's chapter on the Fisher King, whose land lies waste with him; the poem turns house into lands.

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

satisfied. Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: Isaiah 32 and 38
AUTHOR: The Bible (King James Version)
DATE: 8th century BC; translation 1611
WHAT: “the shadow of a great rock in a weary land”; “Set thine house in order.”
LINES: 25, 426
NOTE: Eliot’s notes do not cite Isaiah. The “shadow of a great rock in a weary land” of chapter 32 has long been recognised behind the “shadow under this red rock” of line 25; in Isaiah the shadow is promised together with “rivers of water in a dry place,” and the red rock of the poem gives shade but no water. In chapter 38 King Hezekiah, told to set his house in order because he will die, turns his face to the wall and weeps, and is granted fifteen more years. At line 426 the speaker, fishing on the shore, asks “Shall I at least set my lands in order?” and gets no answer; Eliot’s note on the line before cites Weston’s chapter on the Fisher King.

## source tennyson

**As it stood**

TITLE: Maud, and The Princess
AUTHOR: Alfred Tennyson   DATE: 1855; 1847   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: poem
WHAT: "a handful of dust"; "O swallow swallow."
PASSAGE: Dead, long dead, / Long dead! / And my heart is a handful of dust, / And the wheels go over my head, / And my bones are shaken with pain, / For into a shallow grave they are thrust, / Only a yard beneath the street . . . (Maud, II.v) /  / O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South, / Fly to her, and fall upon her gilded eaves, / And tell her, tell her, what I tell to thee. (The Princess)
NOTE:
The mad speaker of *Maud*, who imagines himself buried alive under a city street with the traffic going over him, is a Victorian ancestor of the man in rats' alley; the swallow song in *The Princess* gives Eliot the English half of a Latin line. Eliot's respect for Tennyson's ear ("the finest ear of any English poet since Milton," he wrote in 1936) shows in what he took.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Maud</i> Part II, section V, 1 (Gutenberg, <i>Maud, and Other Poems</i>): the seven lines as quoted. <i>The Princess</i>, Part IV (Gutenberg #791): "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South" is sung by the Prince ("one myself had made"); it was in the first edition of 1847 (the songs between the parts were added in 1850). Eliot, "In Memoriam" (1936): "the finest ear of any English poet since Milton" (text at uwyo.edu). Conrad, "Youth" (Gutenberg #525): "the heat of life in the handful of dust". Site HTML: line 30, line 115 ("rats' alley"), line 429 ("Quando fiam ceu chelidon—O swallow swallow"; Eliot's 428).

Substance:
1. "And my heart is a handful of dust" presented as Eliot's source. Eliot's notes give none for line 30, and the attribution is the annotators', one parallel among several: Conrad's "Youth" (1898; printed with <i>Heart of Darkness</i> in the 1902 volume) has "the heat of life in the handful of dust", and the phrase is proverbial. Say "annotators cite", not "gives".
2. "the swallow song in <i>The Princess</i> gives Eliot the English half of a Latin line": same point. Eliot's note on 429 cites only the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>; Tennyson is the usual source given (Southam), and Swinburne's "Itylus" (1866), which addresses "sister swallow", is sometimes cited too.
3. "who imagines himself buried alive under a city street": in II.v the speaker, in a madhouse (Tennyson's own gloss), imagines himself dead and buried in a shallow grave a yard under the street, hearing the wheels and hoofs above. "Dead and buried, still hearing the traffic" is what the text says; "buried alive" is the annotator's summary. "A Victorian ancestor of the man in rats' alley" (line 115) is a reading; mark it as one or drop it.
4. "'the finest ear of any English poet since Milton,' he wrote in 1936": exact. Name the essay ("In Memoriam", 1936, the introduction to a Tennyson selection, reprinted in <i>Essays Ancient and Modern</i>); one phrase from a post-1929 text is within the rule.
5. Dates 1855 and 1847 are right; the swallow song was in the 1847 text, which is worth a word since the best-known songs of <i>The Princess</i> were added in 1850. Passage: both quotations exact. Line 429 is right in the site's count.

Draft:
TITLE: Maud, and The Princess
BODY: "A handful of dust" is in <i>Maud</i> (1855), Part II, section V, where the speaker, in a madhouse, imagines himself dead and buried a yard beneath a city street with the traffic going over him. Eliot's notes give no source for line 30; annotators cite <i>Maud</i> among other parallels, and Conrad's "Youth" (1898), printed with <i>Heart of Darkness</i> in the 1902 volume, has "the heat of life in the handful of dust". "O swallow swallow" at line 429 is usually traced to the song the Prince sings in Part IV of <i>The Princess</i> (1847), "O Swallow, Swallow, flying, flying South"; Eliot's note gives only the Latin source, the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>. In his essay "In Memoriam" (1936) Eliot said Tennyson "had the finest ear of any English poet since Milton".

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. The note is filed under line 30 and never mentions the handful of dust. I opened it for that. The WHAT and the PASSAGE are doing the body's work.
2. "is a Victorian ancestor of the man in rats' alley" — a critic's metaphor, and a jump to Part II that a reader at line 30 has not reached. If the comparison stays, mark it as one and give the line.
3. "gives Eliot the English half of a Latin line" — clever, and I had to work out what it meant. Say that line 429 is half Latin and half English and where each half comes from.
4. "shows in what he took" — the bow. The 1936 quotation can stand on its own.
Keep: the description of Maud's speaker, in substance; the 1936 quotation.
Draft:
NOTE: "A handful of dust" (line 30) is in <i>Maud</i>, where the mad speaker imagines himself buried alive under a city street with the traffic going over him. Line 429 is half Latin and half English: "Quando fiam uti chelidon," when shall I be like the swallow, and then "O swallow swallow," from the song in <i>The Princess</i>. The buried-alive passage has also been set beside "rats' alley" in Part II (line 115). Eliot wrote in 1936 that Tennyson had "the finest ear of any English poet since Milton."
(The gloss of the Latin is mine; the scholar should check it.)

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "In 1936 Eliot wrote that Tennyson 'had the finest ear of any English poet since Milton.'" The quotation is exact but carries no source, and a reader will want one for a quotation from a post-1929 text. It is from Eliot's essay "In Memoriam" (1936), the introduction to a Nelson selection of Tennyson, reprinted in <i>Essays Ancient and Modern</i> (1936) and <i>Selected Essays</i> (1951). Put: "In his essay "In Memoriam" (1936) Eliot wrote that Tennyson "had the finest ear of any English poet since Milton.""

Round one: made. The attributions are now marked as others' ("has been cited"), Swinburne's "Itylus" is in (its first line, "Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow", is right: <i>Poems and Ballads</i>, 1866), "buried alive" is gone, and line 429 is right in the site's count. Still needs: the essay's name (above). Dropped without loss: Conrad's "Youth", which belongs on no Tennyson page.

**Reader, round 2**

Not yet.
1. “Eliot cites neither poem” and then twice “has been cited for” — the same verb for Eliot’s notes and for the critics, and “cited for” is not how a person says “given as the source of”. Replace the second and third sentences with: “The mad speaker of <i>Maud</i>, who imagines himself buried under a city street with the traffic going over him, is the source usually given for “a handful of dust” (line 30), and the passage has been compared with the “rats’ alley” of Part II. The Prince’s song in <i>The Princess</i>, with Swinburne’s “Itylus” (“Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow”) beside it, is the source usually given for “O swallow swallow” at line 429, the English half of a line whose Latin half is from the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>.”

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: Maud, and The Princess
AUTHOR: Alfred Tennyson
DATE: 1855; 1847
WHAT: “a handful of dust”; “O swallow swallow.”
LINES: 30, 429
NOTE: Eliot cites neither poem. The mad speaker of <i>Maud</i>, who imagines himself buried under a city street with the traffic going over him, has been cited for “a handful of dust” (line 30), and the passage has been compared with the “rats’ alley” of Part II. The Prince’s song in <i>The Princess</i>, with Swinburne’s “Itylus” (“Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow”), has been cited for “O swallow swallow” at line 429, the English half of a line whose Latin half is from the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>. In 1936 Eliot wrote that Tennyson “had the finest ear of any English poet since Milton.”

Proposed on top of it:
Present: the note from “The mad speaker” to the end.
Proposed: “The mad speaker of <i>Maud</i>, who imagines himself buried under a city street with the traffic going over him, is the source usually given for “a handful of dust” (line 30), and the passage has been compared with the “rats’ alley” of Part II. The Prince’s song in <i>The Princess</i>, with Swinburne’s “Itylus” (“Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow”) beside it, is the source usually given for “O swallow swallow” at line 429, the English half of a line whose Latin half is from the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>. In his essay “In Memoriam” (1936) Eliot wrote that Tennyson “had the finest ear of any English poet since Milton.””
Reason: “Eliot cites neither poem” followed twice by “has been cited for” uses one verb for Eliot’s notes and for the critics, and “cited for” is not how a person says “given as the source of.” The 1936 quotation is from a text after 1929 and should name its essay (“In Memoriam,” the introduction to a Nelson selection of Tennyson, reprinted in <i>Essays Ancient and Modern</i>).

**As it now stands**

The front-matter thread's text, as it now stands. With a proposal from Part I in shared-proposals-part1.md.
TITLE: Maud, and The Princess
AUTHOR: Alfred Tennyson
DATE: 1855; 1847
WHAT: “a handful of dust”; “O swallow swallow.”
LINES: 30, 429
NOTE: Eliot cites neither poem. The mad speaker of <i>Maud</i>, who imagines himself buried under a city street with the traffic going over him, has been cited for “a handful of dust” (line 30), and the passage has been compared with the “rats’ alley” of Part II. The Prince’s song in <i>The Princess</i>, with Swinburne’s “Itylus” (“Swallow, my sister, O sister swallow”), has been cited for “O swallow swallow” at line 429, the English half of a line whose Latin half is from the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i>. In 1936 Eliot wrote that Tennyson “had the finest ear of any English poet since Milton.”


## source whitman-lilacs

**As it stood**

TITLE: When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd
AUTHOR: Walt Whitman   DATE: 1865   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: poem
WHAT: Lilacs, spring, death, and a hermit thrush.
PASSAGE: When lilacs last in the dooryard bloom’d, / And the great star early droop’d in the western sky in the night, / I mourn’d, and yet shall mourn with ever-returning spring. . . . /  / In the swamp in secluded recesses, / A shy and hidden bird is warbling a song. / Solitary the thrush, / The hermit withdrawn to himself, avoiding the settlements, / Sings by himself a song. / Song of the bleeding throat, / Death’s outlet song of life . . .
NOTE:
Whitman's elegy for Lincoln, shot in April 1865, binds three things together: the lilac, the evening star, and the hermit thrush singing in the swamp. Eliot's poem opens on lilacs out of dead land and, three hundred and fifty lines later, longs for the hermit-thrush's "water-dripping song." He rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush say otherwise.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Leaves of Grass</i>, 1891–92 text (Gutenberg #1322): sections 1, 4 and 16 of "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd" as quoted ("Lilac and star and bird twined with the chant of my soul", 16); Lincoln is not named in the poem. Eliot, introduction to Pound's <i>Selected Poems</i> (1928): "I did not read Whitman until much later in life, and had to conquer an aversion to his form, as well as to much of his matter". Eliot, "Whitman and Tennyson", <i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926 (a review of Emory Holloway's biography): "When Whitman speaks of the lilacs or of the mocking-bird, his theories and beliefs drop away like a needless pretext" (wording from secondary quotation, Whitman Archive and Project MUSE). Eliot's note 357; site line 357 is the hermit-thrush line, as the header says.

Substance:
1. "He rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush say otherwise." Misleading as it stands. What is on record: in 1928 Eliot wrote that he "did not read Whitman until much later in life" and had had to overcome "an aversion to his form"; but in 1926, four years after the poem, he had already written of "the lilacs" and the mocking-bird as the places where Whitman's theories fall away. Neither text says whether he knew "Lilacs" before 1922. The parallel between the two poems is the annotators'; Eliot's own note on the thrush cites Chapman's bird handbook and his own hearing of the bird in Quebec County, not Whitman. Give the two statements and mark the parallel as a reading.
2. "Whitman's elegy for Lincoln, shot in April 1865": right (14 April; he died the next day). Worth adding that the poem never names him, and that it appeared that autumn in <i>Sequel to Drum-Taps</i>.
3. "three hundred and fifty lines later": 355 (line 2 to line 357). Give the line number.
4. "longs for the hermit-thrush's 'water-dripping song'": the phrase is Eliot's note's, not the poem's ("Its 'water-dripping song' is justly celebrated"). Say so.
5. "binds three things together: the lilac, the evening star, and the hermit thrush": right; "the great star early droop'd in the western sky" is Venus as evening star. Passage: exact (the ellipsis after "Death's outlet song of life" drops Whitman's parenthesis).

Draft:
TITLE: When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd
BODY: Whitman's elegy for Lincoln, shot on 14 April 1865, printed that autumn in <i>Sequel to Drum-Taps</i>; Lincoln is not named in it. Its three recurring images are the lilac, the evening star and the hermit thrush singing in the swamp: "Lilac and star and bird twined with the chant of my soul" (section 16). <i>The Waste Land</i> opens on lilacs out of the dead land and at line 357 wishes for the hermit-thrush, whose "water-dripping song" Eliot's note praises, citing a bird handbook rather than Whitman; the parallel is the annotators'. Eliot wrote in 1928 that he "did not read Whitman until much later in life" (introduction to Pound's <i>Selected Poems</i>), but in 1926 he had already written that "when Whitman speaks of the lilacs or of the mocking-bird, his theories and beliefs drop away like a needless pretext" ("Whitman and Tennyson", <i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926).

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "He rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush say otherwise." — the two-sentence bow, with the flowers as witnesses. The fact, that he rarely admitted it, is worth keeping; the second sentence should say plainly what the evidence is.
2. "three hundred and fifty lines later" — a count for effect. The line is 357; give it.
3. "binds three things together:" — the three are Whitman's own symbols, so the triad is fair, but "turns on" is quieter than "binds together".
4. WHAT: "Lilacs, spring, death, and a hermit thrush." — a list for the ear. Point at the lines instead.
Keep: the first two sentences in substance.
Draft:
WHAT: The lilacs of line 2 and the hermit-thrush of line 357.
NOTE: Whitman's elegy for Lincoln, who was shot in April 1865, turns on three things: the lilac, the evening star and the hermit thrush singing in the swamp. Eliot's poem opens with lilacs bred out of dead land, and at line 357 it longs for the hermit-thrush's "water-dripping song." Eliot rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush are the main evidence that he had, and had remembered this poem.
(If the scholar confirms it, one more sentence would help: that Eliot's own note on line 357 cites a bird handbook and not Whitman.)

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. Missing: Eliot's earlier statement, which bears directly on the 1928 one now quoted. Reviewing Emory Holloway's life of Whitman in 1926, four years after the poem, he wrote: "When Whitman speaks of the lilacs or of the mocking-bird, his theories and beliefs drop away like a needless pretext" ("Whitman and Tennyson", <i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926, p. 426; wording confirmed in the Whitman Archive's entry on Eliot and in Project MUSE's listing). A 1926 text may be quoted freely. Put, after the 1928 sentence: "Two years earlier, reviewing a life of Whitman, he had written that "when Whitman speaks of the lilacs or of the mocking-bird, his theories and beliefs drop away like a needless pretext" ("Whitman and Tennyson", <i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926)."

Round one: made. "He rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush say otherwise" is gone; the line number is given; the "water-dripping song" is attached to Eliot's note; and the Musgrove reference is a real one (S. Musgrove, <i>T. S. Eliot and Walt Whitman</i>, Wellington: New Zealand University Press, 1952). Still needs: the 1926 review. Not needed: that Lincoln is unnamed, or <i>Sequel to Drum-Taps</i>.

**Reader, round 2**

Not yet.
1. “and at line 357 wants the hermit-thrush’s “water-dripping song”; his note on that line…” — the poem “wants”: the poem as a person, and I stopped to ask who was wanting. Replace with: “and the hermit-thrush’s “water-dripping song” is at line 357; Eliot’s note on that line…” (the rest of the sentence unchanged).

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom’d
AUTHOR: Walt Whitman
DATE: 1865
WHAT: Whitman’s elegy for Lincoln: lilacs, spring, and the hermit thrush.
LINES: 2, 357
NOTE: Whitman’s elegy for Lincoln, who was shot in April 1865, brings together the lilac, the evening star and the hermit thrush singing in a swamp. Eliot’s poem opens with lilacs bred out of the dead land (line 2) and at line 357 wants the hermit-thrush’s “water-dripping song”; his note on that line describes the bird and quotes Chapman’s <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i>, and says nothing of Whitman. In 1928 Eliot wrote that he “did not read Whitman until much later in life, and had to conquer an aversion to his form, as well as to much of his matter, in order to do so.” The case that the poem drew on Whitman all the same is made at length in S. Musgrove’s <i>T. S. Eliot and Walt Whitman</i> (1952).

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “and at line 357 wants the hermit-thrush’s “water-dripping song”; his note on that line describes the bird …” and, after the 1928 quotation, nothing before the Musgrove sentence.
Proposed: “and the hermit-thrush’s “water-dripping song” is at line 357; Eliot’s note on that line describes the bird …” and, after the 1928 quotation: “Two years earlier, reviewing a life of Whitman, he had written that “when Whitman speaks of the lilacs or of the mocking-bird, his theories and beliefs drop away like a needless pretext” (“Whitman and Tennyson”, <i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926).”
Reason: the poem does not want; the reader stopped to ask who was wanting. And the 1926 review bears directly on the 1928 disclaimer now quoted: four years after the poem Eliot was already writing about Whitman’s lilacs. A 1926 text may be quoted freely.

**As it now stands**

Eliot’s poem opens with lilacs bred out of the dead land (line 2) and at line 357 wants the hermit-thrush’s “water-dripping song”; his note on that line describes the bird and quotes Chapman’s <i>Handbook of Birds of Eastern North America</i>, and says nothing of Whitman. In 1928 Eliot wrote that he “did not read Whitman until much later in life, and had to conquer an aversion to his form, as well as to much of his matter, in order to do so.” The case that the poem drew on Whitman all the same is made at length in S. Musgrove’s <i>T. S. Eliot and Walt Whitman</i> (1952).

TITLE: The White Devil
AUTHOR: John Webster   DATE: 1612   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: play
WHAT: Cornelia's dirge ("keep the wolf far thence") and Flamineo on the spider and the winding-sheet.
PASSAGE: Call for the robin-redbreast and the wren, / Since o’er shady groves they hover, / And with leaves and flowers do cover / The friendless bodies of unburied men. / Call unto his funeral dole / The ant, the field-mouse, and the mole, / To rear him hillocks that shall keep him warm, / And (when gay tombs are robb’d) sustain no harm; / But keep the wolf far thence, that’s foe to men, / For with his nails he’ll dig them up again. (V.iv) /  / O men, / That lie upon your death-beds, and are haunted / With howling wives! ne’er trust them; they’ll re-marry / Ere the worm pierce your winding-sheet, ere the spider / Make a thin curtain for your epitaphs. (V.vi)
NOTE:
Webster's tragedy of Vittoria Corombona, the "white devil," and her murderous lover Brachiano. Cornelia sings the dirge over her son Marcello, killed by his brother Flamineo; Flamineo gives the speech about widows as he rises from a feigned death, having just learned that his sister would have let him die. Eliot's essays on the Elizabethan dramatists and his poem "Whispers of Immortality" ("Webster was much possessed by death / And saw the skull beneath the skin") explain why these two speeches were in his head.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>The White Devil</i> (Gutenberg #12915): the dirge in Act V scene iv, with the stage direction "Cornelia doth this in several forms of distraction"; "Flamineo runs Marcello through" in V.ii; V.vi: Vittoria and Zanche fire the pistols, Flamineo feigns dying, Zanche promises to "drive a stake / Through thy body" and to give out that he killed himself, then "[Flamineo riseth.] The pistols held no bullets; 'twas a plot / To prove your kindness to me", and the speech "O men, / That lie upon your death-beds..." follows. 1612 quarto title (Wikipedia): "The White Divel; or, The Tragedy of Paulo Giordano Ursini, Duke of Brachiano. With The Life and Death of Vittoria Corombona the famous Venetian Curtizan". Eliot's note 407 ("Cf. Webster, <i>The White Devil</i>, V, vi"), which the site attaches to line 408. "Whispers of Immortality" (<i>Poems</i>, 1920): couplet exact.

Substance:
1. "having just learned that his sister would have let him die": understates what happens. Flamineo hands Vittoria and Zanche pistols he has secretly left unloaded, proposes that all three die, and has them shoot him first; they fire, and while he feigns dying they gloat over him (Zanche: she will drive a stake through his body and say he killed himself). He rises, "The pistols held no bullets; 'twas a plot / To prove your kindness to me", and the widow speech is his answer. They shot him, as they thought; they did not merely let him die.
2. "Cornelia sings the dirge over her son Marcello, killed by his brother Flamineo": right (V.ii). "Vittoria Corombona, the 'white devil,' and her murderous lover Brachiano": right; the 1612 title page names both.
3. "explain why these two speeches were in his head": the essays and the poem show that Eliot knew Webster well; they do not explain a choice. A reading; "show how well he knew Webster" is what the evidence bears. Note that Eliot's essays on the dramatists before 1922 are on Marlowe (1919), Jonson and Massinger (1920); his piece on Webster is a review of 1928.
4. Passage: a modern edited text, correct in wording. The stage direction is worth adding to the (V.iv) tag.

Flamineo's speech on widows comes in the last scene: he has given Vittoria and Zanche pistols, secretly unloaded, and had them shoot him first; they fire and gloat over him as he feigns dying; he rises ("The pistols held no bullets; 'twas a plot / To prove your kindness to me") and turns on them. Eliot quotes that passage in his note to line 408, and had written of Webster two years earlier in "Whispers of Immortality": "Webster was much possessed by death / And saw the skull beneath the skin".

Round one: made. The feigned death is now described from the text, the killing of Marcello, the note references and the dirge's changes are right, and "Whispers of Immortality" is dated to 1918 (its first printing, in the <i>Little Review</i>). Nothing else outstanding.

## source shakespeare-tempest

**As it stood**

TITLE: The Tempest, Act I scene ii
AUTHOR: William Shakespeare   DATE: c. 1611   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: play
WHAT: Ariel's song, "Those are pearls that were his eyes"; Ferdinand on the bank; "This music crept by me upon the waters."
PASSAGE: FERDINAND: Where should this music be? i’ th’ air or th’ earth? / It sounds no more: and sure, it waits upon / Some god o’ th’ island. Sitting on a bank, / Weeping again the king my father’s wreck, / This music crept by me upon the waters, / Allaying both their fury and my passion / With its sweet air . . . /  / ARIEL (sings): Full fathom five thy father lies; / Of his bones are coral made; / Those are pearls that were his eyes: / Nothing of him that doth fade / But doth suffer a sea-change / Into something rich and strange. / Sea-nymphs hourly ring his knell: / Ding-dong. / Hark! now I hear them,—Ding-dong, bell.
NOTE:
Ferdinand, washed ashore after the shipwreck, believes his father Alonso drowned; Ariel's song tells him so, beautifully and falsely. Alonso is alive, and by the end of the play everyone has suffered a sea-change for the better. Eliot quotes the song twice and Ferdinand's speech twice, and never allows the change. The drowned father with pearls for eyes becomes the drowned Phoenician and Phlebas; Ferdinand weeping on the bank becomes the Fisher King by the gasworks.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>The Tempest</i> I.ii (Gutenberg text, lines 387–405): Ferdinand's speech and Ariel's song as quoted, then "The ditty does remember my drown'd father." Site HTML: line 48; line 125; lines 191–192 ("Musing upon the king my brother's wreck / And on the king my father's death before him"); line 257; line 190 reads "On a winter evening round behind the gashouse". Eliot's notes at 125 ("Cf. Part I, l. 37, 48"), 192 ("Cf. <i>The Tempest</i>, I, ii") and 257.

Substance:
1. "the Fisher King by the gasworks": the 1922 text has "gashouse" (line 190). And the man fishing in the canal is the Fisher King only by a reading; Eliot's notes attach the Fisher King to the Man with Three Staves at line 46 ("quite arbitrarily") and to Weston's chapter at 424–425, and give line 189 no note. Say "the fisherman behind the gashouse", or mark the identification as a reading.
2. "Eliot quotes the song twice and Ferdinand's speech twice": the song twice, yes (48, 125). Ferdinand's speech is quoted verbatim once (257) and altered once (191–192, where "Weeping again the king my father's wreck" becomes the brother's wreck and the father's death). Say "uses twice, altering it the first time".
3. "by the end of the play everyone has suffered a sea-change for the better": overstated. Alonso is alive and restored to his son; Antonio and Sebastian say nothing of repentance. Keep to Alonso.
4. "and never allows the change": a reading (the annotator's). Mark it or give it to someone. Phlebas's bones are picked by the current (lines 315–316); whether that is a sea-change refused is interpretation.
5. Right: Ferdinand believes his father drowned; the song "tells him so" and it is untrue. Date c. 1611 (performed at court 1 November 1611; printed 1623). Passage exact.

Draft:
TITLE: The Tempest, Act I scene ii
BODY: Ferdinand, cast ashore, believes his father Alonso drowned in the wreck; Ariel's song, "Full fathom five thy father lies", tells him so ("The ditty does remember my drown'd father"), and it is not true: Alonso is alive, and the play ends with father and son restored to each other. Eliot uses the song twice, at lines 48 and 125, and Ferdinand's speech twice: altered at lines 191–192, where the king's wreck becomes a brother's and the father's death is added, and verbatim at line 257. The drowned man with pearls for eyes becomes the drowned Phoenician Sailor and Phlebas; Ferdinand weeping on the bank becomes the man fishing behind the gashouse at line 189. Whether the poem allows Shakespeare's sea-change or refuses it is a matter of reading.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "beautifully and falsely" — a paired-adverb flourish. "and it is not true" is what I need.
2. "and never allows the change" — the poem as a person making decisions. The reading is fair; say it as a reading, in plain words.
3. "the Fisher King by the gasworks" — shorthand for the admirer. The poem says "gashouse", and the lines are 189–191; give them.
4. The last sentence's two parallel "becomes" clauses are the cross-references I want; they only need line numbers and less balance.
Keep: the first sentence, in substance; "Alonso is alive".
Draft:

Ariel's song tells him so, and it is not true: Alonso is alive, and by the end of the play the shipwreck has done everyone good. Eliot quotes the song at lines 48 and 125 and Ferdinand's speech at lines 191 and 257. In Eliot's poem the drowning is not undone. The father with pearls for eyes becomes the drowned Phoenician Sailor of line 47 and Phlebas of Part IV; Ferdinand weeping on the bank becomes the man fishing behind the gashouse at lines 189–191.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

Round one: all made. "Gashouse" is restored; the fisherman is a fisherman, not the Fisher King; "everyone has suffered a sea-change" and "never allows the change" have given way to what the poem does (the Phoenician Sailor and Phlebas stay drowned); the alteration at 191–192 is stated as an alteration. The quotation from Eliot's note on Tiresias ("is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples") is exact (Gutenberg #1321, note 218), and "the song Ariel sings to him, unseen" matches the stage direction ("Enter Ariel, invisible, playing and singing").

## source frazer

**As it stood**

TITLE: The Golden Bough (Adonis, Attis, Osiris)
AUTHOR: Sir James George Frazer   DATE: 1906–15 (third edition); one-volume abridgement 1922   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: anthropology
WHAT: The dying and reviving god; the buried corpse that sprouts; the Hanged God; the effigy thrown into the sea.
NOTE:
Frazer's twelve-volume comparative study of magic and religion, which Eliot's headnote says "has influenced our generation profoundly," argued that behind the myths of Adonis, Attis, Osiris and, by implication, Christ lay a single agricultural drama: the god who dies with the harvest and returns with the spring, helped along by ritual (a sacrificed king, an effigy buried or drowned). The volumes Eliot names describe the "gardens of Adonis," pots of quick-sprouting seed thrown into the sea, and the effigies of Osiris "made of earth and corn," buried to sprout. Stetson's planted corpse, Phlebas in the current, the Hanged Man and the whole question of whether April can bring anything back are Frazer's. The book's title and its opening page come from Turner's painting *The Golden Bough* (1834), which Frazer took for a view of Nemi; it shows the Sibyl at Lake Avernus.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>The Golden Bough</i>, 1922 abridgement (Gutenberg #3623): first sentence, "Who does not know Turner's picture of the Golden Bough? The scene ... is a dream-like vision of the little woodland lake of Nemi"; ch. XXXIII, "The Gardens of Adonis": "baskets or pots filled with earth, in which wheat, barley, lettuces, fennel ... were sown and tended for eight days ... carried out with the images of the dead Adonis, and flung with them into the sea or into springs"; the Osiris chapters: "the priests used to bury effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn ... the corn would be found to have sprouted from the body of Osiris". <i>Adonis Attis Osiris</i>, third edition, 2 vols, 1914 (archive.org scan of vol. I): "The Hanged God" is Book I chapter V, pp. 288–97 (Marsyas, Odin, Attis). Editions (Wikipedia): 2 vols 1890; 3 vols 1900; 12 vols 1906–15; abridgement 1922; <i>Adonis, Attis, Osiris</i> separately 1906 and 1907, then as Part IV of the third edition, 1914; the crucifixion discussion of 1900 moved to an appendix in the third edition and dropped from the abridgement. Turner's <i>The Golden Bough</i>, exhibited RA 1834, Tate: Lake Avernus, the Sibyl with sickle and bough; Frazer used it as his frontispiece in 1890 and called it Nemi (Wikipedia, "The Golden Bough (painting)").

Substance:
1. "The book's title and its opening page come from Turner's painting": the opening page does; the title does not. It is Virgil's golden bough, the branch Aeneas must pluck before going down to the underworld (<i>Aeneid</i> VI), which Servius's commentary connected with the priesthood of Diana at Nemi; that connection is Frazer's starting point, and the painting is how he introduces it.
2. "which Frazer took for a view of Nemi; it shows the Sibyl at Lake Avernus": right, and worth keeping; the painting was Frazer's frontispiece in 1890.
3. "and, by implication, Christ": Frazer made the comparison explicit in the second edition (1900) and moved it to an appendix, "The Crucifixion of Christ", in the third; the 1922 abridgement leaves it out. Say that rather than "by implication".
4. "Stetson's planted corpse, Phlebas in the current, the Hanged Man and the whole question of whether April can bring anything back are Frazer's": a reading stated as fact. What Eliot's headnote claims is that a reader of Frazer "will immediately recognise in the poem certain references to vegetation ceremonies", and his note on the Tarot that the Hanged Man "is associated in my mind with the Hanged God of Frazer". "The Hanged God" is a chapter of the very volumes he names; give the chapter and let the rest stand as what readers have found.
5. "the 'gardens of Adonis,' pots of quick-sprouting seed thrown into the sea" and "effigies of Osiris 'made of earth and corn,' buried to sprout": both exact. "Twelve-volume": right for the third edition. The date line might add the first edition, 1890.

Draft:
TITLE: The Golden Bough (Adonis, Attis, Osiris)
BODY: Frazer's comparative study of magic and religion, two volumes in 1890 and twelve by its third edition (1906–15), argued that behind the myths of Adonis, Attis and Osiris lay one agricultural rite: a god who dies with the harvest and returns with the spring, helped by a sacrificed king or by an effigy buried or drowned. The second edition (1900) put the crucifixion into the same pattern; the third moved that discussion to an appendix, and the 1922 abridgement left it out. The two volumes Eliot's headnote names (he spells them "Atthis Adonis Osiris") describe the "gardens of Adonis", pots of quick-sprouting seed carried out with images of the dead god and flung into the sea, and the "effigies of Osiris made of earth and corn" buried at the festival of sowing so that the grain would sprout from the god's body; their chapter "The Hanged God" is what Eliot's note on the Tarot means by "the Hanged God of Frazer". Frazer's first page describes Turner's painting <i>The Golden Bough</i> (1834), which he took for a view of Nemi; it shows the Sibyl of Cumae, sickle and bough in hand, at Lake Avernus.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "Stetson's planted corpse, Phlebas in the current, the Hanged Man and the whole question of whether April can bring anything back are Frazer's." — a list built to a climax, ending in a claim of ownership. Separate what Eliot's own note says (the Hanged Man and the Hanged God) from what the commentators add.
2. "which Frazer took for a view of Nemi; it shows the Sibyl at Lake Avernus" — I could not tell which lake the painting shows. Say it in order: Frazer describes it as Nemi; it is in fact Avernus, with the Sibyl.
3. The first sentence is a paragraph. It is clear but long; split it.
4. "a single agricultural drama:" — the colon-formula. "one story" does the same work.
Keep: the "gardens of Adonis" sentence, which is the concrete matter a reader wants.
Draft:
NOTE: Frazer's comparative study of magic and religion ran to twelve volumes in its third edition; Eliot's headnote says it "has influenced our generation profoundly." Frazer argued that behind the myths of Adonis, Attis and Osiris, and by implication of Christ, lay one agricultural story: a god who dies with the harvest and returns in spring, helped by ritual, whether a king sacrificed or an effigy buried or drowned. The volumes Eliot names describe the "gardens of Adonis," pots of quick-sprouting seed thrown into the sea, and effigies of Osiris "made of earth and corn," buried to sprout. Eliot's note on line 46 links the Hanged Man with Frazer's Hanged God. The corpse planted in Stetson's garden (line 71) and Phlebas in the current (Part IV) are read against the Adonis gardens and the Osiris effigies. The title and opening page of <i>The Golden Bough</i> come from Turner's painting of that name (1834). Frazer describes it as a view of the lake of Nemi; it in fact shows Lake Avernus, with the Sibyl.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "and by implication of Christ": Frazer says it outright, in the very volumes Eliot names. <i>Adonis Attis Osiris</i>, third edition (1914), vol. **Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: The Golden Bough (Adonis, Attis, Osiris)
AUTHOR: Sir James George Frazer
DATE: 1906–15 (third edition); one-volume abridgement 1922
WHAT: The god who dies with the harvest and returns in spring: the Hanged God, the buried effigy, the image thrown into the sea.
LINES: 46, 71, 312
NOTE: Frazer’s twelve-volume study of magic and religion, which Eliot’s headnote says “has influenced our generation profoundly,” argued that the myths of Adonis, Attis and Osiris, and by implication of Christ, tell one story, that of a god who dies with the harvest and returns with the spring, helped by rites in which a king is sacrificed or an effigy is buried or drowned. The two volumes Eliot names describe the “gardens of Adonis,” pots of quick-sprouting seed carried out with images of the dead god and “flung with them into the sea,” and the effigies of Osiris “made of earth and corn” that the priests buried so that grain would sprout from the god’s body. Eliot’s note on the Tarot says the Hanged Man “is associated in my mind with the Hanged God of Frazer”; the corpse planted in Stetson’s garden (line 71) and Phlebas in the current (line 312) have been connected with the same rites. Frazer put a reproduction of Turner’s painting <i>The Golden Bough</i> (1834) at the front of his first edition and opened his first page with it, describing it as a view of Lake Nemi; it in fact shows Lake Avernus and the Sibyl of Cumae, the prophetess of the poem’s epigraph, holding the bough that admits Aeneas to the underworld.

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “argued that the myths of Adonis, Attis and Osiris, and by implication of Christ, tell one story, that of a god who dies with the harvest and returns with the spring, helped by rites in which a king is sacrificed or an effigy is buried or drowned.” And the last sentence, from “Frazer put a reproduction” to “the underworld.”
Proposed: “argued that the myths of Adonis, Attis and Osiris tell one story, that of a god who dies with the harvest and returns with the spring, helped by rites in which a king is sacrificed or an effigy is buried or drowned, and that the Church’s Easter was adapted from the spring festivals of Adonis and Attis.” And: “Frazer put a reproduction of Turner’s painting <i>The Golden Bough</i> (1834) at the front of his first edition and opened his first page with it, describing it as a view of Lake Nemi. It in fact shows Lake Avernus, with the Sibyl of Cumae, the prophetess of the poem’s epigraph, holding the bough that admits Aeneas to the underworld.”
Reason: “by implication” is wrong; Frazer says it outright in the two volumes Eliot names. <i>Adonis Attis Osiris</i> (third edition, 1914), vol. I, by its own table of contents: “the Christian festival of Easter perhaps grafted on a festival of Adonis” (pp. 254–257) and “the festival of Easter apparently adapted to the spring festival of Attis” (pp. 305–310). The last sentence carried the frontispiece, the first page, the mistake, the real lake, the Sibyl, the epigraph and Aeneas in one breath.

**As it now stands**

WHAT: The god who dies with the harvest and returns in spring: the Hanged God, the buried effigy, the image thrown into the sea.
LINES: 46, 71, 312
NOTE: Frazer’s twelve-volume study of magic and religion, which Eliot’s headnote says “has influenced our generation profoundly,” argued that the myths of Adonis, Attis and Osiris, and by implication of Christ, tell one story, that of a god who dies with the harvest and returns with the spring, helped by rites in which a king is sacrificed or an effigy is buried or drowned. The two volumes Eliot names describe the “gardens of Adonis,” pots of quick-sprouting seed carried out with images of the dead god and “flung with them into the sea,” and the effigies of Osiris “made of earth and corn” that the priests buried so that grain would sprout from the god’s body. Eliot’s note on the Tarot says the Hanged Man “is associated in my mind with the Hanged God of Frazer”; the corpse planted in Stetson’s garden (line 71) and Phlebas in the current (line 312) have been connected with the same rites. Frazer put a reproduction of Turner’s painting <i>The Golden Bough</i> (1834) at the front of his first edition and opened his first page with it, describing it as a view of Lake Nemi; it in fact shows Lake Avernus and the Sibyl of Cumae, the prophetess of the poem’s epigraph, holding the bough that admits Aeneas to the underworld.


## source weston

**As it stood**

TITLE: From Ritual to Romance
AUTHOR: Jessie L. Weston   DATE: 1920   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: anthropology
WHAT: The title, the plan, the Fisher King, the Chapel Perilous, the Syrian merchants.
NOTE:
Weston (1850–1928) was a scholar of Arthurian romance who came to believe, under the influence of Frazer, that the Grail stories preserved the remains of an ancient fertility cult: a wounded king, a land gone waste, a cup and a lance that were sexual symbols before they were Christian relics, and a ritual of initiation whose secrets travelled Europe with Syrian merchants. Almost no medievalist now accepts the argument. It does not matter. Eliot's note says the book suggested "not only the title, but the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism," and thirty-four years later he said he regretted "having sent so many enquirers off on a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail." Read chapters II (The Task of the Hero), IX (The Fisher King) and XIII (The Perilous Chapel) and you have the poem's skeleton; read the rest and you have a very good bad book.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> (Gutenberg #4090): chapter list (I Introductory; II The Task of the Hero; III The Freeing of the Waters; IV Tammuz and Adonis; V Medieval and Modern Forms of Nature Ritual; VI The Symbols; VII The Sword Dance; VIII The Medicine Man; IX The Fisher King; X–XI The Secret of the Grail; XII Mithra and Attis; XIII The Perilous Chapel; XIV The Author). Ch. VI: Lance and Cup "are sex symbols of immemorial antiquity ..., the Lance, or Spear, representing the Male, the Cup, or Vase, the Female, reproductive energy", and "They exist to-day as the four suits of the Tarot". Ch. XII, after Cumont: the cults were carried across the Roman Empire by three channels, "Syrian merchants", Eastern slaves and officials, and "most important ... the soldiers, the foreign legions"; she then asks whether the cult "may have been known in Great Britain". Weston 1850–1928; the book 1920, Cambridge University Press (Wikipedia; the Gutenberg text has no imprint). Eliot's headnote; his Part V note, "the approach to the Chapel Perilous (see Miss Weston's book)"; note 424, "chapter on the Fisher King". Eliot, "The Frontiers of Criticism" (1956): "I regret having sent so many enquirers off on a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail" (wording from secondary quotation). Later reception: rejected even by Roger Sherman Loomis (Wikipedia, citing Juliette Wood).

Substance:
1. "a ritual of initiation whose secrets travelled Europe with Syrian merchants": a simplification of chapter XII, where Weston, following Cumont, names three carriers, merchants, slaves and, "most important", the legions, and only asks whether the cult reached Britain. "Syrian merchants" is presumably kept for the sake of Mr Eugenides; fair, but say "with Syrian merchants and the legions" or "among others".
2. "Almost no medievalist now accepts the argument. It does not matter." The first sentence is right (Loomis, once sympathetic, gave it up). The second is a judgement; the point of substance is that Eliot's use of the book does not depend on the thesis being true. Say that.
3. "thirty-four years later he said he regretted...": right; 1956, "The Frontiers of Criticism". Name the lecture. One phrase from a post-1929 text: within the rule.
4. "Read chapters II ..., IX ... and XIII ... and you have the poem's skeleton": the numbers and titles are right. Missing is chapter VI, "The Symbols", where the four Grail hallows become the four suits of the Tarot, which is what Eliot's note on line 46 presupposes; and chapter III, "The Freeing of the Waters", is the drought. "A very good bad book" is a judgement.
5. "Weston (1850–1928) was a scholar of Arthurian romance", "under the influence of Frazer", "a cup and a lance that were sexual symbols before they were Christian relics": all right (chapter VI; chapter XIV on the Crusades' identification of the symbols with the Passion relics).

Draft:
TITLE: From Ritual to Romance
BODY: Weston (1850–1928), a scholar and translator of Arthurian romance, argued, following Frazer, that the Grail stories preserved an ancient fertility ritual: a wounded king, a land gone waste, a lance and a cup that were "sex symbols" before they were relics of the Passion (chapter VI), and a rite of initiation carried across the Roman Empire by Syrian merchants, Eastern slaves and the legions (chapter XII). Medievalists have not accepted the argument; Roger Sherman Loomis, once sympathetic, rejected it. That does not touch Eliot's use of the book. His headnote says it suggested "not only the title, but the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism"; his notes send the reader to her chapters on the Fisher King (IX) and the Perilous Chapel (XIII), and her chapter on the symbols (VI) is where the Grail hallows become the suits of the Tarot. In 1956, in "The Frontiers of Criticism", he said he regretted "having sent so many enquirers off on a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail".

Reads as a model wrote it: yes
Problems:
1. "It does not matter." — the short quotable sentence. What is meant is that the poem's debt does not depend on the argument being right; say that.
2. "Read chapters II ... and you have the poem's skeleton; read the rest and you have a very good bad book." — second-person instruction, antithesis and bow in one sentence. The chapter numbers are the useful part.
3. The 1956 sentence about the wild goose chase is also the PASSAGE of the eliot-frontiers page. Not a fault, but the two pages could point at each other rather than both quoting it.
Keep: the first sentence in substance; the Eliot quotation from the headnote.
Draft:

It is where Weston makes the four Grail hallows (Cup, Lance, Sword, Dish) the suits of the Tarot: "They exist to-day as the four suits of the Tarot" (Gutenberg #4090, chapter VI). AUTHOR: Jessie L. Weston
DATE: 1920
WHAT: The book Eliot’s headnote credits with the title and the plan: the Fisher King, the waste land, the Chapel Perilous.
LINES: 0, 46, 386, 424
NOTE: Weston (1850–1928) was a scholar of Arthurian romance. Following Frazer’s <i>Golden Bough</i>, she argued that the Grail stories preserved the remains of an ancient fertility cult: a king whose wound lays his land waste, a cup and a lance that were sexual symbols before they were Christian relics, and a rite of initiation descended from the mystery cults of Attis and Mithra. Those cults, she says, were carried across the Roman Empire by Syrian merchants, slaves and soldiers (chapter XII). Medievalists have not accepted the argument. ## source petronius

**As it stood**

TITLE: Satyricon, chapter 48
AUTHOR: Petronius   DATE: 1st century AD   LANGUAGE: Latin, with Greek   KIND: prose
WHAT: The epigraph: the Sibyl who wanted to die.
PASSAGE: Nam Sibyllam quidem Cumis ego ipse oculis meis vidi in ampulla pendere, et cum illi pueri dicerent: Σίβυλλα τί θέλεις; respondebat illa: ἀποθανεῖν θέλω.
TRANSLATION: For I myself, with my own eyes, saw the Sibyl at Cumae hanging in a jar, and when the boys said to her, "Sibyl, what do you want?" she answered, "I want to die."
NOTE:
The speaker is Trimalchio, the freed slave turned millionaire whose dinner party is the surviving centre of the *Satyricon*, showing off between courses. The Sibyl of Cumae, in Ovid's telling, asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand and forgot to ask for youth, and shrank with age until only her voice was left. A prophetess kept as a curiosity; a poem introduced by a fat man's boast.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Satyricon</i> 48 (Latin Library): the sentence as quoted, with the Greek. Context: Trimalchio has asked the rhetorician Agamemnon what he declaimed on today, boasted of his own libraries, and asked whether Agamemnon knows the twelve labours of Hercules "or the story of Ulysses, how the Cyclops wrenched off his thumb with pincers; I used to read these things in Homer as a boy" ("Solebam haec ego puer apud Homerum legere"); the Sibyl follows at once. Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV.130–53 (Latin Library): "ostendens cumulum, quot haberet corpora pulvis, / tot mihi natales contingere vana rogavi; / excidit, ut peterem iuvenes quoque protinus annos" (136–39); "voce tamen noscar; vocem mihi fata relinquent" (153).

Substance:
1. "asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand": Ovid's Sibyl points to a heap ("cumulum") of sand and asks for as many birthdays as it has grains; there is no hand. "In her hand" makes Ovid echo line 30. Correct to "a heap of sand".
2. "shrank with age until only her voice was left": in Ovid she is seven hundred years old and foretells this, three hundred years more, then no body, only a voice. Future, not past.
3. "a fat man's boast": Petronius gives Trimalchio a bald head, rings, a scarlet cloak and a napkin at his neck (27, 32); he does not call him fat. The rest of the sentence is right: the anecdote caps a display of garbled learning ("showing off between courses" is exact; the pig has just gone out to the kitchen).
4. "whose dinner party is the surviving centre of the <i>Satyricon</i>": right; the Cena (chapters 26–78) is the longest continuous stretch to survive.
5. Missing: that the epigraph took the place of the Conrad quotation early in 1922 (see conrad-hod). Translation exact; "jar" for "ampulla" (a flask or bottle) is the conventional rendering.

Draft:
TITLE: Satyricon, chapter 48
BODY: The speaker is Trimalchio, the freed slave grown rich whose dinner party is the longest surviving stretch of the <i>Satyricon</i>. He has just asked the rhetorician Agamemnon about his declamation and offered his own learning in return, garbling Homer as he goes; the Sibyl is his clincher, something he claims to have seen with his own eyes. In Ovid (<i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV) the Sibyl of Cumae tells Aeneas that Apollo offered her whatever she wished: she pointed to a heap of sand and asked for as many years as it had grains, forgot to ask for youth, and will dwindle until nothing is left of her but a voice. Eliot put the passage in place of the Conrad epigraph early in 1922.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "A prophetess kept as a curiosity; a poem introduced by a fat man's boast." — two fragments in parallel, a bow. The one thing in it I needed, that Trimalchio's claim is a boast, can be said plainly.
2. "whose dinner party is the surviving centre of the Satyricon, showing off between courses" — "showing off" has floated away from Trimalchio and attached itself to the dinner party. Reorder.
Keep: the Ovid sentence, which explains why she wants to die and is the reason I opened the note.
Draft:
NOTE: The speaker is Trimalchio, a freed slave turned millionaire, showing off to his guests between courses; his dinner party is the centre of what survives of the <i>Satyricon</i>. The Sibyl of Cumae, in Ovid's telling, asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand and forgot to ask for youth, and shrank with age until only her voice was left. Trimalchio's claim to have seen her with his own eyes is one of his boasts.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet

1. "asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand": Ovid's words are "ego pulveris hausti / ostendens cumulum, quot haberet corpora pulvis, / tot mihi natales contingere vana rogavi" (<i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV.136–138, Latin Library): dust, not sand, in a heap she had scooped up and holds out. The Part I gloss's "a heap of dust she had picked up" is the exact rendering, and the library note should agree with it: yes to the editor's question. My round-one objection that "there is no hand" was too strict; "hausti" means she had taken the dust up, so a hand is implied. The errors are "sand" for dust and the heap left out. Put: "asked Apollo for as many years as there were grains in a heap of dust she had scooped up, and forgot to ask for youth to go with them". Ovid's word is the word of line 30, "dust"; if the editor wants that resonance noted, it must be marked as a parallel, since Eliot's notes give no source for line 30.

Round one: made. "Fat" is gone, the shrinking is foretold, and the replacement of the Conrad epigraph is in. Still needs: the sentence above.

**Reader, round 2**

Not yet, on two words.
1. “Eliot put Petronius in its stead before publication” — “in its stead” is not something a person says across a table. Replace with: “Eliot put Petronius in its place before publication.”

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: Satyricon, chapter 48
AUTHOR: Petronius
DATE: 1st century AD
WHAT: The epigraph: the Sibyl who wanted to die.
LINES: 0
NOTE: The speaker is Trimalchio, a freed slave grown enormously rich, boasting to his guests between courses; his dinner party is the longest continuous episode in what survives of the <i>Satyricon</i>. The Sibyl of Cumae, in Ovid’s telling (<i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV), asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand and forgot to ask for youth to go with them; she foresees that she will shrink until only her voice is left. The typescript of the poem carried a different epigraph, from Conrad’s <i>Heart of Darkness</i>, ending “The horror! the horror!”; Pound doubted that Conrad was weighty enough for the place, and Eliot put Petronius in its stead before publication.

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand and forgot to ask for youth to go with them” and “Eliot put Petronius in its stead before publication.”
Proposed: “asked Apollo for as many years as there were grains in a heap of dust she had scooped up, and forgot to ask for youth to go with them” and “Eliot put Petronius in its place before publication.”
Reason: Ovid’s words are “ego pulveris hausti / ostendens cumulum, quot haberet corpora pulvis” (<i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV, 136–137): dust, not sand, in a heap she had taken up and holds out. The Part I gloss on the epigraph says the same, and the two should agree; Ovid’s word is also the word of line 30. “In its stead” is not something a person says across a table.

**As it now stands**

The Sibyl of Cumae, in Ovid’s telling (<i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV), asked Apollo for as many years as the grains of sand in her hand and forgot to ask for youth to go with them; she foresees that she will shrink until only her voice is left.
