## source dante-purg26

**As it stood**

TITLE: Purgatorio, canto XXVI
AUTHOR: Dante Alighieri   DATE: c. 1315   LANGUAGE: Italian and Provençal   KIND: poem
WHAT: "il miglior fabbro" (the dedication) and "Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina" (line 428).
PASSAGE: «Tan m’abellis vostre cortes deman, / qu’ieu no me puesc ni voill a vos cobrire. / Ieu sui Arnaut, que plor e vau cantan; / consiros vei la passada folor, / e vei jausen lo joi qu’esper, denan. / Ara vos prec, per aquella valor / que vos guida al som de l’escalina, / sovenha vos a temps de ma dolor!». / Poi s’ascose nel foco che li affina.
TRANSLATION: "So much does your courteous question please me that I cannot and will not hide myself from you. I am Arnaut, who weep and go singing; in thought I see my past folly, and I see with joy, ahead, the joy I hope for. Now I pray you, by that power which guides you to the top of the stair, be mindful in time of my pain." Then he hid himself in the fire that refines them.
NOTE:
On the seventh terrace of Purgatory, where the lustful walk in flame, Dante meets Guido Guinizelli, who points out Arnaut Daniel as "miglior fabbro del parlar materno," the better craftsman of the mother tongue (line 117). Arnaut answers in his own Provençal, the only passage in the *Comedy* in a vernacular other than Italian, and steps back into the fire. Eliot took the dedication to Pound from line 117, the title of his 1920 collection *Ara Vos Prec* from line 145, and line 428 of the poem from line 148. Modern texts read "li affina"; Eliot's "gli" follows the editions he owned.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI (it.wikisource, Petrocchi text): "fu miglior fabbro del parlar materno" (117); Arnaut's speech 140–47, "Ara vos prec" at 145; "Poi s'ascose nel foco che li affina" (148). Eliot's note 427 prints "gli affina" and "sovegna vos" (site note-427). <i>Ara Vos Prec</i> (Ovid Press, 1920): title page "Ara Vus Prec", corrected on the spine label; Eliot to Donald Gallup, 1936, blamed the Italian edition of Dante he had used (Evi Heinz; the Cambridge Eliot chapter). Pound, <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910): chapter II, on Arnaut Daniel, is titled "Il Miglior Fabbro". Site line 428 (Eliot's 427). The dedication: not in the 1922 printings; written in Pound's copy in January 1923, first printed in <i>Poems 1909–1925</i> (the site's own dating).

Substance:
1. "Eliot took the dedication to Pound from line 117": right, and worth adding that the phrase was already Pound's own, the title of his chapter on Arnaut in <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910), and that the dedication was added after 1922.
2. TITLE: Heart of Darkness
AUTHOR: Joseph Conrad   DATE: 1899   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: novella
WHAT: The epigraph the poem almost had.
PASSAGE: Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender during that supreme moment of complete knowledge? He cried in a whisper at some image, at some vision,—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath— / “The horror! The horror!”
NOTE:
Kurtz's last words, as Marlow reports them, stood at the head of the typescript. Pound doubted whether Conrad was "weighty enough to stand the citation"; Eliot defended it as "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," then replaced it with Petronius. ---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: <i>Heart of Darkness</i> (Gutenberg #219): the passage as quoted (the book text punctuates "at some vision—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath: 'The horror! The horror!'"); the opening: the Nellie, "a cruising yawl", at anchor in the sea-reach waiting for the tide, "The air was dark above Gravesend ... brooding motionless over the biggest, and the greatest, town on earth"; "Mistah Kurtz—he dead". Serialised in <i>Blackwood's</i>, February to April 1899; in book form 1902, with "Youth". The letters (wording from secondary quotation; I could not open the <i>Letters</i>): Pound, "I doubt if Conrad is weighty enough to stand the citation"; Eliot, "It is much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative"; Pound, "Do as you like about Conrad; who am I to grudge him his laurel crown?" Pound's first letter is dated in his own calendar, "24 Saturnus, An 1", which the 1971 facsimile reads as 24 December 1921 and later editors (Rainey; the revised <i>Letters</i>, vol. 1) as 24 January 1922; Eliot's reply and Pound's are of late January 1922. The typescript title page (facsimile, p. 3) carries the Conrad passage.

Substance:
1. The quotations from Pound and Eliot are right, but the exchange should be dated ("in January 1922"; the date of Pound's first letter is disputed) and sourced (the facsimile; <i>Letters</i>, vol. 1). It should also say that Pound gave way ("Do as you like about Conrad") and that Eliot nonetheless replaced it: the change was his, not Pound's. Rights: the Eliot phrases are within the rule.
2. "The 'heart of light' at line 41 still answers to it": a reading, and the note gives it as fact. Mark it ("readers have taken", or the site's gloss on 41 can carry it). Marlow's last words in the book are "the heart of an immense darkness".
3. "The book's opening, a boat on the Thames at Gravesend at dusk with London behind it": right (a yawl at anchor off Gravesend, waiting for the tide; sunset follows). "Is worth rereading with Part III in mind" is instruction; the fact behind it is Marlow's "And this also has been one of the dark places of the earth", the Thames as a river of empire, which the note can state.
4. Missing: Eliot went back to the same book for the epigraph of "The Hollow Men" (1925), "Mistah Kurtz—he dead". One clause.
5. Date 1899 is right for the serial; the book is 1902. Passage: punctuation differs from the book text (the site's ",—" and "breath—" look like the typescript's); not an error unless the site claims to print one or the other.

Draft:
TITLE: Heart of Darkness
BODY: Kurtz's last words, as Marlow reports them, stood on the title page of the typescript as its epigraph. Pound, sending the poem back in January 1922, doubted whether Conrad was "weighty enough to stand the citation"; Eliot answered that it was "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative", and Pound left it to him; but by the time the poem was printed the Petronius had taken its place (the letters are in the 1971 facsimile and in the <i>Letters</i>, volume 1). Eliot went back to the book for the epigraph of "The Hollow Men" (1925), "Mistah Kurtz—he dead". Readers have taken "the heart of light" at line 41 as Conrad's title turned round. The novella opens, like Part III, on the Thames: a yawl at anchor off Gravesend at the turn of the tide, the gloom over London behind, and Marlow's remark that this too "has been one of the dark places of the earth".

## source eliot-frontiers

**As it stood**

TITLE: The Frontiers of Criticism (Eliot on his own notes)
AUTHOR: T. S. Eliot   DATE: 1956   LANGUAGE: English   KIND: essay
WHAT: What Eliot later said about the notes.
PASSAGE: . . . the remarkable exposition of bogus scholarship that is still on view to-day. . . . I regret having sent so many enquirers off on a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail.
NOTE:
A lecture given at the University of Minnesota in 1956 to an audience of nearly fourteen thousand, in which Eliot, thirty-four years on, described the notes as padding that had got out of hand: he had meant only to list his references, to spike the guns of critics who had called him a plagiarist, and then, when the poem proved too short to make a book on its own, expanded them to fill pages. He also admitted they could not now be removed: readers had "bought" the notes with the poem. Take the notes as part of the poem and as a joke at its expense; both are true.

**B. Voice labels (the Voices lens: each voice has a LABEL shown on the line and a NOTE shown in the legend). Shared file; proposals only.**

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct

Checked: "The Frontiers of Criticism", the Gideon Seymour Memorial Lecture, Williams Arena, University of Minnesota, 30 April 1956 (pamphlet, University of Minnesota Press, 1956; <i>Sewanee Review</i>, 1956; <i>On Poetry and Poets</i>, 1957). Wording verified in secondary quotation only (archive.org copies are lending-only): "with a view to spiking the guns of critics of my earlier poems who had accused me of plagiarism"; "it was discovered that the poem was inconveniently short, so I set to work to expand the notes"; "the remarkable exposition of bogus scholarship that is still on view to-day"; "now they can never be unstuck"; "anyone who bought my book of poems, and found that the notes to <i>The Waste Land</i> were not in it, would demand his money back"; "I regret having sent so many enquirers off on a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail". Audience: 14,000 (Wikipedia); 13,523 in some accounts. Thirty-four years: right.

Substance:
1. "readers had 'bought' the notes with the poem": the quotation marks mark a word Eliot did not use in that sense. What he said is that the notes "can never be unstuck", because anyone who bought his collected poems and found them missing "would demand his money back". Rephrase without the false quotation.
2. "he had meant only to list his references, to spike the guns of critics who had called him a plagiarist": right ("critics of my earlier poems who had accused me of plagiarism"). "when the poem proved too short to make a book on its own, expanded them to fill pages": right ("inconveniently short"). "Padding that had got out of hand": a fair gloss on "bogus scholarship".
3. Missing, from the same lecture: the poem had no notes in its first two printings, the <i>Criterion</i> (October 1922) and the <i>Dial</i> (November 1922); they came with the Boni and Liveright book in December. A site that reproduces the notes should say so here.
4. "Take the notes as part of the poem and as a joke at its expense; both are true": an instruction and a judgement. What can be said: this is Eliot's account thirty-four years later, told for laughs, and the notes have been printed with the poem in every edition since 1922.
5. "nearly fourteen thousand": the usual figure. Passage: two phrases from a post-1929 text, within the rule; "to-day" is Faber's spelling.

Draft:
TITLE: The Frontiers of Criticism (Eliot on his own notes)
BODY: A lecture given at the University of Minnesota on 30 April 1956, in a sports arena, to an audience of about fourteen thousand. Eliot said that the poem had had no notes in the <i>Criterion</i> or the <i>Dial</i>; that he had first meant only to list his references, to spike the guns of critics who had accused his earlier poems of plagiarism; that when the poem was found "inconveniently short" for a book he expanded them to fill pages; and that they could not now be removed, since anyone who bought his poems and found the notes missing would want his money back. This is Eliot's account thirty-four years after the event, and it is told for laughs; the notes have been printed with the poem in every edition since 1922.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "Take the notes as part of the poem and as a joke at its expense; both are true." — an instruction and a bow. Cut it.
2. The first sentence runs from "A lecture" to "fill pages" with a colon and three stages inside it. Split it into three sentences.
3. "padding that had got out of hand" — the site's phrase in Eliot's mouth. If it is a paraphrase, make it read as one.
4. "to spike the guns of critics" — if this is Eliot's own phrase from the lecture it should be in quotation marks; if not, it reads as if it were.
Keep: "He also admitted they could not now be removed: readers had "bought" the notes with the poem."
Draft:
NOTE: Eliot gave the lecture at the University of Minnesota in 1956, to an audience of nearly fourteen thousand. Looking back thirty-four years, he said the notes had begun as a list of his references, meant to answer critics who had called him a plagiarist, and had then been expanded to fill pages when the poem proved too short to make a book on its own. He also admitted they could not now be removed: readers had "bought" the notes with the poem.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

Round one: all made. The false quotation of "bought" is gone, the <i>Criterion</i> and <i>Dial</i> printings without notes are in, the closing instruction is gone; date, place, audience and the 1957 printing are right, and the three short phrases from the lecture ("spiking the guns", "bogus scholarship", "a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail"), two of them already in the passage above the note, keep within the rule for a post-1929 text.

**Reader, round 2**

Not yet.
1. “A lecture given at the University of Minnesota on 30 April 1956, to an audience of about fourteen thousand, and printed in <i>On Poetry and Poets</i> (1957).” — a fragment as the opening sentence, the fault I raised on chaucer and dickens in round one. Replace with: “Eliot gave the lecture at the University of Minnesota on 30 April 1956, to an audience of about fourteen thousand; it was printed in <i>On Poetry and Poets</i> (1957).”

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:

AUTHOR: T. S. Eliot
DATE: 1956
WHAT: What Eliot later said about the notes.
LINES: 0
NOTE: A lecture given at the University of Minnesota on 30 April 1956, to an audience of about fourteen thousand, and printed in <i>On Poetry and Poets</i> (1957). Thirty-four years on, Eliot said he had meant only to list his references, “spiking the guns” of critics who had accused his earlier poems of plagiarism. The poem’s first printings, in two magazines, the <i>Criterion</i> and the <i>Dial</i>, had no notes; when Boni & Liveright were to make it a book, it was found too short, and he expanded the notes to fill the pages, with the result he called “bogus scholarship.” He regretted sending readers on “a wild goose chase after Tarot cards and the Holy Grail,” and said the notes could not now be removed, since anyone who bought his poems and found them missing would ask for his money back.

## voice poem

**As it stood**

LABEL: The poem's own voice
NOTE: The voice that opens the poem, describes the room in Part II, elegises Phlebas, and speaks the thunder's glosses. It is never quite a person.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: The 1922 text of Part I (work/poem-part1.md). The claims about other parts (the room in Part II, Phlebas, the thunder's glosses) are consistent with the poem; the note gives no line numbers, so none to check.
Substance:
1. "The voice that opens the poem" — where this voice ends and Marie begins (7/8) is an editorial choice, not a datum. The "us" of line 5 ("Winter kept us warm") recurs at line 8 ("Summer surprised us"), which is why many readers take 1–18 as a single voice. One sentence, here or in the lens intro, saying the divisions are the site's own would cover all ten labels.
2. "speaks the thunder's glosses" — an assignment, not a fact; and the glosses speak as an "I" with a "friend" ("My friend, blood shaking my heart", Part V), which sits oddly with "It is never quite a person".
3. Internal inconsistency: the sosostris label has "the poem's voice" speaking 43–46, which this note's list of appearances omits. Either add the introduction of Sosostris or drop the enumeration.
Draft: NOTE: The unassigned narrating voice: it opens the poem, introduces Madame Sosostris (43–46), describes the room in Part II, elegises Phlebas, and is given the thunder's glosses here. The divisions between it and the named voices are the site's own.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "It is never quite a person." — the aphorism at the end. What it means is plain and useful: this voice belongs to no character. Say that.
2. "speaks the thunder's glosses" — I stopped at "glosses". "interprets the thunder" is what happens in Part V.
3. The label "The poem's own voice" makes the poem a person, but for a legend it is the least bad short name. I would keep it unless the scholar has a better one.
Keep: the label; the list of where the voice speaks.
Draft:
LABEL: The poem's own voice
NOTE: The voice that opens the poem, describes the room at the start of Part II, speaks the elegy for Phlebas in Part IV, and interprets the thunder in Part V. It belongs to no named character.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet (one objection; the line numbers are right)
Checked: the Part V spans against the standard text, converting Eliot's marginal numbers to the site's (Eliot's notes at 401 "Datta", 411 the key, 424 the Fisher King = site 402, 412, 425): 396–400 runs from "Ganga was sunken" to "Then spoke the thunder", and 402–410, 412–417, 419–423 are exactly the three glosses; 322–330 is the opening block of Part V, ending "With a little patience". All correct.
Objections:
1. Missing: that the break between this voice and Marie at 7/8 is a reading. The "us" of line 5 ("Winter kept us warm") returns at line 8 ("Summer surprised us"), and readers from Cleanth Brooks on ("The Waste Land: Critique of the Myth", 1939 — the protagonist's reverie "gliding" into remembered talk in the Hofgarten) have taken 1–18 as one voice modulating. The legend now owns its other assignments (stranger, walker); this one should be owned too. Replace "It opens the poem," with: "It opens the poem (lines 1–7; that Marie begins at 8 rather than at 1 is this edition's reading),".
Round one: made — the introduction of Sosostris (43–46) is listed; the thunder glosses carry line numbers; the "never quite a person" sentence is gone. Still needed — the 7/8 boundary owned as a reading (above).

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
LABEL: The poem’s own voice
NOTE: The voice that belongs to no named character. It opens the poem, describes the room at the start of Part II, introduces Madame Sosostris (43–46), speaks the lines on the drowned Phlebas in Part IV and the opening of Part V (322–330), brings on the thunder (396–400), and answers each DA (402–410, 412–417, 419–423).

Proposed on top of it:
Present: the note ends “… and answers each DA (402–410, 412–417, 419–423).”
Proposed: add a last sentence: “That Marie begins at line 8 rather than at line 1 is this edition’s reading: the “us” of line 5 returns at line 8, and Cleanth Brooks (1939) read lines 1–18 as one voice.”
Reason: the legend now owns its other assignments (the voice in German, the walker) as readings; the break at 7/8 is one too, and the oldest reading runs the other way.

## voice marie

**As it stood**

LABEL: Marie
NOTE: A displaced Austrian aristocrat, talking over coffee. Lines 8–18, with one stranger's sentence at line 12.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Lines 8–18 against the 1922 text. The Larisch identification cited from memory of Valerie Eliot's note in the 1971 facsimile (not consultable online).
Substance:
1. "A displaced Austrian aristocrat" — an inference given as fact. The text supplies the archduke cousin (13), German speech and a rootless routine (18); "Austrian" and "displaced" rest on those and on the identification of the speaker with Countess Marie Larisch, niece of Empress Elisabeth (proposed from her memoir <i>My Past</i> by G. L. K. Morris, 1954; Valerie Eliot's facsimile note records that Eliot had met Larisch and that the sledding lines followed her conversation). Defensible, but the legend should either lean on the documented identification or claim only what the text gives ("an aristocrat of the old Austrian circle — the archduke of line 13 was her cousin").
2. "talking over coffee" — the coffee is inside the memory (11: "And drank coffee, and talked for an hour", in the remembered Hofgarten). That she speaks over coffee now is an inference from the conversational run of 8–18, not something the poem says.
3. "with one stranger's sentence at line 12" — that line 12 is another speaker is a reading, stated here as fact; the poem marks no change of speaker (see voice stranger).
Draft: NOTE: An aristocrat of the old Austrian circle — the archduke of line 13 was her cousin — remembering a summer and a childhood. Lines 8–18; the site gives the German sentence at 12 to another voice.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: no
Problems:
1. "displaced" — a mood word that reads a whole history into eleven lines. The poem gives an archduke cousin, a Lithuanian birthplace and winters in the south. If "Austrian aristocrat" rests on the identification with Countess Marie Larisch, the scholar should say whether the legend can carry a hedge.
2. "with one stranger's sentence at line 12" — this decides a disputed line (is line 12 Marie or someone else?) in passing. The legend may state the site's choice, but "stranger" presumes.
Keep: "Marie"; "talking over coffee"; the line range.
Draft:
LABEL: Marie
NOTE: An Austrian aristocrat remembering her childhood over coffee. Lines 8–18, except the German sentence at line 12, which the site gives to another voice.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: Valerie Eliot's note in the facsimile (p. 126, as quoted in secondary accounts): Eliot met Countess Marie Larisch, "when and where is not known", and "his description of the sledding … was taken verbatim from a conversation he had with this niece and confidante of the Austrian Empress"; the Hofgarten is Munich's, the Starnbergersee just south of the city.
Round one: made — "displaced Austrian aristocrat" replaced by what the text and the facsimile support; the coffee placed inside the memory; line 12 owned as the edition's choice. Nothing still needed. (Not an objection: "a sled ride in the mountains" joins line 17 to 13–16, which the poem leaves as separate sentences.)

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
LABEL: Marie
NOTE: A woman remembering a summer: coffee and talk in the Hofgarten in Munich, and a sled ride in the mountains as a child with her cousin the archduke. Lines 8–18, except the sentence of German at line 12, which this edition gives to another speaker. Valerie Eliot’s edition of the drafts records that Eliot had met Countess Marie Larisch, niece of the Empress Elisabeth of Austria, and that the sledding story was hers.

Nothing to add: both sides satisfied with the text as it stands.

**As it now stands**

The front-matter thread's text, as it now stands. Part I had nothing to add.

**Scholar, round 1**

2. "overheard" — the poem marks no new speaker at 12; hearing the sentence as another party's talk rather than Marie's own is a reading (it follows from taking Marie for an Austrian, since the line claims Lithuanian birth and true Germanness). Both readings are current; the legend should own the choice as the site's.

## voice prophet

**As it stood**

LABEL: The prophet
NOTE: Ezekiel's and Isaiah's voice, addressing the 'son of man' from under the red rock. Lines 19–30.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Lines 19–30 against the 1922 text and Eliot's notes (20: Ezekiel II.i; 23: Ecclesiastes XII.v); KJV wording of Ezekiel 2:1, Ecclesiastes 12:5 and Isaiah 32:2 verified at gutenberg.org/ebooks/10.
Substance:
1. "Ezekiel's and Isaiah's voice" — Eliot's own notes cite Ezekiel (II.i) and Ecclesiastes (XII.v). Isaiah is a commentators' parallel for the rock (XXXII.2, "the shadow of a great rock in a weary land"; so Southam, Rainey, Ricks–McCue), not Eliot's. The legend asserts the uncited prophet as fact and omits a book Eliot cites.
2. "Ezekiel's… voice, addressing the 'son of man'" — in Ezekiel the voice that says "Son of man, stand upon thy feet" (II.i) is the Lord's, addressing the prophet. The passage speaks as that commanding voice; it is not Ezekiel who says "son of man".
Draft: NOTE: A voice out of the Old Testament, addressing the hearer as 'Son of man' — the address the Lord uses to Ezekiel (Eliot's note to line 20 cites Ezekiel II.i; his note to 23, Ecclesiastes XII.v; commentators add Isaiah XXXII.2 for the rock's shadow). Lines 19–30.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: keep
Reads as a model wrote it: no
Problems:
1. "from under the red rock" — the speaker invites the listener under the rock; the note has him already there. Small, and only worth touching if the legend is being edited anyway.
Keep: all of it.
Draft (optional):
NOTE: A voice in the manner of Ezekiel and Isaiah, addressing the listener as "son of man" and offering the shadow of the red rock. Lines 19–30.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Round one: made — Ecclesiastes restored as Eliot's citation at 23; Isaiah marked as a link made by others; "Son of man" given to God addressing Ezekiel. Nothing still needed.

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
LABEL: The prophet
NOTE: Lines 19–30: a voice speaking as the Old Testament prophets do, addressing its hearer as “Son of man,” the words with which God addresses Ezekiel (Eliot’s note cites Ezekiel 2:1), and offering the shade of the red rock. Eliot’s note on line 23 cites Ecclesiastes; “the shadow of this red rock” has been linked to Isaiah 32:2.

Nothing to add: both sides satisfied with the text as it stands.

## voice sosostris

**As it stood**

LABEL: Madame Sosostris
NOTE: The clairvoyante, dealing the cards. Lines 46–59, framed by the poem's voice at 43–46.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Lines 43–59 against the 1922 text.
Substance:
1. "Lines 46–59, framed by the poem's voice at 43–46" — line 46 is claimed for both voices, and "framed" implies enclosure on both sides. In the text her speech begins inside line 46 ("Here, said she" — "said she" being the narrator's tag), after an introduction in the narrating voice (43 to mid-46); nothing follows her at 59 but a new scene. Say "introduced by", and place the join inside 46.
2. The parenthesis at 48, "(Those are pearls that were his eyes. Look!)", is Ariel's song (The Tempest I.ii) and returns at line 125; some readers hear it as an interpolating voice rather than the fortune-teller's patter. Giving it to Sosostris is a defensible choice, but it is a choice the gloss could own.
Draft: NOTE: The clairvoyante, dealing the cards. Her speech runs from inside line 46 ("Here, said she") to 59, after an introduction in the poem's voice, 43–46.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: keep
Reads as a model wrote it: no
Problems:
1. "Lines 46–59, framed by the poem's voice at 43–46" — line 46 is in both ranges. It is right, since she starts mid-line at "Here, said she", but it made me look twice.
Keep: all of it.
Draft (optional):
NOTE: The clairvoyante, dealing the cards. From "Here, said she" at line 46 to line 59; lines 43–46, introducing her, are the poem's own voice.

**Scholar, round 2**

Satisfied.

## voice walker

**As it stood**

LABEL: The walker in the City
NOTE: The 'I' who crosses London Bridge in the fog, cries out to Stetson, meets Mr. Eugenides, and walks from the Strand to Lower Thames Street. Lines 60–76, 207–211, 257–265.

**C. Drafts records (the Drafts page and the Drafts lens). The first is in Part I’s own file; the other two are in the shared site file (proposals only).**

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Lines 60–76 against the 1922 text of Part I; the Part III ranges against the standard text, anchored by Eliot's own notes (210 currants; 257 "V. The Tempest, as before"; 264 St. Magnus Martyr) — site numbers agree with Eliot's below 346.
Substance:
1. "Lines … 207–211" — the Eugenides encounter runs to 214. Line 211 ends "C.i.f. London: documents at sight," and 212–214 — "Asked me in demotic French / To luncheon at the Cannon Street Hotel / Followed by a weekend at the Metropole." — are still the walker's narration ("Asked me"). As given, the range cuts its own speaker off mid-sentence. It should read 207–214.
2. "walks from the Strand to Lower Thames Street" — the text has the music creeping "along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street" (258), and the walker "can sometimes hear" the mandoline "Beside a public bar in Lower Thames Street" (259–260). The named walk is Strand to Queen Victoria Street; Lower Thames Street is where he sometimes hears the music, not a stated terminus.
3. That the "I" of 257 resumes this walker rather than continuing Tiresias's sequence (215 ff.) is a reading — fine as the lens's assignment if the lens owns its divisions.
Draft: NOTE: The 'I' of the City: he watches the crowd flow over London Bridge and cries out to Stetson; Mr. Eugenides asks him to luncheon; he hears the mandoline by the bar in Lower Thames Street. Lines 60–76, 207–214, 257–265.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: keep
Reads as a model wrote it: no
Problems: none. The four clauses are a list of where the voice speaks, not a rhythm, and each is a fact I can check against the line numbers.
Keep: all of it.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
Objections:
1. Wrong: "Mr. Eugenides (207–211)". The sentence about him runs 209–214, and the walker speaks all of it: "Mr. Eugenides, the Smyrna merchant / … / Asked me in demotic French / To luncheon at the Cannon Street Hotel / Followed by a weekend at the Metropole." Source: the 1922 text; Eliot's own note at 210 (the currants) fixes the numbering, and site numbers agree with Eliot's below 346. Replace with: "Mr. Eugenides (207–214)".
On the lens's span (207–211 walker, 212–214 "eugenides"): wrong, not a reading. Reasons for the other thread:
(a) Syntax. 209–214 is one sentence — subject "Mr. Eugenides" (209), verb "Asked" (212), object "me", the walker. The split at 211/212 puts a subject and its verb in different mouths.
(b) Eugenides has no words in the poem. The invitation is reported, not quoted — an infinitive construction, "Asked me … / To luncheon …" — and the poem pointedly withholds the words themselves: "in demotic French" is the walker's description, and judgement, of speech we never hear. A voice with no words is not a voice.
(c) The poem, and the lens, mark speech where it is given: Sosostris's after "said she", the hyacinth girl's and the cry to Stetson in quotation marks. The lens's own precedent runs the other way from this span: at 15–16 the archduke's quoted words ("He said, Marie, / Marie, hold on tight") stay inside Marie's span. No voice is made for words the poem does quote; none should be made for words it does not.
(d) The most that could be defended as a reading is 213–214 heard as free indirect speech — the walker echoing the invitation's terms — and that is weak, since "Followed by a weekend at the Metropole" is the narrator's own summarising, knowing connective. 212 can never be Eugenides's: it is the walker's verb and the walker's "me". If a eugenides span is kept at all, it is 213–214 at most, labelled as a reading; the clean course is 207–214 to the walker, which is what the note's own description already says ("The 'I' of three passages").
Round one: made — the Strand and Lower Thames Street described as the text has them; the one-voice reading owned ("read here as one voice"). Still needed — 207–214.

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
LABEL: The walker in the City of London
NOTE: The “I” of three passages, read here as one voice: the crowd on London Bridge and the meeting with Stetson (60–76), Mr. Eugenides (207–211), and the music along the Strand and the bar in Lower Thames Street (257–265).

**C. Drafts records**

The two records about the whole poem, as the other thread now has them:

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “Mr. Eugenides (207–211)”, and in the spans, [207, 211, "walker"] and [212, 214, "eugenides"].
Proposed: “Mr. Eugenides (207–214)”, and one span, [207, 214, "walker"].
Reason: lines 209–214 are one sentence, with “Mr. Eugenides” as its subject (209), “Asked” as its verb (212) and the walker as its object (“me”); the split at 211/212 puts a subject and its verb in different mouths. Eugenides has no words in the poem: the invitation is reported (“Asked me in demotic French / To luncheon …”), and “in demotic French” is the walker’s description of speech the poem withholds. The lens’s own precedent runs the other way: at lines 15–16 the archduke’s quoted words stay inside Marie’s span. The most that could be defended as a reading is 213–214 heard as the walker echoing the invitation’s terms, and that is weak. These are Part III’s lines, so the Part III thread may want a say; if the eugenides span goes, the “eugenides” voice has no lines left and its record wants looking at.

## draft boston

**As it stood**

TITLE: Before "April": a night in Boston
BODY:
The typescript of Part I opens with fifty-four lines in a loose, chatty verse about a night out with the boys in Boston: drinks at "Tom's place," a burlesque show, a girl, a run from a policeman, a sunrise walk home. The whole passage is struck through in pencil, apparently by Eliot before Pound saw it. What remains begins already tired, and "April is the cruellest month" is the second first line the poem had. Pound's comments on the rest of Part I are light; he queried a few words and let the section stand.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Edmund Wilson's review of the facsimile, NYRB, 18 Nov 1971 (nybooks.com/articles/1971/11/18/the-first-waste-landii/): the excision of the opening was "made by Eliot himself"; the narrator is picked up by the police and rescued by an acquaintance; Myrtle refuses him but gives him bed, bath and breakfast. Draft lines as quoted in criticism ("First we had a couple of feelers down at Tom's place"; "When we got into the show, up in Row A"). Accounts derived from the facsimile give 54 lines, struck lightly in pencil, with "April" at the top of the second typescript page. I could not consult the 1971 facsimile itself.
Substance:
1. "a burlesque show" — the draft says only "the show" ("When we got into the show, up in Row A", where the narrator kicks at the drum and the girl squeals). No account I can reach calls it a burlesque; "a show" is what the page supports.
2. "a run from a policeman" — there is no run. The narrator is picked up by a plain-clothes man (a "fly cop") and got off when a well-connected acquaintance intervened (Wilson's account of the facsimile). "A brush with a policeman" would be accurate.
3. "struck through in pencil, apparently by Eliot before Pound saw it" — the pencil cancellation is generally credited to Eliot (so Wilson, reviewing the facsimile in 1971), and the leaf carries no comment by Pound; but nothing records whether Pound had read the lines first. "Before Pound saw it" is conjecture stated as near-fact; say what is known.
4. No source is named. The cancelled page is known from Valerie Eliot's 1971 facsimile; a record about the drafts should say so once.
5. "What remains begins already tired" — an interpretation in the register the site is purging (compare the brief's "a poem that starts already exhausted"), stated as description; and "the second first line the poem had" is the counting flourish, true only trivially.
6. "fifty-four lines" — matches the published descriptions of the leaf; I could not count them against the facsimile. Worth one check against the book (pp. 4–5) before this ships.
Rights: the record describes and quotes only "Tom's place" — compliant; a rewrite should hold to that.
Draft: BODY: In Valerie Eliot's 1971 facsimile, the typescript of Part I opens with fifty-four cancelled lines: a night out in Boston told in a loose vernacular — drinks at "Tom's place", a show, a call at Myrtle's, a brush with a policeman smoothed over by a well-connected friend, a walk home at sunrise. The lines are struck through in pencil, generally taken to be Eliot's own cancellation; the leaf carries no comment by Pound, and whether he read it is not recorded. The published opening, "April is the cruellest month", stood at the top of the typescript's second page. Pound's marks on the rest of Part I are few.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "What remains begins already tired" — the tell named in the common brief ("a poem that starts already exhausted"). Cut.
2. ""April is the cruellest month" is the second first line the poem had" — a trick of phrase made to be quoted. The fact is simply that it was not the first line of the draft.
3. TITLE: "Before "April": a night in Boston" stages the record. "The cancelled opening: a night out in Boston" says what it is.
Keep: the first sentence (the list after the colon is what was in the passage, which is what I want to know); "The whole passage is struck through in pencil, apparently by Eliot before Pound saw it."; the last sentence.
Draft:
TITLE: The cancelled opening: a night out in Boston
BODY: The typescript of Part I opens with fifty-four lines of loose, chatty verse about a night out with the boys in Boston: drinks at "Tom's place," a burlesque show, a girl, a run from a policeman, a walk home at sunrise. The whole passage is struck through in pencil, apparently by Eliot before Pound saw it. "April is the cruellest month" was not the poem's first line until it was cut. Pound's comments on the rest of Part I are light: he queried a few words and let the section stand.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "Burlesque", "a run from a policeman", "what remains begins already tired" and "the second first line" go; the facsimile named as the source; the cancellation stated as the facsimile and Wilson state it; the sequence of the night made to agree with the gloss on line 1, which both sides have settled.
TITLE: The cancelled opening: a night out in Boston
BODY:
In the typescript, Part I opens with fifty-four lines about a night out in Boston, told by one of a party of young men in loose, chatty verse: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call on a woman who turns him away, a policeman who picks him up and a man he knows who gets him off, and a walk home at sunrise. The lines are struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile (1971) attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after it; whether Pound read the lines is not recorded. "April is the cruellest month" stood at the top of the typescript's second page. Pound's comments on the rest of Part I are few: he queried a few words and let the section stand.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet (one objection; the rest is right)
Checked: Edmund Wilson, NYRB, 18 November 1971 (the excision "made by Eliot himself"; Myrtle "refuses him but provides a bed, a bath, and ham and eggs"; picked up by the police, rescued by an acquaintance); the accounts derived from the facsimile's first leaf (fifty-four lines, pencil, "April" at the top of the second page). I still could not open the facsimile.
Objections:
1. Wrong: "a call on a woman who turns him away". She refuses him as a customer but does not send him off: she gives him a bed, a bath and breakfast (Wilson's account of the facsimile; the woman is Myrtle, who keeps the house). Replace with: "a call at Myrtle's, where he is refused but given a bed, a bath and breakfast,". No draft wording is quoted, so the rights rule holds.
Not objections, verification notes: "Valerie Eliot's facsimile (1971) attributes the stroke to Eliot" — I could not check the facsimile's own apparatus; Wilson, reviewing it, is the witness I can cite, and the accounts derived from the facsimile say the page "appears to have been" crossed out by Eliot. If the facsimile hedges, the record should read "the stroke is taken to be Eliot's". "Fifty-four lines" and "Pound's annotations begin only after it" match every account I can reach; both deserve one look at the book (facsimile pp. 4–5) before this ships.
Round one: made — "burlesque" and "a run from a policeman" gone; the pick-up and the friend who gets him off stated as Wilson states them; the facsimile named; "before Pound saw it" replaced by "whether Pound read the lines is not recorded"; "begins already tired" and "the second first line" gone. Still needed — only the Myrtle wording.

**Reader, round 2**

Not yet.
1. “a policeman who picks him up and a man he knows who gets him off” — both idioms have more than one meaning: a policeman can pick a drunk up off the pavement, and a friend can get a man off a charge or off the street. Replace with: “a policeman who takes him in and a man he knows who gets him released”. If that is not what the draft shows, the scholar’s account of the facts governs, but the idioms should still go.
2. “Valerie Eliot’s facsimile (1971) attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound’s annotations begin only after it; whether Pound read the lines is not recorded.” — “the stroke”, coming after “struck through”, reads as a different word, and “after it” has nothing firm to hold on to. Replace with: “Valerie Eliot’s facsimile (1971) attributes the cancellation to Eliot, and Pound’s annotations begin only after the cancelled lines; whether Pound read them is not recorded.”
3. “Pound’s comments on the rest of Part I are few: he queried a few words and let the section stand.” — “few” twice in one sentence. Replace with: “Pound wrote little on the rest of Part I: he queried a few words and let the section stand.”

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
Editor's decision: rewrite. "Burlesque", "a run from a policeman", "what remains begins already tired" and "the second first line" go; the facsimile named as the source; the cancellation stated as the facsimile and Wilson state it; the sequence of the night made to agree with the gloss on line 1, which both sides have settled.
TITLE: The cancelled opening: a night out in Boston
BODY:
In the typescript, Part I opens with fifty-four lines about a night out in Boston, told by one of a party of young men in loose, chatty verse: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call on a woman who turns him away, a policeman who picks him up and a man he knows who gets him off, and a walk home at sunrise. The lines are struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile (1971) attributes the stroke to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after it; whether Pound read the lines is not recorded. "April is the cruellest month" stood at the top of the typescript's second page. Pound's comments on the rest of Part I are few: he queried a few words and let the section stand.

Editor's draft for round three:
Decision: the reader's three wording changes taken, with the policeman sentence put in the scholar's terms (the narrator is stopped by a policeman, whom an acquaintance talks round) rather than either side's idiom.
TITLE: The cancelled opening: a night out in Boston
BODY:
In the typescript, Part I opens with fifty-four lines about a night out in Boston, told by one of a party of young men in loose, chatty verse: drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call on a woman who turns him away, a policeman who stops him and a man he knows who talks the policeman round, and a walk home at sunrise. The lines are struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile (1971) attributes the cancellation to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after the cancelled lines; whether Pound read them is not recorded. "April is the cruellest month" stood at the top of the typescript's second page. Pound wrote little on the rest of Part I: he queried a few words and let the section stand.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: not yet (one objection)
Checked: Edmund Wilson's review of the facsimile, NYRB, 18 November 1971 (nybooks.com/articles/1971/11/18/the-first-waste-landii/), re-read for the sequence; and, as a second witness, a 2025 account that paraphrases the draft's episodes in order (thecolumbogame.com, "POETS Day! 'The Waste Land' Lees"). I still could not open the facsimile.
Objections:
1. Wrong: the order of the night. A list of events in one sentence reads as a sequence whatever the intention, and the sequence given — policeman, then Myrtle's — is the reverse of Wilson's, in whom the narrator "goes to a brothel where a woman named Myrtle … tells him he is too drunk", and then: "Leaving the brothel, he is picked up by 'a fly cop,' … but he is rescued by a man he knows, with whom he goes off in a cab". Replace the list with: "drinks at "Tom's place," a show, a call at Myrtle's, where he is refused but given a bed, a bath and breakfast, a brush with a policeman on the way out that a man he knows smooths over, and a walk home at sunrise." — "on the way out" is Wilson's "Leaving the brothel", and fixes the order without a second sentence. No draft wording is quoted.

The order question. Wilson's sequence is: drunk at Tom's place; the brothel, where Myrtle refuses him; "Leaving the brothel", the fly cop; rescue by a man he knows; the cab, in which the cabman and another passenger get out to run a hundred yards on a bet; "so I got out to see the sunrise, and walked home". The show is not in Wilson's summary but is in the draft before Myrtle's (the drum in Row A, the girl who squealed), and the second witness gives the whole run in the same order: Tom's place, the show, the loss of Steve, Myrtle's, the alley and the fly cop, the man who knows the officer, the German Club, the cab and the bet, the sunrise walk. So the gloss on line 1 (Tom's place, the show, Myrtle's at two, the policeman, a man he knows, a cab at sunrise, a walk home) has the order right and needs no correction; the round-two placing of the policeman first was the error, and the wording above puts it back.

One caution, not an objection. Wilson reads the Myrtle passage as the narrator's own; the typescript introduces it with "Then we lost Steve" and sets it in parentheses, and the second witness gives it to Steve. The site follows Wilson, and the wording above keeps his "he". If the editor would rather not decide, "a call at Myrtle's — refused, but given a bed, a bath and breakfast —" says the same without a subject.

**Reader, round 3**

Not yet, on punctuation only.
1. "…a call at Myrtle's, where he is refused but given a bed, a bath and breakfast, and a walk home at sunrise." — the Myrtle item now has a list inside it, so at "a bath and breakfast, and a walk home" I read the walk as one more thing she gives him before I saw the outer list had resumed. Semicolons between the night's events fix it, keep every settled word, and imply no order. Replace the list with: "drinks at "Tom's place"; a show; a brush with a policeman that a man he knows smooths over; a call at Myrtle's, where he is refused but given a bed, a bath and breakfast; and a walk home at sunrise."

Everything else in the record passes: the cancellation sentence, the second-page sentence and the Pound sentence read as a person would say them, and nothing in it tells the reader what to feel.

**As it now stands**

Decision after round three: the scholar's order taken (Wilson's: Tom's place, the show, Myrtle's, the policeman on the way out, the man he knows, the walk home at sunrise), which also agrees with the gloss on line 1; the reader's semicolons kept, so that the bed, the bath and the breakfast do not run into the walk home.
TITLE: The cancelled opening: a night out in Boston
BODY:
In the typescript, Part I opens with fifty-four lines about a night out in Boston, told by one of a party of young men in loose, chatty verse: drinks at "Tom's place"; a show; a call at Myrtle's, where he is refused but given a bed, a bath and breakfast; a brush with a policeman on the way out, which a man he knows smooths over; and a walk home at sunrise. The lines are struck through in pencil. Valerie Eliot's facsimile (1971) attributes the cancellation to Eliot, and Pound's annotations begin only after the cancelled lines; whether Pound read them is not recorded. "April is the cruellest month" stood at the top of the typescript's second page. Pound wrote little on the rest of Part I: he queried a few words and let the section stand.


## draft title-draft

**As it stood**

TITLE: The title that was crossed out
BODY:
Across the top of the typescript of the first two parts Eliot typed "He Do the Police in Different Voices," from Dickens's *Our Mutual Friend*. "The Waste Land" replaced it, with Weston's Grail country and Malory's "waste land" of the Dolorous Stroke behind it. The Dickens title survives in the notes only as a habit of mind: the poem reads the world's news aloud in every voice it can do.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: <i>Our Mutual Friend</i>, Book the First, ch. 16, at gutenberg.org/ebooks/883: "You mightn't think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices." Malory, <i>Le Morte d'Arthur</i>, Book XVII ch. iii, at gutenberg.org/ebooks/1252: "men call it the lands of the two marches, the waste land, for that dolorous stroke". Weston, <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, at gutenberg.org/ebooks/4090 (the Waste Land motif "the very heart of our problem"). Eliot's 1922 headnote (work/poem-part1.md) crediting the title to Weston. Wikipedia and centenary accounts confirm the heading on the typescripts of the first two parts only.
Substance:
1. "The Dickens title survives in the notes only as a habit of mind" — false on its face: the 1922 Notes nowhere mention Dickens. The working title was not public at all until the drafts — sold to John Quinn in 1922, resurfaced in the Berg Collection and published in 1971. The rest of the sentence ("the poem reads the world's news aloud in every voice it can do") is a reading given as fact, with the bow tied.
2. TITLE "The title that was crossed out" — I find no account that the typed heading was itself struck through; what is reported is that the title was dropped. Unless the facsimile shows a cancellation stroke through the heading, call it the title that was dropped.
3. "with Weston's Grail country and Malory's 'waste land' of the Dolorous Stroke behind it" — Weston is documented: Eliot's own headnote says "Not only the title, but the plan…" were suggested by her book. Malory is not cited by Eliot anywhere. The phrase is genuinely Malory's (Book XVII ch. iii — and there of King Hurlame's stroke; Balin's "dolorous stroke" is Book II), and commentators adduce it as the Grail tradition's term; but as written the note makes Malory a source of the title, which is an editorial inference.
4. Missing: the Dickens sentence itself, which a reader would want and which is freely quotable (1865) — Betty Higden praising the foundling Sloppy's newspaper reading.
Draft: BODY: The typescripts of the first two parts are headed "He Do the Police in Different Voices". The phrase is Betty Higden's, in Dickens's <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (1865), praising the foundling Sloppy: "You mightn't think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices." Eliot dropped the heading before publication. His 1922 headnote credits the title he kept to Jessie L. Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, whose subject is the Grail legend's Waste Land; the phrase itself is as old as Malory — "the waste land, for that dolorous stroke".

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "The Dickens title survives in the notes only as a habit of mind: the poem reads the world's news aloud in every voice it can do." — the poem as a person, a colon-formula, and a sentence I could not parse: which notes? Eliot's notes do not mention Dickens. Cut it, and put something a reader can use in its place, such as when the title became known.
2. "with Weston's Grail country and Malory's "waste land" of the Dolorous Stroke behind it" — compressed to the point of code. I do not know from this what the Dolorous Stroke is. One clause would tell me.
3. The TITLE says "crossed out"; the body only says it was replaced. If it was struck through on the typescript, say so in the body; if not, change the title.
Keep: the first sentence.
Draft:
TITLE: The title that was dropped (or keep "crossed out", if the typescript shows it)
BODY: Across the top of the typescript of the first two parts Eliot typed "He Do the Police in Different Voices," a line from Dickens's <i>Our Mutual Friend</i>. "The Waste Land" replaced it. The phrase comes from the Grail legend: the blighted country of Weston's book, and of Malory, where the land is laid waste by the Dolorous Stroke. The Dickens title was not known until the drafts were published in 1971.
(The scholar should confirm the Malory clause and the last sentence.)

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet (one objection)
Checked: <i>Our Mutual Friend</i>, Book I ch. 16 (gutenberg.org/ebooks/883): the quotation is exact, and Sloppy's mangle and foundling state are Dickens's. Eliot's headnote as in work/poem-part1.md. The Conrad quotation "stands at the front of this manuscript" (British Library account of the Berg typescript), and Pound's letter of 24 January 1922 discusses it while proposing cuts.
Objections:
1. Wrong: "The title page Eliot typed for the finished poem". The typed title leaf carries the Conrad epigraph, which Pound was objecting to on 24 January 1922 while the poem was still being cut, and which Eliot then replaced; the leaf was not typed for the finished poem, and saying so misstates the sequence. Source: the facsimile's title leaf; Pound to Eliot, [24 January 1922] (Letters, vol. 1). Replace with: "The title page of the typescript reads "The Waste Land," and his headnote to the notes says …" — which also matches the epigraph record's description of the same leaf.
Round one: made — "survives in the notes" replaced by "Dickens is not mentioned in the notes"; "crossed out" gone from the title; Malory gone; the Dickens sentence quoted with book and chapter. Nothing else still needed.

**Reader, round 2**

Not yet.
1. The second sentence runs from “The words are Betty Higden’s” through the mangle and the police news to the end of the quotation, sixty words without a stop. Replace it with: “The words are Betty Higden’s, in Dickens’s <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (Book I, chapter 16). She is praising Sloppy, the foundling who lives with her and turns her mangle, for the way he reads the police news aloud from the paper, doing the voices: “You mightn’t think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices.””

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: The earlier title
BODY: The typescripts of Parts I and II carry the heading “He Do the Police in Different Voices,” with “Part I” and “Part II” beneath it. The words are Betty Higden’s in Dickens’s <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (Book I, chapter 16), praising Sloppy, the foundling who lives with her and turns her mangle, for the way he reads the police news in the paper aloud, doing the voices: “You mightn’t think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices.” The title page Eliot typed for the finished poem reads “The Waste Land,” and his headnote to the notes says that the title, the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism were suggested by Jessie L. Weston’s book on the Grail legend, <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>. Dickens is not mentioned in the notes.

Proposed on top of it:
Present: “The words are Betty Higden’s in Dickens’s <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (Book I, chapter 16), praising Sloppy, the foundling who lives with her and turns her mangle, for the way he reads the police news in the paper aloud, doing the voices: “You mightn’t think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices.” The title page Eliot typed for the finished poem reads “The Waste Land,” and his headnote to the notes says …”
Proposed: “The words are Betty Higden’s, in Dickens’s <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (Book I, chapter 16). She is praising Sloppy, the foundling who lives with her and turns her mangle, for the way he reads the police news aloud from the paper, doing the voices: “You mightn’t think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices.” The title page of the typescript reads “The Waste Land,” and his headnote to the notes says …”
Reason: the title leaf carried the Conrad epigraph that Pound was objecting to on 24 January 1922, while the poem was still being cut, and that Eliot then replaced; it was not typed for the finished poem, and the epigraph record beside this one describes the same leaf as the typescript’s title page. The reader asked for a stop in a sentence of sixty words.

**As it now stands**

The front-matter thread's text, as it now stands. With a proposal from Part I in shared-proposals-part1.md.
TITLE: The earlier title
BODY: The typescripts of Parts I and II carry the heading “He Do the Police in Different Voices,” with “Part I” and “Part II” beneath it. The words are Betty Higden’s in Dickens’s <i>Our Mutual Friend</i> (Book I, chapter 16), praising Sloppy, the foundling who lives with her and turns her mangle, for the way he reads the police news in the paper aloud, doing the voices: “You mightn’t think it, but Sloppy is a beautiful reader of a newspaper. He do the Police in different voices.” The title page Eliot typed for the finished poem reads “The Waste Land,” and his headnote to the notes says that the title, the plan and a good deal of the incidental symbolism were suggested by Jessie L. Weston’s book on the Grail legend, <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>. Dickens is not mentioned in the notes.


## draft epigraph-draft

**As it stood**

TITLE: The epigraph that was replaced
BODY:
Kurtz's "The horror! the horror!" from Conrad's *Heart of Darkness* stood at the head of the typescript. Pound doubted Conrad was "weighty enough to stand the citation"; Eliot called it "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and then substituted the Sibyl from Petronius. The change turns a dying man's verdict on his life into an immortal's wish to be done with hers.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Conrad's text at gutenberg.org/ebooks/219: "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender during that supreme moment of complete knowledge? He cried in a whisper at some image, at some vision—he cried out twice, a cry that was no more than a breath: 'The horror! The horror!'" The Pound–Eliot exchange as quoted in accounts of the facsimile and the Letters ("weighty enough to stand the citation"; "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative"; Pound's "Do as you like about Conrad: who am I to grudge him his laurel crown?"), confirmed at towntopics.com/2022/11/09/soundings-and-echoes-the-waste-land-at-100/. The Sibyl's myth from Ovid, Metamorphoses XIV (from knowledge; not fetched).
Substance:
1. "Kurtz's 'The horror! the horror!' … stood at the head of the typescript" — what stood there was the fuller passage: Marlow's account of Kurtz's death, from "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender…" down to the cry. Conrad (1899) is out of copyright, so the record can give the passage or its close rather than the two words alone.
2. Sequence: after Eliot's defence Pound gave way ("Do as you like about Conrad: who am I to grudge him his laurel crown?") and Eliot changed it anyway. As written, the note implies Eliot yielded to Pound; the half-sentence restores what happened. The exchange should also carry its source: letters of January 1922, quoted in Valerie Eliot's 1971 facsimile.
3. "an immortal's wish to be done with hers" — the Sibyl is not an immortal. Apollo granted her as many years as the grains of sand in her hand, and she did not ask for youth (Ovid, Metamorphoses XIV); she is withering toward nothing, which is why she wants to die. The closing antithesis, besides being in the manner under purge, misstates the myth. Cut or correct.
Rights: Conrad may be quoted freely (1899); the phrases from Pound's and Eliot's letters are within the phrase-or-two rule.
Draft: BODY: On the typescript the epigraph was from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i>: Marlow's account of Kurtz's death — "Did he live his life again in every detail of desire, temptation, and surrender…?" — ending in the cry "The horror! the horror!" Pound demurred: Conrad, he wrote, might not be "weighty enough to stand the citation". Eliot defended the passage as "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative"; Pound gave way ("who am I to grudge him his laurel crown?"); and Eliot replaced it all the same with the Sibyl of Petronius's <i>Satyricon</i>, who, asked by the boys what she wants, answers that she wants to die (letters of January 1922, quoted in Valerie Eliot's 1971 facsimile).

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: in places
Problems:
1. "The change turns a dying man's verdict on his life into an immortal's wish to be done with hers." — the balanced closing sentence, a reading presented as what the change "does". It is a thought for an essay; here it tells me what to make of the swap before I have read either epigraph. Cut it, or mark it as a reading and say it plainly.
Keep: the first two sentences, which are the record.
Draft:
BODY: Kurtz's "The horror! the horror!" from Conrad's <i>Heart of Darkness</i> stood at the head of the typescript. Pound doubted Conrad was "weighty enough to stand the citation"; Eliot called it "much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative," and then substituted the Sibyl from Petronius.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: Conrad's text (gutenberg.org/ebooks/219); the date — Pound's "24 Saturnus, An 1" is 24 January 1922 (Paige's 1950 edition misread it as December); Eliot's reply and Pound's "Do as you like about Conrad" as quoted in accounts of the facsimile; the epigraph itself (Satyricon ch. 48, in work/poem-part1.md): Trimalchio, the boys, "I want to die"; Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV, for the years without youth.
Round one: made — the epigraph described as the passage ending in the cry; Pound's climb-down, and Eliot replacing it anyway; the date; the Sibyl no longer "immortal". Nothing still needed. (Optional: "(Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> XIV)" after the last sentence, for the reader who wants the myth's source.)

**Reader, round 2**

Satisfied.

**Editor, after round 2**

As the front-matter thread now has it:
TITLE: The epigraph that was replaced
BODY: The title page of the typescript carries, under “The Waste Land,” a passage from Conrad’s <i>Heart of Darkness</i> ending with the dying Kurtz’s words, “The horror! the horror!” Pound wrote, on 24 January 1922, that he doubted Conrad was “weighty enough to stand the citation”; Eliot answered that it was “much the most appropriate I can find, and somewhat elucidative”; Pound then told him to do as he liked about Conrad. Eliot nevertheless replaced it with the sentence from Petronius’s <i>Satyricon</i> in which Trimalchio, the host of the dinner, says that he saw the Sibyl of Cumae hanging in a jar, and that when the boys asked her what she wanted she answered, “I want to die.” The Sibyl had been granted long life, but not youth.

## The scholar's summary, round 1

1. tristan: "four words" is five ("Oed' und leer das Meer"). The 1909 Boston performance is undocumented: the evidence is the poem "Opera," dated November 1909 (Brooker hedges "or heard an orchestral arrangement"; the Met's Boston Tristan was April 1908; the Boston Opera Company opened 8 November 1909). "Senior" is wrong: AB June 1909, graduate student and assistant in philosophy 1909–10. Ludwig was found dead in the lake and drowning is disputed (match batch 1). The Act I ship is under way, not setting out. Eliot's "verses 5–8" and "verse 24" both check against the printed libretto, which is worth saying.
2. huxley-crome-yellow: "the immediate source" is Eliot's own recollection (letter to Grover Smith, 10 March 1952, "almost certain," unconscious) and is disputed by Rainey's dating of the typescript of Parts I–II to May 1921, before Huxley finished the novel (August 1921). Give both. What was borrowed is the name; Scogan reads palms, and the cards are Eliot's. Huxley's event is "the Fair," not a fête.
3. death-of-st-narcissus: the saint "had been" a tree, a fish and a girl, not "wanted to be"; the red rock is already in the 1915 poem's fifth line, so "made red" misdescribes the borrowing; add the first printing (Poems Written in Early Youth, 1950; trade 1967) to agree with batch 1.
4. ecclesiastes: there is no "dry cistern" in the chapter ("the wheel broken at the cistern"); the drought is Part V's (line 385). The cricket is Eliot's word for the King James grasshopper.
5. ezekiel: "dry stone" is not in Ezekiel; "broken images" (ch. 6) is a commentator's link, not Eliot's note. "Son of man" is 93 times.
6. chaucer: not "English poetry's first great spring" (earlier English springs exist and the opening follows a convention); the poem does not "end" at the Chapel Perilous, which is in Part V; the comparison is the commentators', not Eliot's.
7. baudelaire-au-lecteur: first printed 1 June 1855 in the Revue des deux mondes; 1857 is the book. Name the essays the two quotations come from ("Baudelaire," 1930; "What Dante Means to Me," 1950).
8. dante-inf3 and dante-inf4: "are these" is a reading, and Eliot's own notes put the London crowd in two places (III, the neutrals; IV, Limbo); the notes should say so. "Great-hearted" misrenders "spiriti magni" (the great spirits). Eliot's tercets are the Temple Classics text, which is why they differ from the Petrocchi passages displayed.
9. isaiah: both links are commentators' (Southam; Ricks and McCue), not Eliot's; in 32:2 the shelter is a man's in a righteous reign, not "the prophet's"; Isaiah's "house" becomes Eliot's "lands."
10. baudelaire-sept-vieillards stands; the "method" sentence is a reading that Eliot's 1950 talk supports and could be attributed to it.
11. The line numbers this batch gives for Part V (391, 426) are right in the every-line count; 385 for "empty cisterns" is my own count from the 1922 text.

1. whitman-lilacs: "He rarely admitted to reading Whitman. The lilacs and the thrush say otherwise" misstates the record: Eliot's 1928 disclaimer ("did not read Whitman until much later in life") is real, but in 1926 he had already praised Whitman's "lilacs" in print (<i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926); the parallel is the annotators', not Eliot's note, which cites a bird handbook.
2. tennyson: <i>Maud</i> and <i>The Princess</i> are annotators' parallels, not Eliot's sources (his notes give the <i>Pervigilium Veneris</i> only); Conrad's "Youth" has "the heat of life in the handful of dust". "Buried alive" is loose. The 1936 quotation is exact and should name the essay.
3. webster: Vittoria and Zanche shot Flamineo, as they thought, and gloated; "would have let him die" understates V.vi. The rest is right.
4. shakespeare-tempest: "gasworks" should be "gashouse" (line 190); the Fisher King at line 189 and "everyone has suffered a sea-change" are readings, the second overstated; Ferdinand's speech is altered at 191–192, quoted at 257.
5. frazer: the title is Virgil's, not Turner's (the painting supplies the first page, and Frazer did take Avernus for Nemi); the crucifixion was explicit in the 1900 edition, not "by implication"; "The Hanged God" is a chapter of the volumes Eliot names.
6. weston: "Syrian merchants" simplifies chapter XII (merchants, slaves, and "most important" the legions); chapter VI, the Tarot suits, is the chapter Eliot's note on line 46 presupposes.
7. petronius: Ovid's Sibyl points to a heap of sand, not sand "in her hand"; she foretells her shrinking; Petronius never calls Trimalchio fat.
8. dante-purg26: add that the Ovid Press title page reads "Ara Vus Prec", that "Il Miglior Fabbro" was already Pound's chapter title in <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910), and that the dedication was added after 1922.
9. dickens-omf: the heading is typed over Parts I and II of the typescript, not "written across" it; "including its author" and "the poem's river in prose" should go.
10. conrad-hod: date and source the Pound–Eliot exchange (January 1922; the facsimile and <i>Letters</i> vol. 1), say Pound gave way and Eliot made the change, and mark "heart of light" as a reading; "Mistah Kurtz—he dead" (1925) is worth a clause.
11. eliot-frontiers: "'bought'" is a false quotation; add that the notes were absent from the <i>Criterion</i> and <i>Dial</i> printings; cut the closing instruction.
Aside for the editor: the site's line count agrees with Eliot's marginal numbers through 366 (the hermit-thrush is 357 in both) and runs one ahead from 367 ("What is that sound"), not from 346 as the common brief says; every line reference in this batch is right in the site's count.

The corrections that matter most:
1. voice walker: 207–211 must be 207–214 — line 211 ends mid-sentence and 212–214 ("Asked me in demotic French…") is still the walker speaking.
2. voice shepherd: "Four words" — the line has five ("Oed' und leer das Meer").
3. voice lover: "the poem's one admission of feeling" is false ("And I was frightened", line 15; Part V's "blood shaking my heart").
4. voice prophet: Eliot's notes cite Ezekiel and Ecclesiastes; Isaiah is a commentators' addition; and in Ezekiel the voice saying "Son of man" is the Lord's, not Ezekiel's.
5. voice marie / voice stranger: giving line 12 to an overheard stranger is a reading stated as fact, and the scene is the Hofgarten, not a café.
6. draft boston: the draft says "the show", not a burlesque; the policeman picks the narrator up and is talked round — no run; the pencil cancellation is credited to Eliot (Wilson, 1971) but "before Pound saw it" is conjecture; name the facsimile as source; verify "fifty-four" against the book.
7. draft title-draft: the Dickens title does not "survive in the notes" — the 1922 Notes never mention Dickens; Weston is Eliot's stated source for the title and Malory an editorial inference; "crossed out" is unattested — the heading was dropped.
8. draft epigraph-draft: the typescript epigraph was the full Conrad passage ending in the cry, not the two words; and the Sibyl is no immortal (Ovid: years like grains of sand, without youth) — the closing antithesis misstates the myth.
Sailor and hyacinth stand as written.


## The reader's summary, round 1

What I threw out most often was the last sentence: the bow that sums the note up in a line made to be quoted (ecclesiastes, ezekiel, narcissus, whitman, petronius, weston, dickens, frontiers, tennyson, the Boston and epigraph drafts, the lover's legend). Under it the notes are mostly sound. Next came readings stated as what the poem is or does ("are these", "never allows the change", "are Frazer's", "still answers to it", "the poem's one admission of feeling"); I marked them as readings and pointed them at line numbers. Then fragments used as openers (chaucer, inf4, webster, dickens), balanced pairs ("a ship setting out and a ship not arriving", "beautifully and falsely", "a dying man's verdict ... an immortal's wish"), and instructions to the reader (weston, conrad, frontiers). Modern shorthand ("commuters", "office crowd", "gasworks", "in drag") I asked to have replaced with the poem's or the source's own words.

What works: the source pages are best where they say where the passage sits in its source and who is speaking (tristan, inf3, webster, huxley, purg26). That is what a reader opens them for, and purg26 needs nothing. Two notes forget the line they are filed under: tennyson never mentions the handful of dust, and webster never says that the wolf became a Dog. The voice legends are mostly plain and should be left alone; three want a word changed and one (lover) wants its note replaced. Verdicts: 10 keep, 14 trim, 11 rewrite, 0 cut.


## The scholar's summary, round 2

Satisfied: chaucer, ecclesiastes, baudelaire-sept-vieillards, dante-inf4, ezekiel, isaiah.
Not yet, with the objections above: tristan (source Stravinsky's words: "Memories of T. S. Eliot," Esquire, August 1965, and December 1956 for the meeting); baudelaire-au-lecteur (untouched since round one: DATE should give the 1855 first printing, and the two essays should be named); death-of-st-narcissus (two manuscripts, both in the 1971 facsimile; the early poem's own red rock behind the fire, which bears on "gray changed to red"); huxley-crome-yellow ("the Fair," not a fête; Rainey's May 1921 dating of the typescript, which "the accepted source" hides); dante-inf3 (name the 1950 talk).
Everything else from round one has been made in the new text. The line numbers in the new text (5–6, 35–41, 69–70, 385, 391, 426) are right in the every-line count.

Satisfied: shakespeare-tempest, eliot-frontiers.
Not yet, the corrections that matter most:
1. frazer: "by implication of Christ" is wrong; Frazer argues in the two volumes Eliot names that Easter was adapted from the spring festivals of Adonis and Attis (vol. I, pp. 254–57, 305–10).
2. petronius: Ovid's Sibyl shows a heap of dust she had scooped up ("pulveris hausti ... cumulum", XIV.136–137), not "grains of sand in her hand"; the library note should agree with the Part I gloss, and my round-one "no hand" was over-strict.
3. dickens-omf: Sloppy was brought up in the workhouse, not by Betty Higden; he lives with her and turns her mangle.
4. webster-white-devil: two pistols, fired by Vittoria and Zanche ("They shoot ... Kill'd with a couple of braches!").
5. conrad-hod: the epigraph stood on the typescript's title page, not "at the head of the typescript of Part I"; date and source the letters (January 1922; facsimile; <i>Letters</i>, vol. 1).
6. weston: add chapter VI (The Symbols), where the Grail hallows become the suits of the Tarot.
7. whitman-lilacs: add Eliot's 1926 remark on Whitman's "lilacs" (<i>Nation and Athenaeum</i>, 18 December 1926) beside the 1928 disclaimer.
8. dante-purg26: "Il Miglior Fabbro" was Pound's own chapter title in <i>The Spirit of Romance</i> (1910); the 1920 title page reads "Ara Vus Prec".
9. tennyson: name the 1936 essay ("In Memoriam").

Eight of thirteen are satisfied: marie, prophet, sailor, hyacinth, lover, shepherd, sosostris, epigraph-draft. Five are not yet, each with one objection:
1. voice walker: 207–211 must be 207–214. The lens's eugenides span is wrong, not a reading: 209–214 is one sentence with the walker as its speaker, Eugenides has no words in the poem (the invitation is reported, the "demotic French" withheld), and the lens's own precedent at 15–16 keeps quoted words inside the narrator's span. At most, 213–214 as free indirect speech, marked as a reading; 212 never.
2. draft title-draft: "the title page Eliot typed for the finished poem" — the leaf carried the Conrad epigraph Pound was objecting to on 24 January 1922, mid-revision; say "the title page of the typescript".
3. draft boston: Myrtle does not turn him away — she refuses him but gives him a bed, a bath and breakfast (Wilson, 1971). The rest of the editor's draft stands; the facsimile's own wording on the cancellation and the count of fifty-four are worth one look at the book.
4. voice poem: own the 7/8 break as the edition's reading, as the other assignments now are.
5. voice stranger: add that the German is feminine ("Russin"), since it bears on whether the line is Marie's.


## The reader's summary, round 2

The other thread’s rewrite passes almost everywhere: 21 of 35 items satisfied outright, and of the 14 not yet, 11 have a single objection. Nothing left reads as a model’s flourish; the bows, the counts and the antitheses are gone, and readings are marked as readings. What remains is sentence length (tristan, inf3, tempest, frazer, conrad, title-draft), two opening fragments (narcissus, frontiers), a few phrases nobody says across a table (“in its stead”, “has been cited for”, “the office crowd”), and the poem made a person once (“wants”, in whitman). The voice legend is done. Draft boston needs three changes, all wording and none touching the facts the editor settled.



# Batch 5: ways in, pathway stops, the artist's card notes


## way-1

**As it stood**

HEADING: Hear it before you read it
BODY: Virginia Woolf, who heard Eliot read the poem at dinner in June 1922, wrote in her diary that "he sang it & chanted it rhythmed it." Start with his own voice: the listening room has his recordings, and the first section is where the chant is plainest. Then read the seventy-six lines silently and notice where his stresses fell.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: stands
Checked: Woolf's diary entry of 23 June 1922 (Bell's edition, vol. II; transcriptions at the Library of Congress Bibliomania blog and elsewhere): "Eliot dined last Sunday & read his poem. He sang it & chanted it rhythmed it." The dinner was Sunday 18 June 1922; "at dinner in June 1922" is right, and the quotation matches the accepted transcription (some secondary sources add a comma; the site's wording is the diary's). Part I is 76 lines in the site's count.
Substance: none

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: keep

Reads as a model wrote it: no

Problems: none.

Keep: all of it. The Woolf diary line is exactly what I want at the head of the part, it carries its source, and the rest tells me what the listening room has and what to do with it. "Notice where his stresses fell" is an instruction, but this is a list of ways in; instructions are what it is for.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. "The first section is where the chant is plainest" was the one claim in it, and it is an impression; the rest is what the tool offers. Woolf's ampersand stays, since it is her diary's.
