**As it stood**

HEADING: Lay out the cards
BODY: Madame Sosostris deals seven cards and one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each. Then ask what she leaves out: no Hanged Man, no water except the kind that drowns.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: lines 46–56 of the 1922 text. Dealt: the drowned Phoenician Sailor, Belladonna, the man with three staves, the Wheel, the one-eyed merchant, and the blank card — six. The Hanged Man is looked for and not found ("I do not find / The Hanged Man"); "I see crowds of people" is the vision. The site's own Sosostris card shows six cards on her table (five face down, one blank face up), and the cards tool itself has six dealt cards plus the missing Hanged Man plus the vision.
Substance:
1. "Madame Sosostris deals seven cards and one vision" — she deals six. Seven is right only if it counts the Hanged Man, which the same paragraph says she leaves out. This is the kind of tidy count Ethan flagged, and it is wrong.
Draft (body): Madame Sosostris turns up six cards, fails to find a seventh, and reports one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each. Then ask what the reading leaves out: no Hanged Man, and no water except the kind that drowns.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "no Hanged Man, no water except the kind that drowns." — the ending is a riddle tied in a bow. The first half is a fact I want. The second half is a phrase built to be quoted, and I had to stop and work out that it means the only thing she says about water is "Fear death by water." Say that.

Keep: "Madame Sosostris deals seven cards and one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each."

Draft:
HEADING: Lay out the cards
BODY: Madame Sosostris deals seven cards and one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each. Then ask what she leaves out. She cannot find the Hanged Man, and the only thing she says about water is "Fear death by water."

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: correct. Six cards, not seven (the scholar's count, and the site's own drawing agrees); the riddle at the end replaced by what she actually says about water, as the reader asked.
TITLE: Lay out the cards
BODY: Madame Sosostris turns up six cards, fails to find a seventh, and reports one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each. Then ask what she leaves out. She cannot find the Hanged Man, and the only thing she says about water is "Fear death by water."

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "the only thing she says about water is 'Fear death by water.'" Not so: her first card is "the drowned Phoenician Sailor", and she adds "Those are pearls that were his eyes" (47–48). The original's point ("no water except the kind that drowns") was right; the rewrite narrows it into a false count. Replace the last sentence with: She cannot find the Hanged Man, and the only water in her reading is the kind that drowns: a drowned sailor, and "Fear death by water."

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "fails to find a seventh" in the first sentence and "She cannot find the Hanged Man" in the last say the same thing twice, and for a moment I took them for two different missing cards. Keep the first sentence and put, for the last: "The seventh card, the one she cannot find, is the Hanged Man, and the only thing she says about water is "Fear death by water.""

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader: the seventh card said twice. Scholar: "the only thing she says about water" is a false count, since her first card is a drowned sailor. Both taken; the original's "no water except the kind that drowns" comes back as a sentence.
TITLE: Lay out the cards
BODY: Madame Sosostris turns up six cards, fails to find a seventh, and reports one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each. Then ask what she leaves out. The seventh card, the one she cannot find, is the Hanged Man, and the only water in her reading is the kind that drowns: a drowned sailor, and "Fear death by water."

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: Turn the tarot cards
BODY:
Madame Sosostris turns up six cards, fails to find a seventh, and reports one vision. Turn them over one at a time on the table and read the note on each. Then ask what she leaves out. The seventh card, the one she cannot find, is the Hanged Man, and the only water in her reading is the kind that drowns: a drowned sailor, and "Fear death by water."


## way-4

**As it stood**

HEADING: Walk the commute
BODY: London Bridge, King William Street, St Mary Woolnoth, the stroke of nine. It is a route of about six hundred yards, the one Eliot walked to Lloyds Bank. Follow it on the map, then read lines 60–68 as a man who has just walked it in fog.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Rennie's London Bridge (the bridge of 1922, opened 1831): 928 ft, five stone arches (Wikipedia, "London Bridge"). St Mary Woolnoth stands at the corner of Lombard Street and King William Street (Wikipedia, "St Mary Woolnoth"; coordinates 51.5128, −0.0881). Measured from those positions: bridge ≈ 310 yards; north end of the bridge up King William Street to the church ≈ 580 yards; the whole named route, Southwark end of the bridge to the church, ≈ 850–900 yards.
Substance:
1. "a route of about six hundred yards" — six hundred yards fits only the stretch from the north end of the bridge to the church. The route as listed ("London Bridge, King William Street, St Mary Woolnoth") is about half a mile. Say "about half a mile", or start the route at the foot of the bridge.
2. "the one Eliot walked to Lloyds Bank" — stated as fact, and I can find no evidence for it. In 1922 Eliot lived at 9 Clarence Gate Gardens, Marylebone, north-west of the City; nothing I can reach documents a daily walk over London Bridge. The route in the poem is the commuters' way into the City from London Bridge station; what Eliot's own note (to 68) claims is only that the dead sound at nine was "a phenomenon which I have often noticed" — his bank stood at the end of the route. Guides and walking tours assert the commute; the scholarly annotations (Southam, Ricks and McCue) do not.
Draft (body): London Bridge, King William Street, St Mary Woolnoth, the stroke of nine. About half a mile, the way thousands walked into the City from London Bridge station every morning; Eliot's bank stood at the end of it, and his note to line 68 calls the dead sound at nine "a phenomenon which I have often noticed". Follow it on the map, then read lines 60–68 as someone who has just walked it in fog.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "London Bridge, King William Street, St Mary Woolnoth, the stroke of nine." — a list standing in for a sentence, and it is the opener, so I meet the manner before I meet the information.
2. "the one Eliot walked to Lloyds Bank" — given flat. I would like to know how it is known that this was his route (he lived in Marylebone; did he come in over the bridge?). For the scholar. If it is an inference, say "the walk a clerk from London Bridge station made every morning" or the like.
3. "read lines 60–68 as a man who has just walked it in fog" — casts me in a part. "With the walk in mind" does the same job without telling me who to be.

Keep: "It is a route of about six hundred yards."

Draft:
HEADING: Walk the commute
BODY: Lines 60–68 follow a route: over London Bridge, up King William Street, past St Mary Woolnoth as its clock strikes nine. It is about six hundred yards, and it is the walk Eliot made to Lloyds Bank. Follow it on the map, then read the lines again with the walk in mind.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The claim that this was Eliot's own walk is undocumented (he lived in Marylebone); the route is the clerks' way in from London Bridge station, and what Eliot's note vouches for is the church clock. The distance corrected to about half a mile for the route as listed. Sentence form throughout; "with the walk in mind" for the part the reader was cast in.
TITLE: Walk the commute
BODY: Lines 60–68 follow a route: over London Bridge, up King William Street, past St Mary Woolnoth as its clock strikes nine. It is about half a mile, the way into the City that thousands of clerks walked every morning from London Bridge station, and Eliot's bank stood at the end of it; his note on line 68 calls the dead sound on the final stroke "a phenomenon which I have often noticed". Follow it on the map, then read the lines again with the walk in mind.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "over London Bridge, up King William Street" — the poem says the crowd "Flowed up the hill and down King William Street" (66). A reader with the lines beside the note will see the contradiction, and the direction has been argued over. Use the poem's words. Replace the first sentence with: Lines 60–68 follow a route: over London Bridge, "up the hill and down King William Street", and past St Mary Woolnoth as its clock strikes nine.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. The second sentence runs three clauses and a semicolon and I had to read it twice. Put: "It is about half a mile, the way into the City that thousands of clerks walked every morning from London Bridge station. Eliot's bank stood at the end of it, and his note on line 68 calls the dead sound on the final stroke "a phenomenon which I have often noticed"."

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader: the three-clause sentence split. Scholar: the poem says "up the hill and down King William Street", so "up King William Street" contradicts it; quote the line. Both taken.
TITLE: Walk the commute
BODY: Lines 60–68 follow a route: over London Bridge, "up the hill and down King William Street", and past St Mary Woolnoth as its clock strikes nine. It is about half a mile, the way into the City that thousands of clerks walked every morning from London Bridge station. Eliot's bank stood at the end of it, and his note on line 68 calls the dead sound on the final stroke "a phenomenon which I have often noticed". Follow it on the map, then read the lines again with the walk in mind.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: Follow the route in Part I
BODY:
Lines 60–68 follow a route: over London Bridge, "up the hill and down King William Street", and past St Mary Woolnoth as its clock strikes nine. It is about half a mile, the way into the City that thousands of clerks walked every morning from London Bridge station. Eliot's bank stood at the end of it, and his note on line 68 calls the dead sound on the final stroke "a phenomenon which I have often noticed". Follow it on the map, then read the lines again with the walk in mind.


## way-5

**As it stood**

HEADING: Put Chaucer beside it
BODY: Read the first twelve lines of the General Prologue, then Eliot's first seven. Same month, same rain, same roots. Decide what has changed between 1387 and 1922, and whether it is the weather or the people.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: stands
Checked: General Prologue 1–12 (Aprill, shoures soote, roote) against the standard text; the first sentence of the poem runs to line 4 and winter to line 7, so "Eliot's first seven" is the right span.
Substance:
1. Minor: 1387 is the conventional date for the Prologue's composition, usually given as "c. 1387". "Between 1387 and 1922" is acceptable shorthand; "c." would be exact.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Same month, same rain, same roots." — three fragments for the beat. The content (April, showers reaching roots, in both) is good and should be a sentence.
2. "and whether it is the weather or the people" — the closing antithesis. It exists to be quoted. "Decide what has changed" is enough, and it leaves me to decide what kind of change it is.

Keep: "Read the first twelve lines of the General Prologue, then Eliot's first seven."

Draft:
HEADING: Put Chaucer beside it
BODY: Read the first twelve lines of the General Prologue, then Eliot's first seven. Both begin with April, with rain, and with roots. Decide what has changed between 1387 and 1922.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. The date dropped, since 1387 is conventional for the Prologue and the comparison does not need it.
TITLE: Put Chaucer beside it
BODY: Read the first twelve lines of the General Prologue, then Eliot's first seven. Both begin with April, with rain, and with roots. Decide what has changed between Chaucer's April and Eliot's.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: Put Chaucer beside it
BODY:
Read the first twelve lines of the General Prologue, then Eliot's first seven. Both begin with April, with rain, and with roots. Decide what has changed between Chaucer's April and Eliot's.


## way-6

**As it stood**

HEADING: Follow the water
BODY: Spring rain, forgetful snow, a shower of rain, dry stone, no sound of water, a handful of dust, a drowned sailor, "Fear death by water." The section's weather is its argument. The Water lens tints every line by what it is made of.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: each phrase in the list against lines 4, 6, 9, 24, 30, 47, 55 — all present and in order. The lens's behaviour against the build brief's own description of the Water lens (lines that name water, drought or fire are tinted, tagged by hand and by sense).
Substance:
1. "The Water lens tints every line by what it is made of" — the lens tints the lines that name water, drought or (in Part III) fire; it does not tint every line. As written it misdescribes the tool.
2. "The section's weather is its argument" — a reading stated as fact, and the same figure Ethan quoted from the Water intro. On substance it asserts what is at most a critical commonplace; it should go or be grounded.
Draft (body): Spring rain, forgetful snow, a shower of rain, dry stone, no sound of water, a handful of dust, a drowned sailor, "Fear death by water." Switch on the Water lens: lines that name water, or its absence, are tinted — by sense, not by keyword — so you can see where the section is wet and where it is stone.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "The section's weather is its argument." — Ethan's own example ("The poem's argument is a weather report") in five words. A metaphor about the poem in place of a statement about the lines.
2. "The Water lens tints every line by what it is made of." — a second metaphor. I had to guess that it means the lens colours lines according to whether they name water or its lack. Say what the lens does (the editor should check what it actually tints).
3. The opening run of eight fragments. I would keep its contents, because it is the one useful thing here: it tells me where water is in seventy-six lines. But as a sentence, not a run.

Keep: the contents of the list.

BODY: Part I names water, or the lack of it, over and over: spring rain and snow in the first sentence, a shower over the Starnbergersee, a dry stone with no sound of water, a handful of dust, a drowned sailor, and then "Fear death by water." The Water lens tints the lines that name water and the lines that name its absence.

**Editor, after round 1**

The Water lens tints the lines that name water and the lines that name its absence, so you can see where the section is wet and where it is stone.

HEADING: Leave the German alone

2. "as sound, and as a wall." — the paired ending. "A wall" is a metaphor for not understanding; say "without understanding them."
3. "one café sentence" — I had to work out that this is line 12, the German sentence in the Hofgarten talk. Give the line number.

Keep: "Switch on Tongues to see the translations, then switch it off".

TITLE: Read it without the translations

## way-10

**As it stood**

HEADING: Three questions with no answer
BODY: When were you "neither living nor dead"? What corpse have you planted in a garden, and have you checked on it? Whose shadow at evening rises to meet you? The section asks them; it does not answer. Neither need you, yet.

**B. Pathway stops in Part I. A stop's text appears beside its line when the reader walks that pathway. Each pathway's title and intro are shared (they live on the Pathways page) and are given for context only.**

---
**pathway “A first reading” (id first). INTRO, for context: Twelve stops for someone meeting the poem for the first time. Each stop is a place to stand, not an explanation. Read the lines, read the note, move on.**

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: lines 28–30, 39–40, 71–73. "Has it begun to sprout?" is asked in the text (of Stetson); "I was neither / Living nor dead" and "your shadow at evening rising to meet you" are statements, which the way-in has recast as questions to the reader.
Substance:
1. "The section asks them; it does not answer" — the section asks one of the three. The other two are its statements, turned into questions by the site. Say "raises" for "asks", or own the recasting ("Three questions the section leaves you with").

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "When were you 'neither living nor dead'? What corpse have you planted in a garden, and have you checked on it? Whose shadow at evening rises to meet you?" — the poem turned into a questionnaire about me. Lines 28–29 and 39–40 are not questions in the poem; the note has made them questions and then says "The section asks them," which it does not. "Have you checked on it?" is arch.
2. "The section asks them; it does not answer." — the poem as a person who withholds.
3. "Neither need you, yet." — a bow, with an inversion for the flourish.

Keep: the heading, and the idea of reading with the questions open, which is a fair way in once the questions are the poem's own.

Draft:
HEADING: Three questions with no answer
BODY: Part I asks questions and answers none of them. What roots clutch and what branches grow out of this stony rubbish (19–20)? Where are you lingering (34, in Wagner's German)? Has the corpse in the garden begun to sprout (72)? One way in is to read the section with those questions open. The section does not answer them, and a first reading does not have to.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The questionnaire goes; the three questions are now the poem's own (19–20, 34, 72), which answers the scholar's objection that the section asked only one of the original three; the bow goes.
TITLE: Three questions with no answer
BODY: Part I asks questions it does not answer: what roots clutch and what branches grow out of this stony rubbish (19–20); where are you lingering (34, in Wagner's German); has the corpse in the garden begun to sprout (72). One way in is to read the section with those questions open.

**B. The pathway stops**

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

TITLE: Three questions with no answer
BODY:
Part I asks questions it does not answer: what roots clutch and what branches grow out of this stony rubbish (19–20); where are you lingering (34, in Wagner's German); has the corpse in the garden begun to sprout (72). One way in is to read the section with those questions open.


## first@1

**As it stood**

STOP at line 1: Start with the sound. Read the first seven lines aloud. Five of the seven lines end on a hanging participle: breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding. The poem is about things that will not finish.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: line-endings 1–7 of the 1922 text: breeding, mixing, stirring, rain., covering, feeding, tubers. Five of the seven end on a participle, and they are the five named. The count is right.
Substance:
1. "The poem is about things that will not finish." — a reading stated as fact (and close kin to "Growth that will not finish" on the brief's list of tells). The observable is the syntax: the participles hold the sentence open across the line-ends. Either ground the claim in that or drop the sentence.
Draft (text): Start with the sound. Read the first seven lines aloud: five of them end on a participle — breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding — so the sentence hangs open at line after line.

2. "hanging participle" — for a moment I read "dangling participle," the grammar fault. What is meant is a participle left hanging at the line end.

Keep: "Start with the sound. Read the first seven lines aloud." And the count of five, which is the point of the stop, not decoration.

Draft: Start with the sound. Read the first seven lines aloud. Five of the seven end on a participle left hanging at the line break: breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. The line about the whole poem goes; the count of five stays, being the point.
Start with the sound. Read the first seven lines aloud. Five of the seven end on a participle left hanging at the line break: breeding, mixing, stirring, covering, feeding.

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

**pathway “Water” (id water). INTRO, for context: The poem's hydrology, from spring rain to the last rain. Follow the water and you follow the argument: water is what the waste land needs, water is what drowns you, and the two are never told apart.**

**Scholar, round 1**

Checked: bridge, street and clock are lines 62, 66, 67–68; the dead from Dante is Eliot's note 63. Substance:
1. "The poem's trick, learned from Baudelaire" is in fact Eliot's own testimony, not just a reading — but nothing on the page says so. If the stop keeps the claim, the editor should know its warrant is the 1950 lecture (quote nothing beyond a phrase; it is post-1929) plus the 1922 note. As written it survives, since the note to 60 is on the same screen.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. "The real London: a bridge, a street, a clock." — the colon and the three.
2. "And the dead, from Dante, walking over it to work." — a fragment. The idea is good and wants a sentence with Eliot's note behind it.
3. "The poem's trick, learned from Baudelaire, is that both are true at once." — the poem as a conjuror, and "both are true at once" is the bow. What Baudelaire supplies (Eliot's note on 60) is a city where a ghost stops a passer-by in daylight; say that instead.

Keep: nothing as it stands; all of the content.

Draft: The place is real: London Bridge, King William Street, St Mary Woolnoth and its clock. The crowd crossing the bridge is also Dante's crowd of the dead; Eliot's notes on lines 63 and 64 give the passages. His note on line 60 quotes Baudelaire, whose Paris is a city where a ghost stops a passer-by in daylight.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite, as the reader drafted; the Baudelaire point rests on Eliot's note on line 60, which the scholar confirmed is on the same screen.

The crowd crossing the bridge is also Dante's crowd of the dead; Eliot's notes on lines 63 and 64 give the passages. His note on line 60 quotes Baudelaire, whose Paris is a city where a ghost stops a passer-by in daylight.

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

## water@55

**As it stood**

STOP at line 55: "Fear death by water." The reading's one instruction, and the reverse of what the Grail legend advises.

---
**pathway “The dead” (id dead). INTRO, for context: Burials, drownings, bones and the crowd that death has undone. The poem's first section is called The Burial of the Dead, from the Book of Common Prayer, and the dead never quite stay in it.**

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: line 55; Weston's From Ritual to Romance (the freeing of the waters; the life-giving water of the Grail material); the inversion reading is Cleanth Brooks's, "The Waste Land: Critique of the Myth" (Modern Poetry and the Tradition, 1939) — the fortune-teller warns against the one death that leads to life.
Substance:
1. "the reverse of what the Grail legend advises" — a critical reading stated as fact. The Grail legend does not "advise" about death by water; the point is Brooks's (built on Weston and Frazer): in the fertility pattern behind the poem, death by water precedes renewal, so the warning gets the rite backwards. Mark it as a reading or name it.
2. Minor: "The reading's one instruction" — "Look!" (48) is also an imperative; "the reading's one piece of advice" would be exact.
Draft (text): "Fear death by water." The one piece of advice in the reading — and, in a reading that goes back to Cleanth Brooks, exactly wrong: in the vegetation rites behind the poem, death by water is what renews the land.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "The reading's one instruction" — there is another: "Look!" at 48. The count is there to be neat, and it is not right.
2. "the reverse of what the Grail legend advises" — lost me. A legend does not advise, and I am not told what the legend says about water. The note assumes I have read Weston.

Keep: the quotation.

Draft: "Fear death by water." Madame Sosostris warns against water. In the Grail legend that Eliot's headnote points to, water is what the waste land lacks and needs. Part IV of the poem is called Death by Water.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. "The reading's one instruction" goes (there is also "Look!"); the Grail point given as Weston's waste land and the inversion given to Brooks, as the scholar asked.
"Fear death by water." Madame Sosostris warns against water. In the Grail legend that Eliot's headnote points to, water is what the waste land lacks and needs, and Cleanth Brooks (1939) read her warning as getting the rite backwards. Part IV of the poem is called Death by Water.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: Brooks's sentence, reached today through Danica Igrutinović's article on water in the poem (Književnost i jezik, 2022), citing the 1968 reprint, p. 64: Madame Sosostris warns "against death by water, not realizing any more than do the other inhabitants of the modern waste land that the way into life may be by death itself." "Getting the rite backwards" states him fairly.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "read her warning as getting the rite backwards" — "the rite" has not been mentioned, so I do not know which rite, and "getting it backwards" is chatty. Put: "read her warning as the reverse of what the legend means".

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader: "the rite" has not been mentioned. Taken, with Brooks's point put in his own terms (the scholar verified his words: she warns against death by water "not realizing ... that the way into life may be by death itself").
"Fear death by water." Madame Sosostris warns against water. In the Grail legend that Eliot's headnote points to, water is what the waste land lacks and needs, and Cleanth Brooks (1939) read her warning as a warning against the one death that might bring life. Part IV of the poem is called Death by Water.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: Brooks (1947 reprint): "She finds that his card is that of the drowned Phoenician Sailor, and so she warns him against death by water, not realizing any more than do the other inhabitants of the modern waste land that the way into life may be by death itself." "A warning against the one death that might bring life" states him fairly.

Verdict: satisfied

"Fear death by water." Madame Sosostris warns against water. In the Grail legend that Eliot's headnote points to, water is what the waste land lacks and needs, and Cleanth Brooks (1939) read her warning as a warning against the one death that might bring life. Part IV of the poem is called Death by Water.

## dead@23

**As it stood**

STOP at line 23: "the dead tree gives no shelter." Ezekiel's valley, Ecclesiastes' long home.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Eliot's notes: 20 → Ezekiel II.i ("Son of man, stand upon thy feet"); 23 → Ecclesiastes XII.v ("the grasshopper shall be a burden… man goeth to his long home"). The valley of dry bones is Ezekiel 37, which Eliot does not cite.
Substance:
1. "Ezekiel's valley" — Eliot's citation is Ezekiel 2:1, the address to the son of man, not the valley of dry bones. The valley is a critics' association (a natural one for this landscape), and if kept it must be marked as such; as written it misreports the note.
2. "Ecclesiastes' long home" is right: "long home" is in the verse Eliot cites at this line.

Eliot's own notes cite Ezekiel 2:1 and Ecclesiastes 12:5; the stop should tell me what those say. ## eliot@35

STOP at line 35: The hyacinth garden: Emily Hale in Boston, or Jean Verdenal in the Luxembourg Gardens with a branch of lilac.

Verdict: correct
Checked: the Verdenal association rests on Eliot's "A Commentary", Criterion, April 1934 (the friend crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac" — the stop's "with a branch of lilac" is within the rights rule); the Hale identification is Lyndall Gordon's, argued since Eliot's Early Years and grounded in the Hale letters (opened 2020; her The Hyacinth Girl, 2022).
Substance:
1. The "or" honestly marks two rival identifications, but neither carries its warrant, and this is exactly where a reader wants one. Name Gordon for Hale; date the Criterion memory for Verdenal.
Draft (text): The hyacinth garden has two claimed originals: Emily Hale, in Lyndall Gordon's reading of the letters Eliot wrote her; or Jean Verdenal, whom Eliot remembered in 1934 crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac".

Reads as a model wrote it: no

Problems:

Keep: the two candidates and the lilac.

Draft: The hyacinth garden. Two people from Eliot's life have been proposed for it: Emily Hale, in Boston, and Jean Verdenal, whom Eliot later remembered crossing the Luxembourg Gardens with a branch of lilac. The poem names neither.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The two identifications given their owners: Gordon for Hale, the 1934 <i>Criterion</i> for Verdenal.
The hyacinth garden. Two people from Eliot's life have been proposed for it: Emily Hale, in Lyndall Gordon's reading of the letters Eliot wrote her, and Jean Verdenal, whom Eliot remembered in 1934 crossing the Luxembourg Gardens "waving a branch of lilac". The poem names neither.

## eliot@66

**As it stood**

STOP at line 66: Lloyds Bank, Lombard Street, across the road from St Mary Woolnoth. The clock struck nine as he arrived.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Lloyds' head offices filled the block between Lombard Street and Cornhill (the listed building is 15–22 Cornhill; the head-office address 71 Lombard Street); the department Eliot joined on 19 March 1917 was the Colonial and Foreign Department, usually addressed 17 Cornhill in the scholarship; popular accounts also say Threadneedle Street. St Mary Woolnoth stands at Lombard Street and King William Street, a minute away at most.
Substance:
1. "Lloyds Bank, Lombard Street, across the road from St Mary Woolnoth" — the bank's block fronted both Lombard Street and Cornhill, and I cannot verify "across the road" for the office Eliot sat in. Say the head offices stood between Lombard Street and Cornhill, by the church, or verify the street before asserting one.
2. "The clock struck nine as he arrived." — stated as fact; no source gives his arrival time. What exists is his own note: the dead sound on the final stroke of nine was "a phenomenon which I have often noticed". Lean on that.
Draft (text): Lloyds Bank's head offices, between Lombard Street and Cornhill, a minute from St Mary Woolnoth. Eliot's note to line 68 says he "often noticed" the dead sound on the final stroke of nine.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "The clock struck nine as he arrived." — a scene, given as a fact. What is known is Eliot's note on line 68, that the dead sound on the final stroke is "a phenomenon which I have often noticed." That is better than the scene, and it is his.

Keep: "Lloyds Bank, Lombard Street, across the road from St Mary Woolnoth."

Draft: Lloyds Bank, Lombard Street, across the road from St Mary Woolnoth. Eliot's own note on line 68 says the dead sound on the final stroke of nine is "a phenomenon which I have often noticed."

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The scene at nine replaced by Eliot's note; the bank placed as the sources allow (the head office at 71 Lombard Street, a few doors from the church; the exact office he sat in is not certain).
Lloyds Bank's head office, at 71 Lombard Street, a few doors from St Mary Woolnoth. Eliot's note on line 68 says the dead sound on the final stroke of nine was "a phenomenon which I have often noticed."

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: Lloyds' head office at 71 Lombard Street, on the block that ran through to Cornhill; St Mary Woolnoth at the Lombard Street–King William Street corner. "A few doors" is fair, and the sentence no longer claims which office Eliot sat in.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

BODY:
Lloyds Bank's head office, at 71 Lombard Street, a few doors from St Mary Woolnoth. Eliot's note on line 68 says the dead sound on the final stroke of nine was "a phenomenon which I have often noticed."


## eliot@0

**As it stood**

STOP at line 0: Paris, January 1922: Pound's flat, the pencil, "il miglior fabbro."

**C. Tarot card notes (the “Turn the cards” tool: each card drawn for the site has a SHOWS text describing the drawing and an ANSWERS text tying it to the poem; written by the artist; these live in a shared file, so this debate produces proposals for them)**

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Eliot passed through Paris in mid-November 1921 and again, for about two weeks, from early January 1922; the decisive work on the drafts was done then (Rainey's chronology; the facsimile). The dedication: inscribed by Eliot in a copy for Pound in 1923, first printed with the poem in Poems 1909–1925 — as the site's own front matter says ("added 1923/1925"). The phrase is Dante's, Purgatorio XXVI, of Arnaut Daniel.
Substance:
1. "Pound's flat" — where the January sessions happened is not documented; the flat is scene-setting. "Paris, January 1922: Pound and the drafts" asserts only what is known.
2. Grouping "il miglior fabbro" under January 1922 misleads: the dedication came in 1923 (inscription) and 1925 (print). The stop should not fold it into that month.
Draft (text): Paris, January 1922: Pound worked over the drafts. "Il miglior fabbro" — Dante's phrase for Arnaut Daniel — came later, inscribed in a copy for Pound in 1923 and printed with the poem from 1925.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Pound's flat, the pencil, 'il miglior fabbro.'" — three fragments. "The pencil" means nothing unless I already know that Pound marked the typescript. A stop on this pathway should say what happened in Paris.
2. "il miglior fabbro" is left untranslated and unsourced; a reader at the dedication wants both.

Keep: "Paris, January 1922."

Draft: Paris, January 1922. On his way back from Lausanne Eliot stopped at Pound's flat, and Pound went through the typescript, cutting it hard. The dedication, added later, calls him "il miglior fabbro," the better craftsman, which is what Dante calls the poet Arnaut Daniel in the <i>Purgatorio</i>. (Scholar to confirm the sequence, "added later," and the Dante reference.)

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The three fragments made sentences; "Pound's flat" dropped as undocumented; the dedication dated to 1923 and 1925, not folded into January 1922.
Paris, January 1922. On his way back from Lausanne Eliot stopped in Paris, and Pound went through the typescript with him, cutting it hard. The dedication came later: Eliot wrote "il miglior fabbro", the better craftsman, Dante's phrase for the poet Arnaut Daniel, in Pound's copy in 1923, and printed it with the poem from 1925.

**C. The artist's card notes (proposals for the shared file)**

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: the presentation copy to Pound carries the dedication as an autograph inscription (King's College, Cambridge, "The Waste Land at 100"); first printed in Poems 1909–1925. The 1923 date is the usual one for the inscription and I found nothing against it.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Paris, January 1922. On his way back from Lausanne Eliot stopped in Paris" — Paris twice in nine words. Put: "Paris, January 1922. Eliot stopped there on his way back from Lausanne, and Pound went through the typescript with him, cutting it hard."
2. The dedication sentence puts two appositions between the verb and the rest ("wrote "il miglior fabbro", the better craftsman, Dante's phrase for the poet Arnaut Daniel, in Pound's copy in 1923") and I lost the thread. Put: "The dedication came later. In 1923 Eliot wrote "il miglior fabbro" in Pound's copy, and from 1925 it was printed with the poem. The words are Dante's, for the poet Arnaut Daniel, and mean the better craftsman."

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader: "Paris" twice in nine words; the dedication sentence split. Both taken.
Paris, January 1922. Eliot stopped there on his way back from Lausanne, and Pound went through the typescript with him, cutting it hard. The dedication came later. In 1923 Eliot wrote "il miglior fabbro" in Pound's copy, and from 1925 it was printed with the poem. The words are Dante's, for the poet Arnaut Daniel, and mean the better craftsman.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

BODY:
Paris, January 1922. Eliot stopped there on his way back from Lausanne, and Pound went through the typescript with him, cutting it hard. The dedication came later. In 1923 Eliot wrote "il miglior fabbro" in Pound's copy, and from 1925 it was printed with the poem. The words are Dante's, for the poet Arnaut Daniel, and mean the better craftsman.


## card sibyl

**As it stood**

SHOWS: A glass jar hung from a cord in a cave of layered rock; inside it, tiny, a crone crouched in profile with her hair down to her knees. Two boys on the cave floor, one pointing up at her.
ANSWERS: The epigraph, from Petronius: the Sibyl of Cumae, who asked for life without asking for youth, hanging in her jar while the boys ask "Sibyl, what do you want?" and she answers "I want to die."

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Petronius, Satyricon 48 (Trimalchio's boast: the Sibyl in her ampulla, the boys' question, "I want to die"); Ovid, Metamorphoses XIV.132–153 (years as many as the grains of sand; youth not asked for).
Substance:
1. "from Petronius: the Sibyl of Cumae, who asked for life without asking for youth" — the bargain is not in Petronius; it is Ovid's. As written the whole is fathered on Petronius. Split the attribution.
Draft (ANSWERS): The epigraph, from Petronius: the Sibyl of Cumae hangs in her jar while the boys ask "Sibyl, what do you want?" and she answers "I want to die." The bargain behind her state is Ovid's: years as many as the grains of sand in her hand, and no youth to live them in.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: keep

Reads as a model wrote it: no

Problems: none. "Who asked for life without asking for youth" tells me the story in one clause, and the answer text gives me the epigraph in English.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: correct. The bargain is Ovid's, not Petronius's; and Ovid's word is dust, not sand (the batch 1 scholar's finding, which the epigraph note now carries).
ANSWERS: The epigraph, from Petronius: the Sibyl of Cumae hangs in her jar while the boys ask "Sibyl, what do you want?" and she answers "I want to die." The bargain behind her state is Ovid's: as many years as the grains of dust in her hand, and no youth to live them in.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "The bargain behind her state is Ovid's" — "her state" is vague and "is Ovid's" made me ask whose bargain it was; the bargain is hers, the telling is his. Put: "The bargain that brought her to this is told by Ovid: as many years as the grains of dust in her hand, and no youth to live them in."

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader: "the bargain behind her state is Ovid's" made the bargain his. Taken.
ANSWERS: The epigraph, from Petronius: the Sibyl of Cumae hangs in her jar while the boys ask "Sibyl, what do you want?" and she answers "I want to die." The bargain that brought her to this is told by Ovid: as many years as the grains of dust in her hand, and no youth to live them in.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied

**As it now stands**

ANSWERS: The epigraph, from Petronius: the Sibyl of Cumae hangs in her jar while the boys ask "Sibyl, what do you want?" and she answers "I want to die." The bargain that brought her to this is told by Ovid: as many years as the grains of dust in her hand, and no youth to live them in.

ANSWERS: 13–18, "Marie, / Marie, hold on tight. And down we went. / In the mountains, there you feel free." (The Countess Marie Larisch, the archduke's cousin.)

**Scholar, round 1**

Checked: Valerie Eliot's note in the 1971 facsimile: the sledding lines came from Eliot's conversation with the Countess Marie Larisch (the identification's one source; the lines are not in her memoir My Past). Larisch was the niece of the Empress Elisabeth of Austria and first cousin of Crown Prince Rudolf. Lines 15–17 quoted exactly; 13–18 is the right span.
Substance:

What is documented: niece of the Empress Elisabeth, cousin of Crown Prince Rudolf, and — the fact that matters — Valerie Eliot's note that the sledding lines record Eliot's conversation with her. For a shared-file proposal, carry the source.

Keep: SHOWS as it stands; the quotation.

Draft (the bracket only): (Marie is usually identified with the Countess Marie Larisch, whose cousin was the archduke.)

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: correct. The bracket carries the source and drops "the archduke's cousin".
ANSWERS (bracket only): (Marie Larisch, niece of the Empress Elisabeth; Valerie Eliot's note in the 1971 facsimile says the sledding lines came from Eliot's conversation with her.)

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

## card belladonna

**As it stood**

SHOWS: A woman seated in a grotto of stone, in a black mantle and gown that leave only her face and hands bare; the pupils of her eyes are wide (belladonna drops); her lips are violet; she holds up a sprig of deadly nightshade, leaves, bells and berries. Sea visible through the mouth of the cave.
ANSWERS: 49–50. "The Lady of the Rocks" is Leonardo's *Virgin of the Rocks* (and Pater's Mona Lisa, older than the rocks among which she sits). Belladonna is the plant, the poison, the cosmetic and "beautiful lady".

Draft (ANSWERS): 49–50. "The Lady of the Rocks" recalls Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>, and Walter Pater's Mona Lisa, who "is older than the rocks among which she sits." Belladonna is the plant, the poison, the cosmetic and "beautiful lady."

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: "is" becomes "is usually traced to"; Pater given as a quotation.
ANSWERS: 49–50. "The Lady of the Rocks" is usually traced to Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>; the drawing also remembers Walter Pater's Mona Lisa, who "is older than the rocks among which she sits." Belladonna is the plant, the poison, the cosmetic and "beautiful lady".

**Scholar, round 1**

Substance: none ("O you who turn the wheel and look to windward" is site line 320; the four-station formula regno, regnavi, sum sine regno, regnabo is the medieval Wheel of Fortune's, and the stations listed match it in order.)

Decision: the drawing shows three arches; the bridge of 1922 (Rennie's, 1831–1967) had five. The text describes the drawing truthfully, so the wording stands; the proposal to the art and build threads is that the drawing has the wrong bridge, and that if it is redrawn the description should say "five arches", or if not, "its arches".

## The scholar's summary, round 1

The corrections that matter most, in order:
1. way-3 (and anything else that repeats it): Sosostris deals six cards, not seven — the Hanged Man she cannot find and the crowds are neither of them dealt. The site's own card drawing agrees (six on the table).
2. way-4 / cities@66 / eliot@66: the commute. "The one Eliot walked to Lloyds Bank" and "Eliot's route to work" are undocumented — he lived in Marylebone; the route is the London Bridge commuters', and his own note claims only that he "often noticed" the nine o'clock stroke. Also "about six hundred yards" undercounts the named route (bridge included it is about half a mile), and "Lloyds Bank, Lombard Street, across the road from the church" plus "the clock struck nine as he arrived" assert more than the sources do.
3. dead@2: the lilacs, not the dead land, are the first thing named after April.
4. dead@23: Eliot cites Ezekiel 2:1 (son of man), not the valley of dry bones; "Ezekiel's valley" misreports the note.
5. first@35: the passage sits between a four-line and a one-line Wagner quotation, not "two lines of Wagner"; and "the one moment of feeling in Part I" overclaims.
6. first@43 / women@43: "everything she deals comes true" / "right about everything" — Eliot's note underwrites four fulfilments; the rest are readings. "Afraid of the police" is the usual inference, not the text.
7. Readings passed as fact to mark or attribute: water@55 (the "reverse of the Grail legend" inversion is Brooks's), way-6 ("the section's weather is its argument", plus the lens does not tint every line — a misdescription of the tool), first@1 ("the poem is about things that will not finish"), eliot@35 (name Gordon and the 1934 Criterion).
8. Cards: sibyl (the youth-less bargain is Ovid's, not Petronius's), marie (carry Valerie Eliot's facsimile note as the source; "the archduke's cousin" is the poem read back onto her), crowd-on-london-bridge (Rennie's bridge had five arches, not three), belladonna ("usually traced to" for the Leonardo).
9. eliot@0: the dedication ("il miglior fabbro") belongs to 1923/1925, as the site's own front matter says, not to the January 1922 Paris work; "Pound's flat" is undocumented scene-setting.
Line references check out throughout in the site's count (312–321, 207–214, 320, 424, 360–366 all verified), as do the Woolf diary quotation, the fifty-four-line Boston opening, the participle count in first@1, and the Munich, Mylae, Webster, Ecclesiastes, Petronius and Tristan facts.


## The reader's summary, round 1

What I threw out most often was the ending. Nearly every note that went wrong went wrong in its last sentence, where a fact gave way to a phrase built to be quoted: "no water except the kind that drowns," "whether it is the weather or the people," "as sound, and as a wall," "Neither need you, yet," "The poem is about things that will not finish," "both are true at once," "The first water is unwelcome," "in a suburb." Next came the colon-and-three or the counted fragment standing in for a sentence: "a bridge, a street, a clock," "Two lines, in quotation marks, and a garden," "a card, a poison, a painting," "with a cold, afraid of the police, right about everything," "frightened and free." Then the poem as a person with a method, a trick or an argument: "The section's weather is its argument," "the poem's method for four hundred lines," "The poem's trick, learned from Baudelaire." Last, and worst for trust, the neat count that turned out wrong against the text: "between two lines of Wagner," "the first thing named after April," "the reading's one instruction."

What works and should stay: the ways in that simply say what a tool shows (way-1, way-2, the first three sentences of way-8); the stops that are a quotation and a source and nothing else (water@42, dead@62, dead@74, all the Cities and Eyes stops); and almost all the card texts, which are written by someone describing a drawing and say what is there. Two card texts carry developer notes ("back.svg," "group id=") that must not reach a reader.

Facts I have queried for the scholar, not changed: that this was Eliot's own walk to the bank (way-4, cities@66, eliot@66); "everything she deals comes true" (first@43, women@43); the king in the lake (eliot@8); the Marie Larisch and hyacinth-garden identifications, which want "usually identified" and a name (card marie, eliot@35); "Ezekiel's valley" against Eliot's citation of Ezekiel 2:1 (dead@23); jar or bottle (women@0).

Pathway intros, which are not in this batch and are proposals only: the Water intro is where Ethan's example lives ("The poem's hydrology... Follow the water and you follow the argument") and should be rewritten in the manner of the brief's own "After" sentence. "Each stop is a place to stand, not an explanation" (A first reading), "Every bird in the poem is a musician, and every song is a complaint" (Birds), "The poem's people cannot see, or see too much" (Eyes), "a map of where he was standing" (Eliot) and "two are queens; one is a bird" (Women) are the same tics at the head of each walk.



# Batch 6: the card notes in the tarot tool


## card-note phoenician-sailor

**As it stood**

NAME: The drowned Phoenician Sailor
NOTE: Not in any Tarot pack. He is Phlebas of Part IV, the merchant of Part III, and Ferdinand’s drowned father in *The Tempest*, whose eyes are pearls. “Fear death by water,” says the clairvoyante; Part IV obliges.
DECK: In this pack: a sailor in a striped jersey sinking head first through the water, a ship’s wheel already on the bottom, two gold pearls for eyes. He is drawn inverted on purpose: he is the Hanged Man the reading cannot find, gone into the water instead.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Eliot's notes 46 ("The Phoenician Sailor and the Merchant appear later… Death by Water is executed in Part IV") and 218 (the merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor, and the latter is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples"); The Tempest I.ii (Ariel's song, sung to Ferdinand about his father); "Fear death by water" at 55. "Not in any Tarot pack" is right — Eliot's note 46 says he departed from the pack to suit his own convenience.
Substance:
1. "He is Phlebas of Part IV, the merchant of Part III, and Ferdinand's drowned father" — three flat identities of unequal warrant. Phlebas has Eliot's note behind it. The merchant has the note's own, more careful verb: "melts into" — worth quoting rather than flattening to "is". "Ferdinand's drowned father" is wrong twice over: Eliot's note ties the Sailor to Ferdinand himself ("not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples"), not to the father; and in the play the father is not drowned — the pearls are Ariel's fiction, sung to Ferdinand. If the card wants the Tempest link, it is the song, and the song is a false report.
2. "Part IV obliges" — the substance is Eliot's own ("Death by Water is executed in Part IV"); the quip is the manner. The note's phrase does the work better.
Draft (NOTE): Not in any Tarot pack. He is Phlebas of Part IV; the one-eyed merchant, Eliot's note says, "melts into" him. The pearls are from Ariel's song in <i>The Tempest</i> — sung to Ferdinand about a father who has not in fact drowned. "Fear death by water," says the clairvoyante, and Eliot's note confirms it: "Death by Water is executed in Part IV."

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Not in any Tarot pack." — the fragment opener. One word makes it a sentence.
2. "He is Phlebas of Part IV, the merchant of Part III, and Ferdinand's drowned father in The Tempest, whose eyes are pearls." — three identifications given flat, with no source, when the source is Eliot's own notes (on 46: the Sailor "appears later"; on 218: the merchant "melts into the Phoenician Sailor, and the latter is not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples"). Two things for the scholar: Eliot's note makes him Ferdinand, not Ferdinand's father; and in the play the father is not drowned, Ferdinand only believes he is, which is what Ariel's song tells him. A reader who knows the play stops here.
3. ""Fear death by water," says the clairvoyante; Part IV obliges." — "Part IV obliges" is the bow: the poem as an obliging servant, a joke at the end of the note.
4. DECK: "he is the Hanged Man the reading cannot find, gone into the water instead." — the artist's idea stated as if it were the poem's. Same ruling as in batch 5.

Keep: the DECK's first sentence.

Draft:
NOTE: He is not a card in any Tarot pack. Eliot's note on line 46 says he appears later: he is Phlebas the Phoenician of Part IV, and Eliot's note on line 218 says that the one-eyed merchant "melts into" him and that he is "not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples" in <i>The Tempest</i>. "Those are pearls that were his eyes" is from Ariel's song to Ferdinand about his father, whom Ferdinand believes drowned. Madame Sosostris says "Fear death by water," and Part IV is called Death by Water.
DECK (last sentence only): He is drawn head down on purpose, in the posture of the Hanged Man on a tarot card, which is the card Madame Sosostris cannot find.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The fragment opener and "Part IV obliges" go; the three identifications replaced by what Eliot's notes say, with his verbs; "Ferdinand's drowned father" corrected (the note ties the Sailor to Ferdinand, and the father is not drowned).
NOTE: He is not a card in any Tarot pack. Eliot's note on line 46 says he appears later: he is Phlebas the Phoenician of Part IV, and the note on line 218 says that the one-eyed merchant "melts into" him and that he is "not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples" in <i>The Tempest</i>. "Those are pearls that were his eyes" is from Ariel's song to Ferdinand about his father, whom Ferdinand believes drowned; the father is alive. Madame Sosostris says "Fear death by water," and Eliot's note adds that "Death by Water is executed in Part IV."
DECK (last sentence only): He is drawn head down on purpose, in the posture of the Hanged Man on a tarot card, which is the card Madame Sosostris cannot find.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: both quotations from Eliot's note on line 218 are exact ("melts into"; "not wholly distinct from Ferdinand Prince of Naples"); the note on 46 is quoted exactly; Alonso lives.

**Reader, round 2**

## card-note belladonna

**As it stood**

NAME: Belladonna, the Lady of the Rocks
NOTE: “Beautiful lady,” the deadly nightshade, and the drops women used to widen their pupils. Leonardo’s *Virgin of the Rocks* is behind the title; the woman in the chair of Part II is in front of it. “The lady of situations” is gossip-column English.
DECK: In this pack: a woman in a grotto of stone, wrapped in black to the chin, pupils wide with the drops, lips violet, a sprig of nightshade held up; sea through the mouth of the cave.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: bella donna, the plant (Atropa belladonna), and the cosmetic use of the drops — all sound. The Leonardo gloss is the standard one; Part II's woman sits in "The Chair… like a burnished throne" (77).
Substance:
1. "Leonardo's Virgin of the Rocks is behind the title" — the universal gloss, but a gloss: Eliot names no painting. "Is usually traced to" (the register the site's model notes use) states it exactly.
2. "the woman in the chair of Part II is in front of it" — a reading passed as fact. Eliot's note 46 lists which cards reappear, and Belladonna is not among them; the identification with the woman of "A Game of Chess" is critical tradition (standard since Grover Smith), and should be marked as the usual reading.
3. "'The lady of situations' is gossip-column English." — an assertion about idiom with no source I can find. The guides guess variously (social intrigue; the "situations" of the newspaper columns); no one knows. Mark it as a guess or cut it.
Draft (NOTE): "Beautiful lady": the deadly nightshade, and the drops women used to widen their pupils. The title is usually traced to Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>, and the woman in the chair of Part II is often read as this card come true. Nobody has settled what "the lady of situations" means.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: yes

Problems:
1. ""Beautiful lady," the deadly nightshade, and the drops women used to widen their pupils." — three meanings with no verb. The facts are the right ones and want a sentence.
2. "Leonardo's Virgin of the Rocks is behind the title; the woman in the chair of Part II is in front of it." — an antithesis built for the semicolon. I had to work out that "behind" means source and "in front of" means what the card foretells. Both are readings and should be said as readings.
3. ""The lady of situations" is gossip-column English." — a quip given as a fact. If it is a known reading, say whose; if it is the site's, mark it as one.

Keep: DECK as it stands. It describes the drawing and stops.

Draft:
NOTE: Belladonna means "beautiful lady." It is also deadly nightshade, and the drops made from it that women once used to widen their pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" recalls Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>. The card is usually taken to foretell the woman at her dressing-table at the start of Part II. [Scholar: "gossip-column English" for "The lady of situations" needs an owner or a "one reading is."]

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The verbless triad made a sentence; the antithesis replaced by two readings marked as readings; "gossip-column English" cut, having no owner.
NOTE: Belladonna means "beautiful lady." It is also deadly nightshade, and the drops made from it that women once used to widen their pupils. "The Lady of the Rocks" is usually traced to Leonardo's <i>Virgin of the Rocks</i>, and the card is usually taken to foretell the woman at her dressing-table at the start of Part II. Nobody has settled what "the lady of situations" means.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: satisfied

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "is usually traced ... and the card is usually taken" — "usually" twice in one sentence. Put, for the second: "and the card is often taken to foretell the woman at her dressing-table at the start of Part II."

**Editor, after round 2**

Reader: "usually" twice. Taken.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: stands

2. "the Fisher King, who sits on the shore at line 424" — that the fisher at 424 is the Fisher King is a reading, if the usual one. "Is usually taken to be" costs four words.

## card-note blank

**As it stood**

NAME: The blank card
NOTE: “This card, / Which is blank, is something he carries on his back, / Which I am forbidden to see.” The one card the poem refuses to read. Weston’s merchants carried the Grail mysteries along the trade routes; whatever this is, it is not shown.
DECK: In this pack: bare paper inside the frame. Faintly, the merchant’s bundle shows through from the other side, reversed, as if the print on the back had bled. Something is there and cannot be read. Hold the pointer over it.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: the quotation of 52–54 (exact). Weston, From Ritual to Romance, ch. XII ("Mithra and Attis"): the Mysteries of Attis and Mithra travelled the Roman Empire by three channels, "First, commercial, through the medium of Syrian merchants… 'founding their chapels at the same time as their counting-houses'" (Weston citing Cumont; text checked on Gutenberg). The application of that passage to this merchant is Cleanth Brooks's (1939) and has been standard since.
Substance:
1. "Weston's merchants carried the Grail mysteries along the trade routes" — three slips in one clause. They are Syrian merchants; what they carried, in her account, were the Mysteries of Attis and Mithra — the cults she argues lie behind the Grail — not "the Grail mysteries"; and the whole is her thesis (built on Cumont), so it should be marked as her argument, not common fact. The reading that Mr. Eugenides therefore carries a mystery he knows nothing of is Brooks's.
Draft (NOTE): "This card, / Which is blank, is something he carries on his back, / Which I am forbidden to see." The one card the reading cannot open. Weston argued that the old Mysteries travelled the empire with the Syrian merchants, who founded "their chapels at the same time as their counting-houses"; read with her, the merchant carries a mystery he knows nothing of.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. The opening quotation repeats the lines on the card and in the poem beside it. It can go.
2. "The one card the poem refuses to read." — a fragment, and the poem as a person who refuses, which the brief names. It is also not what happens: Madame Sosostris is forbidden to see it, and the poem says nothing.
3. "Weston's merchants carried the Grail mysteries along the trade routes; whatever this is, it is not shown." — the Weston fact is the useful thing here, but the connection to the bundle is left for me to make, and "whatever this is, it is not shown" is a shrug for an ending that repeats "forbidden to see."
4. DECK: "Hold the pointer over it." — right to tell me, but say what happens, and remember a phone has no pointer.

Keep: DECK, first three sentences. "Something is there and cannot be read" is the sentence for this card.

Draft:
NOTE: Madame Sosostris is forbidden to see what the merchant carries on his back, and the poem never says. In Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> the Grail mysteries travelled with merchants along the trade routes, and one reading is that the bundle holds them.
DECK (last sentence only): Hold the pointer over it, or touch it, and the bundle shows more clearly. [Editor: if that is what the hover does.]

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The repeated quotation and "the poem refuses" go; Weston's claim corrected (Syrian merchants, the Mysteries of Attis and Mithra, her thesis) and the application to this merchant given to Brooks; the hover sentence made true for a phone.
NOTE: Madame Sosostris is forbidden to see what the merchant carries on his back, and the poem never says. Weston argued in <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> that the old Mysteries of Attis and Mithra travelled the Roman empire with Syrian merchants, who founded "their chapels at the same time as their counting-houses"; Cleanth Brooks (1939) read this merchant, on her account, as carrying a mystery he knows nothing of.
DECK (last sentence only): Hold the pointer over it, or touch it, and the bundle shows more clearly.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Syrian merchants, who founded 'their chapels at the same time as their counting-houses'" — the quoted words are Cumont's, which Weston quotes (From Ritual to Romance, ch. XII, with a footnote to Cumont); as written the note hands them to Weston. Replace the second sentence with: Weston argued in <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> that the old Mysteries of Attis and Mithra travelled the Roman empire with Syrian merchants, "founding their chapels at the same time as their counting-houses", as she quotes Cumont; Cleanth Brooks (1939) read this merchant, on her account, as carrying a mystery he knows nothing of.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. The Weston sentence is long, and "on her account" can be read as "on her behalf". Split it and say what is meant: "Weston argued in <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> that the old Mysteries of Attis and Mithra travelled the Roman empire with Syrian merchants, who founded "their chapels at the same time as their counting-houses". Following her, Cleanth Brooks (1939) read this merchant as carrying a mystery he knows nothing of."

**Editor, after round 2**

NOTE: Madame Sosostris is forbidden to see what the merchant carries on his back, and the poem never says. Following her, Cleanth Brooks (1939) read this merchant as carrying a mystery he knows nothing of.

What he carries on his back and what the fortune-teller was forbidden to see is evidently the knowledge of the mysteries (although Mr Eugenides himself is hardly likely to be more aware of it than Madame Sosostris is aware of the importance of her function)." ## card-note hanged-man

**As it stood**

NAME: The Hanged Man
NOTE: Card XII: a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, serene, haloed. Eliot linked him to Frazer’s Hanged God, the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain, and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus. Madame Sosostris does not find him. No sacrifice, no resurrection, in this deal.
DECK: In this pack: a dead tree with one long bough, a rope with its noose hanging open, and where the Hanged Man should hang, inverted, one leg crooked, the paper is left bare in his shape. Round the head that is not there, a gold halo. Far off on a road, two walkers and a hooded third.

---

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: Waite's card XII (hanged by one foot from living wood, face serene, nimbed) — the description is right. Eliot's note 46: "associated in my mind with the Hanged God of Frazer", and the hooded figure of the Emmaus passage — both quoted rightly in substance. Frazer's "Hanged God" is the chapter in Adonis Attis Osiris (the volumes Eliot names in his headnote): Attis, the god hanged in effigy on the pine, whose death and return renew vegetation.
Substance:
1. "the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain" — not Frazer's Hanged God. The Hanged God's office is the renewal of the year's growth; rain-making kings are a different part of The Golden Bough. The poem's drought makes the slide natural, but the clause states as Frazer what is the poem's own economy. "Whose death renews the year" (or "the land") is both true to Frazer and truer to Eliot's note, which claims only an association "in my mind".
2. "No sacrifice, no resurrection, in this deal." — the standard reading of the missing card (Brooks's line of argument) delivered as a verdict, in the fragment manner. Mark it as the reading it is, or let the fact ("Madame Sosostris does not find him") carry it.
Draft (NOTE): Card XII: a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, serene, haloed. Eliot's note associates him with "the Hanged God of Frazer" — the god whose death renews the year — and with the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus. Madame Sosostris does not find him; on the usual reading, the one death that would redeem the rest is missing from her pack.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: trim

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Card XII: a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, serene, haloed." — the colon fragment with a trailing pair. As with the Three of Wands, the living tree and the halo are the Rider-Waite design, and the note should say so. Scholar to confirm.
2. "the sacrificed king whose death brings the rain" — for the scholar: is the rain Frazer's or the poem's? Frazer's hanged god dies so that the land and crops are renewed; the rain is what the waste land wants. I do not change it, but a reader should not be handed the poem's need as Frazer's fact.
3. "No sacrifice, no resurrection, in this deal." — the bow: a fragment, a pair, and a reading of the missing card given as a fact. Cut it. If the point is wanted, mark it as a reading.
4. DECK: the batch-5 card text has a green shoot on the dead bough, and this one does not. If the drawing has the shoot it is the point of the dead tree, and worth the four words.

Keep: "Eliot linked him to Frazer's Hanged God ... and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus." "Madame Sosostris does not find him." The DECK.

Draft:
NOTE: He is card XII of the major arcana. In the Rider-Waite pack he is a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, calm-faced and haloed. Eliot's note on line 46 links him to the Hanged God of Frazer's <i>Golden Bough</i>, the king put to death so that the land will be renewed [scholar to settle "brings the rain"], and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris does not find him. [Optional, as a reading: One reading is that the sacrifice that would renew the land is missing from her cards.]

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: trim. The opener made a sentence and the pack named; "whose death brings the rain" corrected to Frazer (the Hanged God renews the year's growth; the rain is the poem's); the closing verdict cut, with the reading given to its owner.
NOTE: He is card XII of the major arcana. In the Rider pack he is a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, calm-faced and haloed. Eliot's note on line 46 links him to "the Hanged God of Frazer", the god whose death renews the year, and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris does not find him; since Cleanth Brooks (1939) the missing card has been read as the missing thing in her reading, the death that would be followed by a return.
DECK: unchanged, with "a green shoot on the dead bough" added at the end if the drawing has it (the artist's note says it does).

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
Checked: I could not reach the full text of Brooks's essay today (the reprints online were all refused). What I could verify: Brooks defines the Hanged Man as "the hanged god of Frazer (including the Christ)", associated with the hooded figure of Part V; his thesis is that sacrificial death may be life-giving; and of Madame Sosostris he says she warns "against death by water, not realizing any more than do the other inhabitants of the modern waste land that the way into life may be by death itself" (1968 reprint, p. 64). I found no sentence in which he reads the absence of the card itself.
1. "since Cleanth Brooks (1939) the missing card has been read as the missing thing in her reading, the death that would be followed by a return" — this gives Brooks a reading of the absence that I cannot find in him; what he wrote is about the card's meaning and about the death-by-water warning. Give him what he said and the absence-reading to those who drew it from him. Replace the last sentence with: Madame Sosostris does not find him. For Cleanth Brooks (1939) he is the sacrificed god whose death leads to new life, the thing she cannot see; readers since Brooks have taken the missing card for the missing thing in her reading, the death that would be followed by a return.

**Reader, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "since Cleanth Brooks (1939) the missing card has been read as the missing thing in her reading, the death that would be followed by a return" — "missing ... missing ... reading" made me read it twice, and "the missing thing" is vague. Put: "Madame Sosostris does not find him. Since Cleanth Brooks (1939) the absent card has been read as the one thing her reading lacks, a death that is followed by a return."

**Editor, after round 2**

Scholar: Brooks does not read the card's absence; give him what he said and the absence-reading to readers since. The batch 2 scholar, who reached the text, found Brooks's own explanation of her failure, the hood; it is used here. Reader: "missing ... missing ... reading" made plain. All taken.
NOTE: He is card XII of the major arcana. In the Rider pack he is a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, calm-faced and haloed. Eliot's note on line 46 links him to "the Hanged God of Frazer", the god whose death renews the year, and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris does not find him. Cleanth Brooks (1939) explained that by the hood ("That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him"); readers since Brooks have taken the absent card for the one thing her reading lacks, a death that is followed by a return.
DECK: unchanged, with "a green shoot on the dead bough" added at the end if the drawing has it.

**Scholar, round 3**

Verdict: satisfied
Checked: the hood sentence is verbatim in the 1947 reprint: "That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him" (the possessive as printed). For the record, Brooks goes straight on: "or rather, here again the palaver of the modern fortune-teller is turned to new and important account by the poet's shifting the matter into a new and serious context" — so the hood is his explanation, offered and then reframed rather than withdrawn. The note quotes him exactly and claims no more than he says; the absence-reading is rightly given to readers since him.

**Reader, round 3**

Verdict: not yet
1. "Cleanth Brooks (1939) explained that by the hood (...); readers since Brooks have taken the absent card for the one thing her reading lacks" — "explained that" leaves me asking what "that" is, and "taken ... for" can be read as "mistaken for". The facts stay as they are. Put: "Cleanth Brooks (1939) explained her failure by the hood: "That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him." Readers since Brooks have read the absent card as the one thing her reading lacks, a death that is followed by a return."

**As it now stands**

NOTE: He is card XII of the major arcana. In the Rider pack he is a young man hanging by one foot from a living tree, calm-faced and haloed. Eliot's note on line 46 links him to "the Hanged God of Frazer", the god whose death renews the year, and to the hooded figure on the road to Emmaus in Part V. Madame Sosostris does not find him. Cleanth Brooks (1939) explained her failure by the hood: "That he is hooded accounts for Madame Sosostris' inability to see him." Readers since Brooks have read the absent card as the one thing her reading lacks, a death that is followed by a return.
DECK: unchanged, with "a green shoot on the dead bough" added at the end if the drawing has it.


## card-note crowd-in-a-ring

**As it stood**

NAME: Crowds of people, walking round in a ring
NOTE: Not a card but what she sees: Dante’s neutrals running after their banner, Frazer’s villagers round the maypole, the commuters on London Bridge four lines later, the hooded hordes of Part V.
DECK: In this pack: a ring of walkers in bowlers and cloche hats, seen from above, going round clockwise on a bare plain, the path worn into the ground under their feet. The centre of the ring is empty.

**Scholar, round 1**

Verdict: correct
Checked: the vision is line 56; "Unreal City" is 60; the crowd flows over London Bridge at 62; the hooded hordes are Part V (site 369). Inferno III: the neutrals wheel after the whirling banner, and Eliot's note 63 cites that canto for the bridge crowd. Frazer's May-dances exist (The Magic Art), but no note of Eliot's points the ring there.
Substance:
1. "the commuters on London Bridge four lines later" — six lines later. Line 56 to 62 is six; four lines later is "Unreal City" (60), which has no commuters in it yet. The count is the checkable kind and it is off.
2. "Frazer's villagers round the maypole" — an association with no citation behind it, listed level with two links Eliot's own notes support (Dante at 63; the crowds at 46). Keep it marked as an association, or cut it.
Draft (NOTE): Not a card but what she sees. The crowd comes true six lines later, flowing over London Bridge — Eliot's note sends it to Dante's neutrals — and again in the hooded hordes of Part V.

**Reader, round 1**

Verdict: rewrite

Reads as a model wrote it: in places

Problems:
1. "Not a card but what she sees: Dante's neutrals running after their banner, Frazer's villagers round the maypole, the commuters on London Bridge four lines later, the hooded hordes of Part V." — the whole note is a colon and a list of four, with no verb and no sources, and it mixes what Eliot's note says (the "crowds of people" appear later) with two comparisons that are somebody's readings. Say which is which.
2. "four lines later" — the crowd on London Bridge is at line 62; "I see crowds of people" is line 56. Six lines. A count that exists to be neat and is wrong against the text.

Keep: DECK as it stands. "The centre of the ring is empty" is a description and it is the drawing's point.

Draft:
NOTE: This is not a card but something she sees. Eliot's note on line 46 says the "crowds of people" appear later: the crowd flowing over London Bridge at line 62 is the first, and the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" of Part V are usually taken to be another [scholar to add the site's line numbers]. The ring has been compared to the neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III, who run after a banner, and to Frazer's villagers dancing round the maypole.

**Editor, after round 1**

Decision: rewrite. The colon and the list of four replaced by what Eliot's note says and two comparisons marked as comparisons; "four lines later" corrected to line 62; the maypole kept only as an association with no owner, and the scholar may strike it.
NOTE: This is not a card but something she sees. Eliot's note on line 46 says the "crowds of people" appear later: the crowd flowing over London Bridge at line 62 is the first, and the "hooded hordes swarming / Over endless plains" at lines 369–70 are usually taken to be another. The ring has been compared to the neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III, who run for ever after a banner, and, with less behind it, to Frazer's villagers dancing round the maypole.

**Scholar, round 2**

Verdict: not yet
1. "and, with less behind it, to Frazer's villagers dancing round the maypole" — nothing is behind it: no note of Eliot's, no draft, no critic I can name, and the crowd walks rather than dances. The editor allowed me to strike it, and I do. Replace the last sentence with: The ring has been compared to the neutrals in <i>Inferno</i> III, who run for ever after a banner.
